Rationale
The CCIS series is devoted to the publication of proceedings of computer science conferences. Its aim is to efficiently disseminate original research results in informatics
in printed and electronic form. While the focus is on publication of peer-reviewed full
papers presenting mature work, inclusion of reviewed short papers reporting on work in
progress is welcome, too. Besides globally relevant meetings with internationally representative program committees guaranteeing a strict peer-reviewing and paper selection
process, conferences run by societies or of high regional or national relevance are also
considered for publication.
Topics
The topical scope of CCIS spans the entire spectrum of informatics ranging from foundational topics in the theory of computing to information and communications science
and technology and a broad variety of interdisciplinary application fields.
Information for Volume Editors and Authors
Publication in CCIS is free of charge. No royalties are paid, however, we offer registered
conference participants temporary free access to the online version of the conference
proceedings on SpringerLink (http://link.springer.com) by means of an http referrer from
the conference website and/or a number of complimentary printed copies, as specified
in the official acceptance email of the event.
CCIS proceedings can be published in time for distribution at conferences or as postproceedings, and delivered in the form of printed books and/or electronically as USBs
and/or e-content licenses for accessing proceedings at SpringerLink. Furthermore, CCIS
proceedings are included in the CCIS electronic book series hosted in the SpringerLink
digital library at http://link.springer.com/bookseries/7899. Conferences publishing in
CCIS are allowed to use our online conference service (Meteor) for managing the whole
proceedings lifecycle (from submission and reviewing to preparing for publication) free
of charge.
Publication process
The language of publication is exclusively English. Authors publishing in CCIS have
to sign the Springer CCIS copyright transfer form, however, they are free to use their
material published in CCIS for substantially changed, more elaborate subsequent publications elsewhere. For the preparation of the camera-ready papers/files, authors have to
strictly adhere to the Springer CCIS Authors’ Instructions and are strongly encouraged
to use the CCIS LaTeX style files or templates.
Abstracting/Indexing
CCIS is abstracted/indexed in DBLP, Google Scholar, EI-Compendex, Mathematical
Reviews, SCImago, Scopus. CCIS volumes are also submitted for the inclusion in ISI
Proceedings.
How to start
To start the evaluation of your proposal for inclusion in the CCIS series, please send an
e-mail to ccis@springer.com

Preface

This book compiles the best submitted papers to the Eight International Conference on
Optimization and Learning (OLA 2025). The choice was made according to the score
of all accepted long papers in the blind review process of the conference. OLA 2025
took place in Dubai, United Arab Emirates, April 23 to April 25. The main objective of
the conference is to attract influential researchers from all over the world in the fields
of complex problems optimization, machine and deep learning, synergies between both
research fields, and their applications on real-world problems. The conference builds a
nice atmosphere where relevant researchers present their innovative works.
Three categories of papers were considered in OLA 2025, namely ongoing research
works, high-impact journal publications (both of them in the shape of an extended
abstract), or regular papers with novel contents and important contributions. A selection
of the best papers in this latter category is published in this book.
Fifty-one papers were presented at OLA 2025, arranged into ten sessions, covering topics such as Deep Learning and Large Language Models, the synergies between
optimization and learning techniques, as well as quantum computing and applications
of learning and optimization problems in different domains. Also, four special sessions
were organized: i) Quantum optimization; ii) AutoML: Automatic design of ML models;
iii) Generative AI and its applications; and iv) Optimization and Large Langage Models
(LLMs).
The conference received a total of 70 papers, from which 30 compose this book, making 42.9% of all submitted papers. The review process was single-blind, and submissions
received on average two reviews each.

Advanced Models

KAN-Based On-Policy PPO Method
for Continuous Reinforcement Learning
Ali Bayeh , Malek Mouhoub , and Samira Sadaoui(B)
Department of Computer Science, University of Regina, Regina, Canada
{alibayeh,mouhoubm,sadaouis}@uregina.ca

Abstract. This study explores the integration of the KolmogorovArnold Network (KAN) model into continuous reinforcement learning
(CRL). We incorporate KAN and its improved variant, MultKAN, into
the on-policy PPO algorithm, resulting in a new approach called KANPPO. Using the Mujoco Half-cheetah environment, we conduct extensive
experiments to compare the performance and generalization of KANPPO models against MLP-PPO structures. Additionally, we examine the
eﬀects of dropout regularization through the DropKAN method in dense
network settings. Our ﬁndings demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of KAN and
MultKAN models in CRL, highlighting their potential for complex learning tasks.
Keywords: Kolmogorov-Arnold Network (KAN) · MulKAN · Policy
Proximal Optimization (PPO) · Continuous Reinforcement Learning
(CRL) · Actor-Critic Networks

1

Introduction

Reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved signiﬁcant progress in addressing complex tasks in various domains, but several challenges remain to be solved, including the generalization and robustness of its process. These challenges are further
ampliﬁed in continuous RL (CRL) due to the diversity and high dimensionality of the action and state spaces, which require more sophisticated methods to
ensure stable and eﬃcient learning. Traditional neural network (NN) architectures, while eﬀective, often fail to take advantage of the structural properties of
the agents and the environment in RL. Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs)
[13], based on the Kolmogorov-Arnold representation theorem, approximates a
function by decomposing it into structured components, potentially leading to
improved interpretability and generalization capabilities. Despite KAN success,
there is minimal research on the adoption of KAN models for RL tasks. To the
best of our knowledge, the use of KANs in RL has been limited to a single study
[9] that incorporated KAN models without hidden layers into a Proximal Policy
Optimization (PPO) framework for CRL tasks.
Our research aims to bridge this gap by integrating KAN and MultKAN
models into the on-policy PPO algorithm, tailored for CRL. We propose the
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 3–15, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_1

4

A. Bayeh et al.

KAN-PPO algorithm and evaluate its performance in the Mujoco Half-cheetah
robotic environment. To deepen our analysis, we investigate the impacts of denser
KAN architectures and the dropout-based regularization technique and assess
their inﬂuences on model robustness and performance. The common dropout
approach employed for MLP networks is ineﬀective for KANs [2]. We hence adopt
the DropKAN approach to implement dropout for the KAN-PPO algorithm. We
employ the average returns as a quality metric to compare the performance of
PPO and KAN-PPO across various MLP, KAN and MultiKAN architectures.
Furthermore, we conduct two other experiments: (1) assess the performance
of the MLP and KAN models using PPO and KAN-PPO in diﬀerent dropout
conﬁgurations and (2) analyze the impact of varying dropout rates on these
model eﬃciency.
This paper is organized as follows. Section 2 provides an overview on the KAN
and MultKAN architectures, the on-policy PPO algorithm, and regularization
in RL and KAN. Section 3 describes the related work on KAN and its numerous variations. Section 4 presents the steps of our KAN-PPO algorithm and its
parameter deﬁnitions. Section 5 describes the setup of the experiment, including the case study and parameter ﬁne-tuning. Section 6 evaluates our KANPPO method under several conﬁgurations. Lastly, Sect. 7 concludes our work
and includes some future directions.

2

Background

2.1

Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks

KANs [12, 13], based on the Kolmogorov-Arnold Representation theorem, can
learn the activation functions during the training process. This theorem indicates
that a continuous multivariate function .f (x) = f (x1 , . . . , xn ) can be expressed
as a sum of univariate functions applied to a linear combination of inputs [13]:
2n+1

f (x) =

Φq

.

where .ϕq,p : [0, 1] → R are univariate inner functions and .Φq : R → R are
univariate outer functions. KAN directly parametrizes the above equation by
representing the inner and outer univariate functions using smooth, learnable
components such as B-splines. Each KAN layer composed of two structural layers: (1) a middle layer with .2n + 1 neurons, corresponding to the inner functions
.ϕq,p and (2) the output layer representing the summation of the outer functions
.Φq . In KAN architecture, the activation values are deﬁned recursively between
layers as follows [13]:

MultKAN:

Multiplicative KANs (MultKANs) [12] extend the structure of

KANs [13] by incorporating explicit multiplicative operations into their computational framework. Similarly to the KAN architecture, a MultKAN layer
transforms an input vector .xl ∈ Rnl into an output vector .zl ∈ Rml , but a
MultKAN layer has additional multiplicative operation which applies elementwise multiplications on pairs of sub-nodes produced by the previous step. The
output of a MultKAN is presented as follows:
.Ψl (xl ) = Ml (Φl (xl )) where .Ψl combines the output of the KAN layer .(Φl )
and the multiplication layer .(Ml ) A deep MultKAN architecture can be deﬁned
recursively layer by layer. The complete computation for a MultKAN is the
composition of all layers [12]:
M ultKAN (x) = (ΨL ◦ ΨL−1 ◦ . . . Ψ0 ) (x)

.

2.2

On-Policy Algorithms

The on-policy algorithms in RL, like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), operate by leveraging data generated directly from the current policy. The objective
of these algorithms is to optimize the policy while maintaining stability and preventing drastic updates that could lead to divergence [17]. In order to achieve
this objective, several structural rules have been developed for on-policy algorithms. The purpose of PPO is to enhance the stability and eﬃciency of training in RL by estimating the policy gradient. This method can be employed
within a stochastic gradient ascent algorithm, which adjusts the policy parameters on an iterative basis. The standard form of the gradient algorithm [17]:
.ĝ = Êt ∇θ log πθ at |st Ât where .πθ is a stochastic policy, .Ât is an estimator
of .At under the current policy at timestep .t, and .Êt [·] represents the empirical
average computed over a ﬁnite batch of data. The advantage function .Ât is estimated using generalized advantage estimation (GAE), which attempts to reduce
the variance at the cost of an extra variable and is as below [17]:
2

At = δt + (γλ) δt+1 + (γλ) δt+2 + . . .

.

where .γ is the discount factor, .λ is a parameter controlling the balance between
bias and variance in .At , and .δt is the temporal diﬀerence (TD) error at time
step .t, which is deﬁned as, .δt = rt + γV (st+1 ) − V (st ), where .rt is the reward at
time step .t and .V (st ) is the value function for state .st . To control the signiﬁcant
alterations in the policy, PPO utilizes a clipped surrogate objective function. The
policy is then updated using the ratio of probabilities between the new and old

6

A. Bayeh et al.

policies, subject to a clipping threshold. The ﬁnal objective function is therefore
deﬁned as follows [17]:
LCLIP +V F +S θ = Êt LCLIP θ − c1 LV F θ + c2 S πδ st

.

where .LCLIP θ is the clipped objective, .LV F θ is the value function loss, and
S πδ st is the entropy bonus term, while .c1 and .c2 being constants that control
the trade-oﬀ between these components.

.

2.3

Regularization

Regularization in RL: Dropout is a widely recognized regularization technique in deep learning. This technique is used to mitigate overﬁtting by introducing stochasticity during the training phase of NNs. Researchers have developed a
wide range of dropout methods, tailored to diﬀerent architectures and learning
tasks [16]. Despite its extensive use in supervised learning, the dropout technique remains underutilized in RL, where the continuous and dynamic nature
of observed environments poses unique challenges [6]. Recent studies indicate
that incorporating dropout into RL tasks can yield beneﬁts comparable to those
observed in supervised learning. However, there is evidence that the direct application of standard dropout techniques to RL is frequently problematic due to the
distinctive characteristics of RL tasks, such as temporal dependencies and policy optimization. This limitation highlights the necessity for developing dropout
methods speciﬁcally customized to the nature of RL algorithms [4,6,7].
Regularization in KANs: Adopting dropout in KAN was explored in [2],
where the authors proposed the DropKAN approach as a customized dropout
strategy for KANs. For MLP models, dropout is typically applied between hidden
layers. However, their analysis [2] revealed that applying conventional dropout
between layers in KANs can lead to unpredictable behavior during the feedforward pass, making this approach unsuitable for the unique KAN structure. In
conventional MLPs, when a node is dropped, all associated weights are eﬀectively deactivated during both the feed-forward and backward passes, since the
dropped node contributes a zero input, resulting in zero gradients and no impact
on the loss function during that training step. In contrast, in KAN layers, simply
applying dropout to the outputs does not completely exclude the masked nodes
from the network computations. Although the outputs of these nodes are set to
zero, they serve as inputs to subsequent activation functions, which generally
do not map zero to zero. Consequently, the nonzero activations propagated to
the next layer cause the corresponding spline coeﬃcients to be updated during backpropagation. DropKAN incorporates the dropout mechanism directly
within the KAN layers to address this issue. Speciﬁcally, dropout masks are
embedded in the KAN computation graph, where speciﬁc activation outputs are
randomly masked during training. Experimental results [2] demonstrated that
DropKAN consistently achieved improved generalization performance compared
to the standard dropout strategy when applied to KAN architectures.

KAN-Based On-Policy PPO Method for Continuous Reinforcement Learning

3

7

Related Work

KANs, developed for decades, have been explored as alternatives to traditional
MLP architectures in various applications. The study [20] investigated the eﬃcacy of KANs in time series forecasting, with a particular focus on satellite
traﬃc prediction. Their ﬁndings indicate that KAN-based models demonstrated
superiority over MLP models by consistently recording lower errors. Overall,
KANs achieve performance comparable to conventional methods when using
fewer parameters.
The paper [5] examined the performance of KAN models in vision tasks by
incorporating a KAN-based architecture into the original MLP-Mixer framework. It also assessed the eﬃcacy of the modiﬁed KAN-Mixer model on the
MNIST, CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 datasets. The results showed that KANMixer attained competitive performance, particularly on the MNIST dataset,
with a test accuracy of 98.16%. These ﬁndings were derived from comprehensive
experiments with a training batch size of 32, which revealed the potential of
KAN models to enhance vision-related tasks.
Another work [3] analyzed the potential of KANs as an alternative to MLPs
in Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). The authors suggested two KAN-based GNN
architectures, KAGCN and KAGIN, analogous to GCN and GIN, respectively,
building on the KAN architecture. A comparative evaluation of KANs and MLPs
on node classiﬁcation, graph classiﬁcation and graph regression tasks revealed
that while both models perform similarly in classiﬁcation tasks, KANs demonstrate a clear advantage in regression problems.
The research [8] introduced Graph Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (GKANs),
which extended the KAN framework to encompass graph-structured data. Compared to traditional GCNs, GKANs oﬀer a ﬂexible alternative by replacing ﬁxed
linear weights in traditional graphs with learnable spline-based functions. The
authors proposed two architectural frameworks, distinguished by the learnable
functions: before or after aggregation. The outcomes indicate that the proposed
model exhibited superior accuracy and parameter eﬃciency on the Cora dataset
compared to GCNs.
The study [1] proposed a neural operator based on KANs, which incorporates
Gaussian RBFs in place of the B-splines which is used in the original KAN
architecture. The DeepOKAN framework employs KAN models as substitutes for
MLPs within the branch and trunk components of the operator. The model was
evaluated on a series of physics-based problems, including 1D sinusoidal waves,
2D orthotropic elasticity, and transient Poisson’s equations. The experimental
results highlighted the superiority of DeepOKAN in terms of accuracy and the
reduction of training losses in comparison to the DeepONets framework.
Lastly, [10] examined the use of KAN applications within the context of
neural ordinary diﬀerential equations. It conducted experiments on Physics systems including the Lotka–Volterra model, wave propagation and the Schrödinger
equation. The experiment showed that KAN-ODEs exhibited superior performance to MLP-based Neural ODEs regarding training eﬃciency, accuracy, and
scalability. Moreover, the framework exhibited advantages in terms of inter-

8

A. Bayeh et al.

pretability, enabling the visualization of learned activation functions and symbolic regression of source terms.

Fig. 1. Integration of KAN Model into the Actor and Critic of PPO Algorithm.

4

Methodology

This study enhances the PPO algorithm by leveraging KAN as the core component of its architecture. The KAN framework is integrated into the actor and
critic networks of PPO, replacing the traditional MLP commonly used in RL.
Throughout this study, we refer to this enhanced version of PPO as KAN-PPO.
The algorithm 1 presents the steps for the KAN-PPO method, which is also
illustrated in Fig. 1.
To evaluate the performance of KAN models compared to MLPs, we design
several network architectures for the actor and critic networks. We consider identical architectures for the actor and critic networks in all variations. The network
architectures were designed to evaluate the impact of varying the number of hidden layers and neurons per layer. Four conﬁgurations are considered, diﬀering in
the number of hidden layers and the number of units in each layer. Speciﬁcally,
architectures with one hidden layer and two hidden layers were implemented.
For each hidden layer, networks with 32 or 64 neurons were constructed. To
evaluate the eﬀect of dropout on model performance, variations of each network conﬁguration were created by incorporating dropout layers. This approach
allows us to systematically explore the eﬀects of both architectural complexity
and regularization through dropout.
The structural diﬀerences between KANs and MLPs require the implementation of distinct dropout strategies. The common dropout approach, which has
been successfully employed for MLP networks, was found to be ineﬀective for
KANs [2]. We adopt the DropKAN approach to implement dropout for the
KAN-PPO algorithm. In addition to the standard hyperparameters required for
PPO algorithm, two additional hyperparameters are introduced for KAN-based
algorithms: (1) the number of grid intervals .(g), which essentially controls how

ﬁnely the domain of the input is divided when approximating the activation

All models were trained by minimizing their respective objective functions

where .α0 is the initial learning rate and .δ is the decay rate. The KAN-policy
model and KAN-value function are parameterized by .θ and .μ, respectively. Since
KAN models operate based on learnable activation functions, these functions are
deﬁned with a set of coeﬃcients, with .θ and .μ referring to these coeﬃcients.

5

Experiment Setup

All experiments were conducted using Python, leveraging the PyTorch framework for training deep learning models. The main structure of PPO algorithm
was implemented using the StableBaselines3 library [15].
5.1

Mujoco Half-cheetah Environment

For evaluating the numerous CRL models, we utilize the robotic model called
“Mujoco Half-cheetah” [18,19]. This environment is widely used to assess ML
algorithms for CRL systems. This legged agent aims to maximize its forward
velocity by learning a coordinated movement patterns. The state space of the
agent comprises the positions and velocities of all its joints, and the action space
corresponds to the torques applied to those joints. The agent receives a reward

10

A. Bayeh et al.

proportional to its forward velocity, encouraging it to learn eﬃcient and coordinated locomotion strategies. All experiments were evaluated using version 5 of
the Half-cheetah environment from the Gymnasium library.
5.2

Hyper-parameter Tuning

Table 1 summarizes the hyperparameter’s values for each conﬁguration. These
values are ﬁxed and remain consistent across all model variations.

Validation

6.1

PPO and KAN-PPO Performance

3
3
0.2

Our study utilizes the average returns as the quality metric to compare the
performance of diﬀerent models. Figure 2 presents the performance of PPO and
KAN-PPO under various MLP, KAN and MultKAN architectures. For MLP
models without dropout, all conﬁgurations produced comparable results. The
best performance was achieved by the model with a single hidden layer containing
32 neurons, while the worst performance was observed for the model with two
hidden layers and 64 neurons per layer. This trend suggests that increasing the
number of neurons in the absence of dropout can lead to overﬁtting. In contrast,
the inclusion of dropout consistently improved the performance of MLPs. The
best results were obtained from the MLP conﬁguration with a single hidden

KAN-Based On-Policy PPO Method for Continuous Reinforcement Learning

11

layer and 64 neurons, closely followed by the model with two hidden layers and
the same number of neurons. The poorest performance was recorded for the
MLP model with a single hidden layer and 32 neurons. This outcome indicates
that, for models with fewer neurons, dropout may overly diminish the eﬀective
capacity, thereby reducing performance relative to conﬁgurations with higher
neuron counts.

Fig. 2. Performance of PPO and KAN-PPO under various architectures in Half-cheetah
environment. Plotted curves represent the mean returns averaged over three independent trials with diﬀerent random seeds. Shaded regions indicate the range of returns,
spanning from the minimum to the maximum observed during the trials.

For the KAN and MultKAN models without dropout, the results indicate
superior performance compared to the MLP models. The KAN-based models
not only achieved higher average returns, but also showed greater stability during training. While in RL, typically the stability of the process can be achieve
by converging to a suboptimal solution, the higher average return observed with
KAN suggests that (although its stability also stems from a suboptimal solution) it generally converges to a better outcome than MLP models. The best
performance among the KAN and MultKAN models was achieved by conﬁgurations with two hidden layers of 32 neurons each, followed by KAN models with
a single hidden layer comprising 32 or 64 neurons. For KAN models, the poorest
performance was observed in the conﬁguration with two layers of 64 neurons,
while all other KAN conﬁgurations achieved comparable results.
With respect to the MultKAN models, the lowest performance was recorded
for single-layer architectures. Overall, the MultKAN models achieved higher
average returns compared to their KAN counterparts, suggesting that additional

12

A. Bayeh et al.

multiplication operations in the MultKAN layers enhance the robustness of the
model for more complex tasks. However, the inclusion of this multiplication
layer appears to reduce the performance of single-layer MultKAN models relative to single-layer KAN models. This observation highlights the need for further
investigation to understand the impact of the multiplication layer on model performance across diﬀerent conﬁgurations. For KAN-based models incorporating
dropout, the results were signiﬁcantly degraded compared to their counterparts
without dropout. The best performance among these models was only marginally
better than the worst performance of the models without dropout.
These observations highlight the detrimental eﬀect of dropout on KAN-based
models and underscore the need for a deeper understanding of its impact. To
address this, we conduct additional studies to further investigate the inﬂuence
of dropout on the performance and stability of KAN models.
6.2

Impact of Dropout Layers

Fig. 3. Performance of MLP and KAN models, evaluated using PPO and KAN-PPO
algorithms and analyzed under various dropout conﬁgurations in Half-cheetah environment. Solid lines in the plots represent the average returns across trials. Shadow areas
represent the range of returns.

The study [2] analyzed the eﬀect of dropout on KAN and introduced the DropKAN model. However, their study was limited to models with a single hidden
layer. We extend this study by evaluating the dropout eﬀect on more complex
tasks and denser architectures. We ﬁrst conduct a comparative study using the
DropKAN model with two hidden layers. Each conﬁguration was evaluated with
two neuron counts per layer: 32 and 64 units. We also include MLP models with
similar conﬁgurations to provide a comprehensive comparison. This comparison
allowed us to measure the impact of dropout within KAN architectures relative
to conventional MLP models.
For this study, all hyperparameters were set according to Table 1, except for
the batch size, which was reduced to 512. The results of the study are presented
in Fig. 3. Each conﬁguration is labeled with an abbreviated name. For example,

KAN-Based On-Policy PPO Method for Continuous Reinforcement Learning

13

“Mlp 32 d 0.0,0.2” denotes an MLP model with 32 neurons per layer, with
dropout of 0 for the ﬁrst layer and 0.2 for the second layer.
The results show that dropout consistently impacts the performance of MLP
models. The best performance was achieved with the conﬁguration that included
64 neurons per layer and a dropout applied to both layers. All other MLP conﬁgurations exhibited relatively similar outcomes, indicating a degree of robustness
to architectural variations. However, in the MLP model with 32 neurons per
layer and dropout applied to both, the performance was notably unstable and
reduced compared to other MLP conﬁgurations. This suggests that, in this speciﬁc case, the application of dropout may have diminished the eﬀectiveness of
certain neurons, thereby negatively impacting overall performance.
In contrast to MLP models, KAN models showed variable and less predictable
performance with respect to dropout conﬁgurations. Interestingly, KAN models
with 32 and 64 neurons per layer performed best when the dropout was applied
only to the second layer. The worst performance was recorded for the KAN
model with 64 neurons per layer when dropout was applied only to the ﬁrst
layer, highlighting the sensitivity of KAN models to the placement of the dropout
layers.
Although the results of [2] showed that DropKAN model signiﬁcantly outperforms the traditional dropout approach for KAN architectures, the results
of our ablation study indicate that the proposed model exhibits unpredictable
behavior when applied to more complex, densely structured models and tasks.
These observations suggest that the application of dropout regularization to
KAN-based architectures requires further investigation to better understand its
eﬀects and optimize its implementation in such scenarios.
6.3

Impact of Dropout Factor

Building on the results of the ﬁrst study, we conduct a follow-up analysis to
examine the impact of varying dropout rates. For this study, we select the best
performing model from the previous experiment: the DropKAN model with 32
neurons per layer, where dropout was applied exclusively to the second layer.
Starting with a dropout rate of 0.1, the rate incrementally increased to 0.5
for the second layer. Figure 4 presents this experiment’s results, illustrating the
DropKAN model’s performance across diﬀerent dropout rates. The results indicate a clear decline in model performance as the dropout probability increases
from 0.1 to 0.5. Despite this decline, the performance remained consistent across
trials for each dropout rate, demonstrating the model’s predictable response to
changes in the dropout factor. These ﬁndings highlight that the unpredictable
behavior observed in the main study was primarily attributable to the placement
of dropouts within diﬀerent layers, emphasizing the sensitivity of KAN-based
models to the speciﬁc location of dropout application.

14

A. Bayeh et al.

Fig. 4. Performance of KAN models under diﬀerent dropout conﬁgurations in Halfcheetah environment. Lines show the mean of returns across trials. Shadow regions
represent the range of returns.

7

Conclusion and Future Work

In this study, we introduced the KAN-PPO algorithm by integrating KANbased models into the PPO architecture. By replacing traditional MLPs with
KAN-based architectures in the actor and critic networks, we aimed to leverage KAN’s unique function approximation capabilities within RL frameworks.
We performed an empirical analysis and comparison using the Half-Cheetah
environment to demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of the proposed method. Furthermore, we investigated the impact of dropout on KAN-based models through
two conﬁgurations: (1) the placement of the dropout layer and (2) variations
in the dropout factor. Although the ﬁndings reveal certain challenges with the
current dropout implementation for KANs that require further attention, the
overall results highlight the robustness of KAN-based architectures in maintaining consistent performance across diﬀerent conﬁgurations. This work opens
several future directions, including the integration of KAN or MultKAN into
oﬀ-policy algorithms such as Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG) and
Soft Actor-Critic (SAC).

Bayesian and Partition-Based
Optimization for Hyperparameter
Optimization of LLM Fine-Tuning
Nathan Davouse(B)

and El-Ghazali Talbi

Univ. Lille, CNRS, Inria, Centrale Lille, UMR 9189 CRIStAL, 59000 Lille, France
nathan.davouse@inria.fr, el-ghazali.talbi@univ-lille.fr

Abstract. Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized natural language processing (NLP) by achieving state-of-the-art performance
across diverse tasks. However, ﬁne-tuning these models for domainspeciﬁc applications is signiﬁcantly constrained by the computational
costs associated with their training. In this paper, we propose two complementary approaches to address the Hyperparameter Optimization
(HPO) challenge in LLM ﬁne-tuning: Bayesian Optimization based on
Gaussian Process (BO-GP) and Partition-Based Optimization (PBO).
On the one hand, BO eﬃciently exploits historical knowledge to achieve
optimal results within a limited number of evaluations, but its inherently
sequential nature poses scalability challenges. On the other hand, PBO
enables massive parallelization, making it more scalable but requiring
signiﬁcantly more evaluations to converge. To leverage their complementary strengths for optimizing expensive objective functions, we investigate these methods and propose a hybrid BO-PBO algorithm. This work
represents a foundational step toward harnessing the potential of parallel
Bayesian Optimization-based algorithms for solving expensive optimization problems in exascale computing environments.
Keywords: Bayesian Optimization · Fine-tuning · Partition-Based
Optimization · Large Language Model

1

Introduction

With the advent of transformer architecture [38], coupled with signiﬁcant
advancements in the ﬁeld of High Performance Computing (HPC), Large Language Models (LLM) have demonstrated exceptional abilities in language understanding and text generation, ﬁrmly establishing themselves as the State-of-theart (SOTA) in Natural Language Processing (NLP). Models such as GPT-4
[30] and LLaMA 3 [17] have rapidly penetrated the personal and professional
domains, transforming workﬂows in academic, industrial and everyday applications.
Despite their success, the computational and data requirements for training
LLMs from scratch make this approach impractical for most users. Fine-tuning
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 16–33, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_2

BO and PBO for HPO of LLM Fine-Tuning

17

has emerged as a critical paradigm, adapting pre-trained models to specialized
tasks using smaller domain-speciﬁc datasets. Although ﬁne-tuning reduces costs
compared to full training, it remains resource intensive, particularly for largescale models. To address this, Parameter Eﬃcient Fine-Tuning methods (PEFT)
[18], such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) [22], have become prevalent. LoRA
and its derivatives (e.g., QLoRA [12], LoRA + [19]) reduce computational overhead by using reparametrized weight matrices, where large weight matrices are
approximated with lower-rank decomposition. This approach enables ﬁne-tuning
even on limited-hardware setups.
Even with advancements in PEFT, a signiﬁcant barrier persists: selecting
optimal hyperparameters. Unlike model parameters, which are learned during
training, hyperparameters must be manually speciﬁed and profoundly inﬂuence
model performance. For LoRA-based ﬁne-tuning, hyperparameters such as rank
and scaling factors are critical [37], along with classical parameters such as learning rate and weight decay.
Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) is a well-studied problem [6,7,14] in
Automated Deep Neural Networks (autoDNN) design [34]. However, HPO for
LLM ﬁne-tuning presents unique challenges due to the prohibitive cost of evaluating the objective function. Each evaluation entails computationally expensive training or ﬁne-tuning process, often requiring hours to days on highperformance hardware. These constraints necessitate the adoption of highly eﬃcient optimization techniques.
Bayesian Optimization (BO) [32] is a widely recognized approach for tackling
expensive black-box optimization problems. Its ability to model the search space
using a surrogate function allows it to minimize costly evaluations. However, BO
is by nature sequential, limiting the use of HPC cluster, or exascale computer.
In contrast, Partition Based Optimization (PBO) methods [28,29], which divide
the search space into subregions, enable parallel evaluations but typically require
more function calls to converge. The complementarity of these methods made
way for hybrid algorithms, using surrogate to enhance PBO algorithms.
In this work, we present the HPO problem for LLM ﬁne-tuning, to address
it with Bayesian Optimization and Partition Based Optimization methods. By
comparing these methods, and trying the hybridization, we aim to open the
way for parallel Bayesian algorithm for optimization of very expensive objective
function with exascale computing.

2

Problem Definition

Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) of LLM Fine-Tuning is a very recent problem in literature, with a ﬁrst review on article [26], but HPO for Deep Neural
Network (DNN) is a known ﬁeld with the autoML community. For further reading, Talbi write a taxonomy of autoDNN in article [34].
This problem can be classiﬁed as a black-box objective function optimization, i.e. the objective function cannot be formulated analytically and can’t be
derived. This characteristic constrains the sets of methods to be used directly.

18

N. Davouse and E.-G. Talbi

Following article [35] notation, this problem is also a Mixed-Variables Optimization Problem (MVOP), w.r.t. the set of values possible for each hyperparameter.
The last key aspect of this optimization problem is the cost of evaluating a solution, it can take dozens of minutes to hours, restraining the number of possible
evaluations.
Given a model M, a training dataset Dtrain , a validation dataset Dval
and a set of hyperparameters η, the black-box function can be expressed as
F(η, m, Dtrain , Dval ). This function includes the training of the model, and also
the evaluation using Dval , and allow linking hyperparameters to training and to
evaluation. In this work, the optimization is solely single objective, so the result
of the function F is in R.
Given this function, the optimization problem can be expressed as Eq. 1,
where H is the search space of hyperparameters. The aim of this problem is to
ﬁnd one value being the maximum of the function F (·, ·, ·, ·).
η ∗ ∈ arg max F(η, M, Dtrain , Dval )
η∈H

2.1

(1)

Search Space

The search space is deﬁned with the choice of hyperparameters, theirs bounds,
theirs types and even theirs scales. Well-deﬁned search space is crucial for a
correct application of HPO : if the space is too high-dimensional, the HPO
algorithm will need too many shots to converge to a good solution, if it’s too
small, we are missing it’s relevance.

The search space is composed of 5 hyperparameters, from classical training
hyperparameters to LoRA speciﬁc ones. Equation 2 summarize LoRA application, w.r.t. it’s hyperparameter, with W0 the weights of the pre-trained model
and W = ΔW the additional gradients to add to obtain W the weights of the
ﬁne-tuned model. A detailed presentation of hyperparameters is just below, but
one can look at table 1 for a summary.
– LoRA rank : the common rank of matrices A and B, scaling the reduction in
terms of number of parameters. It’s an integer, and it’s value range from 1 to
64 to deal with hardware constraints.
– LoRA scale (α) : α is used to scale the values of B ∗ A when added to W0 .
On this work, due to LitGPT framework, it’s an integer, from 1 to 64.
– Learning rate : the learning rate is a classical hyperparameter used in HPO,
weighting the gradient of each weight when doing back-propagation. It is
often tuned in a logarithmic scale, to manage eﬀectively the exploration.
Hyperparameters value is between 10−10 and 10−1 .

BO and PBO for HPO of LLM Fine-Tuning

19

– Dropout probability : based on article [33], dropout is a method used to
prevent over-ﬁtting, by randomly ﬁxing cells/layers to zeroes during one iteration. Being a probability, it’s bounds by 0 and 1.
– Weight decay : weight decay is used to improve generalization capacity, as
proved in article [24], by reducing the weights at each iteration by a small
value, to force the model to use new inputs. Typically, the parameter for
weight decay is set on a logarithmic scale between 10−3 and 10−1 .
Table 1. Summary of Hyperparameter Search Space
Hyperparameters Optimization range
Type Conversion

f (x) = 10x

cont. f (x) = x

−3

−1

log.

f (x) = 10x

For the 2 integers variables (LoRA rank and LoRA scale), to adapt to continuous optimization algorithms, relax and round methods will be applied. It
means that the integers constraints is relaxed when generating a solution, and is
rounded when evaluating a solution. Others methods like computing with lower
and upper discrete value can be used, but this one was kept for simplicity and
computation costs. For the 2 variables with logarithmic scale (learning rate and
weight decay), to explore with consistency the search space, the optimization
algorithm will be bound between the exponential values of the bounds, and a
logarithm will be applied when evaluating a solution.
2.2

Objective Function

Classically with DNN, the function to optimize might be the loss, sometimes
normalized like Cross-entropy Loss or the accuracy. To avoid data leakage, the
function is computed over a validation dataset, and even a testing dataset, different from the training one. For LLM, the datasets are a lot more diverse than
for images classiﬁcation, and the loss is biased to value the performance of a
LLM, since it take all probabilities inside the function.
A second option to evaluate LLM ﬁne-tuning is to use the accuracy over
standard benchmarks, using multiple-questions choices to process an eﬃcient
evaluation of the performance of the LLM. Article like [39] explain that ﬁnetuned model have better generalization performance, so it’s relevant to evaluate
on a diﬀerent dataset than the training, even if the layout of the inputs change.
State-of-the-art example for ﬁne-tune model are HELLASWAG [42] or MMLU
[20,21]. In this paper, the function to maximize with the HPO is the accuracy
on the Hellaswag datasets.

20

2.3

N. Davouse and E.-G. Talbi

Related Work

To locate this work on a global ﬁeld, this section aims to review relevant problematic and contributions close to this paper.
– AutoDNN : the global autoDNN ﬁelds has been thoroughly explored last
years. Article [34] make a review and a taxonomy of this global ﬁelds. HPO
applied to LLM ﬁne-tuning diﬀers from this especially by the cost of evaluation and the evaluation of the model. Methods or speciﬁc problem of autoDNN
are now infeasible with LLM due to the costs. Speciﬁc methods are then
needed.
– LLM applied to EA : when looking at interaction between LLM and EA,
a lot of articles [9,10,25] are using LLM to enhance or popularize EA and
optimization algorithms. This approach isn’t relevant to our contribution,
since our contribution focus on our optimization expertise.
– Prompt optimization : when linking optimization and LLM, some papers
[13, 41] aims to optimize the prompt, and work with the entire LLM as a
generating black box, without thinking about the architecture or the weights.
This approach has limited eﬀects on LLM, since the performance are still
bound by model training, and they replace the end users for chatbot deployment.
– HPO applied to generation hyperparameter : some hyperparameters like the
temperature are only used when LLM are generating tokens. Some are working
on these hyperparameters, to avoid training costs of working directly with the
ﬁne-tuning hyperparameters.

3

Design and Implementation

In this work, two approaches are considered to perform Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) : Bayesian Optimization based on Gaussian Process (BO-GP)
and Partition Based Optimization (PBO) algorithms like Simultaneous Optimistic Optimization (SOO) [28]. To extract the best of these two approaches, a
hybrid approach will also be presented.
Figure 1 describe the global workﬂow of Hyperparameter Optimization
(HPO), to link with following sections aiming to describe sections of the workﬂow.
The workﬂow is budget-based, meaning all algorithms will be compared
with the same number of evaluations, i.e. the budget. This approach is eﬀective because it ensures a fair comparison by focusing on the evaluation cost,
which is the primary bottleneck in optimization algorithms, rather than computational overhead, which is negligible in this context. The computing time of
the algorithm is negligible in front of evaluation time, making the number of
evaluation a relevant budget metric.

BO and PBO for HPO of LLM Fine-Tuning

21

Fig. 1. HPO workﬂow

3.1

Fine-Tune and Evaluate LLM

HPO is a problem deﬁned for speciﬁc black-box function, and must adequate
with the characteristic of this objective function. As said in Sect. 2, in this work,
the function can be deﬁned as an expensive, mixed-variables, noisy black-box
function. This part aims to present this function, allowing to reproduce it.
The backbone model is LlaMa 3.2-1B [17], a model of the Llama 3 family
of model. Llama 3 models were released by Meta AI in the second part of 2024
(from July to December), as open-source (topology and weights) decoder-only
models achieving state-of-the-art performance. Among Llama 3 models, Llama
3.2 release propose lightweight models (from 1B to 3B weights, excluding vision
models), enabling reproduction of the experiments.
The ﬁne-tuning is performed using AdamW [27] optimizer, along LoRA methods to keep eﬃcient ﬁne-tuning. To achieve performance comparable with full
ﬁne-tuning, LoRA is used on keys, queries, values and attention head output
weight matrices, enabling the training of whole Multi-Head Attention (MHA)
layers.
Then, the training data Dtrain is the Alpaca dataset, published after the
Standford Alpaca Project. It’s composed of 52k lines of IA generated and cleaned
inputs, aiming to improve the behavior of a LLM. This dataset is widely used
[11,12,43] for ﬁne-tuning, guiding our choice for a standard conﬁguration.
For evaluation, Hellaswag [42] dataset was used as Dval , with accuracy as
the metrics to optimize with HPO. It’s a 40k lines datasets released in 2019 as
a challenge datasets, with a random performance of 25 % . All models are also
evaluated on MMLU [21] as a testing dataset, to observe HPO over-ﬁtting.
In terms of framework, all this part is done using LitGPT [36], a framework
develop by Lightning AI team, based on Pytorch [3] environment. This framework is thought for a command line interface utilization, and will be kept as
it is in this work. Behind this framework, it’s PyTorch for training the model,
Huggingface for loading model and datasets, and lm_eval library for managing
evaluation.

22

3.2

N. Davouse and E.-G. Talbi

Bayesian Optimization (BO) : Gaussian Process (GP) Based
Optimization

Bayesian Optimization is often deﬁned as a "sequential model-based approach to
solving problem" [32]. A surrogate model is used to build a posterior considering
prior knowledge formed on known points. On this posterior, an acquisition function is computed to act as the surrogate function for the function to optimize.
Many surrogate models can be used like regression tree [2] or Gaussian Process
(GP) [31]. For further details about BO, one can read review [32].
On this work, a focus is done on GP for the BO surrogate. GP use the kernel
trick to build a Bayesian non-parametric regression model. It uses a mean vector
mi and a covariance matrix Ki,j to deﬁne the prior function as Eq. 3.
f|X ∼ N (m, K)

(3)

Algorithm 1 oﬀer an overview of the BO process. To ease the ﬁrst build
of the surrogate, it’s crucial, as proven in article [40], to sample eﬃciently the
search space. This sampling provides information for the Gaussian Process to
estimate the function. Like article [8], Latin Hypercube Sampling (LHS) is used
as a sampling method, for a deﬁned budget called n_init.
Algorithm 1. BO-GP
Input: Ω,f ,KD ,O,facq ,ninit ,nopt
1: for i = 1 to ninit do
2:
λ ← LHS(Ω, D, ninit )
3:
D ← D ∪ {(λ , f (λ ))}
4: end for
5: for i = 1 to nopt do
6:
KD , μD ← Fit(GP(KD , μD ), D)
7:
λ ← Optimize(facq (KD ), O)
8:
D ← D ∪ {(λ , f (λ ))}
9: end for
10: return best of {(λ∗ , f (λ∗ )) ∈ D}

Initiate with Latin Hypercube Sampling
Sample one point
Add solution and evaluation to set of data
Optimization loop
Generate new point
Evaluate new point

After this preliminary phase, a second phase is done with loop containing the
update of the Gaussian Process, the optimization of the acquisition function to
obtain a new point to evaluate and the evaluation. After the evaluation of the
point, the point is added to the history D and so on. The loop end based on a
budget nopt , with the budget nmax = ninit + nopt
For this algorithm, the ﬁrst requirement is the search space, and the objective
function already described in 2.1 and 2.2 respectively. On the GP part, we need to
deﬁne a Kernel function KD , an acquisition function facq and an Inner Optimizer
O. The acquisition function is logEI, more reliable than EI, based on article [1].
The kernel and the inner optimizer are the standard implementation of Botorch,
introduced in the next paragraph, with a radial basis function kernel and multistart optimization method.

BO and PBO for HPO of LLM Fine-Tuning

23

BoTorch [4] is a Bayesian Optimization library built on PyTorch, designed
for eﬃcient and scalable optimization of expensive black-box functions. Leveraging PyTorch’s GPU acceleration and integration with GPyTorch [16] for Gaussian Processes, BoTorch enables advanced surrogate modeling and optimization.
Botorch is used on this work for all tasks using GP, including this part and
Sect. 3.4
3.3

Partition Based Optimization : Simultaneous Optimistic
Optimization (SOO)

In global optimization, a lot of methods are based on the partition of the search
space [23, 28, 29]. These approaches are mostly deterministic, and enhance intrinsic parallelization ability. For these methods, the dimensionality of the problem is
a key to choose the speciﬁc algorithm. With a dimensionality around 5, based on
benchmarks at the end of article [15], the Simultaneous Optimistic Optimization
(SOO) [28] algorithm seems a good way to start.

Algorithm 2. SOO
Input: Ω,f ,K,nmax
1: x0,0 ← center(Ω),f0,0 ← f (x0,0 ),T1 = {x0,0 , f0,0 , Ω}
Initiate the tree with the
center of Ω
2: n ← 1
3: while n < nmax do
4:
νmax ← −∞
5:
for h = 0 to depth(Tn ) do
Select best open leaf for depth h
6:
j ← arg maxj∈{j|(h,j)∈Ln } f (xh,j )
7:
if fh,j > νmax then
perform K-section of
8:
Ωh+1,kj+1 , . . . , Ωh+1,kj+K ← section(Ωh,j , K)
Ωh,j
9:
for i = 1 toK do
10:
n←n+1
11:
xh+1,kj+i ← center(Ωn )
evaluate the point, the scoring
12:
fh+1,kj+i ← f (xh+1,kj+i )
Add leafh+1,j+i to tree Tn
13:
Tn ← {(xh+1,kj+i , fh+1,kj+i , Ωn+1 )}
14:
end for
15:
νmax ← fh,j
16:
end if
17:
end for
18: end while
19: return best of xh,j , f (xh,j )

SOO is a tree-based space partitioning method for black-box optimization,
inspired by Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) methods. SOO is called optimistic
since it assume the existence of l such that f (x∗ ) − f (x) ≤ l(x, x∗ ) where x∗ is
the maximizer of x. The algorithm partitions the space Ω by building a tree with

24

N. Davouse and E.-G. Talbi

smaller and smaller subspace Ωh,j . The node (h, j), node number j of depth h,
is scored at the center of his space.
An expanded node have K children, making the tree a K-nary tree, K = 3
here. Ln is the open list of the tree, to avoid expanding the same nodes over and
over. At each round, SOO expand a maximum of one node by depth, meaning
that each round score a maximum of depth ∗ (K) solution, enhancing the parallel
evaluation of the solution. Summary of SOO is present in algorithm 2
The original algorithm manages the end of the loop with the hmax (n) function, limiting the depth of the tree search. To compare diﬀerent algorithm, the
stopping criterion here is nmax , the evaluation budget.
3.4

Hybrid Approach : Bayesian Multi-Scale Optimistic
Optimization (BaMSOO)

Surrogate Model-Based Optimization (SMBO) algorithms harness the exploitation of the information to deﬁne a cost-reduced function to optimize. This approach ensure exploitation but have several limitations, including the intrinsic
sequential nature. On the other hand, Partition-based approach are massively
parallel, but are computation costly in front of very expensive objective function. To overcome both limitations, hybrid methods, using surrogates and space
partition, were developed.
In this work, we focus on BaMSOO, a SOO based algorithm. Like SOO,
BaMSOO performs a K-inary partitioning of the space, using the center of the
partition to evaluate.
UCB(x|Dt ) = μ(x|Dt ) + BN ∗ σ(x|Dt )
with BN =

2 log(π 2 N 2 /6η), η ∈ (0, 1)

(4)

The diﬀerence lies primarily in the scoring g(.) of the leaf, with algorithm 3
replacing the scoring of SOO (line 12 of algorithm 2). In the face of an expensive
objective function, BaMSOO leverages a GP surrogate to estimate the potential
of a point, using the Upper Conﬁdence Bound (UCB) as a measure of expected
performance.
Given a partition with center x and historic evaluations Dt , the UCB of x,
deﬁned in Eq. 4, is compared against the best evaluation so far, f + . In this equation, η is a hyperparameter to deﬁne manually, and N the number of evaluations.
If the UCB is higher than f + , the algorithm evaluates x directly using the objective function f (.). Otherwise, the partition is scored using the Lower Conﬁdence
Bound (LCB) of x, reﬂecting the lower bound of potential improvement.
To sum up, this algorithm prevents unpromising evaluations in order to allocate more budget for exploring more promising areas than SOO. This hybrid
approach harness a part of BO-GP exploitation of knowledge without losing the
intrinsic parallel abilities.
For the implementation of the GP components, including the calculation of
LCB and UCB scores, the BoTorch library was employed. This choice ensures

1: if UCB(xh+1,kj+i , DN ) ≥ f + then
2:
gh+1,kj+i ← f (xh+1,kj+i )
3:
N ←N +1
4: else
5:
gh+1,j+i ← LCB(xh+1,kj+i , DN )
6: end if
7: if gh+1,j+i > f + then
8:
f + ← gh+1,j+i
9: end if

if x may be better than previous score
Evaluate x
index of the number of real evaluation
Penalize with LCB
+

is the highest score of the tree

computational eﬃciency and robustness, as BoTorch provides a modular framework for Bayesian optimization and GP modeling, seamlessly integrating with
the partition-based structure of BamSOO. By adhering to the implementation
outlined in Sect. 3.2, the framework ensures consistency in surrogate modeling
and acquisition function computation.

4

Computation Experiments

Experiments are done using Grid5000 [5], “a large-scale and ﬂexible testbed for
experiment-driven research in all areas of computer science, with a focus on
parallel and distributed computing including Cloud, HPC and Big Data and
AI.” In speciﬁc, the chuc cluster, in the Lille center, composed of nodes of 4
GPU A100 with 40G of VRAM was used.
Apart from aforementioned hyperparameters (learning rate, LoRA rank ... )
and conﬁguration (weight matrix to apply LoRA), all arguments of LitGPT CLI
is used with default value. The only exception is the number of epochs, ﬁxed
to 1 to reduce computation costs. For next sections, a diﬀerence will be made
between variables (value inside optimization range), and hyperparameters (value
used by the training function) to clarify the reading of the value.
Using this conﬁguration, one epoch of ﬁne-tuning is taking around 31 min.
For the evaluation on both datasets, it’s taking around 12 min. Based on previous
articles, and evaluations durations, the total evaluation budget for experiments is
50 evaluations by each algorithm, including a sampling budget of 10 for Bayesian
Optimization.
The implementation of the previous algorithm, the objective function and
next experiments are all stored in GitHub, following this link : https://github.
com/Kiwy3/BO_PBO_HPO_LLM.
4.1

Sampling Experiment

For the sampling experiment, Latin Hypercube Sampling (LHS) was used with
the same budget as other, i.e. 50 evaluations. Whole experiments took 36 h. The
aims of these experiments is dual: ﬁrst, make a lower bound reference for others

26

N. Davouse and E.-G. Talbi

experiments, and second, explore the search space and the score behavior. To
use as a lower bound, LHS achieve scores of 37.6% for MMLU, and 47.9% for
Hellaswag. These score are inside table 2 (Fig. 2).

Fig. 2. Results of Latin Hypercube Sampling experiment

The correlation between MMLU and Hellswag tend to conﬁrm the relevance
of their choice as a pair for validation and testing dataset. It’s suﬃciently high
to guess that most of the good solution for one will be good for the other one,
but it’s still less than one, making over-ﬁtting visible if present.
For variables, it looks like LoRA alpha, the scaling parameter of LoRA, is
the most inﬂuent on the score, be it Hellaswag or MMLU. Next to it, LoRA
Rank and weight decay are the most inﬂuent, with a diﬀerence in ranking on
the metrics. With this ﬁrst exploration, it looks like Dropout and Learning rate
are not very eﬀectful for this problem, be it the choice of their range or the
implementation.
On the distribution of the score values, it’s interesting to note that Hellaswag
has a broader range of score, making it useful for eﬃciently discriminating solutions. With a broader thinking, the sampling experiment conﬁrm the relevance
to apply HPO algorithms to the described problem. If scores were independent
of variables, or all scores were the same, HPO would be useless.
4.2

BO-GP Experiment

Figure 3 depicts the performance of Bayesian Optimization based on Gaussian
Process (BO-GP) over 50 iterations, measured in terms of the Hyperparameter
accuracy. This visualization highlights the evolution of the optimization process
as it transitions from sampling to the exploitation, and ultimately converges
towards high-performing solutions.
During the sampling phase, as shown by Fig. 3a, evaluated solutions have
the same diversity of solutions than the full sampling experiment, allowing to
eﬃciently extract knowledge from the search space. The algorithm achieves a
stable convergence to mostly high-performing solution.
If we brieﬂy look at Fig. 3b, it’s interesting to look at the diversity of conﬁguration to obtain high-performance, and the trend of the algorithms for each

variable. For instance, for LoRA alpha, or learning rate, we can observe a clear
trend toward the lower bound of the optimization range.
To summarize, this experiment demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of Bayesian
Optimization in eﬃciently exploiting the knowledge of the search space obtained
in the sampling phase. The optimization process still explores area with high
uncertainty, giving few low-performing solution.
4.3

SOO Experiment

On this experiment, we observe in Fig. 4 the behavior of SOO as it optimizes the
given objective function. The ﬁgure illustrates how the algorithm navigates the
search space, iteratively improving the solution by adjusting the hyperparameters. By examining the trends in performance metrics and variables evolution,
we aim to analyze the convergence behavior, stability, and overall eﬀectiveness
of SOO in this setting.

(a) Score over time

(b) Variable values over time

Fig. 4. Experiment using SOO algorithm

28

N. Davouse and E.-G. Talbi

Figure 4a depicts the accuracy of the hyperparameter dataset as a function
of iterations, with marker colors representing diﬀerent depth conﬁgurations. The
observed trend suggests that most conﬁgurations achieve accuracies around 0.46
throughout the optimization process. Several conﬁgurations converge toward the
higher end of this range, indicating potential stability and eﬀectiveness in learning as iterations progress.
It is evident that higher-depth conﬁgurations tend to cluster around higher
accuracy values, while lower depths display more scattered behavior across the
accuracy spectrum. In speciﬁc, depth 2, with a section on lora alpha seems to
greatly aﬀect the performance on the accuracy. On Fig. 4b, it’s clear on the
limitation of constrained-budget SOO without local exploitation at the end of
the algorithms: Only a few number of values by variables are explored: around 5
by variables, making it equivalent to exploration of a search space of 65 = 7, 776
discrete solutions.
To summarize, SOO achieve a maximum performance closed to the precedent
algorithm, but explore a restricted number of conﬁguration, and lose time by
evaluating unpromising solutions.

BaMSOO Experiment

The last experiment of this work is using BaMSOO, with η = 1 in Eq. 4, to
reduce the conﬁdence interval and promote approximation, to avoid the evaluation of unpromising points. To look after it, Fig. 5a discriminate evaluated and
approximated points, and might be compared with 4a to look after approximated
points.

experiment, 16 points were approximated, to eﬃciently search further than SOO.
Figure 5b compare the budget allowed on each depth, and highlight the focus
of BaMSOO for lower depth exploration. To summarize, BaMSOO achieve to
speed up SOO, but is still slow in comparison with BO at ﬁrst glance. Moreover,
BaMSOO succeed to prevent most of low-performing evaluation of SOO.

have an higher range of results for comparison.

5

Conclusions and Perspectives

For scenarios with a limited number of evaluations, BO demonstrated exceptional eﬃciency, quickly converging to high-performing solutions. In contrast,
SOO, constrained by one-dimensional sections and less promising search dynamics, exhibited slower convergence. The hybrid approach, BaMSOO, successfully
enhanced the convergence rate of SOO while preserving its inherent parallelism
capabilities.
This work pioneers the application of hybrid Bayesian and partition-based
optimization for expensive functions, laying the groundwork for scaling the eﬃciency of Bayesian Optimization to exascale computing environments.
Future research should explore higher-performance architectures, larger models, and expanded datasets to eﬀectively distribute the computational load across
supercomputers. Additionally, extending the search space to include hyperparameters such as Adam momentum would provide new opportunities to further
reﬁne optimization strategies.

and Algorithms at Exascale) project under grant ANR-22-EXNU-0002.

supported by a scientiﬁc interest group hosted by Inria and including CNRS,

grid5000.fr.

On Hyperparameter Optimization
of Generative Models in Recommendation
Systems: A Comparative Study,
and Applications
Sanae Filali-Zegzouti1(B) , Oumayma Banouar2 , and Mohamed Benslimanem1
1

Sciences, Engineering and Management Laboratory, Sidi Mohamed Ben Abdellah
University, Fez, Morocco
sanae.filalizegzouti@usmba.ac.ma
2
Laboratory of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science, Cadi Ayyad
University, Marrakech, Morocco

Abstract. Recommendation systems (RS) have become essential for
providing personalized user experiences across various platforms, suggesting items ranging from products to media content. In recent years,
the incorporation of Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), a groundbreaking generative modeling technique introduced in 2014, has significantly enhanced the capabilities of RS. Despite the promise of GANs
in recommendation systems, their performance is highly sensitive to the
choice of hyperparameters. In this paper, we aim to optimize the hyperparameters of a GAN-based approach for learning user latent factors
in matrix factorization, with the goal of improving top-N recommendations. Our study evaluates diﬀerent Hyperparameters Optimization
methods, additionally, we will study the advantages and limitations of
each method.
Keywords: Generative adversarial networks · recommendation
systems · top-N recommendations · hyperparameter optimization ·
GAN

1

Introduction

In our digital age, the exponential growth of data has led to the necessity for
intelligent systems capable of managing and making sense of this information.
Among these systems, Recommendation Systems (RS) and Generative Models
have emerged as crucial tools in a variety of applications, from eLearning to
e-commerce. RS are designed to suggest relevant items to users, signiﬁcantly
impacting industries like retail, entertainment, and social media. By analyzing user behavior, preferences, historical data and demographic data, RS aim to
deliver personalized content, thus so improving user experience and engagement.
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 34–50, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_3

On Hyperparameter Optimization of GAN in RS

35

There are primarily three types of recommendation systems: content-based ﬁltering, collaborative ﬁltering, and hybrid methods. Content-based ﬁltering recommends items like those a user has liked in the past, whereas collaborative ﬁltering
suggests items based on the preferences of similar users. Hybrid methods combine both approaches to leverage their respective strengths [1–3, 23]. Generative
Models are a subset of machine learning models that can generate new data
samples from the learned data distribution. Unlike discriminative models, which
predict labels given input data, generative models learn the underlying structure of the data, allowing them to create new instances that are indistinguishable from the real data. These models, such as Generative Adversarial Networks
(GANs) and Variational Autoencoders (VAEs), have revolutionized ﬁelds like
image synthesis, text generation, and data augmentation. GANs consist of two
neural networks, a generator and a discriminator, that compete in a zerosum
game, leading to the creation of highly realistic synthetic data [4,22,23]. The
combination of RS and generative models holds the promise of delivering highly
personalized and innovative user experiences by leveraging the strengths of both
technologies. Generative models can enhance RS by creating synthetic data to
address sparsity issues and generating novel content to recommend. However,
both RS and Generative Models face signiﬁcant challenges in achieving optimal performance, often hindered by the complexity of their conﬁgurations. This
is where Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) becomes crucial. HPO involves
ﬁne-tuning the hyperparameters that control the learning process of these models, ensuring they operate at their best. In this paper, we propose a comparative
study to optimize the hyperparameters of a GAN-based approach for learning
user latent factors in matrix factorization to enhance top-N recommendations.
Additionally, we will study the advantages and limitations of each method. The
key contributions of this paper are summarized as follows:
– Hyperparameter Optimization in GAN-Based Models: Conducts a comparative analysis of hyperparameter optimization techniques for improving the
learning of user latent factors in matrix factorization using GANs.
– Enhancing Top-N Recommendations: Investigates the impact of optimized
GAN conﬁgurations on the eﬀectiveness of top-N recommendation performance.
– Comprehensive Method Evaluation: Provides an in-depth assessment of different hyperparameter optimization strategies applied to GAN-based recommendation models, highlighting their respective advantages and limitations.
– Signiﬁcance for Recommendation Systems: Emphasizes the critical role of
hyperparameter optimization in enhancing the practical applicability and performance of GAN-based Recommendation systems.
The remaining of this article is organized as the following. Section 2 presents
GAN for Recommendation Systems. Section 3 details hyperparameter optimization methods used in this comparative study. In Sect. 4, we present and discuss
the obtained results. The conclusion closes the paper.

36

S. Filali-Zegzouti et al.

2

GAN for Recommendation Systems

This section explores the concept of Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs)
based RS. The ﬁrst work to incorporate GANs into RS was IRGAN [5], proposed in 2017, which aimed to merge discriminative and generative paradigms
in information re-trieval (IR). GraphGAN leverages the graph structure of useritem interactions and uses GANs to learn embeddings for nodes in this graph.
CFGAN [6]identiﬁes a problem with IRGAN’s training method and proposes
vector-wise training for GANs in RS. Other works have utilized GANs with additional information beyond user-item interactions [7]to address the sparsity of RS
ratings and to model contextual recommendations [8]. Current GAN approaches
like CFGAN attempt to generate a user’s preferences for all items by learning
from the user’s actual preferences. GANs models consist of two neural networks:
the generator and the discriminator, engaged in a competitive game.
2.1

Mathematical Formulation of GAN

Generative adversarial networks, introduced by Goodfellow et al. in 2014 [22],
are deﬁned as a two-player game involving the following elements:
– Probability Space: Denoted as (Ω, μref ), where Ω represents the sample
space and μref represents a probability measure on that space. This deﬁnes
the GAN game.
– Two Players: The generator and the discriminator.
– Generator’s Strategy Set: Represented as P(Ω), the set of all probability
measures μG on the sample space Ω.
– Discriminator’s Strategy Set: Consists of Markov kernels μD : Ω →
P([0, 1]), where P([0, 1]) is the set of probability measures on the interval
[0, 1].
– Zero-Sum Game: The GAN game is zero-sum, meaning the gain of one
player is balanced by the loss of the other.
– Objective Function: The objective function of the GAN game is deﬁned as
follows:
L(μG , μD ) = Ex∼µref ,y∼µD (x) [ln(y)] + Ex∼µG ,y∼µD (x) [ln(1 − y)],

(1)

where E denotes the expectation operator, x ∼ μref signiﬁes sampling from
the reference distribution, and x ∼ μG signiﬁes sampling from the generator’s
distribution. The generator aims to minimize this objective function, while
the discriminator aims to maximize it.
This adversarial process drives the generator to produce data indistinguishable from real data, improving its generative capabilities. Typically, the generator moves ﬁrst, followed by the discriminator, resulting in a minimax game
formulation (Fig. 1):
min max L(μG , μD ) = Ex∼µref ,y∼µD (x) [ln(y)] + Ex∼µG ,y∼µD (x) [ln(1 − y)].
µG

µD

(2)

On Hyperparameter Optimization of GAN in RS

37

Fig. 1. Architecture of GAN

We now focus on GAN variants applied to recommendation systems (RS),
which improve recommendation quality and address various challenges by adapting components of GANs to speciﬁc tasks.
2.2

GAN-Based Matrix Factorization for Recommendation Systems
(GANMF)

Generative Adversarial Network with Matrix Factorization (GANMF) [9] is
an advanced model designed for user proﬁle generation by integrating Matrix
Factorization into a Conditional Generative Adversarial Network (cGAN). In
GANMF, the generator’s actions are guided by speciﬁc information such as user
identiﬁers, making the model conditional. This allows the generator to produce
more tailored and accurate user proﬁles. The model employs an autoencoderbased discriminator, which distinguishes real user proﬁles from generated ones
using reconstruction error as an energy function. By using an autoencoder, the
discriminator eﬀectively identiﬁes discrepancies between real and synthetic data.
Matrix Factorization is used within the GAN framework to capture essential
data patterns through user and item latent factors, enhancing the model’s ability to represent user preferences. The generator utilizes these latent factors,
along with user identiﬁers, to produce synthetic proﬁles that reﬂect individual user preferences. The generator minimizes the discriminator’s reconstruction
error to deceive it, ensuring the synthetic proﬁles are realistic. Tailored loss
functions, including maximum mean discrepancy, are used to balance the plausibility of generated proﬁles with their ability to capture user traits, ﬁnetuning
the model’s performance. Training involves iterative updates between the generator and discriminator until the discriminator cannot diﬀerentiate between real
and synthetic proﬁles, signifying the generator’s success. This process can be
expressed mathematically as follows:

38

S. Filali-Zegzouti et al.

Conditional GAN (cGAN): GANMF is based on the framework of a Conditional
Generative Adversarial Network, where the generator’s behavior is conditioned
on speciﬁc information, such as a user identiﬁer y. The objective function for
the cGAN is deﬁned as:
min max Ex∼pdata [log D(x | c)] + Ez∼pz [log(1 − D(G(z | c)))].
G

D

(3)

Autoencoder-Based Discriminator: Unlike conventional cGANs that use binary
classiﬁers as discriminators, GANMF employs an autoencoder-based discriminator, where the reconstruction error serves as the energy function:
D(x) = Dec(Enc(x)) − x 22 .

(4)

where Enc and Dec are the encoder and decoder of the autoencoder, respectively.
Matrix Factorization (MF): GANMF innovatively combines GAN architecture
with Matrix Factorization techniques. It includes two embedding layers: one for
user latent factors and another for item latent factors. The synthetic user proﬁle
is generated as:
(5)
G(y) = V Σ[y, :] ,
where V and Σ are the item and user latent factor matrices, respectively.
Loss Functions: To optimize the generator, GANMF employs a composite loss
function that balances adversarial loss, feature-matching loss, and regularization
terms:
LG (x, y) = (1 − α)D(G(y)) + α Enc(x) − Enc(G(y)) 22 + λG ΩG 22 .

(6)

where:
– (1 − α) and α control the trade-oﬀ between adversarial and feature-matching
losses.
– ΩG 22 is a regularization term with weight λG .

Training Algorithm. The following Algorithm 1 outlines the training process
for the GANMF model. It involves alternating updates between the generator
and discriminator, using loss functions tailored to optimize both components.
The generator learns to produce synthetic user proﬁles that mimic real ones,
while the discriminator distinguishes between real and generated proﬁles based
on reconstruction errors.
