This approach enhances the robustness of recommendation systems, particularly in handling sparse data, leading to improved recommendation accuracy.
However, GANMF also faces several limitations. The complexity of integrating
GANs with matrix factorization adds signiﬁcant computational resources and
longer training times. The training stability of GANs is a well-known challenge,
often leading to mode collapse and instability.

3

HPO in GAN-Based Matrix Factorization
for Recommendation Systems

A GAN-based approach for learning user latent factors in matrix factorization
signiﬁcantly enhances top-N recommendations by capturing complex user-item
interactions. However, the performance and stability of such models heavily
depend on selecting the right hyperparameters. Hyperparameter Optimization
(HPO) plays a crucial role in ﬁne-tuning these parameters to improve training
stability, boost performance, and reduce the risk of overﬁtting. Various HPO
techniques, including Grid Search, Genetic Algorithm (GA), Simulated Annealing (SA), Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO), and Bayesian Optimization, can
be employed to eﬃciently explore the hyperparameter space. By leveraging these
advanced optimization methods, we can systematically identify the optimal conﬁguration, ensuring that the model achieves higher accuracy, faster convergence,
and better generalization in real-world recommendation tasks. Hyperparameter
Optimization is the process of tuning the hyperparameters of machine learning

40

S. Filali-Zegzouti et al.

algorithms to achieve the best performance. Unlike model parameters, which
are learned during training, hyperparameters are set before the learning process
begins and signiﬁcantly inﬂuence the model’s behavior and performance.
Mathematical optimization involves ﬁnding the best solution from a set of
candidates to either maximize or minimize an objective function. Optimization
problems are classiﬁed as constrained or unconstrained, depending on whether
there are limitations on decision or solution variables [13]. In unconstrained optimization, a decision variable x can freely take any real value. An unconstrained
optimization problem can be formulated as:
min f (x),
x∈R

(7)

where f(x) is the ob jective function.
In contrast, real-world optimization problems often involve constraints. In
constrained optimization, the decision variable x must adhere to speciﬁc constraints, which can be expressed as mathematical equalities or inequalities [14].
A constrained optimization problem, also known as a general optimization problem, can be formulated as:
⎧
minf (x)
⎪
⎪
⎪
⎪
⎨ Subject to
hj (x) = 0, j = 1, 2, . . . , p,
⎪
⎪
gi (x) ≤ 0, i = 1, 2, . . . , m,
⎪
⎪
⎩
x ∈ X.
Here, gi (x) represents the inequality constraint functions, hj (x) are the equality constraint functions, and X is the domain of x. Constraints restrict the feasible values of solutions to speciﬁc regions in the search space, known as the
feasible region. Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) involves four main components: an estimator with its objective function, a search space, an optimization
method to ﬁnd hyperparameter combinations, and an evaluation function to
compare performance across diﬀerent conﬁgurations [14]. The primary objective
of HPO is to minimize the objective function f(x), , such as error rate or root
mean squared error, by identifying the hyperparameter conﬁguration x within
the search space X. HPO aims to achieve optimal or near-optimal model performance within given resource constraints. Model performance is assessed using
metrics like accuracy, RMSE, F1-score, and false alarm rate. Time constraints are
also critical, as HPO optimization can be time-intensive [12]. The HPO process
includes selecting an objective function, performance metrics, hyperparameters
for tuning, and appropriate optimization techniques [11]. Training ML models
on large datasets is computationally expensive, and HPO techniques often use
data sampling. Eﬀective HPO optimization algorithms should handle approximate objective function values and consider computational costs. Appropriate optimization algorithms are essential for HPO problems to identify optimal
hyperparameter conﬁgurations for ML models [15].

On Hyperparameter Optimization of GAN in RS

3.1

41

Grid Search and Random Search

The traditional approach to optimizing hyperparameters involves using the Grid
Search (GS) method [16]. This technique systematically explores a predeﬁned
subset within the hyperparameter space of the training algorithm. Since the
parameter space of a ML algorithm may include regions with real or unbounded
values for certain parameters, it is essential to deﬁne boundaries for the grid
search. While GS faces challenges in high-dimensional spaces, it oﬀers the advantage of potential parallelization. This is because the values tested for hyperparameters by the algorithm are typically independent of each other, allowing
for concurrent exploration of diﬀerent combinations. Random Search (RS) was
introduced to overcome limitations of GS. Unlike GS, which systematically tests
all values within a hyperparameter space, RS randomly selects a set number of
samples distributed within speciﬁed bounds [16]. This approach aims to identify global optima or approximate them eﬃciently, even with a limited budget.
RS is advantageous due to its parallelization and resource allocation ease, as
each evaluation is independent. Its computational complexity is O(n), where n
is the number of evaluations. While RS is more eﬃcient than GS for large search
spaces, it still involves unnecessary evaluations by not leveraging information
from previously successful conﬁgurations.
The Fig. 2 presents an illustrative comparison between grid search and random search. When evaluating nine conﬁgurations to optimize a function f , which
is strongly inﬂuenced by a secondary function h, it becomes evident that random
search explores nine distinct values of h. In contrast, grid search examines only
three unique values.

Fig. 2. The diﬀerence between random search and grid search [25]

42

S. Filali-Zegzouti et al.

3.2

Bayesian Optimization

Bayesian Optimization (BO), outlined by Snoek [17], is an iterative algorithm
widely used for HPO. Unlike GS and RS, BO uses past results to guide future
evaluations. It employs a surrogate model and an acquisition function: the surrogate model ﬁts observed points to the objective function, and the acquisition function decides which points to evaluate next by balancing exploration
(sampling in unexplored areas) and exploitation (sampling in promising regions
identiﬁed by the posterior distribution). BO aims to eﬀectively identify optimal regions while avoiding premature convergence to suboptimal solutions. The
fundamental procedures of BO are outlined as follows:
1. Build a probabilistic surrogate model of the objective function.
2. Identify the optimal hyperparameter values on the surrogate model.
3. Apply these hyperparameter values to the real objective function for evaluation.
4. Update the surrogate model with new results.
5. Repeat steps 2–4 until the maximum number of iterations is reached.
BO iteratively updates a surrogate model based on objective function evaluations. Its eﬃciency surpasses GS and Random Search by leveraging previously
tested values to ﬁnd optimal hyperparameter combinations. BO’s use of a surrogate model is computationally cheaper than evaluating the full objective function, making it ideal for navigating complex and costly hyperparameter spaces.
Figure 3 demonstrates Bayesian optimization leveraging a Gaussian process
[17] surrogate model with the upper conﬁdence bound as the acquisition function f , following seven evaluations of the target function. The solid blue line
represents the target function to be optimized, while the dashed line and yellow
surface illustrate the surrogate model’s predicted mean and associated uncertainty [25].
3.3

Genetic Algorithm

The Genetic Algorithm (GA), discussed in [18], is a popular metaheuristic approach based on evolutionary principles, mimicking the idea that individuals with
superior survival and adaptability traits are more likely to pass on their capabilities to future generations. In the context of HPO, GA treats each chromosome or
individual as a hyperparameter, representing its value as a decimal in each evaluation. Chromosomes consist of binary genes, and operations such as crossover
and mutation are performed on these genes. The key steps of GA include:
1. Initialization: Randomly initialize a population that represents potential
solutions within a search space, where each chromosome encodes hyperparameter values.
2. Evaluation: Assess the ﬁtness of each individual in the population using a
ﬁtness function that reﬂects the objective of optimizing a machine learning
model.

On Hyperparameter Optimization of GAN in RS

43

Fig. 3. Example of Bayesian Optimization with a Gaussian Process [25].

3. Genetic Operations: Apply selection, crossover, and mutation operations
to create a new generation of hyperparameter conﬁgurations for evaluation.
4. Repeat: Repeat the evaluation and genetic operations iteratively until a
termination condition is satisﬁed.
5. Output: Terminate and output the optimal hyperparameter conﬁguration.
Population initialization is crucial in GA, impacting convergence speed and performance. Random initialization is commonly used to generate hyperparameter
conﬁgurations for the initial population, assigning random values within the
search space. GA’s simplicity allows it to perform well without complex initializations, making it suitable when expertise in deﬁning search spaces is limited.
However, GA introduces additional hyperparameters such as ﬁtness function
type, population size, crossover rate, and mutation rate. Its sequential nature
hinders parallelization eﬀorts, and its time complexity, , can lead to ineﬃciencies
in convergence speed.
3.4

Simulated Annealing

Simulated Annealing (SA) is an optimization algorithm inspired by the metallurgical annealing process [19]. As a metaheuristic technique, SA employs a
probabilistic search approach to uncover the global optimum within complex
and often multimodal search spaces. This algorithm is particularly eﬀective in
scenarios where conventional optimization methods struggle with rugged and
non-convex landscapes. SA’s unique ability to move away from local optima
and explore diverse regions within the search space makes it highly eﬀective
for tackling optimization challenges in intricate landscapes. Additionally, SA
demonstrates versatility and applicability across various optimization problems,
including both combinatorial and continuous optimization tasks. The steps of
SA are as follows:

44

S. Filali-Zegzouti et al.

1. Initialization: Start with an initial solution x0 and initial temperature T0
2. Iteration: Repeat until the stopping condition is met:
(a) Generate a new solution x by making a small random change to x.
(b) Calculate the change in the objective function, Δf = f (x ) − f (x).
(c) Accept x if Δf < 0; otherwise, accept x with probability exp(−Δf /T ).
(d) Update the current solution x to x if accepted.
(e) Reduce the temperature T according to the cooling schedule.
3. Termination: Return the best-found solution when the stopping condition
is met.
3.5

Particle Swarm Optimization

Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO), as detailed in [20], is a class of evolutionary
algorithms widely applied to optimization problems. Inspired by the behaviors
observed in biological populations, where both individual and social dynamics
play a role, PSO algorithms guide a group of particles (a swarm) through the
search space in a semi-random manner [21]. The collaborative and informationsharing interactions among individual particles within the swarm enable PSO
to identify optimal solutions. In the PSO framework, a swarm S comprises n
particles represented as S = {S1 , S2 , . . . , Sn }. Each particle Si is described by a
vector:
(8)
Si = xi , vi , pi
where xi is the current position, vi is the current velocity, and pi is the known
best position of the particle up to that point. The PSO algorithm follows these
steps:
1. Initialization: Initialize the position and velocity of each particle.
2. Evaluation: Evaluate and record each particle’s position and performance
score
3. Update Velocity: Adjust the velocity vi of each particle based on its previous best position pi and the current global optimal position p :
vi := vi + U (0, u1 ) · (pi − xi ) + U (0, u2 ) · (p − xi )

(9)

where U (0, u) represents continuous uniform distributions based on acceleration constants u1 and u2 .
4. Move Particles: Update the particles’ positions based on their new velocity
vectors:
(10)
xi := xi + vi .
5. Repeat: Repeat the steps until convergence or termination criteria are met.
In PSO, particles iteratively adjust their positions and velocities based on local
and global information, exploring the search space.

On Hyperparameter Optimization of GAN in RS

Analysis and Discussion

The results from the analysis reveal that Bayesian Optimization (BO) consistently outperforms other optimization methods across all datasets and metrics,
indicating its eﬀectiveness in ﬁnding optimal solutions. However, this high performance comes with higher execution times. Genetic Algorithm (GA) and Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) also show strong performance, often competing closely, with GA generally having faster execution times than PSO, making
it a more eﬃcient choice in similar performance scenarios. Simulated Annealing (SA), while having moderate execution times, consistently shows the lowest

48

S. Filali-Zegzouti et al.

Fig. 4. Time execusion by dataset and by HOP method

performance across all datasets and metrics, indicating its limited eﬀectiveness
compared to other methods. Random Search (RS) performs better than SA but
worse than the other optimization methods, with high execution times making
it less eﬃcient.
These ﬁndings highlight the importance of selecting appropriate optimization methods based on speciﬁc task requirements, such as performance, execution time, and computational resources. Bayesian Optimization stands out for
its eﬀectiveness, but in time-sensitive applications, GA and PSO oﬀer a good
balance between performance and eﬃciency.
Additionally, to further strengthen the statistical reliability of our ﬁndings,
future experiments will be conducted over 30 independent runs for each nondeterministic optimization algorithm. While our current study already provides
valuable insights into the eﬀectiveness of various hyperparameter optimization
techniques, this additional step will ensure even greater robustness in our conclusions. Given that Genetic Algorithm, Particle Swarm Optimization, and Simulated Annealing rely on stochastic processes, multiple runs will help account
for variability in performance and provide a more comprehensive evaluation. By
averaging key performance metrics and reporting standard deviations, we will
further validate the consistency of our results. Additionally, statistical signiﬁcance tests will be applied to conﬁrm whether observed performance diﬀerences
are meaningful rather than due to random ﬂuctuations. This reﬁnement will
not only reinforce the reliability of our ﬁndings but also enhance the practical applicability of our study, ensuring that our recommendations for optimiza-

On Hyperparameter Optimization of GAN in RS

49

tion techniques in GAN-based recommendation systems are well-supported and
reproducible.

6

Conclusion

In conclusion, integrating Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) into Recommendation systems, particularly as collaborative ﬁltering models, represents
a signiﬁcant advancement in improving top-N recommendations. This comparative study focused on optimizing GAN-based approaches for learning user latent
factors through matrix factorization, yielding valuable insights.
Eﬃcient hyperparameter tuning methods within a deﬁned search space
facilitated the evaluation of optimization techniques—Random Search, Genetic
Algorithm, Simulated Annealing, Particle Swarm Optimization, and Bayesian
Optimization—across multiple datasets (MovieLens 1M, LastFM, and MovieLens HetRec).
These ﬁndings emphasize the importance of selecting optimization methods
based on speciﬁc application requirements, such as performance, execution time,
and computational resources. For time-sensitive applications, Genetic Algorithm
and Particle Swarm Optimization oﬀer competitive performance with faster execution times compared to Bayesian Optimization. Future work could explore
hybrid approaches or enhancements to these methods to improve their eﬃciency
and eﬀectiveness by using quantum algorithms [24], which accelerate the training
process and enhance the performance of generative models by ﬁnding optimal
hyperparameter settings more quickly and eﬀectively.

Enhancing AutoML with Algorithm Selection: A
Path to Better Performance
Yinuo Guo1 , Mustafa Mısır1(B) , and Zhongyi Hu2
1

Division of Natural and Applied Sciences, Duke Kunshan University, Kunshan, China
{yinuo.guo,mustafa.misir}@dukekunshan.edu.cn
2
School of Information Management, Wuhan University, Wuhan, China
zhongyi.hu@whu.edu.cn

Abstract. Machine learning algorithms have been widely adopted across a variety of application domains. However, training models and evaluating their performance is often time-consuming and computationally expensive. Furthermore,
the identification of (near-)optimal learning strategies, including design choices
and hyperparameter configurations, remains a significant challenge, as these decisions substantially influence the quality of learning outcomes. To address these
issues, Automated Machine Learning (AutoML) has been developed with the
aim of minimizing manual intervention and automating the creation of ready-touse machine learning pipelines. Despite the widespread adoption of numerous
successful AutoML systems, their performance is observed to vary across different datasets and learning scenarios. Algorithm selection has been proposed as a
solution to this limitation, enabling the recommendation of algorithms on a perinstance basis. In this study, algorithm selection is utilized to enhance the capabilities of existing AutoML frameworks with minimal additional effort. A comprehensive empirical evaluation is conducted on 39 diverse tasks from the OpenML
platform, involving 6 state-of-the-art AutoML methods. The results demonstrate
that the integration of algorithm selection not only amplifies the strengths of current AutoML systems but also leads to improved performance and robustness.
These findings highlight the practicality and efficacy of algorithm selection as a
critical advancement for the next generation of AutoML technologies.
Keywords: Algorithm Selection · AutoML · Classification · OpenML

1 Introduction
Artificial Intelligence (AI) [1] has been a part of an increasing trend both in academia
and industry. The primary element in this rise is Machine Learning (ML) [2]. ML
is concerned with the algorithmic aspect of AI, aiming at learning without explicit
instructions while relying on experience in the form of data. The success of ML in
a wide range of applications depends on the specifications of the learning algorithms,
the quality of the relevant data, and its size. Focusing on the algorithms, their behavior
is affected by their internal design choices and the values assigned to their parameters, i.e., hyper-parameters. Despite the ML success stories, many surpassing the expert
level human performance, determining the right setting of the ML algorithms may be
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 51–63, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_4

52

Y. Guo et al.

cumbersome. Hyper-parameter Optimization (HPO) [3] targets discovering the best
possible hyper-parameter setting for a particular ML algorithm concerning a certain
learning task. There have been strong algorithmic development efforts to deliver HPO
approaches, lately even with Large Language Models (LLMs) [4]. Beyond HPO, Automated ML (AutoML) [5–7] as an umbrella term, deals with both the design decisions
and hyper-parameters combined thoroughly. AutoML essentially focuses on all the elements of the ML pipelines, with the goal of fully automating the model generation
process while focusing on individual tasks in those pipelines [8]. That being said, modelling the design choices as parameters allows to approach AutoML as a HPO problem.
Additionally, there have been sub-fields such as Architecture Optimization or Neural
Architecture Search (NAS) [9]. NAS has been particularly popular, for delivering taskspecific, (near-)optimal neural network architectures. This job has been critical, especially in the area of Deep Learning (DL), to build complex and effective architectures.
Meta-learning [10] is closely related to this field, utilizing learning from previous experiences. When applying a specific ML model to a particular task, the configuration can
be determined by analyzing the interplay between the model and the task. This prior
experience is characterized by the meta-data or meta-features. Combined with the metadata, the previous knowledge can be transferred to the current study, resulting in significant time savings, especially considering the computational demands of AutoML tasks.
Many AutoML pipelines integrate meta-learning in some way. Auto-sklearn 2.0 [11]
as an example, employs meta-features to define similarities between datasets, including
metrics like the number of instances and missing values, and record the performance of
different algorithms trained on the datasets. When the pipeline encounters a new task,
it recommends an algorithm based on the most similar dataset. Though several successful AutoML systems have emerged, none performs better than the others on every
instance. This fact underscores the importance of per-instance-based analysis. One way
to automatically predict the best performance on an instance level is to use Algorithm
Selection (AS) [12]. This study accommodates AS on top of AutoML. The idea is to
utilize existing AutoML systems for offering improved AutoML performance. In other
words, this setup adds another layer on AutoML systems, providing profound metainformation, similar to the Cross-domain AS (CdAS) idea [13]. It takes the features of
datasets and performance data as input to an dataset-specific AutoML choice.
For the case study, an existing system, ALORS [14], which formulates AS as a
recommendation task, is employed. This choice is motivated by ALORS’s prior application on OpenML, a collaborative ML platform. OpenML’s significance stems from
a previous study [11], which provided the experimental setup analyzed in this work.
That study involved 39 classification tasks and reported a comparative evaluation of
6 AutoML systems. The dataset features were directly obtained from OpenML. The
results demonstrated that AS outperformed each of the 6 standalone AutoML systems.
In the following parts of this paper, Sect. 2 briefly provides an outlook both on
AutoML and AS. Section 3 discusses the methodology alongside the experimental
setup. The empirical results and the discussions are then provided in Sect. 4. Section 5
finalizes this article by a summary and future research plans.

Enhancing AutoML with Algorithm Selection: A Path to Better Performance

53

2 Background
2.1 Automated Machine Learning (AutoML)
Numerous domains benefit from the power of Machine Learning (ML) techniques.
Despite their successes, even exceeding expert performance, building a strong ML
model is not always that straightforward. At the best case, it will require a trial-and-error
phase to come up with a proper ML setup. To simplify the development cycle, AutoML
systems efficiently build machine learning models even when users lack related knowledge. AutoML has been broadly conceptualized as a search and optimization problem.
The search space, .Θ, includes the set of all possible configurations for any part of the
ML pipeline. The strategies along with their hyper-parameters for data preparation, feature engineering, model generation, and model estimation constitute that search space
[8].
∗
.θ = arg min L(fθ , Dval )
θ∈Θ

where .L refers to the criteria assessing the quality of a model .fθ or a loss/objective
function, trained on a dataset .Dtrain , using a validation dataset .Dval . Additionally, various constraints can be identified such as computational budget, the maximum number
of configurations to check, or restrictions come from the underlying hardware both for
training and inference. For explaining the existing AutoML approaches, the key characteristics of several prominent frameworks are outlined below.
– Auto-sklearn 2.0 [11] is an enhanced version of Auto-sklearn 1.0, using a banditbased successive halving approach to allocate resources to promising configurations.
It leverages meta-learning to analyze dataset characteristics and employs Bayesian
optimization to construct portfolio and ensemble models for each instance.
– Auto-WEKA (AW) [15], in a way an earlier variant of Auto-sklearn 1.0, on WEKA,
which is a Java-based Machine Learning (ML) environment. It aims to solve the
Combined Algorithm Selection and Hyper-parameter Optimization (CASH) problem using Sequential Model-based Algorithm Configuration (SMAC) and Treestructured Parzen Estimator (TPE). The follow-up work [16] extends the framework
from classification to regression and integrates it into the WEKA ecosystem.
– H2O [17] is a distributed AutoML framework that efficiently explores large datasets.
It combines the fast random search and stacked ensembles methods, which are relatively straightforward compared with the other frameworks. H2O offers various
choices for the regression, classification, and clustering algorithms, including Gradient Boosting, Deep Learning, Random Forest, and Hierarchical Clustering. It shows
competitive results on the OpenML benchmarks.
– TPOT [18] is a tree-based AutoML pipeline using genetic programming (GP). It
focuses on ML pipelines, including classification, feature preprocessing, selection,
and combination operators. Each operator modifies the dataset, enabling the construction of tree-based pipelines with a top-.k selection and final classification unit.
The genetic tree structures are optimized via GP using the NSGA-II strategy and an
evaluate-select-crossover-mutate cycle.

54

Y. Guo et al.

Many promising AutoML systems exist, such as LightAutoML [19], AMLBID [20],
and AlphaD3M [21]. Additionally, cloud-based AutoML platforms like Amazon SageMaker, IBM Auto-AI, and Google Auto-ML have gained significant popularity and are
widely applied in critical domains such as natural language processing and computer
vision. It is also worth noting that existing ML libraries and frameworks provide various
AutoML capabilities [22].
2.2

Algorithm Selection

Recognizing that algorithm performance can vary significantly across the problem
instances of all the domains, identifying an optimal algorithm for a given problem
solving task is a pivotal pursuit. Particularly, domains such as Boolean Satisfiability
(SAT), Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP), and Hyper-parameter Optimization (HPO)
encounter challenges due to their expensive optimization or search nature. Consequently, Algorithm Selection (AS) has gained significant attention, from finding the
(near-)best solver(s) for any problem solving tasks.
Traditionally, AS has been built in the form of performance prediction models. The
basic idea is to predetermine the expected performance of a set of algorithms for a target
scenario or a problem instance. Following those prediction values, the best algorithm
expectedly is picked to solve that target problem instance. Formally, AS is based on
.F̂ : P × A → R, where .P represents the problem domain’s instances and .A represents
considered algorithms. The outcome selection can be defined in the way of the mapping
.Ŝ : P → A, to determine the optimal solution for
S(i) = argminF (pi , a) , pi ∈ P.

.

a∈A

Notably, all the instances possess identifiable features .fˆ : P → Rd , with .d representing the number of features per instance [23]. Starting from this basic strategy, a
variety of AS designs have been proposed. One of the well-known AS approaches is
SATzilla [24]. It efficiently solves SAT problems, particularly proven by its success in
the relevant SAT competitions. Following a feature extraction process from the SAT
instances, the existing solvers were run to obtain the pre-trained performance data,
and then deploys classification models for predictive tasks. Some AS methods benefit from clustering to group similar instances, then link the best algorithmic choice for
each instance type. ISAC [25] applies clustering strategies based on the feature space,
while SUNNY [26] utilizes the k-nearest neighbor (KNN). This enables performance
estimation within the identified clusters, empowering the anticipation of the most fitting algorithm for new instances. Additionally, combining ranking and regression [27]
directly predicts the rank of algorithms instead of the computation time or other numeric
results, which could be potentially problematic. Extreme algorithm selection [28] concentrates on the optimal algorithm within a pervasive candidate space, considering both
the problem and algorithmic feature spaces. Beyond that, there have been efforts to
deliver feature-free AS systems [29, 30].
Nevertheless, significant challenges remain. Two key components for AS—feature
and performance data—are often incomplete due to resource constraints, resulting in

Enhancing AutoML with Algorithm Selection: A Path to Better Performance

55

sparse training data. The ability to incorporate such sparse data while maintaining comparability is crucial. ALORS [14] addresses this by using matrix completion based on
collaborative filtering (CF) to recommend algorithms.

3 Methodology
We consider 39 benchmarks from the OpenML website, utilizing 6 AutoML systems:
Auto-sklearn 1.0, Auto-sklearn 2.0, Auto-WEKA, TPOT, H2O, and Tuned Random
Forest. As suggested in the previous section, the compelling performance of ALORS
across various fields convinced us to combine ALORS with the AutoML frameworks.
Nevertheless, any other Algorithm Selection (AS) method could have been used as well,
since the focus is to show the effectiveness of AS.
Algorithm 1. ALORS: cold-start functionality
Input
Rank performance matrix M ∈ Rn×m
Instance features representation X ∈ Rn×d
Description x of the current problem instance
Learning latent factor model Φ
Build E = (xi , Ui ) , xi ∈ Rd , Ui ∈ Rk , i ∈ 1 . . . n
Rk from E
Learn the mapping Φ : Rd
Algorithm selection
Compute Ux = Φ(x) as the latent representation
Estimate the performance of j-th algorithm Ux , Vj
Return recommendation argmin Ux , Vj
j=1...m

The initial dataset features were retrieved via the OpenML API, with up to 107 features per dataset. Many datasets contain significant missing values, and only a dozen
features are common to all datasets. To balance missing value tolerance and feature
count, a 10% threshold for missing values was applied, reducing the feature set to 48.
Missing values were imputed using .k-Nearest Neighbors (.k-NN) with .k = 5. Alternatively, a subset of 14 features without any missing values across all datasets was
considered for both training and testing.
Concerning the Algorithm Selection (AS) procedure, Algorithm 1 provides the partial pseudocode of ALORS [14]. Due to the use of Collaborative Filtering (CF), ALORS
allows building AS models with incomplete performance data. However, that aspect is
not a focus area in this work, thus only its cold-start functionality, so its pure recommendation capability is detailed. Referring to the earlier, traditional AS formal description,
ALORS also takes an algorithm-problem instance performance matrix and features representing the instances of the target problem as inputs. Unlike the other AS systems,
ALORS performs performance prediction indirectly, after building a mapping model
between two sets of features. The initially selected, either those 48 or 14 features are
mapped to the latent (hidden) features extracted from the performance data. To be specific, Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) is applied for Matrix Factorization (MF).

56

Y. Guo et al.

The resulting features characterize the problem instances in the eye of the algorithms
rather than solely relying on those initially chosen features. It should be noted that, SVD
additionally outputs features representing the candidate algorithms which are those 6
AutoML techniques. SVD simply operates as
M = U ΣV

≈E UK · VK

.

where .Σ has non-negative values on the first diagonal in a decreasing order. .U and .V
are corresponding square matrices. Then, a low-rank-.k approximation is performed by
zeroing out rows below the .kth row in .Σ and rewriting .U , .V into .Uk , .Vk , representing
the latent features. The latent features are then predicted through the initially provided
descriptive features for each per-instance selection task. Followingly, those features are
used to calculate the rank predictions of the constitutent AutoML systems. Next, those
predictions are converted into a selection decision, with the AutoML method that is
expected to achieve the best performance among the candidates.

4 Computational Results
As mentioned earlier, the experimental setup is adopted from [11], with features directly
retrieved from OpenML. No special adjustments were made to favor Algorithm Selection (AS). ALORS, applied as AS, uses 10-fold cross-validation (10-CV) and a rank5 Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) for factorization. The mapping in ALORS is
implemented using Random Forest (RF).

similar datasets can be identified, given as follows: (bank-marketing, nomao), (albert,

Although not all those similar groups are resembling on paper, not even from the
same domains, their expectations in terms of the learning tasks are closely comparable.
This outcome can be largely beneficial for identifying cheaper datasets to be used for
AutoML training by only providing a diverse yet a small dataset.

ALORS captures subtle differences and enhances overall performance.

Enhancing AutoML with Algorithm Selection: A Path to Better Performance

61

5 Conclusion
This paper explores the use of per-instance Algorithm Selection (AS) to enhance Automated Machine Learning (AutoML). AutoML is designed to optimize the setup for
learning tasks by identifying suitable design choices, components, and hyper-parameter
values. However, no single AutoML system is universally superior due to the limitations posed by the No Free Lunch (NFL) theorem. To address this, AS is leveraged to
identify the most appropriate AutoML system for each task. Specifically, ALORS, an
existing AS method, is utilized to match datasets with the most suitable AutoML approach among six systems, rather than relying on a single method for all datasets. Despite
a limited setup of 39 datasets, it is demonstrated that applying AS across AutoML systems leads to both performance improvement and increased robustness.
As this is the first step towards delivering AutoML with improved robustness, there
are many more steps to cover concerning follow-up research. To be specific, only 39
datasets are present referring to the original study providing the experimental setup.
Referring to the comment pointed out earlier, the number of datasets is quite small for
building strong machine learning (ML) models, so the AS models. Thus, they will be
extended by utilizing the existing, other AutoML benchmarks, also referring to the ones
in the area of Neural Architecture Search (NAS) and Hyper-parameter Optimization
(HPO) particularly. Following this extension, more AutoML or HPO methods will be
accommodated, enhancing both the analysis and quality. Next, since AutoML is mainly
falling into the category of expensive optimization, more criteria beyond performance
will be taken into account such as model building cost for each approach. Alongside
with this idea to explore, the setting will be investigated as a multi-objective task.

Government research funding.

Abstract. Neuromorphic computing (NC) introduces a novel paradigm
called Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs), representing a major shift
from traditional digital computing. NC leverages spiking neurons, adaptive synapses, event-driven processing, and biologically-inspired learning
mechanisms to develop eﬃcient, brain-like systems optimized for realtime, parallel processing and low power consumption.
In this paper, we propose an algorithm that integrates the principles
of evolutionary algorithms (EAs) with NC to create eﬃcient and energyaware metaheuristics. The proposed neuromorphic EA (NEVA) has been
mapped on a SNN which involves deﬁning the neuron model, information
encoding, network architecture, and learning rules. To our knowledge this
is the ﬁrst EA designed using the NC paradigm. Computational experiments on QUBO, 3-SAT, and knapsack problems show the eﬃciency of
the proposed NEVA algorithm. By designing a neuromorphic memetic
algorithm that combines EAs with local search, the results have been
improved both in terms of solution quality and search time.
Keywords: Neuromorphic optimization · Neuromorphic Evolutionary
algorithm · Spiking Neural Networks · Neuromorphic Memetic
Algorithm

1

Introduction

Managing energy consumption in AI and optimization is essential for sustainability, cost-eﬀectiveness, and minimizing carbon footprints. Achieving a balance
between computational demands and energy eﬃciency is a central focus in AI
and optimization research and development.
Digital and NC represent two distinct paradigms with signiﬁcant diﬀerences
in architecture, functionality, and energy eﬃciency. Digital computing, based on
the traditional Von Neumann architecture, separates processing and memory
units and relies on binary logic gates and sequential execution of instructions
stored in memory. Although modern digital processors have greatly improved
energy eﬃciency through advances in semiconductor technology and power management techniques, they can still consume considerable power, particularly
when running complex algorithms or handling data-intensive tasks.
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 64–79, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_5

NEVA: A Neuromorphic Evolutionary Algorithm

65

Inspired by biological brains, NC systems mimic neural networks with interconnected neurons and synapses. These systems aim to emulate the parallel and
event-driven processing observed in the brain. NC operates with SNNs where
information is encoded in the timing of spikes (events). NC systems are designed
to be highly energy-eﬃcient and are particularly suitable for tasks that beneﬁt
from parallelism and low-power operation.
NC has primarily been applied to cognitive tasks such as machine learning,
where its brain-inspired architecture excels in processing sensory data, pattern
recognition, and real-time decision-making. An emerging area of interest involves
extending the use of NC to solve optimization problems. It has been acknowledged that neuromorphic computers possess several characteristics that make
them appealing for optimization algorithms. These characteristics include [1]:
inherent massively parallel computation, native scalability, sparsity of activity,
event-driven computation, stochastic operations, collocated processing and memory, and simple spike-based communication between components. By harnessing
these unique features, NC can eﬀectively handle various types of computational
tasks. This capability opens up opportunities to apply NC in optimization, leveraging its eﬃciency and adaptability. The contributions of this paper can be
summarized as follows:
– A neuromorphic evolutionary algorithm (NEVA) that organizes a population
of individuals as SNNs has been proposed. To our knowledge this is the ﬁrst
evolutionary algorithm designed using SNNs computational paradigm.
– The proposed NEVA algorithm has been used to solve the Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO), 3-SAT and knapsack problems. Those
problems have gained prominence as versatile models for tackling various
combinatorial optimization challenges, such as Max-Cut, Graph Coloring and
Partitioning, among others.
– A neuromorphic memetic algorithm (NEMA) combining EAs and local search
has been designed to improve the performances of NEVA.
The paper is organized as follows. Section 2 (resp. Sect. 3) presents the main
concepts of NC (resp. parallel EAs). In Sect. 4, the proposed neuromorphic evolutionary algorithm (NEVA) is detailed in terms of design and implementation.
Section 5 provides some computational experiments on the most popular binary
optimization problems: QUBO, 3-SAT and knapsack. Finally, Sect. 6 draws the
main conclusions of this work and gives some perspectives.

2

Neuromorphic Computing

The history of NC began in the 1980s when Carver Mead introduced the concept
of building computer systems inspired by the brain’s neural architecture, aiming
to overcome the limitations of traditional Von Neumann designs [2]. Early eﬀorts
focused on analog circuits mimicking neuron-like behavior, but it wasn’t until
the 2010s that signiﬁcant progress was made with the development of digital
neuromorphic chips like IBM’s TrueNorth and Intel’s Loihi, which used SNNs to

66

E.-G. Talbi and N. Bouvier

simulate brain functions and enable real-time adaptation. These advancements
have positioned NC as a promising approach for energy-eﬃcient and parallel
processing.
NC is a brain-inspired approach for designing hardware and software systems
that mimic the structure and functionality of neural networks. Unlike traditional
computing, which follows a sequential Von Neumann architecture, NC systems
emulate the parallel, event-driven processing of biological neurons, enabling eﬃcient, adaptive computation with lower power consumption. In NC, several key
concepts have to be deﬁned in designing an optimization algorithm: neuron and
synapse models, information encoding, and the network topology of the SNN.
Neuron models are the core processing units in NC systems. Instead of continuous outputs like in traditional artiﬁcial neural networks (ANNs), these neurons communicate via discrete electrical pulses called “spikes”. The neuron model
typically aims to capture the essential features of biological neurons while being
computationally eﬃcient. It deﬁnes how input spikes inﬂuence the neuron’s membrane potential and how it generates output spikes. These models vary both in
the biological characteristics of real neurons they can replicate and in their computational complexity.
In NC, information is encoded in the timing and rate of spikes (spike trains)
rather than in continuous values like in digital ANNs. Key aspects of information encoding include spike timing, rate timing and population coding. Synapses
transmit spikes between neurons, and their weights inﬂuence the learning process. Static synapses are used for ﬁxed interactions. Dynamic synapses allow the
network to learn patterns or adapt to changing environments through plasticity
mechanisms, like Spike-Timing-Dependent Plasticity (STDP), where the synaptic weight changes based on the timing of pre- and post-synaptic spikes. The
structure of the SNN, or topology, deﬁnes how neurons are connected to one
another. Common topologies include feedforward networks and recurrent networks. Neuromorphic systems may also incorporate hierarchical organization,
where layers or modules of neurons perform diﬀerent levels of processing. This
allows for abstraction of information and complex feature extraction.

3

Parallel Evolutionary Algorithms

EAs are a family of metaheuristic algorithms inspired by biological evolution
and natural selection. They are used to solve diﬃcult optimization problems by
mimicking the process of natural selection, reproduction, mutation, and survival
of the ﬁttest [3]. The search components that are diﬃcult to design using the NC
paradigm are the selection and the replacement of the population. They require a
globally shared memory of what “good” solutions are in the population. Mapping
sequential EAs onto a SNN, each neuron will need information from all the
other synapses. However, biological neurons only have access to the information
available from neighbor neurons. Hence, parallel structured populations of EAs
are more adapted to NC paradigm.

NEVA: A Neuromorphic Evolutionary Algorithm

3.1

67

Cellular Evolutionary Algorithms

Parallel EAs are a class of optimization algorithms designed to improve performance and scalability by distributing computation across multiple processing
elements. The most suited parallel model for NC is the CEA one, in which
the population of individuals is mapped on the SNN. The proposed algorithm
takes into account the sparsity of synapses by using interaction graphs with low
degree, which are beneﬁcial in order to contain local optima with spacial separation. Moreover, information is only shared locally between neurons. Cellular EAs
(CEAs) combine principles from cellular automata and EAs. The population of
an EA is mapped on a spatially structured graph (e.g. grid, lattice), where the
vertices represent the individuals, and the edges represented the neighborhood
of the individuals. Selection and mating are restricted to the neighborhood. The
overlapping of the neighborhoods allow some interaction among all the individuals. This local interaction promotes diversity and delays convergence, making
it well-suited for massively parallel asynchronous systems. This CEA model has
been initially investigated in [4,5]. This family of parallel EAs is also named
ﬁne-grained parallel genetic algorithm [6] or cellular genetic algorithms [7]. This
model has a high degree of parallelism, local interaction, and slow information
ﬂow between distant individuals.
In designing a CEA, the main design questions to be answered are:
– The exchange decision criterion (When?): The exchange of information
between individuals can be determined through diﬀerent approaches: blind
(periodic or probabilistic) or adaptive criteria. Unlike periodic or probabilistic
methods, adaptive exchanges adjust communication based on evolving conditions such as the quality or performance of solutions. For example, adaptive
exchanges may consider metrics like the improvement in the best local solution found so far.
– The exchange topology (Where?): The communication exchange topology
determines how individuals interact. In general, regular graphs such as ring,
grid, mesh are used.
– The information exchanged (What?): this parameter speciﬁes the information to be exchanged between the individuals. In general this information is
composed of current or best found individuals.
– The replacement policy (How?): the replacement policy deals with the usage
of the received individual. Any deterministic or probabilistic elitist replacement strategy may be applied.

4

A Neuromorphic Evolutionary Algorithm (NEVA)

The neuromorphic structure of SNNs makes modeling population evolution intuitive. Individuals interact via time-based signals and maintain sparse connections
with one another. The goal of NEVA is to promote the formation of clusters that
share solutions with their neighboring individuals. Typically, multiple synaptic

68

E.-G. Talbi and N. Bouvier

signals connected to the same output are summed as integers, and the synaptic weight acts as a multiplier for any spike traveling through the connection.
Another challenge to face is the limitation of today’s neuromorphic devices,
mainly the sparsity of synapses and the locality of memory. Should an algorithm
be designed to match these inherent properties, or can we assume that future
hardware will improve their performances on these points?
4.1

The Generic Algorithm Design of NEVA

The goal is to optimize a function f : {0, 1}D → R. Without loss of generality, we will try to maximize f . In NEVA, each individual in the population is
modeled as a neuron with its own unique solution, interacting with neighboring
neurons. Sparse communication is a critical factor when working with neuromorphic devices. We deﬁne our SNN as a graph G = (V, E) that encodes the
relations between neurons (e.g. grid). In this paper, we use a torus topology
(Fig. 1), but any other topology might be used such as rings, grids and random
graphs.

.
.

s

.
(1)

.

Fig. 1. A torus topology of spiking neurons, where a given neuron S is ﬁring a spike
to its neighbours.

Each en,m ∈ E represents D + 1 synapses going from n to m. We will refer
to post synaptic values as mc and md1 . . . mdD . Once n’s pre-synapse nc receives
only one spike from it’s neighbours, it will read nd1 . . . ndD in a vector temp and
recombine it with its internal values datan .
Each neuron n ∈ V will carry out a crossover operation with its neighbours
if not in reset period (rn > 0), using the crossover in e. If f (e) > f (datan ),
datan is set to the newly generated solution e. The n’s period for sending datan
to neighbours τ is then set to 0, resulting in an increase in ﬁring rate. When
ﬁring, this period is randomly increased (τn ← τn + Random(0, 1)), and a spike
is sent to all post-synapses mc neighbouring n as well as solutionn,i to mdi .
The uniform crossover (resp. ﬂip mutation) operators are deﬁned as follows:

If τn > τmax , it indicates that no new solutions have been found for a certain
period, marking the start of a mutation phase. We set datan ← Mutate(datan ),
and reset the period τ to 0. This process ensures the exploration of the search
space. The selection of which neighbor to perform crossover with depends on the
one with the highest ﬁring frequency, meaning the neuron that has most recently
undergone successful recombination or mutation. In practice, if a neuron has a
higher ﬁring rate, it is more likely to be the only one sending spikes through nc .

When executing this algorithm, it is crucial that the objective function f can
be computed within a reasonable time, as each neuron is expected to evaluate
it freely, as in the case of a Max-3-SAT instance. However, certain speciﬁc problems, like QUBO, the objective function can be computed through an additional
neuromorphic layer.
4.2

Neuromorphic Computation of the QUBO Objective Function

When solving a QUBO instance Q ∈ Mn,n , it is possible to keep neurons to
a very basic form and compute the matrix-vector product with another SNN
layer, thus not relying on neurons being able to do arbitrarily large computation
tasks. Be aware that this construction heavily relies on the fact that multiple
synaptic signals connected to the same output are added as integers. In such a
conﬁguration, each neuron n with synapses nc and nd1 . . . ndD can be split in
2D + 2 neurons M , x1 . . . xD , y1 . . . yD and , where M is a mediator neuron
that will coordinate actions during the spiking phase, x will be the layer storwill contain the
ing best and current solution, y is a computing layer and
computed xT Qx. M then receives information from nc , and xi recombines its
saved personal best value pbi with outside information ndi . We deﬁne weighted
directed internal synapses as follows Sx,y = {sxi ,Qxj := Qi,j | ∀i, j ∈ [[1, D]]}
which will computes Qx. Then additional connections are added from M to x,

E.-G. Talbi and N. Bouvier

then sends xT Qx to M , which then
compares it to the last found value of pbT Qpb kept in memory. If xT Qx >
pbT Qpb, M sends a spike to x for it to update its personal best pb, and to let
them start retrieving information from the ndi ’s again. All other values such as
τn and tn can be computed inside n’s M neuron in most of recent SNNs. Once
a cycle has ended, pb will send it’s output like in the original algorithm, and
x will start retrieving data again. Note that the f layer might be redundant if
the SNN allows the usage of more complex synapses that can compute products
instead of sums.
This method computes the QUBO objective function in O(D) time computation if we assume that ﬁring s spikes to the same node has a O(s) computation
time because of the underlying summation of spikes. Additionally, it may not be
necessary to have more than a few of these computing units, as a neuron does
not constantly need to compute f . To simplify the simulation and achieve faster
results, we will call the CPU each time a QUBO value is required, while keeping
the total number of neurons low to accommodate this factor. It is important to
note that, due to the Turing-complete nature of NC, this model is not limited
to the QUBO problem and can be adapted to address other binary problems as
well [8].
4.3

A Neuromorphic Memetic Algorithm (NEMA)

Memetic algorithms (MAs) are a type of EAs that integrate local search or
improvement techniques into their evolutionary process. The term “memetic” is
derived from “meme” which refers to a behavior, or pattern that spreads within
a culture. In the context of algorithms, it represents the inclusion of cultural
knowledge or local learning mechanisms to reﬁne and enhance the evolutionary
process [9–11].
For certain speciﬁc problem, we may want to increase convergence time
through local search algorithms. For example if trying to solve QUBO for a matrix
Q ∈ MD,D , given that computing f (X) for a solution X is done O(D2 ), turning
X into X = {n ∈ V | m∈X Qn,m ≥ 1} takes the same amount of time and signiﬁcantly improves the convergence rate. Indeed, this method can also be used
by itself if mapping the matrix Q to a neural network, and computing those
transitions locally, while adding noise to broaden the searching space [12,13].
The simplest way to incorporate a memetic factor into our algorithm is to
modify the mutation operator, which will be invoked if a neuron has not improved
its solution for a certain period. For example, if M utate0 is the random bit ﬂip,
and Enhance our local search algorithm, we could choose to deﬁne M utate1 as
if Random([0, 1]) < p then Mutate0(x) else Enhance(x). In our memetic NEVA,
9
. The value of p should be set high enough to maintain the
we initialize p to 10
exploration factor, but not so high that it leads to frequent calls to the Enhance
function during execution. Thus, if we introduce an Enhance function capable of
signiﬁcantly improving poor solutions, such as the QUBO heuristic mentioned
earlier, and can be mapped to an SNN, we can eﬀectively accelerate the search
process without sacriﬁcing the exploration of the search space oﬀered by EAs.

Computational Experiments

The benchmarks used include QUBO instances [14] and SATLIB uniform 3-SAT
instances [15]. The neuromorphic algorithms were implemented using the LAVA
simulator. An alternative implementation, which does not rely on LAVA, uses
Numpy matrices for time step operations, making it less dependent on external
libraries. This implementation runs signiﬁcantly faster—about 70 times faster
than the LAVA-based algorithm. The arbitrary default parameters used in the
study are: Torus architecture, τmax = 5, k = 4, Uniform random crossover, and
Uniform random bit ﬂips mutation with γ = log(D)
probability of mutation.
D
When comparing to simulated annealing or other algorithms, it’s important to
note that the term “Time step” doesn’t have much relevance in an asynchronous
device. It is merely a convenient metric used to compare the algorithms. Furthermore, it is not accurate to claim that y neurons are equivalent to y algorithms
running in parallel. Even with a large number of neurons, the black-box function
is not called at every time step for each neuron. In our tests, approximately 40
calls were made per step on a 128-grid formation for QUBO instances, which
makes direct comparisons somewhat misleading.

In this section, we present our results using the NEVA algorithm, comparing it
to simulated annealing (SA). As shown in Fig. 3, the inherently parallel structure
of the NEVA algorithm enables much faster convergence, ultimately achieving
better results than SA.

almost impossible, and lower values makes it prominent in front of crossover,
thus resulting in a random walk with almost no coordination between individuals.

Computational Experiments on 3-SAT Problems

Our generic NEVA neuromorphic algorithm can be applied to solve any binary
optimization problem. An issue with NP-hard problem is that sometimes most
random instances are not “hard” to solve. Actually, NP-hardness only accounts
for the hardest problems in the category [16]. It is now only natural to try our
algorithm on other binary optimization problems, such as 3-SAT. The ob jective

similar to those of SA.

6

Conclusions and Perspectives

In this paper, we introduced an algorithm that integrates EAs with NC to create eﬃcient and energy-aware metaheuristics. The proposed NEVA algorithm is
implemented on an SNN, involving the design of the network architecture, the
deﬁnition of neuron and synapse behaviors, and the establishment of information
encoding for the problem at hand. To our knowledge, this is the ﬁrst evolutionary
algorithm developed using the neuromorphic computational paradigm. Additionally, we have developed a memetic neuromorphic algorithm that combines EAs
with local search.
All the proposed implementations have been extensively tested using the
LAVA simulator. There is little doubt that on neuromorphic hardware, such as
Intel’s Loihi2, both computation time and energy consumption would be signiﬁcantly reduced. Other hardware platforms could also run these simulations,
but they would require customizable internal neuron models to achieve optimal performance. Computational experiments on QUBO, 3-SAT, and knapsack
optimization problems demonstrate the eﬃciency of the NEVA algorithm. The
neuromorphic memetic algorithm shows signiﬁcant improvements in both solution quality and search time.
The NEVA algorithm could be adapted to continuous spaces, such as using
spike bursts with frequencies proportional to speciﬁc values in synaptic communication. Permutation optimization problems present a distinct challenge, where
information encoding could be modiﬁed to use time-based spikes. However, the
simpler the objective function is to compute, the more practical it becomes to
map onto a neuromorphic device. For optimization problems with costly objective functions, alternative approaches like surrogate model-based optimization
should be explored.

NEVA: A Neuromorphic Evolutionary Algorithm

Enhancing Academic Advising with AI:
Leveraging Large Language Models
and Retrieval-Augmented Generation
for Smarter Student Support
Raghav Khare , Anuraj Sule , Aldrich Mendes ,
and Abdul-Rahman Mawlood-Yunis(B)
Computer Science and Physics Department, Waterloo, ON N2L3C5, Canada
{khar9020,sule0010,mend3010}@mylaurier.ca, amawloodyunis@wlu.ca

Abstract. Academic advising is essential for student success, encompassing a wide range of inquiries, from general program information to
more personalized concerns. To streamline the process and limit human
interaction in this work, we introduce an innovative AI-driven chatbot
that harnesses the power of large language models (LLM), speciﬁcally
LLaMA, along with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and ﬁnetuning (FT) techniques. This approach aims to automate and improve
the student advising process, oﬀering more eﬃcient and accurate support
for students. Unlike conventional chatbots constrained by rigid, intentbased frameworks, our system dynamically retrieves and synthesizes
information from continuously updated knowledge bases. This approach
enables the chatbot to provide accurate, context-aware, and reliable
responses to student inquiries. Compared to existing systems, the proposed solution eﬀectively mitigates common issues such as hallucinations
and outdated information, providing factual, grounded, and scalable performance. This paper presents an overview of the development lifecycle,
encompassing dataset creation, system architecture, and experimental
validation. Our experiments show that Retrieval-Augmented Generation
(RAG) outperforms Fine-Tuning in accuracy, scalability, and relevance
for academic advising chatbots. RAG improves the handling of individual queries, automates routine tasks, and reduces the advisor’s workload,
allowing them to focus on complex cases. This solution improves student
support by balancing eﬃcient automation with personalized guidance.
Keywords: Academic advising · large language model ·
retrieval-augmented generation · knowledge base · chatbot

1

Academic advising plays a critical role in guiding students through course selection, academic progression, co-op eligibility, overcoming challenges, and various other tasks. Although complete automation of academic advisory remains

B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 80–97, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_6

Enhancing Academic Advising with AI

81

a research area that still requires development [1] [2], considerable eﬀorts are
underway to automate many of these functions. Several chatbots that utilize
natural language processing (NLP) have been proposed to assist in academic
advice. However, these existing systems face several challenges, such as hallucinations, limited factual accuracy, and scalability issues. Moreover, many traditional
models are restricted by rigid, predeﬁned intents, dependence on proprietary and
costly models, and an inability to provide real-time updates.
To address these challenges, we propose a new solution that utilizes large language models (LLM), speciﬁcally the open source LLaMA [3] model, to enhance
the academic advising process. Our system integrates Retrieval-Augmented
Generation (RAG) [4], a methodology that retrieves contextually relevant
information from a curated knowledge base to generate accurate and personalized responses. Unlike traditional advising chatbots, which may generate hallucinated or irrelevant responses, RAG ensures that the information provided is
accurate and relevant while maintaining the human element for more complex
cases that require personalized interaction.
The proposed solution employs two primary techniques: Fine-Tuning and
RAG. Fine-tuning adapts the LLM parameters to a speciﬁc task using a custom
dataset, while RAG utilizes an external knowledge base to provide dynamic
fact-based answers. Our experiments demonstrate that RAG outperforms FineTuning in terms of accuracy, scalability, and relevance of responses, positioning
it as a better solution for academic advising.
The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2, we discuss previous chatbot implementations and their limitations. Section 3 provides details
about the dataset used. Section 4 details the methodologies employed. Section 6
presents the experimental results, and ﬁnally, Sect. 7 concludes the paper and
identiﬁes some future work.

2

Related Work

Academic advising has made notable progress with the adoption of AI-driven
tools and chatbot systems. However, many existing solutions still face signiﬁcant
challenges, such as limited ﬂexibility, the inability to provide real-time factual
updates, and scalability issues. For example, Abdelhamid & Alotaibi (2021) use
a multi-agent framework that provides modularity but lacks real-time updates,
while Akiba et al. (2023) explore ChatGPT, which shows promise in generative
AI but faces reliability issues, including hallucinations. Similarly, Ghazala et
al. (2022) introduce a bilingual chatbot with predeﬁned intents, which limits
scalability and ﬂexibility.
In contrast, our project utilizes a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)
model, which integrates dynamic, real-time information retrieval from a curated
knowledge base. This approach ensures both accuracy and scalability, addressing the limitations found in existing systems. Furthermore, while some studies,
such as those by Achraf & Hamza (2024), rely on proprietary large language
models, our project diﬀerentiates itself by using open-source alternatives, oﬀering a cost-eﬀective and scalable solution. This comparison demonstrates how our

82

R. Khare et al.

RAG-based method overcomes many challenges faced by previous works, with a
focus on factual accuracy and practical, real-world implementation in academic
advising.
The incorporation of AI in our chatbot not only improves the eﬃciency of
academic advising but also paves the way for smarter student support systems.
This project will signiﬁcantly impact how educational institutions approach student guidance, ensuring that students receive accurate, timely, and personalized
advice throughout their academic journeys.

advising, examining their approaches, strengths, limitations, and how they relate
to the methodology used in the current project.

Dataset

The datasets for this project were carefully created to support both Fine-Tuning
and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) methodologies. For the ﬁne-tuning
approach, a synthetic question-and-answer (Q&A) dataset was generated using
content from Wilfrid Laurier University’s academic calendar, departmental websites, and co-op resources. This data was transformed into a Q&A format using
ChatGPT (versions 3.5 and 4.0) with structured prompts. Initially, the model
produced 25 questions along with their corresponding answers based on the academic content provided.
To address issues such as irrelevant general knowledge introduced by the
model, prompts were reﬁned to ensure the generated Q&A pairs were contextually speciﬁc to the university’s academic advising. This reﬁnement also included
variations of the same question to increase the dataset’s diversity. As a result,
two distinct versions of the dataset were created:
– Advising Variation: This version includes diverse variations of the same
questions to capture a broader range of potential queries.
– Advising Unique Values: This version consists of unique, non-repetitive
Q&A pairs to avoid redundancy and ensure a more varied dataset.
In total, approximately 1,100 Q&A pairs were carefully curated, stored in
CSV format, and uploaded to the Hugging Face hub for easy access and version
control. A sample of the dataset is shown in Table 2.

4

Methodology

The development of the academic advising chatbot leveraged two primary
methodologies: Fine-Tuning and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). Both
approaches were designed to maximize the chatbot’s ability to provide accurate, contextually relevant, and eﬃcient responses tailored to academic advising
scenarios.
Fine-Tuning. The Fine-Tuning approach involved adapting a pre-trained Large
Language Model (LLM) to a domain-speciﬁc dataset, focusing on academic
advising at Wilfrid Laurier University. This required the creation of a custom
dataset derived from the 2019 academic calendar, departmental websites, and
co-op resources. The dataset, consisting of approximately 1,100 question-answer
pairs, was carefully curated and formatted to ensure relevance and accuracy.
Two distinct versions of the dataset were created: Advising Variation, which

Enhancing Academic Advising with AI

85

includes varied question forms, and Advising Unique Values, consisting of
unique, non-repetitive Q&A pairs.
To enhance computational eﬃciency, the ﬁne-tuning process incorporated
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) [11] using huggingface’s library called peft [12].
This technique selectively trains key parameters within the model, signiﬁcantly
reducing computational overhead while maintaining performance. The Llama
3.0 model with 8 billion parameters was chosen for ﬁne-tuning and optimized
using Quantized LoRA (QLoRA) [11]. By quantizing the model’s weights to 4bit precision, memory usage was reduced, enabling faster training and inference.
The ﬁne-tuning process followed an iterative cycle with early stopping strategies
to prevent overﬁtting. After the ﬁne-tuning process of Llama 3 was completed,
it was merged with the pre-trained Llama 3. This ensured that the original
pre-trained Llama 3’s can be utilized for our speciﬁc domain.
Model performance was assessed using human evaluation, Bilingual Evaluation Understudy BLEU scores of the responses generated, response relevancy
scores of the responses generated, which is calculated by obtaining the cosine
similarity of the generated response with the ground truth, and QAEvalChain
by LangChain.
Although Fine-Tuning was eﬀective for adapting the model to the academic
domain, it encountered challenges when handling dynamic datasets. The static
nature of the ﬁne-tuned dataset meant that updates to academic policies or new
information required extensive re-training, limiting the model’s scalability and
adaptability to evolving content.
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). The RAG approach was implemented
to address the limitations of Fine-Tuning, oﬀering a more dynamic and scalable
solution. RAG combines retrieval-based and generative capabilities, enabling the
chatbot to ground its responses in a curated knowledge base. This ensures high
factual accuracy and reduces the risk of hallucinations. The RAG pipeline began
with the construction of a knowledge base sourced from academic resources,
including PDFs containing course descriptions, grading policies, lab details, and
co-op program information. These documents were preprocessed into manageable text chunks, which were then embedded into a vector database using the
FAISS library [13]. This vectorization allowed for eﬃcient semantic search, ensuring that the chatbot retrieved the most relevant information for a given query.
When a user submitted a query, its vector representation was generated and
compared against the stored vectors in the knowledge base. The system used
cross-encoders to re-rank the retrieved results, prioritizing the most relevant
chunks. These top-ranked chunks were combined with the user query and passed
to the LLM (Llama 3.1) for response generation. The use of system prompts
ensured that the LLM provided concise, accurate, and contextually appropriate
answers. The architecture also incorporated iterative reﬁnement, with retrieved
contexts ﬁltered to optimize the coherence and relevance of the ﬁnal response.
Figure 2 shows the workﬂow of the RAG implementation.

86

R. Khare et al.

Fig. 2. Workﬂow of the RAG implementation

Enhancing Academic Advising with AI

87

Fine-Tuning vs. RAG. While Fine-Tuning tailored the LLM for domain-speciﬁc
tasks, its static nature posed challenges in adapting to new or updated datasets.
In contrast, RAG oﬀered a ﬂexible and dynamic solution, seamlessly integrating
new information into the knowledge base without requiring re-training. Additionally, RAG ensured high factual reliability by grounding responses in curated,
up-to-date sources. The combination of retrieval and generative techniques made
RAG more suitable for academic advising scenarios, where accuracy and adaptability are critical. By integrating these methodologies, the project developed a
chatbot that balances the strengths of generative LLMs with the precision and
reliability of retrieval-based systems, ensuring an eﬃcient and accurate academic
advising solution.

5

System Implementation and Tools

Both Fine-Tuning and RAG relied on advanced libraries and tools to streamline
development and ensure robust functionality. Key components included:
– Transformers Library: To access pre-trained LLMs and manage natural
language processing tasks.
– FAISS (Facebook AI Similarity Search): For eﬃcient vector storage and
retrieval, enabling semantic search across the knowledge base.
– LangChain: A framework used to construct the RAG pipeline, including
document preprocessing, embedding generation, and evaluation.
– QLoRA and LoRA: These techniques allowed eﬃcient ﬁne-tuning by reducing the number of trainable parameters and leveraging quantization for faster
computation.
– Sentence Transformers: For generating high-quality embeddings used in
semantic search and re-ranking processes using cross-encoder re-ranking. [14]
Solution Workflow: Document Processing and Retrieval Optimization. The document processing phase in the RAG system is critical for transforming unstructured text into a format that can be eﬀectively utilized by the LLM. Initially,
documents are converted into raw text to facilitate vector embedding generation.
This conversion ensures that unstructured information is made meaningful and
interpretable by the LLM. For this purpose, the PyPDF2 library was utilized to
extract text from PDF ﬁles.
After text extraction, a text fragmentation process was implemented. The
extracted raw text was divided into manageable chunks using LangChain’s
RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter library. Chunking allows the text to be processed in batches, which simpliﬁes embedding generation and improves eﬃciency. Two key parameters were optimized during this process, chunk size and
chunk overlap. A large chunk size might overwhelm the LLM with too much
information, while a small chunk size may fail to provide suﬃcient context for
retrieval. Similarly, chunk overlap balances coherence and repetition too much
overlap results in redundancy, while too little risks losing the context. These

88

R. Khare et al.

parameters were experimentally adjusted (chunk overlap=3, chunk size=300,
chunk overlap=5, chunk size=500) to achieve optimal performance.
Once chunked, each text segment was transformed into a vector embedding.
These embeddings capture the semantic meaning of the text and enable similarity comparisons. We utilized the llama-index-embeddings-huggingface package and a sentence transformer model, all-MiniLM-L6-v2. The same model was
also employed for embedding user queries to ensure consistent semantic representation. The embeddings were temporarily stored using Meta’s FAISS library,
which supports eﬃcient similarity searches and accelerates document retrieval
by leveraging GPU memory.
To further reﬁne the relevance of retrieved embeddings, a re-ranking
process was applied. After retrieving the top-k most relevant results from
the embedding database, these results were re-ranked using a cross-encoder
model, ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2, provided by the sentence-transformers
library. The cross-encoder method signiﬁcantly outperformed alternatives like
bi-encoders in ranking relevance, ensuring that the highest-quality information
was prioritized for response generation.
Finally, the response generation phase used the LLM (Llama 3.1, unquantized) to synthesize the ﬁnal answer. A system prompt was provided to guide
the LLM on its task, ensuring the user query and retrieved context were accurately processed. The context, derived from the highest-ranked results, allowed
the LLM to generate a coherent and relevant response to the user’s query. These
steps are depicted in Fig. 2. The workﬂow was rigorously tested to ensure performance and accuracy, forming the backbone of the RAG chatbot’s functionality.
Further experimentation and results analysis are detailed in subsequent sections.
The following outlines the key steps involved in the document processing
phase of the RAG system, which transforms unstructured text into structured
data that can be eﬀectively utilized by the LLM:
1. Document Conversion:
– Objective: Convert unstructured documents into raw text for further
processing.
– Action: Use the PyPDF2 library to extract text from PDF ﬁles, transforming the unstructured data into a format suitable for processing by
the LLM.
2. Text Chunking:
– Objective: Break the extracted text into manageable segments for eﬃcient processing.
– Action: Implement LangChain’s RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter to
divide the raw text into chunks.
– Parameters Adjusted:
. chunk size: Optimized to ensure enough context is provided without
overwhelming the LLM.
. chunk overlap: Adjusted to balance coherence and avoid redundancy
in the chunks.
3. Vector Embedding Generation:

Enhancing Academic Advising with AI

89

– Objective: Transform text chunks into embeddings that capture semantic meaning.
– Action: Use the llama-index-embeddings-huggingface package along
with the sentence transformer model all-MiniLM-L6-v2 to create embeddings for both the text chunks and user queries.
– Storage: Temporarily store embeddings in Meta’s FAISS library to support fast similarity searches and retrieval.
4. Re-Ranking of Retrieved Results:
– Objective: Reﬁne the relevance of the embeddings retrieved from the
database.
– Action: After retrieving the top-k most relevant embeddings, re-rank
them using the ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2 cross-encoder model, which
improves ranking accuracy compared to bi-encoders.
5. Response Generation:
– Objective: Generate a meaningful response to the user’s query using the
LLM.
– Action: Use the LLaMA model (version 3.1, unquantized) to synthesize
the ﬁnal answer, utilizing the highest-ranked results as context.
– Guidance: A system prompt is used to ensure proper understanding of
the task by the LLM.
6. Testing and Evaluation:
– Objective: Ensure the entire workﬂow operates with optimal performance and accuracy.
– Action: Extracted cosine similarity for response relevancy, BLEU scores
for semantic similarity, and utilized QAEvalChain by LangChain for ﬁnding correctness of a response, which is judged by a diﬀerent LLM.

6

Results and Experiments

To thoroughly assess the performance of the Retrieval-Augmented Generation
(RAG) chatbot compared to the Fine-Tuned chatbot, various evaluation metrics
and methodologies were employed. These evaluations focused on the correctness,
relevance, and eﬃciency of responses generated by both systems.
For testing both chatbots performance, we created a question, answer and
context set. The ’answer’ in the set, was a similar-written response that we
expected our chatbot to generated, and the context was provided for the evaluation metrics to compare the generated answer’s relevancy. Afterwards, these
questions were passed to both the chatbot solutions. There were 2 diﬀerent sets
which were created for testing. The ﬁrst test contained 2 pairs of question, answer
and context, and the second set contained 5 pairs question, answer and context.
The ﬁndings consistently highlighted the superiority of the RAG-based system in most evaluation criteria.

90

R. Khare et al.

– Human Evaluation: The initial evaluation involved manual testing, where
human evaluators assessed the correctness of responses generated by both
chatbots. This qualitative evaluation method required human judges to classify each response as either correct or incorrect, based on their domain knowledge and familiarity with the dataset. For instance, in the RAG chatbot
provided an accurate response, correctly referencing the context from which
the information was retrieved. In contrast, the Fine-Tuned chatbot generated
incorrect responses, such as including courses irrelevant to the query due to
its inability to dynamically retrieve updated or context-speciﬁc information,
snapshots of the responses generated are shown in Figs. 4 and 5. This test was
pivotal in establishing that the RAG chatbot could consistently produce factually accurate and context-aware responses, addressing a critical limitation
of Fine-Tuning.

Fig. 3. QAEvalChain’s Result for the ﬁnetuned chatbot.

Fig. 4. Evaluation results for ﬁne-tuned chatbot showing relevance and correctness
metrics for speciﬁc questions.
