91

Fig. 5. Fine-tuned chatbot’s evaluation results, showing generated answers, expected
answers, and relevance metrics for speciﬁc queries.

Fig. 6. BLEU Score plot for the RAG chatbot.

– BLEU Scores: The BLEU (Bilingual Evaluation Understudy) score was
employed to measure the semantic overlap between the chatbot-generated
response and a reference answer. BLEU evaluates responses on a scale of 0 to
1, with 0 indicating no overlap and 1 representing an exact match. The ﬁnetuned chatbot’s mean BLEU score for a test set of 2 questions was 0.0071,
and for the test set of 5 questions was 0.0029, as visible in Figs. 7 and 7. Both

92

R. Khare et al.

Fig. 7. BLEU scores for ﬁne-tuned chatbot in experiment 1 (2 questions).

Fig. 8. BLEU scores for ﬁne-tuned chatbot in experiment 2 (5 questions).

these scores indicate a very poor performance. Moreover, the RAG chatbot
also scored poorly on this metric, as seen in Fig. 6. The primary reason for
RAG chatbo’s low score was the inclusion of detailed context in the chatbot’s responses, which increased word count and reduced the likelihood of
an exact word-for-word match with the reference answers. While this negatively impacted BLEU scores, it underscored the chatbot’s ability to generate
context-rich, informative responses, a strength that BLEU cannot adequately
capture.

Enhancing Academic Advising with AI

93

– LangChain’s Evaluation Library: To enhance evaluation, LangChain’s
QAEvalChain was used to validate the generated responses. This library
leverages a pre-trained LLM to compare a chatbot’s generated response with
a reference answer and determine correctness. The 2-question validation set
was utilized for this and converted in JSON format. QAEvalChain’s evaluate method compares the generated response to the correct answer of this
validation set. The RAG chatbot performed comparatively well, as demonstrated in Fig. 9 where all responses were graded as “CORRECT”, where in
Fig. 3 we can observe that the ﬁnetuned model was highly incorrect with both
responses graded “INCORRECT”. By leveraging this approach, the RAG system demonstrated its ability to integrate context from the knowledge base
eﬀectively, thereby producing accurate and relevant answers.

Fig. 9. QAEvalChain’s Result for RAG implementation.

– Response Relevancy: Response relevancy was measured using cosine similarity, a metric that evaluates the semantic similarity between two vector
representations. Cosine similarity values range from –1 (completely dissimilar) to 1 (identical). The ﬁne-tuned chatbot’s score on the 2-question set was
0.272 and for the 5-question set was 0.3578. These plots can be observed in
Figs. 10 and 11. In comparison, the RAG chatbot’s responses scored a similarity score of 0.43 for question one and 0.06 for question two. However,
responses to simpler questions, such as those requiring a single-word answer,
resulted in lower scores due to the inclusion of longer, more detailed context in
the generated responses. This discrepancy brought the mean cosine similarity
score for the RAG chatbot to 0.244, as can be observed in Fig. 12. With these
scores being stated, human evaluation and QAEvalChain’s results favored the
RAG responses more as compared to the ﬁne-tuned chatbot. The response
relevancy scores for the RAG chatbot highlights a need for optimization in
response length to maintain a balance between brevity and contextual depth
(Fig. 3, 8 and 9).

Fig. 11. Response relevancy plot for ﬁne-tuned chatbot in experiment 2 (5 questions).

7

Conclusion and Future Work

This project aims to automate academic advising as much as possible by developing a system that streamlines common tasks. We identiﬁed that while students often need personalized guidance, many of their inquiries are repetitive
and straightforward, which unnecessarily burdens academic advisors. To address
this, we have created a solution that utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) to
instantly provide accurate answers to frequently asked questions, automating
routine inquiries and enabling advisors to focus on more complex, individualized cases
The implementation of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) was a cornerstone of this system, as it enabled eﬃcient retrieval of relevant, accurate, and
up-to-date information from a curated knowledge base. This knowledge base was
created using datasets we built by scraping Wilfrid Laurier University’s website
and extracting content from PDF ﬁles containing information on academic programs, course descriptions, policies, co-op guidelines, and other relevant details.
These data sources were processed into structured text to create a comprehensive
dataset that could be utilized for both ﬁne-tuning and retrieval purposes. These
datasets allowed us to ensure coverage of all relevant topics and the inclusion of
up-to-date information, providing a reliable foundation for the chatbot. Through
extensive experimentation, we demonstrated that RAG signiﬁcantly outperforms
the ﬁne-tuning approach in human evaluations, especially in the context of this
study on academic advising, where providing accurate and up-to-date information is crucial for student success. Unlike ﬁne-tuned systems that rely solely on
static training data, RAG dynamically retrieves knowledge, making it a more
robust solution for delivering contextually accurate responses.

96

R. Khare et al.

Future Work. Our preliminary ﬁndings have opened doors for numerous future
opportunities. First, response generation could be optimized by reducing the
inclusion of unnecessary context in answers, which currently hinders evaluation
metrics such as BLEU scores and response relevancy measurements. Additionally, the system generates complete responses before displaying them, which
is less eﬃcient than real-time response streaming. Implementing a streaming
mechanism would align the chatbot’s performance with state-of-the-art LLMs,
enhancing user experience.
Another area for improvement is the introduction of a web interface, which
would allow academic advisors to manage the knowledge base dynamically by
uploading or removing documents. This interface could also enable users without advisor credentials to access the chatbot for general queries, increasing its
accessibility and utility.
At present, the knowledge base is hosted using Kaggle’s dataset features.
Transitioning to cloud-based knowledge-base technologies would enhance scalability and reliability. We also aim to improve response generation speed by optimizing the steps of the RAG process, enabling quicker retrieval and response
times.
Expanding the chatbot’s scope beyond academic advising to other domains
within educational institutions is another priority, allowing it to serve a broader
audience while maintaining its core functionality.
Finally, we plan to incorporate multimodal capabilities into the system. Currently, the chatbot supports only textual data. However, enabling it to process
and understand graphs, ﬁgures, and diagrams would greatly enhance its versatility and application across diverse academic scenarios. These future developments aim to create a system that is not only eﬃcient and scalable but also
highly adaptable to the evolving needs of academic environments. By leveraging
datasets created from university resources and enhancing the RAG pipeline, this
system is positioned to transform the landscape of academic advising, ensuring
it remains both accessible and eﬀective for all stakeholders.

for their invaluable help and guidance throughout the course of this work. Their expertise, support, and constructive feedback have greatly contributed to the success of this
project, and I am deeply appreciative of their time and eﬀorts.

Overlay_dx - Automating Forecasting
Evaluation
Long H. Ngo1(B) , Mohammed Amine Chamli1 , Jonathan Rivalan1 ,
and Thomas Jaillon2
1

Smile, Asnières, France
long.ngo@smile.fr
2
Paris, France

Abstract. Traditional evaluation metrics provides numerical values
but often lack comprehensibility, hindering eﬀective diﬀerentiation of
model performances. Our work addresses this challenge by introducing .overlay_dx, a novel evaluation metric measuring the performance
of time series prediction models. .Overlay_dx is a visual metric that
represents the percentage of predictions falling within a conﬁdence interval around actual values. Additionally, once evaluation results are plotted, .overlay_dx computes the area under the overlay curve, providing a quantitative measure of alignment between predicted and actual
values across diﬀerent thresholds and predictions. Through extensive
experiments, we demonstrate that our approach oﬀers a uniﬁed evaluation framework that combines both visual and numerical assessments,
enabling improved model comparison and providing valuable insights for
further research and optimization eﬀorts in time series prediction.
Keywords: Machine learning · Evaluation metric · Time series data ·
Forecasting · Optimization

1

Introduction

Time series prediction models have become increasingly central in various
domains, from ﬁnancial forecasting to industrial monitoring. However, evaluating these models presents a challenge. Traditional evaluation metrics provide
numerical scores that, while mathematically sound, often fail to capture the
nuanced performance characteristics that practitioners need to make informed
decisions. This limitation becomes particularly apparent when comparing multiple models or when communicating model performance to stakeholders with
varying levels of technical expertise.
For example, when comparing two forecasting models using RMSE, a difference between scores of 0.15 and 0.17 provides little intuitive understanding
of real-world performance implications. Traditional metrics also fail to capture
important aspects like timing of predictions and consistency across diﬀerent
scales, which are crucial for domain-oriented applications such as energy forecasting or ﬁnancial markets analysis.
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 98–110, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_7

Overlay_dx - Automating Forecasting Evaluation

99

Current approaches to time series evaluation typically fall into three categories: point-wise metrics (e.g. Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE), Mean Absolute Error (MAE)), distribution-based metrics (e.g. Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence), and shape-based metrics (e.g. Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)). While
each category oﬀers speciﬁc insights, they often fail to provide a uniﬁed framework that combines statistical rigor with intuitive interpretability. This fragmentation in evaluation approaches makes it diﬃcult for practitioners to make
holistic assessments of model performance.
In this work, we address this fundamental challenge through the introduction
of .overlay_dx, a novel evaluation metric that combines visual interpretability
with quantitative rigor. This new understandable evaluation metric helps to
compare the performance of multiple models, not only through its score but also
through visualization of prediction performance. The key contributions of our
work include: 1) Development of a new visual evaluation metric (.overlay_dx)
that represents prediction accuracy through an intuitive conﬁdence interval approach; 2) Introduction of a quantitative scoring mechanism based on the area
under the overlay curve; 3) Demonstration of the metric’s eﬀectiveness across
various time series prediction scenarios; 4) Provision of a framework that bridges
the gap between technical evaluation and interpretability.
The .overlay_dx metric oﬀers several advantages over traditional evaluation
approaches, including enhanced visual interpretability for eﬀective communication with non-technical stakeholders, robustness to outliers and extreme values,
ability to capture performance characteristics across varying prediction thresholds, and seamless integration with existing evaluation workﬂows.
.Overlay_dx is implemented as an open source project to ensure reproducibility and community adoption. The codebase, along with documentation and usage
examples, is publicly available on GitHub1 . This implementation supports seamless integration with popular machine learning frameworks and includes utilities
for visualizing overlay curves and computing Area Under Curve (AUC) scores [1].
The rest of the paper is organized as follows: Sect. 2 introduces the related
works; Sect. 3 presents the design principles; Sect. 4 shows the experiments;
Sect. 5 concludes the paper while listing the contributions and future works.

2

Related Works

The quality of time series predictions is assessed using evaluation metrics. Evaluation metrics measure the accuracy of predictions by comparing predicted values
with actual values. The most commonly used evaluation metrics for assessing
predictions include MAE [2,3] and its family, RMSE [3, 4] and its family, and
Akaike’s entropy-based Information Criterion (AIC) [5, 6]. Below, we review the
most commonly used metrics for forecasting models evaluation.
Mean Absolute Error (MAE) represents the average of the absolute diﬀerences between predicted and actual values. This measure shows us what level of

proportionally very high or low errors. With MAE, lower values indicate better
predictions.

Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) [7] represents the proportion of
the mean diﬀerence between actual and predicted values divided by the actual
value. This measure works best with data without zeros and extreme values due
to the denominator. The smaller the MAPE, the better the model.

Weighted Mean Absolute Percentage Error (WMAPE) [8] is similar to
MAPE, but the errors are weighted according to the absolute value of the target
value. This can be useful in preventing large errors in target values from overly
inﬂuencing the evaluation measure.

Mean Squared Error (MSE) [9] is deﬁned as the average of the squared errors.
This measure integrates variance and bias, and solves the extreme value and zero
problems of MAE and MAPE. The smaller the score, the better the prediction.

Overlay_dx - Automating Forecasting Evaluation

where .Ymax /.Ymin denote maximum/minimum actual values and .Y actual values.
Additionally, Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) [11] is a traditional metric that
measures similarity between temporal sequences by allowing elastic transformation of time series. Unlike point-wise metrics, DTW can capture phase shifts
and temporal distortions. However, its computational complexity and lack of
intuitive interpretation limit its practical application.
These approaches, while valuable, often focus on speciﬁc aspects of model performance rather than providing a comprehensive evaluation framework. Recent
advances in time series evaluation have explored multi-objective metrics that
combine multiple aspects of prediction quality. For instance, TIGER [12] incorporates domain-speciﬁc constraints into the evaluation framework. However, these
approaches often increase complexity without proportionally improving interpretability, highlighting the need for our proposed .overlay_dx method.
While existing metrics have served the ﬁeld well, they share common limitations: (1) diﬃculty in interpreting scores in practical terms, (2) sensitivity to
outliers and noise, and (3) lack of visual interpretability. These limitations particularly aﬀect practitioners who need to make quick, informed decisions about
model selection and optimization. Our proposed .overlay_dx metric directly
addresses these gaps while maintaining mathematical rigor.

3

Methodology

The methodology employed in this study involves the implementation of highly
visual metrics and measures aimed at enhancing understanding through visualization while exploring new possibilities. Subsequently, a new visual metric is
developed and applied to assess the performance of predictive models in time
series analysis.
For instance, the peak overlap rate (local extrema) was implemented to assess
whether predictions were capable of anticipating incidents or extreme values in
a time series. Subsequently, the overlay (overlap rate) is deﬁned, representing
the percentage of predictions falling within a conﬁdence interval drawn around
the actual values of the series. As illustrated in Fig. 1a, the overlay indicates

Fig. 1. Overview of .overlay_dx visualisation.

102

L. H. Ngo et al.

the percentage of forecasted values (in red) falling within the intervals (gray,
orange, and green) around the target values (in blue). The overlay value varies
depending on the size of the interval drawn and can only be equal to or lower
than the previous measure when reducing the threshold interval.
By varying the size of the conﬁdence interval, multiple overlay measures were
conducted on a single prediction. These interval-based measures can be visualized in a single graph (see Fig. 1b, depicting a local CPU usage forecast). In this
graph, the interval size (in decreasing order) is plotted on the x-axis, while the
overlay measure for that interval is plotted on the y-axis, resulting in the curve
proﬁle as depicted in Fig. 1b. This curve inevitably decreases as the size of the
conﬁdence interval diminishes, allowing for the visual identiﬁcation of the optimal curve proﬁle, which remains the highest and longest. Thus, the overlay curve
enables the visualization of a model’s performance through a curve proﬁle. Consequently, within a single graph, it is possible to present multiple curve proﬁles
and visually compare the performance of several models or conﬁgurations. As
shown in Fig. 1b, it is feasible to quantify and visually measure the performance
of one approach compared to another.
Given that the visualization of the .overlay_dx allows for the comparison of
several curves, there must also be a numerical metric to diﬀerentiate between two
very similar curve proﬁles and make the .overlay_dx an evaluation metric. The
.overlay_dx score is obtained by evaluating the area under the overlay curve,
which represents the cumulative .overlay_dx measures for diﬀerent thresholds.
More speciﬁcally, .overlay_dx consists of several measures of the overlay metric,
which draws an interval around the target values and returns the percentage of
forecasted values that fall within this interval. .Overlay_dx calculates diﬀerent
measures of the overlay metric by reducing the size of its interval. Speciﬁcally,
.overlay_dx computes the percentage of values where the absolute diﬀerence
between the forecast and actual values is less than or equal to a threshold .x.
A high score indicates better alignment between predicted and actual values,
while a low score indicates a larger deviation from the ideal scenario where
perfect alignment is achieved at threshold = 100. For instance, a score of 77%
represents how well the forecasted values align with the actual values at diﬀerent
thresholds. It indicates that the achieved score is 77% of the maximum possible
score, where perfect alignment would occur at 100% thresholds.
The score quantiﬁes overall accuracy relative to the ideal scenario. The higher
the score, the better the alignment between forecasted and actual values, while
a lower score suggests larger deviations. The overlay curve intuitively evaluates
model performance, providing insights into accuracy across diﬀerent thresholds
and highlighting areas for optimization.
The major advantage of this new metric lies in its visual representation
through the overlay curve. Unlike traditional numerical measures, the overlay
curve allows for a rapid understanding of the model’s performance without
being signiﬁcantly inﬂuenced by outliers. It provides an overview of the model’s
accuracy across chosen thresholds. By examining the overlay curve, it is possible to assess the model’s performance for diﬀerent threshold levels. The point

where deviations become more signiﬁcant can be identiﬁed, highlighting areas
requiring potential improvement or speciﬁc attention. The overlay curve oﬀers
a more nuanced evaluation of model accuracy, considering performance at different thresholds and identifying speciﬁc thresholds requiring attention. It also
provides a clear and understandable visual representation of model performance,
facilitating communication and interpretation of results.
In summary, the use of the .overlay_dx metric and overlay curve enables a
more comprehensive evaluation of time series prediction accuracy. It provides a
relative measure of alignment between predicted and actual values while oﬀering
a clear visualization of model performance. This approach oﬀers more precise
perspectives for improvement and optimization by highlighting speciﬁc thresholds where deviations become more signiﬁcant and facilitating the identiﬁcation
of areas requiring further research. Algorithm 2 summarizes the approach to
calculate the .overlay_dx metric with the help of Algorithm 1.

Experiments

To demonstrate the eﬀectiveness and utility of the .overlay_dx metric, we conducted extensive experiments using both synthetic and real-world time series
data. These experiments aimed to compare .overlay_dx with traditional metrics, assess its robustness to outliers, demonstrate its utility in model selection,
and validate its applicability across diﬀerent types of time series data.
4.1

Experimental Setup

To validate the performance of .overlay_dx, we utilized three real-world datasets,
namely Beijing Multi-Site Air-Quality (Pollution) [13], Electricity Transformer
Temperature (ETT) [14], and Electricity Load Diagrams 2011–2014 (ELD) [15]
datasets and a simulated dataset.
The simulated dataset comprises diverse time series, each derived from a base
curve modiﬁed by adding noise, introducing outliers, bias, delay, or other alterations (see Table 1). The base curve represents the target, while the modiﬁed
versions simulate predictions made by a model. The objective is to showcase
diverse scenarios and evaluate .overlay_dx’s eﬀectiveness across various situations.

The pollution dataset [13] includes hourly air pollutant measurements from
12 air quality monitoring stations in Beijing. It includes data on six major air
pollutants along with six related meteorological variables. The data, covering
2013 to 2017, were sourced from the Beijing Municipal Environmental Monitoring Center and local weather stations operated by the China Meteorological
Administration. Missing values are represented as NA.

Overlay_dx - Automating Forecasting Evaluation

105

Fig. 2. Heat map of metrics correlation on the simulated dataset.

The ETT dataset [14] contains two years of data from two counties in China,
focusing on long-term electric power system deployment. It includes subsets at
the 1-hour (ETTh1, ETTh2) and 15-minute (ETTm1) levels, with each data
point featuring the target variable “oil temperature” and six power load features.
It is split into training, validation, and test sets at a 12/4/4-month ratio.
The ELD dataset [15] contains electricity consumption of 370 points/ clients
from 2011 to 2014.
We selected eight time series forecasting methods for comparison on the two
real-world datasets, including Linear Regression [16], Random Forest Regressor [17, 18], XGBoost Regressor [19], LightGBM Regressor [20], K-nearest Neighbors Regressor [21,22], Extra Trees Regressor [23], Bagging Regressor [24], and
ARIMA [25].
In our experiments, we used TimeSeriesSplit2 as the cross-validator. Unlike
traditional k-fold cross-validation, TimeSeriesSplit maintains the chronological
order of observations. In the k-th split, the ﬁrst k folds serve as the training set,
and the (k+1)-th fold is used for testing, mimicking real-world scenarios where
models are trained on historical data to predict future outcomes.
4.2

Experimental Results

To ensure robust evaluation, we conducted experiments across multiple dimensions. First, we calculated the correlation of .overlay_dx and other metrics using
the simulated dataset. The heat map in Fig. 2 reveals a high negative correlation
between the .overlay_dx and absolute error metrics (MAE, RMSE, MSE), with
values ranging from .−0.62 to .−0.5, implies that .overlay_dx prioritizes absolute improvements in error magnitudes. It has weak positive correlations with
percentage-based metrics (MAPE, NRMSE), suggesting that its interpretation
2

TimeSeriesSplit.html.

may diﬀer for datasets where relative errors are more critical. The weak correlation with WMAPE suggests .overlay_dx is somewhat eﬀective in capturing
weighted errors but not as strongly as absolute error metrics. In conclusion,
the varying correlations demonstrate that .overlay_dx captures diﬀerent performance aspects compared to traditional metrics, providing a complementary
perspective on model accuracy.
We then compared .overlay_dx with traditional metrics, including MAE,
RMSE, MSE, MAE, MAPE, WMAPE, and NRSME, across all the models on
the two real-world datasets.
Detailed error analysis revealed distinct performance patterns across diﬀerent time scales. Figure 3 illustrates the visual prediction results of 6 forecasting
methods on the ETT dataset. Visualization of predictions vs. actual values shows
that all models sometimes struggle with sudden extreme events.
Table 2, which presents the .overlay_dx scores and traditional metrics, and
Fig. 4, which shows the .overlay_dx graphs for all models, demonstrate how
.overlay_dx metric oﬀers a nuanced evaluation framework that complements
traditional metrics by capturing performance across multiple conﬁdence intervals. In the pollution dataset, ExtraTreesRegressor demonstrates superior performance with the highest .overlay_dx score of 0.952, accompanied by the lowest
RMSE (36.018), MAE (25.9096), and MAPE (0.591). The WMAPE of .0.00 for
ARIMA highlights a computational anomaly, underscoring the limitations of
this metric. This suggests that the WMAPE calculation for ARIMA might have
encountered a computational edge case problem. Similarly, in the ETT and ELD
datasets, RandomForestRegressor and ARIMA lead with .overlay_dx scores of
0.674 and 0.847, respectively, showcasing the metric’s ability to provide comprehensive model assessment beyond single-point evaluations.

5

Conclusion and Future Works

Our introduction of .overlay_dx represents a signiﬁcant advancement in evaluating time series prediction models by combining visual interpretability and
quantitative assessments into a comprehensive framework, addressing a critical
gap in existing methods. The overlay curve provides an intuitive visual representation of model performance, allowing for rapid understanding and identiﬁcation of areas for improvement, while .overlay_dx oﬀers a quantitative measure
of alignment between predicted and actual values. This dual approach enhances
the evaluation process, making it both interpretable and precise. Additionally,
.overlay_dx serves as a comprehensive evaluation framework, accommodating
both technical and non-technical stakeholders to facilitate better communication and decision-making in model selection. Through extensive experimentation, we have demonstrated its reliability across various datasets, model types,
and prediction scenarios.
While .overlay_dx oﬀers signiﬁcant advantages, we acknowledge several limitations. While the computational complexity increases with the number of
threshold levels evaluated, the choice of threshold ranges can inﬂuence the ﬁnal

Overlay_dx - Automating Forecasting Evaluation

109

score. Also, the visual interpretation may still require some training for optimal
use.
This work opens several promising leads for future research. One direction involves the development of algorithms for automated threshold selection,
enabling the determination of optimal threshold ranges for various types of time
series data. Another is the extension of .overlay_dx to accommodate multivariate
time series prediction evaluation. Exploring real-time evaluation methods, such
as streaming variants of .overlay_dx for online assessment of prediction models, also presents valuable opportunities. Additionally, integrating .overlay_dx
into deep learning frameworks by developing .overlay_dx-based loss functions
for neural network training could enhance model performance. Finally, creating
domain-speciﬁc adaptations of .overlay_dx for specialized applications, such as
ﬁnancial forecasting or weather prediction, oﬀers signiﬁcant potential for targeted advancements.
We believe .overlay_dx represents a signiﬁcant step forward in time series
model evaluation, providing a foundation for future research in the forecasting
area. The metric’s ability to combine visual interpretability with quantitative
rigor addresses a fundamental need in the ﬁeld, and its extensibility provides
numerous opportunities for future development and application.
Acknowledgments. This work has been funded by the European Union’s Horizon Europe research and innovation program under grant agreement No. 101070487
(NEPHELE).

Integrated Deep Learning Approaches for Coral
Health Monitoring and Database Development
Dua Weraikat1(B)

, Febin Antony2 , and Sabrina Albukhari1

1 Rochester Institute of Technology, Dubai, United Arab Emirates

dxwcad@rit.edu
2 Westford University College, Sharjah, United Arab Emirates

Abstract. Coral reefs are vital marine ecosystems that play a crucial role in supporting biodiversity, providing coastal protection, and driving global fisheries and
tourism economies. However, these ecosystems are under increasing threat from
coral bleaching, driven by climate change and human-induced stressors. Monitoring and mitigating coral bleaching require scalable, automated systems capable
of accurate detection and classification. This study presents a synthesis of stateof-the-art deep learning models, including VGG16 and YOLO, for coral health
assessment. Additionally, a novel annotated coral bleaching database is introduced,
designed from diverse video and image sources to train machine learning models
effectively. Our results demonstrate that these approaches not only improve the
accuracy of coral health classification but also enhance the scalability and reliability of reef monitoring efforts, supporting marine conservation initiatives. Future
work focuses on expanding datasets and exploring advanced architectures such as
ResNet50 and EfficientNet to improve generalization and robustness.
Keywords: Deep Learning · YOLOv5 · VGG16 · Image Database · Coral
Bleaching

1 Introduction
Coral reefs are one of the most ecologically and economically valuable ecosystems,
providing services such as coastal protection, fishery resources, and tourism, harboring
approximately 25% of marine species despite occupying less than 1% of the ocean floor.
However, the health of coral reefs is increasingly jeopardized by coral bleaching, a phenomenon where corals lose their symbiotic algae (zooxanthellae) due to environmental
stressors like elevated sea temperatures [1]. If stress continues, corals often die, which
severely impacts the entire ecosystem. Global climate change, including ocean warming,
has led to an increase in coral bleaching events, making timely and accurate monitoring
essential for conservation and mitigation efforts [2].
The frequency of bleaching events has increased significantly over the last three
decades, correlating with rising ocean temperatures driven by climate change. Notably,
mass bleaching events during El Niño years have devastated coral populations worldwide. While traditional methods for detecting coral bleaching such as manual surveys,
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 111–121, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_8

112

D. Weraikat et al.

satellite imagery, and aerial photographs have been utilized, they are limited by high
cost, logistical challenges, and inconsistent human interpretation [3, 4]. A growing body
of research suggests that image processing and machine learning algorithms offer more
scalable, cost-effective, and accurate methods of detecting bleaching [5]. Convolutional
neural networks (CNNs), such as VGG16 and YOLO, have shown exceptional potential
in automating coral classification and detection. This can be implemented on underwater
robots that can dive in oceans to collect data.
There is however a significant gap in the availability of comprehensive databases
that integrate these modern technologies with bleaching imagery. Existing databases
are often region-specific, lack integration of advanced computational methods, or are
incomplete in terms of metadata such as environmental conditions [6, 7]. This paper aims
to address this gap by creating a coral bleaching database from diverse image and video
sources, specifically targeting the use of these data for machine learning algorithms. This
database is a crucial resource for developing accurate models for automated detection
and offers substantial potential for global conservation efforts.
The reminder of the article is as follows. The relevant literature is provided in the
Sect. 2, Sect. 3 explains the methodology followed to create and train the database
using two deep learning models followed by results and conclusions n Sects. 4 and 5
respectively.

2 Literature Review
2.1 Coral Bleaching: Causes and Impact
Coral bleaching is primarily caused by prolonged thermal stress, often associated with
climate-induced ocean warming [2, 7]. Studies have highlighted that ocean temperature
irregularities are the leading cause of widespread bleaching, especially during El Niño
events. Mass bleaching events, such as those observed between 2014 and 2017, highlight
the urgent need for global monitoring and intervention [8, 9]. The need for real-time,
high-accuracy monitoring systems is well-established, with remote sensing technologies
being identified as a key tool in this effort [4].
Historically, monitoring efforts have relied heavily on diver surveys and manual
assessments, both of which can be error-prone and resource-intensive [10]. Satellite
imagery and aerial photography have expanded the scope of monitoring, but these methods are limited by cost and resolution constraints [3]. Moreover, there is no universal
standard for data collection and analysis, which limits the comparability of data across
different studies [2].
2.2 Traditional and Deep Learning Methods for Coral Monitoring
Historically, coral monitoring has relied on manual surveys conducted by divers, providing high-resolution, direct observations. However, these methods are resource-intensive,
time-consuming, and prone to observer bias. Satellite-based remote sensing and aerial
photography have expanded monitoring capabilities but face challenges related to spatial resolution, costs, and weather dependency [5]. Deep learning approaches have

Integrated Deep Learning Approaches

113

gained traction as scalable and accurate solutions for coral reef monitoring. VGG16
and VGG19, hallmark CNN architectures, are widely recognized for their robust feature
extraction capabilities and have been applied to classify coral health with remarkable
accuracy. These architectures excel in hierarchical feature extraction, enabling precise
differentiation between healthy and bleached corals.
The application of image processing and machine learning in environmental monitoring has rapidly gained traction, particularly for automating the identification of ecological patterns [11–13]. In the marine environment, image processing techniques such
as object-based image analysis (OBIA) and deep learning models have demonstrated
their utility in mapping coral reefs and classifying different coral species [5].
YOLO, a landmark in object detection, offers real-time efficiency by framing detection as a single regression problem. Its grid-based approach enables simultaneous localization and classification of coral regions, making it ideal for large-scale reef assessments.
These models have the ability to detect bleaching from photographic data, offering realtime, scalable monitoring solutions that traditional methods cannot achieve. However,
the success of these algorithms is contingent on access to high-quality, labeled datasets
[14, 15].
2.3 Challenges in Dataset Development
The lack of high-quality, annotated datasets tailored for coral reef monitoring remains
a significant bottleneck in applying deep learning models. There are some regional
databases for coral health monitoring exist, such as those created by Garza-Perez et al.
[6] for the Gulf of California and Chen et al. [16] for the Great Barrier Reef. While
these datasets provide valuable insights, they are often geographically limited and do
not incorporate the variety of environmental conditions required for machine learning
models to generalize effectively [17].
ReefBase (https://scistarter.org/reefbase) managed by WorldFish, is one of the most
comprehensive coral reef databases, but it focuses more on reef biodiversity than on
bleaching events specifically. Furthermore, these datasets lack the necessary resolution
or frequency of updates to support real-time machine learning applications.
To address this, our proposed database integrates videos and images from various
sources, with complete metadata about environmental conditions. This approach fills
the existing gap by providing a diverse, centralized, and accessible database for training
machine learning algorithms, thus enhancing coral bleaching detection.

3 Methodology
The project employed a systematic, multi-stage approach to achieve effective coral health
detection and classification. Each stage was designed to optimize performance and ensure
the robustness of the detection system, Fig. 1.
3.1 Database and Image Extraction
To build a robust dataset for training models to detect coral bleaching, we used the
Roboflow platform, a widely-used tool in computer vision. Roboflow facilitates data

114

D. Weraikat et al.

collection, preprocessing, annotation, and augmentation, making it efficient to create
and organize datasets.
A workspace titled “Coral Reef Bleaching” was created on Roboflow. Multiple documentaries and videos related to coral reef bleaching, mainly sourced from YouTube,
were imported. Video content was preferred due to the time-intensive nature of manually
collecting images from the internet. Roboflow’s video segmentation feature extracted
individual frames from the videos, significantly increasing the dataset size.
These frames were then automatically segmented into individual images, as shown in
Fig. 2. The “Annotate” feature in Roboflow was used to manually label each image, with
team members highlighting regions of coral bleaching using bounding boxes (Fig. 3).
These annotations are critical for training accurate object detection models.
The dataset was central to the project’s success, tailored specifically for YOLObased object detection. The dataset was constructed from diverse sources, including
documentaries and online videos documenting coral bleaching events. Frames were
extracted from these videos, increasing dataset variability and size.
Preprocessing: Images were standardized through resolution adjustments, denoising,
normalization, and segmentation to isolate regions of interest (ROIs). Augmentation:
Data augmentation techniques, including rotation, flipping, and brightness adjustments,
were applied to enhance model generalization. Figure 4 illustrates the augmented coral
images.
To evaluate the models effectively and avoid data leakage, the dataset was divided
into: Training Set (80%): Used for training the deep learning models, Validation Set
(10%): Used to tune hyperparameters and evaluate model performance during training, and Test Set (10%): Reserved for final evaluation on unseen data. This 80-10-10
split ensured the model’s ability to generalize across different datasets and prevented
overfitting.
3.2 Overview of Deep Learning Models
VGG16. VGG16 are hallmark convolutional networks, renowned for their uniform
architecture, where each convolutional block is followed by max-pooling. The simplicity
of their design—utilizing only 3x3 convolutions filters—belies their effectiveness in
capturing hierarchical image features. These models are tailored for tasks demanding
precise and systematic feature extraction [18]. Figure 5 provides the architecture of the
VGG16 method.
YOLO (You Only Look Once), YOLO is a landmark model in real-time object detection, framing the task as a single regression problem rather than region-based classification [19]. It divides an image into grids, with each grid cell predicting bounding boxes,
confidence scores, and class probabilities. YOLO’s lightweight architecture and unified
approach enable rapid and accurate detection, making it an excellent choice for coral
localization within reef [20].
Figure 6 illustrates YOLO’s grid-based method for predicting bounding boxes and
class probabilities. This approach enhances real-time efficiency by treating detection as
a single regression problem. The figure shows YOLO’s capability to detect and classify
coral types or conditions in reef images.

Integrated Deep Learning Approaches

115

Fig. 1. Proposed Methodology for Coral Health Detection and Classification Method

Bounding boxes around identified objects (like corals) are annotated with class labels
and confidence scores, ranging from 0 to 1. For example, “Healthy Coral: 0.95” indicates
95% confidence in identification, while “Bleached Coral: 0.80” suggests 80% certainty.
YOLO’s accuracy is reflected in the precision of these bounding boxes and its ability to
process entire images quickly. This figure demonstrates how YOLO’s real-time detection
can support large-scale reef health assessments with reliable data. Specific values or
statistics in the figure highlight the model’s accuracy in detecting coral health states
under various conditions.
3.3 Model Development
The project combined object detection and classification models to achieve comprehensive coral health analysis. YOLO for Object Detection: YOLO was configured to

D. Weraikat et al.

Fig. 2. Segmented frames from a documentary for annotation

Fig. 3. Example of the annotation process using the bounding box tool

detect coral regions, with fine-tuning of confidence thresholds and IoU for optimized
performance.
The dataset was exported in the YOLOv5 PyTorch format, optimized for object detection tasks. Model training was conducted on Google Colab, utilizing Python and a tailored
code snippet from Roboflow to ensure a smooth transition from dataset preparation to
model training. VGG16 for Classification: The pre-trained VGG16 model was fine-tuned
on the annotated dataset to classify coral health states as “healthy” or “bleached.”
3.4 Evaluation Metrics
Models were evaluated using precision, recall, F1-score, and mean average precision
(mAP), ensuring robustness across classification and detection tasks.

deep learning architecture used for coral classification. VGG16 achieved perfect training
accuracy but exhibited slight overfitting, with lower validation and testing accuracies of

models were slightly less robust due to overfitting (Tables 1, 2 and Figs. 7, 8, 9).

Similarly, VGG16 demonstrated a validation accuracy of 87.72% but exhibited overfitting, as evidenced by a perfect training accuracy of 100%. This disparity indicates that
while VGG16 excels during training, its generalization to unseen data is limited. Augmentation techniques were employed to enhance generalization, but the slight underperformance suggests the need for additional regularization strategies, such as batch

120

D. Weraikat et al.

normalization or dropout. Fine-tuning these models could help address overfitting and
improve their reliability in real-world applications.
In summary, both YOLO and VGG16 offer complementary strengths: YOLO excels
in real-time detection, while VGG16 provides accurate classification. However, challenges such as overfitting in VGG16 and YOLO’s limitations in detecting nuanced coral
conditions underscore the need for refinement. These insights guide future research
toward enhancing the robustness and scalability of these models, ensuring their effective
deployment in coral reef monitoring systems.

5 Conclusion
Coral reefs, as one of the planet’s most critical ecosystems, face existential threats from
climate change and human-induced stressors. This study demonstrates the transformative
potential of deep learning technologies in monitoring and mitigating these threats. By
leveraging advanced architectures such as VGG16 for classification and YOLO for realtime object detection, this research highlights the strengths of AI in automating coral
health assessments. These models provide scalable, accurate, and efficient alternatives
to traditional monitoring methods, overcoming challenges such as high costs and limited
resolution associated with manual surveys and remote sensing.
A significant contribution of this study is the development of a novel coral bleaching
database. Compiled from diverse video and image sources, the dataset enriches model
training by incorporating metadata such as geolocation and environmental parameters.
This approach addresses critical gaps in existing databases, enabling models to generalize
across diverse reef conditions and improving their real-world applicability.
The results showcase the effectiveness of combining robust datasets with cuttingedge AI tools, achieving high accuracies in detecting and classifying coral health states.
However, challenges such as overfitting in classification models and dataset biases
underline the need for continued refinement. Future research should focus on expanding the database to include global contributions, integrating advanced architectures like
EfficientNet, and exploring real-time deployment of these models in conservation efforts.
This study underscores the essential role of interdisciplinary innovation in tackling
environmental crises. By advancing the integration of AI and marine conservation, this
research lays a foundation for scalable, cost-effective monitoring solutions that can
inform targeted conservation actions and policy interventions, ensuring the preservation
of coral reefs for future generations.
Future work will expand the dataset, include global contributions, and explore
advanced architectures such as ResNet50 and EfficientNet. Real-time deployment of
these models in marine conservation initiatives could revolutionize coral reef monitoring,
aiding in policy-making and targeted conservation actions.

Optimization and Search Algorithms:
Metaheuristics and Hybrid Approaches

Chaotic Binarization of Tasmanian Devil
Optimization for Solving the Knapsack
Problem
Felipe Cisternas-Caneo1 , José Barrera-Garcia1(B) , Broderick Crawford1 ,
Ricardo Soto1 , Giovanni Giachetti2 , and Eric Monfroy3
1

Pontiﬁcia Universidad Católica de Valparaı́so, Valparaı́so, Chile
felipe.cisternas.c@mail.pucv.cl,
{jose.barrera,broderick.crawford,ricardo.soto}@pucv.cl
2
Universidad Andres Bello, Santiago, Chile
giovanni.giachetti@unab.cl
3
Université d’ Angers, Leria, France
eric.monfroy@univ-angers.fr

Abstract. The Knapsack Problem (KP) is a classic combinatorial optimization challenge with applications in several ﬁelds including logistics
and ﬁnance. Traditional solving approaches often struggle to solve largescale and complex instances, necessitating the adoption of advanced
metaheuristic techniques. This study presents a novel approach that integrates chaotic maps into the Tasmanian Devil Optimization (TDO) algorithm to address the KP more eﬀectively. Speciﬁcally, a chaotic binarization mechanism is proposed to improve the balance between exploration
and exploitation. Comparative analyses with state-of-the-art metaheuristics were performed, using KP benchmark datasets. The results demonstrate that the chaotic sine-based TDO outperforms traditional methods
in terms of solution quality, convergence stability, and computational eﬃciency. These ﬁndings highlight the potential of chaotic hybridization as
a promising tool for solving binary combinatorial optimization problems.
Keywords: Chaotic Maps · Tasmanian Devil Optimization ·
Binarization Schemes · Metaheuristics · Knapsack Problem

1

Introduction

The knapsack problem (KP) is a classical combinatorial optimization problem
with applications in various ﬁelds such as logistics, ﬁnance, and operations
research. Traditional algorithms often struggle with complex and large-scale
instances, necessitating the use of metaheuristic techniques.
Hybridization of metaheuristics with chaotic maps has emerged as a prominent approach in optimization research. This hybridization is mainly used to
improve the balance between exploration and exploitation. The low computational cost and adaptability of these maps make them eﬀective for dynamically
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 125–137, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_9

126

F. Cisternas-Caneo et al.

adapting parameters within metaheuristic frameworks. These advantages have
established chaotic hybridization as a breakthrough in solving complex optimization problems.
Tasmanian Devil Optimization (TDO) is a recently introduced metaheuristic
that simulates the hunting and territorial behaviors of Tasmanian devils. While
TDO has demonstrated eﬃcacy in continuous optimization problems, its application to discrete problems remains limited. This paper proposes the integration of
chaotic maps to improve TDO’s binarization process, enhancing its performance
in solving the knapsack problem. The key contributions of this work include:
– Development of a binarization mechanism for TDO using chaotic maps.
– Comparative analysis with state-of-the-art metaheuristics on KP benchmarks.
– Evaluation of the impact of chaotic dynamics on convergence and solution
quality.
The structure of this article is as follows: Sect. 2 mentions in detail the
hybridization between chaotic maps and swarm intelligence, Sect. 3 mentions
the methodology applied in the present work, Sect. 4 mentions the conﬁguration
of experiments, Sect. 5 mentions the experimental analysis to close Sect. 6 with
the conclusions of the work.

2

Chaotic Maps Into Swarm Intelligence Algorithms

Chaos is a deterministic but random method, and the chaotic system can be considered the source of randomness [7, 10]. It is deterministic since it has chaotic
variables that deﬁne the behavior. Therefore, the same chaotic sequence will
always be obtained using the same variables. The main interest shown in the
community for these maps is due to their low computational cost and few parameters [11].
Reviewing the literature, there are works such as [6] where chaotic maps
are incorporated into the Zebra Optimization Algorithm to increase exploratory
capabilities and avoid falling into local optima. In the work [8], the authors
include the cubic chaotic map in Particle Swarm Optimization in the initialization process to improve the diversity of a population. In the work [4], the
authors incorporate chaotic maps into the Salp Swarm Algorithm to improve
exploratory capabilities and perform a more eﬃcient global search. In the work
[9], the authors incorporate chaotic maps into the Sandbox Swarm Optimization
Algorithm to improve the exploration-exploitation capabilities. In the work [1],
the authors integrate chaotic maps in Dwarf Mongoose Optimization to accelerate convergence by modifying Dwarf Mongoose motion components during the
optimization process. In work [16], the authors use a Bernoulli chaotic map in
an Artiﬁcial Bee Colony Algorithm to improve diversity and employability. In
the work [14], the authors use chaotic maps in the Bird Swarm Algorithm to
avoid premature convergence.

Chaotic Binarization of TDO for the Knapsack Problem

127

All these recent works conﬁrm the great interest in chaotic maps in swarm
intelligence, not only because of the diversity of swarm intelligence algorithms
but also because of the diverse applications. This evidence and the No Free
Lunch Theorem [15] motivate us to incorporate chaotic maps in Tasmanian
Devil Optimization.

3

Proposed Methodology

3.1

Canonical Tasmanian Devil Optimization

The Tasmanian Devil Optimization (TDO) is inspired by the feeding behavior
of Tasmanian devils in the wild [5]. These carnivorous marsupials adopt two
main strategies to ﬁnd food: scavenging for carrion left by other predators and
hunting live prey. This dual behavior provides an eﬀective model for balancing
exploration and exploitation.
In the exploration phase, carrion searching is mimicked, where each individual (demon) in the population randomly selects another as a potential carrion.
Movement is modeled based on the relative ﬁtness of the carrion using the equation:
xnew
i,j =

xi,j + r · (ci,j − I · xi,j ),
xi,j + r · (xi,j − ci,j ),

FC < Fi
otherwise

(1)

where r is a random factor in [0, 1], I is an intensity factor, and FC and Fi
represent the ﬁtness of the carrion and the devil, respectively. This phase ensures
a global search across the solution space by encouraging diverse movements.
The exploitation phase emulates the Tasmanian devil’s prey hunting behavior, which consists of attacking and chasing prey. First, the devil selects a random
prey and adjusts its position:
xnew
i,j =

xi,j + r · (pi,j − I · xi,j ),
xi,j + r · (xi,j − pi,j ),

FP < Fi
otherwise

(2)

where FP is the ﬁtness of prey. Then, the devil reﬁnes its position by chasing
the prey in a shrinking neighborhood deﬁned by the radius:

accepted only when the move leads to a better solution. This move acceptance

3.2

Chaotic Binarization Process

The binarization process enables continuous metaheuristics to solve binary combinatorial optimization problems [2]. In the literature, the two-step technique
is the most widely used binarization process [2] where, as its name indicates,
the process lies in two stages: (1) a transfer function maps continuous values to
probabilities within [0, 1], and (2) a binarization rule converts these probabilities
into binary values. This approach bridges the gap between continuous solution
spaces and binary problem requirements.
On the other hand, the chaotic binarization process extends the traditional
method by integrating chaotic maps into the binarization rules [3]. This approach replaces the uniform random numbers typically used in the binarization
rule. This adaptation enhances the balance between exploration and exploitation by introducing chaotic sequences into the two-step technique, improving
optimization performance in solving binary combinatorial problems.

Chaotic Binarization of TDO for the Knapsack Problem

129

One of the virtues of this binarization approach is its rapid integration. For
the case of TDO, this process is integrated before the comparison of Eq. 5, i.e.,
the binarization process is integrated before calculating the ﬁtness.

4

Experimental Setup

4.1

Knapsack Problem Formulation

The Knapsack Problem (KP) is another NP-hard combinatorial optimization
problem. Mathematically, the Knapsack Problem is modeled as follows: Given
N objects, where the j-th object has its own weight wj and proﬁt pj , and a
knapsack that holds a limited weight capacity C, the problem consists of ﬁnding
the objects that maximize the proﬁt without exceeding the knapsack’s capacity.
The objective function is as follows:
N

max f (x) =

N

pj xj

wj xj ≤ C,

subject to

j=1

where xj ∈ {0, 1} ∀j ∈ J

j=1

(6)
where xj represents the binary decision variables (i.e., whether an element is
considered in the knapsack (value 1 in the decision variable) or not considered
in the knapsack (value 0 in the decision variable)).
4.2

Datasets and Benchmarks

The datasets for benchmarking the Knapsack Problem are categorized into three
types: uncorrelated, weakly correlated, and strongly correlated instances [12, 13].
– Uncorrelated instances include two datasets: PI 1 500 1000 1, with an optimum value of 28,857, and PI 1 1000 1000 1, with an optimum value of 54,503.
– Weakly correlated instances consist of PI 2 500 1000 1, which has an optimum value of 4,566, and PI 2 1000 1000 1, with an optimum value of 9,052.
– Strongly correlated instances are represented by PI 3 500 1000 1, with an
optimum value of 7,117, and PI 3 1000 1000 1, with an optimum value of
14,390.

4.3

Parameter Settings

The experiments were conducted using two benchmark metaheuristic algorithms
to compare our proposal. These metaheuristics are Particle Swarm Optimization
(PSO) and Grey Wolf Optimizer (GWO. To ensure the reliability of the results
and account for the stochastic nature of the algorithms, each experimental conﬁguration was executed 31 independent times. This approach allowed the computation of robust statistical metrics, such as the mean, standard deviation, and
relative percentage deviation (RPD).

130

F. Cisternas-Caneo et al.

The general parameter settings included a population size of 20 and an
iteration limit of 500, providing a balance between computational eﬃciency
and solution quality. The S4 transfer function was selected for the binarization process due to its smooth transition properties, which enhance the exploration and exploitation capabilities of the algorithms. The binarization process
was evaluated using four conﬁgurations: Standard (STD), Standard with SINE
(STD SINE), Elitist (ELIT), and Elitist with SINE (ELIT SINE). The Standard rule (STD) served as a baseline for fair comparisons with traditional methods. The Elitist rule (ELIT) was employed to prioritize high-quality solutions,
improving convergence speed and robustness. In both cases, the SINE chaotic
map was integrated to enhance the binarization process further. The SINE
enhancement introduced non-linear dynamics that improved exploration capabilities, helping the algorithms escape local optima and achieve better overall
performance. This setup allowed a comprehensive evaluation of how the chaotic
map impacts diﬀerent binarization strategies across all tested instances.

5

Results and Discussion

The performance of the proposed algorithm is evaluated based on solution quality, convergence speed, and computational time.
5.1

Experimental Analysis

Tables 1, 2 and 3 presents a comparison of the ﬁtness values obtained by TDO,
PSO, and GWO for the diﬀerent knapsack problem instances.
Fitness Analysis for Uncorrelated Instances. Table 1 presents the ﬁtness
values obtained for the instances KP1 1000 1000 and KP1 500 1000, comparing
the performance of the proposed TDO variants with standard PSO and GWO
methods. The reported metrics include the best ﬁtness, average ﬁtness, standard
deviation (Dev-std), and the Relative Percentage Deviation (RPD) from the
optimal solution.
For the instance KP1 1000 1000, the TDA-STD SINE variant achieved the
highest best ﬁtness value of 51914.00, demonstrating its capability to explore the
solution space eﬀectively. This result was closely followed by PSO-STD SINE
(53080.00) and GWO-STD SINE (52660.00), indicating that the chaotic sinebased binarization technique improves performance across diﬀerent metaheuristics. Furthermore, TDA-STD SINE exhibited remarkable consistency, as evidenced by its identical best and average ﬁtness values (51914.00) and zero standard deviation, which underscores its robustness and reliability. In contrast,
GWO-ELIT SINE showed the highest variability, with a standard deviation of
1235.14, suggesting instability in its performance. The Relative Percentage Deviation (RPD) results further conﬁrmed the superiority of TDA-STD SINE, which
achieved the lowest RPD value of 4.75%, highlighting its ability to produce solutions that are closer to the optimal value compared to other methods.

For the knapPI 1 1000 1000 1 instance, the TDA-ELIT conﬁguration
demonstrated the fastest average execution time (62.31 s), followed closely by
TDA-ELIT SINE (61.60 s). Both conﬁgurations signiﬁcantly outperformed their
standard and sine-enhanced counterparts, with GWO-STD being the slowest at
88.57 s. While the chaotic sine-based variants generally improved performance
metrics in terms of ﬁtness, they introduced a slight computational overhead in
comparison to the base TDO conﬁgurations, as seen in TDA-STD SINE (77.66 s)
versus TDA-STD (75.45 s). Similarly, GWO-STD SINE (85.11 s) exhibited a
marginally lower execution time than GWO-STD, though it remained computationally intensive.
For the smaller instance knapPI 1 500 1000 1, similar trends were observed.
TDA-ELIT SINE exhibited the fastest average execution time (30.13 s), while
GWO-STD recorded the slowest time (45.90 s). The chaotic sine-based variants
maintained comparable computational costs to their respective standard conﬁgurations, with slight variations in execution times. For instance, PSO-STD SINE
(43.22 s) was marginally slower than PSO-STD (42.92 s), but both conﬁgurations
showed more signiﬁcant computational requirements than the TDO variants.
For the weakly correlated instances, such as knapPI 2 1000 1000 1, TDAELIT and TDA-ELIT SINE continued to demonstrate superior computational
eﬃciency, with average execution times of 58.79 and 58.84 s, respectively. By
contrast, GWO-STD and GWO-STD SINE required signiﬁcantly longer execution times, averaging 82.35 and 85.31 s, respectively. These results highlight
the computational burden of GWO variants, particularly when enhanced with
chaotic sine-based binarization. For the minor instance knapPI 2 500 1000 1, the
TDO conﬁgurations were again the most eﬃcient, with TDA-ELIT achieving the
fastest 25.26 s.
For the strongly correlated instances, such as knapPI 3 1000 1000 1, TDAELIT SINE demonstrated competitive performance, with an average execution
time of 49.10 s, closely followed by TDA-ELIT (48.69 s). These results conﬁrm
the computational eﬃciency of the elitist TDO conﬁgurations for this instance
type. GWO-STD, however, required the most time, averaging 69.95 s, with its
chaotic sine-based variant, GWO-STD SINE, slightly slower at 70.61 s. For the
smaller instance knapPI 3 500 1000 1, the TDA-ELIT SINE conﬁguration was

135

once again among the fastest, averaging 25.68 s, while the GWO conﬁgurations
exhibited higher execution times, with GWO-STD averaging 35.54 s.

6

Conclusion

This paper presented a novel chaotic sine-based binarization approach integrated
into the Tasmanian Devil Optimization (TDO) metaheuristic and evaluated its
performance on various instances of the knapsack problem. The experimental
results demonstrated that chaotic sine-based TDO conﬁgurations signiﬁcantly
enhance optimization outcomes by improving solution quality, stability, and convergence, while maintaining competitive computational eﬃciency.
Regarding ﬁtness, the SINE-enhanced conﬁgurations consistently outperformed their standard counterparts. TDA-STD SINE emerged as the topperforming conﬁguration across all instance types, achieving high ﬁtness values with minimal deviations and low relative percentage deviation (RPD).
The enhanced exploration and exploitation capabilities introduced by the
chaotic sine-based binarization were instrumental in delivering these superior
results. Conversely, GWO conﬁgurations, particularly GWO-ELIT, often exhibited slower convergence and higher variability, suggesting limitations in handling
the search space eﬀectively.
Regarding execution times, TDA-ELIT and TDA-ELIT SINE emerged as the
most computationally eﬃcient conﬁgurations, delivering faster execution times
while maintaining competitive ﬁtness values. Although chaotic sine-based binarization introduced minor computational overhead, the trade-oﬀ was justiﬁed
by the signiﬁcant improvements in optimization outcomes. GWO conﬁgurations
consistently required longer execution times, reﬂecting their computational complexity.
The proposed TDO conﬁgurations’ scalability was evident across uncorrelated, weakly correlated, and strongly correlated instances, further emphasizing
this methodology’s versatility for combinatorial optimization problems. These
results suggest that the chaotic sine-based TDO method is well-suited for a wide
range of applications beyond the knapsack problem.
Despite its strengths, chaotic sine-based binarization introduces a computational cost, particularly in GWO conﬁgurations. Future work could explore
alternative chaotic maps or hybrid approaches to further reduce execution times
while preserving optimization performance. Additionally, integrating adaptive
mechanisms to dynamically adjust chaotic parameters during the search process
could improve both performance and adaptability.
In conclusion, the chaotic sine-based TDO metaheuristic demonstrated its
potential as a powerful and eﬃcient optimization technique. Its robust performance across diverse problem instances and metrics highlights its promise for
solving complex optimization problems in both theoretical and applied domains.
Extending its application to real-world domains, such as scheduling, resource
allocation, and feature selection, will provide a broader validation of the proposed methodology, opening new avenues for impactful research.

Evol. Comput. 1(1), 67–82 (1997). https://doi.org/10.1109/4235.585893

13(1), 20496 (2023)

Mother Tree Optimization Algorithm
for Feature Selection
Wael Korani1 and Malek Mouhoub2(B)
1

University of North Texas, Denton, TX, USA
Wael.Korani@unt.edu
2
University of Regina, Regina, SK, Canada
Malek.Mouhoub@uregina.ca

Abstract. Feature selection allows reducing the dimensionality of data
sets by selecting the most relevant features. This will lead to more interpretable models, in addition to reducing computational costs. In this
context, we introduce a new nature-inspired feature selection technique
based on a recently developed discrete optimization algorithm called the
Binary Mother Tree Optimization (BMTO) algorithm. We call BMTO
for Feature Selection (BMTO-FS) the new technique we propose. BMTOFS utilizes a binary converter module that converts real numbers into
binary numbers using a sigmoid function and a threshold value. BMO-FS
operates within our new feature selection framework that can be generalized to any other metaheuristic. To assess the performance of BMTOFS, we conducted extensive comparative experiments on several public
benchmark datasets corresponding to low, medium, and high dimensions. In these experiments, genetic algorithms (GA), particle swarm
optimization (PSO), and the whale optimization algorithm (WOA) are
considered. The results are promising, as they show that BMTO-FS outperforms GAs, PSO, and WOA in terms of accuracy rates, number of
reduced features, and computational cost.

1

Introduction

Analyzing and organizing data in high-dimensional spaces often comes with an
exponential growth of the volume of the feature space, which leads to several
challenges that are referred to as the curse of dimensionality [2,12,30]. These
challenges include overﬁtting, increase in model complexity, sparcity, and interpretability of data, in addition to the related learning computational time and
space costs [5]. The curse of dimensionality is addressed through two types of
dimensionality reduction technique: feature extraction and feature selection. In
feature extraction, the original features are combined and transformed into a
smaller number of features to discover more meaningful information. Feature
extraction techniques include Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA), Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF), Autoencoders, and Multidimensional Scaling (MDS) [26]. On the other hand, feature
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 138–149, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_10

MTO for Feature Selection

139

selection reduces the number of features by identifying and selecting the most
signiﬁcant [10].
Feature selection techniques are classiﬁed into ﬁlter-based, wrapper-based,
and embedded [10]. Filter-based techniques are based on statistical measures
and depend on diﬀerent characteristics of the training dataset such as relevancy,
dependency, consistency, and distance to evaluate the goodness of the selected
features [6]. Wrapper techniques use a machine learning model such as .K-Nearest
Neighbor (.KNN) or K-means to evaluate the goodness of the selected group
of features [6,9], which makes it computationally expensive. The latter usually
achieves higher accuracy than the former, as it directly evaluates the performance
of the selected characteristics using a classiﬁcation technique [11]. Finally, the
embedded methods such as the Least Absolute Shrinkage and Selection Operator
regression and those within decision trees apply feature selection during the
training process.
The current study focuses on the introduction of a new wrapper-based feature
selection method based on a recently developed nature-inspired technique called
the Binary Mother Tree Optimization (BMTO) algorithm [13].
Traditional feature selection approaches are based on the statistical significance concept to select the most signiﬁcant features. The main drawbacks of
traditional approaches are that they are unable to select the best features when
there are many statistically signiﬁcant features. However, the goal is to ﬁnd the
smallest subset of features that achieves the best accuracy. This is computationally challenging given that the total number of subsets is as follows:
number of subsets = 2Nf − 1,

.

(1)

where .Nf is the total number of features. Obviously, the search space is
increasing exponentially along with the number of features in the data set.
Finding the best group of features that achieve the best accuracy is a NP-hard
problem [11]. Thus, approximate methods, including metaheuristics, have been
introduced to ﬁnd the relatively best group of characteristics in a reasonable
time frame [3, 8,9,15, 17,18, 21,23, 31].
In [18], the authors proposed an improved feature selection method (IFS)
based on a modiﬁed multi-swarm particle swarm optimization (MSPSO) and a
support vector machine (SVM). IFS has been experimentally compared to standard PSO and Genetic Algorithms (GA) on ten low to medium size benchmark
datasets. The results show that IFS outperforms the two other methods. In [28],
the authors proposed a variant of the PSO-based feature selection approach. The
method was evaluated on ﬁve high-dimensional datasets, and the experimental
results show that the proposed methods outperformed the other three variants
of the PSO.
In [3], the authors proposed a binary GA for feature selection that was superior to other feature selection techniques. In [20], the authors introduced a hybrid
local search and the GA method to ﬁne-tune the search for the best subset of features. In [24], the Whale Optimization Algorithm (WOA) has been implemented
to select the best group of features. The proposed method has been compared

140

W. Korani and M. Mouhoub

to PSO and GA using a number of public datasets. The results show that WOA
outperforms PSO and GA. In [19], the authors introduced a hybrid algorithm
based on WOA and Simulated Annealing (SA). The proposed model was evaluated on low to medium size datasets and the results show the eﬀectiveness of
the proposed method.
In [9], a discrete version of the biogeography-based optimization (BBO) evolutionary technique is introduced to select the best subset of characteristics that
improve the performance of clustering algorithms, such as K-means, in terms of
metrics such as the Davies-Bouldin Index and Silhouette. To evaluate the performance of our proposed method, we conducted a set of comparative experiments.
The results demonstrate the superiority of our method over PSO.
The main motive in this study is to introduce a feature selection method
that achieves high accuracy in a reasonable time frame for any size of data set
and avoids the drawbacks of the exact and traditional methods. In addition, the
current study introduces a feature selection framework to evaluate any further
approximate method.

2

Mother Tree Optimization (MTO)

In [16], we proposed the MTO metaheuristic inspired by the symbiotic relationship between Douglas ﬁr trees and mycorrhizal fungi networks. MTO is based
on a ﬁxed-oﬀspring topology in which potential solutions (represented as agents)
update their positions in the search space according to the group to which they
belong. More precisely, the topology divides the agents into three main groups
as follows [14, 16].
2.1

Top Mother Tree (TMT)

The TMT searches in two stages: root and mycorrhizal stage. At the root level,
it moves with a step .δ in any random direction. The position of TMT is updated
as follows:
P1 (xk+1 ) = P1 (xk ) + δR(d), where R(d) = √

.

(2)

where .R is a random vector, .d is the size of the problem, and .δ is small
deviation. In the mycorrhizal stage, the position is updated as follows:
P1 (xk+1 ) = P1 (xk ) + ΔR(d).

.

(3)

where .Δ is a smaller deviation.
2.2

Partially Connected Trees (PCTs)

PCTs has two parts First-PCTs and Last-PCTs. First-PCTs has some
agents that are in the range [2: . N2T − 1]. The position of any member of this

Proposed Feature Selection Framework

Many feature selection techniques were proposed in the last couple of decades
that are based on SI and EAs algorithms to select the best group of features
in a reasonable time frame. In some cases, a proposed solution for a certain
problem with the same parameters values might produce diﬀerent solutions.
The main reason for this aforementioned behavior is how the proposed solution
is implemented. In this paper, we introduce a feature selection technique based
on a binary optimization algorithm along with a framework that can be used to
implement any meta-heuristic algorithm and achieves consistent results.
The proposed framework includes three independent modules: the optimizer,
binary converter, and K NN modules as shown in Fig. 1-a. The optimizer module
has a continuous optimization algorithm that might be SI or an EA method.
Continuous optimization algorithms deal with real numbers. In the framework,
the output of the optimizer is fed to a binary module to convert the real numbers
to a binary string that has the same length as the number of features. The
output of the binary converter module is represented by 0 and 1, where 1 means
a selected feature and zero means a non-selected feature. The accuracy of the
output features is computed using K NN classiﬁer, and the classiﬁcation error
will be assigned as the ﬁtness value of the associated agent.
The binary converter utilizes a threshold to limit the value of a real number
to a certain value called .thresh. The Sigmoid function
f (x) =

.

1
,
1 + exp(−x)

(7)

142

W. Korani and M. Mouhoub

is used then to convert all real numbers associated with all features to diﬀerent
real numbers between [0, 1]. Finally, to decide whether the feature will be selected
or not, a random number is generated between [0, 1]. Then, if the output of
the sigmoid function is greater than the generated number, then the feature
will be selected (the value is one). On the other hand, if the output of the
sigmoid function is less than the generated random number, the feature will not
be selected (the value is zero) as shown in Fig. 1-b. The vector of the selected
features is passed to the K NN classiﬁer to calculate the classiﬁcation error, and
then the classiﬁcation errors will return back to the optimizer as a ﬁtness value
for the associated agents.
The pseudocode of our proposed BMTO-FS is listed in Algorithm 1. The
core of our method is the BMTO that is a binary variant of MTO [13].

Algorithm 1. .The BMTO-FS algorithm
1: Inputs:

4: .d: The size of the dataset
5: .N : The number of iterations
6: .KN N : K Nearest Neighbor classifier with cross validation
7: .Binary: A function to convert real number to binary number
8: Initialize:
.Distribute t agents uniformly over the search space (P1 , . . . , Pt )

.[classification error]= KN N (Binary(P1 . . . Pt ))

Experimentation

4.1

Parameter Settings

Extensive experiments are conducted to evaluate the performance of our proposed BMTO-FS method and compare the results with the binary version of

Fig. 1. Binary converter module and feature selection framework.

three other nature-inspired techniques: Genetic algorithm (GA), Particle Swarm
Optimization (PSO), and Whale Optimization Algorithm (WOA). These binary
variants are respectively called Binary PSO-FS (BPSO-FS), Binary WOA-FS
(BWOA-FS), and Binary GA-FS (BGA-FS). The K NN classiﬁer is used, and
the number of neighbors (.k) is set to ﬁve. In addition, a 10-fold cross validation
(CV) is implemented to avoid the bias in the results [7]. In the 10-fold CV, the
datasets are split into ten equal folds, where the model is developed using nine
folds for training, and the last fold for testing. These steps are repeated ten times
with shuﬄing the testing fold every time. All techniques are evaluated using nine
public benchmark datasets that are collected from the UCI repository, the scikitfeature project, and the gems-system.org project. The datasets are categorized
based on the number of features (low, medium, and high), ranging from 18 to
10,367 as shown in Table 1. The high dimension datasets are gene expression,
and are known with their limited number of samples. The main reason of choosing these datasets is that they were used before to evaluate the performance of
diﬀerent feature selection techniques, including a variant of PSO [29], diﬀerent
variants of Harris Hawks Optimization [25], and a variant of GAs [27].
BMTO-FS, BPSO-FS, BWOA-FS, and BGA-FS are implemented using the
binary optimizer. The binary optimizer consists of the binary converter and
the optimizer. All the four algorithms are coded in MATLAB R2022a and are
performed on a MacBook Pro with Apple M1 Max and 32 GB memory. The
number of iterations is set to 50, the population size (number of candidate solutions, agents or particles) is set to 20, and the number of Fitness Evaluations
(FEs) is set to 1000. All algorithms are run 30 times for each dataset with different seed values to avoid bias. All the parameters, speciﬁc to each algorithm
and listed in Table 2, are tuned to their best.
