Results and Discussion

The four methods are evaluated in terms of the accuracy of the best run, the
average accuracy, the average selected features, the ﬁtness value, and the average
running time using the aforementioned datasets. The results are listed in Table 3.
The results show that BMTO-FS outperforms the other three algorithms in
eight out of nine datasets, in terms of the best accuracy and average accuracy,
followed by BPSO-FS in one dataset in terms of the best accuracy and average
accuracy. In addition, BMTO-FS has the best balance between exploration and
exploitation search strategies to reach better solutions in eight cases. In addition,
using TMT to update some agents in the population (as described in Sect. 2)
increases the exploration capability of the algorithm.
In terms of the number of selected features, BGA-FS outperforms in four out
of nine, followed by BPSO-FS in three datasets, BWOA-FS in one dataset, and
BMTO-FS in one dataset. Although the number of selected features is low in
some cases for some algorithms, the average accuracy is also decreased. Thus, the
ﬁtness value is utilized as an indicator to combine the average accuracy and the
number of selected features as shown in Eq. 8. The results show that BMTO-FS
outperforms the other three algorithms in terms of the ﬁtness value in seven of
nine datasets, followed by BPSO-FS in one dataset and BGA-FS in one dataset.
Overall, the results of BMTO-FS are very promising and show its capability
to handle diﬀerent data sets with diﬀerent sizes. BMTO-FS achieves the best
accuracy in most cases, while the number of features is reduced to almost half
in the case of high-dimensional datasets. In fact, the selected number of features
is decreased by 47. 0% in the TOX 171 dataset, by 45. 45% in the Brain Tumor
1 dataset, by 49. 55% in the ALLAMAL dataset and by 51. 2% in the Brain
Tumor 2 dataset. The accuracy improvement during the course of the best run
is recorded as shown in Fig. 2 and Fig. 3. The proposed BMTO-FS algorithm is
faster than the other three algorithms, where it achieves fewer errors in fewer
iterations. In Fig. 2-a to Fig. 2-d, BMTO-FS achieves higher accuracy than the
other three algorithms in almost 25–30 iterations. In addition, Fig. 3-a, Fig. 3-b,
and Fig. 3-d show that BMTO-FS achieves higher accuracy in a short time. In
conclusion, the results show that BMTO-FS has the ability to achieve higher
accuracy in fewer iterations (with a short period of time) even if the number of
features increases to 10,367.

5

Conclusion and Future Work

MTO is a continuous optimization algorithm that mimics the symbiotic relationship between Douglas ﬁr trees and the network of mycorrhizal fungi. In
this paper, a novel variant of MTO called BMTO-FS and a framework are proposed. The proposed feature selection framework makes the implementation of
any binary optimization method much easier. BMTO-FS is proposed to select
the best group of features in a data set to enhance accuracy and decrease computation requirements. In order to evaluate the eﬀectiveness and eﬃciency of

148

W. Korani and M. Mouhoub

BMTO-FS to tackle diﬀerent sizes of problems. Extensive experiments are conducted using nine public benchmark datasets of diﬀerent sizes. The results show
that BMTO-FS has the ability to achieve higher accuracy in a few iterations.
In addition, we compare our results to three other feature selection techniques,
BPSO-FS, BWOA-FS, and BGA-FS. The results show that BMTO outperforms
other methods in terms of best accuracy, average accuracy, and ﬁtness values. In
addition, the results indicate that BMTO is well suited for a high-dimensional
data set and a large data set. BMTO-FS is currently being evaluated in other
gene expression datasets. We are also considering other metaheuristics [1, 4,22]
as well as a new hybrid feature selection technique using BMTO-FS and the
coloration matrix.

An Improved NSGA-III Algorithm
for Solving High-Dimensional
Many-Objective Flexible Job Shop
Scheduling Problem
Jianshang Wang, Ying Tan, and Chaoli Sun(B)
Taiyuan University of Science and Technology, Taiyuan 030024, China
s202220110880@stu.tyust.edu.cn, {tanying,chaoli.sun}@tyust.edu.cn

Abstract. The ﬂexible job shop scheduling problem is a complex NPhard challenge critical to modern manufacturing systems, but existing
research predominantly focuses on low-dimensional optimization, practical scenarios often require balancing high-dimensional conﬂicting objectives such as energy consumption, machine load, tardiness, and completion time. Current -objective algorithms struggle to maintain convergence and diversity in such high-dimensional spaces, leading to suboptimal Pareto fronts. To address this problem, we propose an improved
NSGA-III (WDHS-NSGA-III) integrating a reference vector-guided subpopulation partitioning mechanism and a density-aware hybrid search
strategy. The algorithm divides populations into subspaces using reference vectors to enable adaptive cross-subpopulation exploration, then
applies localized neighborhood searches with ﬁve operators to prioritize
under-explored regions. Experiments on benchmark instances demonstrate that WDHS-NSGA-III signiﬁcantly outperforms commonly used
methods, achieving balanced optimization across most objectives. The
results validate its robustness in resolving high-dimensional tradeoﬀs,
oﬀering a valid solution for smart manufacturing scheduling.
Keywords: Many-objective optimization · Flexible job shop
scheduling · Reference point · Subpopulation division · neighborhood
search · Density perception

1

Introduction

Flexible job shop scheduling problem (FJSP) is a critical aspect of modern manufacturing systems, particularly in the context of Industry 5.0, where the integration of advanced technologies and adaptive processes is essential for optimizing
production eﬃciency [1]. The concept revolves around the ability to allocate
resources and schedule tasks in response to varying demands and operational
conditions.
Traditional ﬂexible job shop scheduling has predominantly focused on
temporal-centric objectives such as makespan or total ﬂow time. However, such
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 150–163, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_11

An Improved NSGA-III Algorithm for Solving FJSP

151

single-objective optimization frameworks fail to address the growing demands
for sustainable manufacturing [2], particularly under the dual pressures of carbon neutrality policies and energy-intensive production processes. Furthermore,
as optimization models evolve to incorporate multi-objective criteria spanning
energy eﬃciency, carbon emissions, and resource utilization–the search space
dimensionality escalates exponentially. This poses signiﬁcant challenges to classical metaheuristic algorithms, which exhibit degraded convergence properties
and premature stagnation in high-dimensional Pareto frontiers. Therefore, combining energy consumption considerations with high-dimensional optimization
has become a crucial research frontier.
FJSP has attracted extensive research attention due to its multi-objective
optimization challenges. Sang et al. [3] proposed an improved NSGA-III algorithm for many-objective FJSP, enhancing scheduling eﬃciency and solution
quality. Gong et al. [4] developed a non-dominated ensemble ﬁtness ranking
algorithm incorporating worker ﬂexibility and green factors to optimize energy
consumption and scheduling performance. Duan and Wang [5] addressed energyeﬃcient scheduling in FJSP with machine breakdowns by optimizing idle time
arrangements and speed-level adjustments. Wen et al. [6] designed a two-stage
NSGA-II-based method for green integrated process planning and scheduling in
battery packaging workshops, reducing energy consumption and improving eﬃciency. Sun et al. [7] introduced a hybrid genetic algorithm with variable neighborhood search to minimize makespan and balance machine loads. Wang et al.
[8] improved the MOEA/D algorithm for low-carbon FJSP, emphasizing solution
convergence and diversity. Li et al. [9] enhanced NSGA-II for job shop scheduling
with overtime considerations to reduce delays and overtime costs. Pan et al. [10]
proposed a learning-based multi-population evolutionary optimization, incorporating a reinforcement learning-based mating selection and designing a local
search based on problem properties. Chen et al. [11] proposed a decompositionbased multi-objective evolutionary algorithm to balance eﬃciency and eﬀectiveness. Zhuang et al. [12] designed a genetic algorithm with adaptive mutation to
improve robustness and adaptability.
The above algorithms frequently encounter signiﬁcant limitations. A primary issue is their ineﬃcient exploration of the solution space, particularly in
high-dimensional objective spaces. Traditional multi-objective optimization algorithms often struggle to maintain a balance between convergence and diversity.
These limitations highlight the need for more advanced and adaptive algorithms
to address the challenges of many objective-ﬂexible job shop scheduling problem
(MO-FJSP).
To overcome the above problems, we propose wide-area crossover and densityguided hybrid search-based NSGA-III (WDHS-NSGA-III) speciﬁcally designed
for four-objective ﬂexible job shop scheduling problems. The main work of this
paper includes:
– Propose a strategy of wide-area crossover that enhances diversity by subpopulation partitioning based on reference points. After partitioning the popu-

152

J. Wang et al.

lation into subpopulations, parents are selected for crossover using a roulette
wheel selection method in each subpopulation.
– Deploy ﬁve neighborhood search strategies speciﬁcally on non-dominated
solutions within localized reinforcement based on density perception, enhancing solution diversity and local optima avoidance.
– WDHS-NSGA-III is evaluated at diﬀerent scale FJSP instances. Furthermore,
experimental results show an excellent performance in solving a complex
scheduling model.

2

Problem Description

2.1

A Subsection Sample

FJSP involves assigning n jobs J = {J1 , J2 , J3 ..., Jn } to m machines M =
{M1 , M2 , M3 , · · · , Mm }, where each job Ji comprises a predeﬁned sequence of
hi operations Oi = {Oi1 , Oi2 , ..., Oihi }. Each operation Oij (the j-th operation
of job Ji ) must be processed on one machine selected from its available machine
set Mk ⊆ M .FJSP includes two sub-problems: machine selection and operation processing order. Modern manufacturing systems have to simultaneously
satisfy multiple objectives such as high eﬃciency, low energy consumption, and
on-time delivery, so this paper considers a comprehensive model that includes
four objectives: maximum completion time, total machine energy consumption,
total machine load, and total delay. These four aspects from the production eﬃciency, energy use, equipment use and delivery on time together to evaluate the
scheduling program, the speciﬁc objective function is calculated as follows:
1. Minimize the Maximum Completion Time:

Constraints:
– All machines and jobs are available at time 0 and are assumed to be continuously available.
– etup times of machines and transportation times between operations are negligible.
– A machine can only perform one operation at a time.
– There are no priority constraints between operations of diﬀerent jobs, and
the operation sequence of each job cannot be changed.
– All machines are failure-free during the scheduling period (no breakdown or
maintenance).
– Once an operation starts, it must run to completion.
– No operation of a job can be started before the completion of its preceding
operation.

3

Proposed Algorithm

NSGA-III is a widely recognized as a multi-objective optimization algorithm
[13]. Based on NSGA-III, we propose WDHS-NSGA-III to address the challenges of many-objective ﬂexible job shop scheduling problem by integrating a
hybrid neighborhood search mechanism with a reference vector-guided subpopulation collaboration framework. The WDHS-NSGA-III algorithm’s ﬂowchart
is illustrated in Fig. 1, which provides a visual representation of its steps and
processes. First, a hybrid initialization strategy is used to generate the initial
population, after which the population is divided into multiple sub-populations
by uniformly distributed reference points. During the evolutionary process, the
parents are selected from diﬀerent sub-populations based on a roulette strategy, and the geometrically diverse crossover and mutation are used to generate
oﬀspring Q. Meanwhile, multi-neighborhood strategies are triggered to generate
oﬀspring M for the non-dominated solutions in the low-density sub-populations.
Subsequently, the original population P, oﬀspring Q and M are merged, and individuals are screened by the reference-point association mechanism: Individuals
are ﬁrst assigned to the subpopulation corresponding to the nearest reference

154

J. Wang et al.

point based on the target normalized projection, and then elite retention is
performed by combining the small habitat count and the crowding distance to
ensure an even distribution of the Pareto frontiers. The above process is executed
cyclically, and the ﬁnal output is the solution set.

Fig. 1. The ﬂowchart of WDHS-NSGA-III.

3.1

Encoding and Decoding

The encoding framework is structured into two distinct segments: the Machine
Selection (MS), which allocates speciﬁc machine to each job, and Operation

An Improved NSGA-III Algorithm for Solving FJSP

155

Sequence (OS), which governs the chronological execution of all manufacturing
tasks. An example of dual-segment encoding strategy is depicted in Fig. 2. The
ﬁrst segment assigns machines Mk to each operation in the OS. Each element in
the MS corresponds to the machine selected for the respective operation in the
OS. For instance, the sequence {M1 , M3 , M2 , M5 , M4 , M2 } represents O11 to M1 ,
O12 to M3 and so on, maintaining a strict one-to-one mapping between operations and machines. The second segment encodes the processing order using job
indices Ji (i ∈ 1, 2, 3, 4), arranged left-to-right. The j-th occurrence of Ji denotes
the j-th operation of job Ji .For example, the sequence {J2 , J3 , J1 , J2 , J4 , J3 } represents {O21 , O31 , O11 , O22 , O41 , O32 }, where Oij is the j-th operation of Ji . The
decoding process mainly involves converting chromosomes into feasible scheduling plans. During the decoding process, each operation is arranged from left to
right. Through machine selection encoding, the machines selected for all operations can be determined, while the operation sequencing encoding is responsible
for sorting each operation on each machine to ensure that each operation can be
processed on the corresponding machine.

Fig. 2. Example of dual-segment encoding.

3.2

Hybrid Initialization Strategy

The quality of the initial solution exerts a substantial inﬂuence on the algorithm’s performance. Superior initial solutions can expedite the convergence of
the algorithm, whereas inferior ones may result in slow convergence. In this
study, for the initialization of the machine selection chromosome, we employ the
GLR method [14], which contains global selection (GS), local selection (LS),
and random selection (RS). For the initialization of the operation ordering chromosome, we introduce two initialization methods for the operation sequence
chromosome: a greedy initialization and a random initialization. The greedy
initialization assigns higher priority to jobs with earlier delivery time (EDT),
while the random initialization introduces diversity by randomly arranging the
sequence.

156

3.3

J. Wang et al.

Evolutionary Operators

The proposed algorithm employs specialized crossover operators for machine
selection (MS) and operation sequence (OS) chromosomes to balance solution
feasibility and population diversity. For the MS, the Multi-Point Crossover
(MPX) [15] mechanism utilizes a binary masking strategy. A randomly generated binary mask of equal length to the chromosome determines the exchange
positions between parent solutions, where values of 1 indicate machine assignments to be swapped. Oﬀspring inherit machine assignments from alternating
parents based on the mask’s binary values, ensuring compatibility constraints
are preserved while generating novel machine conﬁgurations. This structured
exchange prevents infeasible solutions by maintaining valid machine-operation
mappings during crossover.
For the OS, the improved precedence operation crossover (IPOX) [7] preserves partial sequence integrity while introducing diversity. A contiguous segment of operations is randomly selected from one parent and inserted into
the counterpart parent’s chromosome, with duplicate operations in non-selected
positions removed to maintain feasibility. This approach retains critical subsequences that may represent eﬃcient local schedules while enabling global exploration through segment insertion. The IPOX mechanism inherently respects
precedence constraints by preserving the original order of non-selected operations, thereby avoiding invalid permutations.
To maintain evolutionary diversity while preserving solution feasibility, distinct mutation strategies are designed for machine selection and operation
sequencing chromosomes.
For the MS, the random machine selection mutation operates by stochastically selecting a gene locus and replacing its current machine with an alternative
from the feasible machine set capable of processing the associated operation.
For the OS, the Reverse Sequence Mutation enhances schedule diversity
through structural inversion. A contiguous subsequence bounded by two randomly selected indices undergoes order reversal, eﬀectively permuting operation priorities while maintaining adjacency relationships. This topological transformation facilitates exploration of alternative scheduling permutations within
feasible precedence constraints, as the inversion preserves the integrity of nonadjacent operation dependencies.
3.4

Subpopulation Division and Wide-Area Crossover

The wide-area crossover mechanism proposed in this study aims to enhance the
exchange of information between diﬀerent search areas and expand the search
range by selecting parents for crossover across subpopulations. Speciﬁcally, after
normalizing the objective values, each solution is assigned to the subpopulation
corresponding to the reference vector that yields the maximum cosine similarity
with the normalized objective vector, the reference vector is the direction from
the origin to the reference point. Subsequently, a directional crossover strategy

An Improved NSGA-III Algorithm for Solving FJSP

157

is implemented where parent pairs are exclusively selected from distinct subpopulations using roulette wheel selection. The process is as follows: To ensure
diversity across the Pareto front, reference vectors are uniformly generated in
the objective space using the uniform point generation method. In the objective space, each coordinate axis is uniformly divided into p segments, yielding
W(M,p) reference points. The number of reference points is determined by the

As shown in Fig. 3, each individual (x1 , x2 , x3 ) is associated with reference vectors by calculating the angular θ between its objective vector and each reference
vector. Speciﬁcally, an individual is assigned to the subspace governed by the
reference vector with (x1 aligns with V2 , x2 aligns with V3 , x3 aligns with V4 ).
Following subpopulation assignment based on reference vectors, a randomized pairing mechanism is employed to enhance diversity: two distinct nonempty
subpopulations are randomly selected for each crossover operation. Within each
chosen subpopulation, parents are selected for reproduction through roulette
wheel selection based on normalized objective values. This process iterates until
the oﬀspring population reaches the predeﬁned size, ensuring balanced exploration across subpopulations while promoting genetic mixing between diverse
regions of the objective space. After crossover, oﬀspring undergo mutation operations with predeﬁned probabilities: single-point mutation for the Machine Selection (MS) replaces a randomly selected machine assignment with an alternative
from its feasible set, while reverse-sequence mutation for the Operation Sequence
(OS) inverts the order of a contiguous subsequence of ﬁxed length.

J. Wang et al.

Fig. 3. Subpopulation division.

3.5

Local Reinforcement Based on Density Perception

To address under-explored regions, the proposed algorithm prioritizes neighborhood search in low-density areas. These subregions are identiﬁed by comparing subpopulation sizes against the average population density. Speciﬁcally,
subpopulations with sizes below the average density threshold are ﬂagged as
low-density, indicating sparse solution coverage in their corresponding objective
space regions.
To identify low-density subregions, the density of each subpopulation is measured by its size. Let Nsub denote the total number of subpopulations and |Pk |
denote the size of subpopulation Pk . The average density ρ̄ is computed as Eq.
(8):
ρ̄ =

1
Nsub

Nsub

|Pk |.

(8)

k=1

A subpopulation Pk is classiﬁed as low-density if |Pk | < ρ̄. These subpopulations
are prioritized for neighborhood search to enhance exploration in under-sampled
regions of the Pareto front. The neighborhood search strategies are subsequently
applied to non-dominated solutions in these low-density subgroups.
As shown in Fig. 4, this diagram illustrates the identiﬁcation of nondominated solutions in low-density subregions for neighborhood search. The red
dashed circles highlight the low-density areas, and the red dots denote nondominated solutions in the subregions. These non-dominated solutions in low-density
regions are prioritized for neighborhood search operations to enhance exploration in under-sampled regions of the Pareto Front. The neighborhood search
strategies in this paper consists of ﬁve heuristics. These heuristic search methods
can be combined into six search strategies, the execution of the cases is chosen
randomly.
For MS:

An Improved NSGA-III Algorithm for Solving FJSP

159

Fig. 4. Neighborhood search in Low-density area.

– Change the machine selection to the machine with the shortest processing
time among the available machines.
– Change the machine selection to the machine with the lowest processing power
among the available machines.
– Move the operation from the machine with the highest workload to the
machine with the lowest workload.
For OS:
– Place the operation with the earlier delivery time in front.
– Randomly swap the positions of two operations from diﬀerent job IDs.

4

Experiments

To demonstrate the validity of WDHS-NSGA-III, we used the Brandimarte
dataset with varying numbers of jobs and machines. These instances are designed
to represent diﬀerent levels of complexity in the job shop scheduling problem.
We compared the performance of WDHS-NSGA-III with the NSGA-III [13],
MOEA/D [16], RVEA [17] in the experiment.
The test instances were conﬁgured with the following parameters: the
crossover rate is set to 0.8 and the mutation rate to 0.1 for all algorithms,
while the population size and maximum generations were standardized to 100
individuals and 100 iterations. Each algorithm is run independently 20 times,
the proposed algorithm has a GS ratio of 0.6, LS of 0.3, RS of 0.1 and EDT for
0.6. For RVEA, the parameter controlling the rate of change of penalty is 2, and
the frequency of employing reference vector adaptation is 0.1. For MOEA/D,
the Tchebycheﬀ decomposition method was chosen, and the neighborhood size
was set to N/10 (where N is the population size).

160

J. Wang et al.

The delivery time of the job is set to be randomly generated and the machine
processing power is randomly generated between [2,5]. Machine power at idle is
randomly generated between [0.3,0.8].
Evaluation metrics: Given that the true Pareto front (PF) cannot be calculated in advance, the Hypervolume (HV) is utilized as an evaluation metric. It
quantiﬁes the volume of the objective space dominated by the set of solutions
(Pareto front) generated by the algorithm. A larger HV value indicates better
performance. Additionally, the average values of the objective functions are also
used as an important metric. The HV metric is calculated as follows:
HV (P, z ∗ ) =

ν(X, z ∗ ),

(9)

x∈P

where P is the set of solutions calculated by the algorithm, z ∗ is the reference
point, and z ∗ = (1, 1, 1, 1). X is the set of non-dominated solutions in P , and X
needs to be normalized.

5

Analysis and Discussion

The data in Table 1 demonstrates the superior performance of the WDHSNSGA-III algorithm in addressing many-objective ﬂexible job shop scheduling problems. Across most test instances, WDHS-NSGA-III achieves optimal
results in makespan minimization and machine load balancing, for example, in
the MK07 instance, it reduces makespan by 16.1% compared to NSGA-III, highlighting the eﬃcacy of its hybrid neighborhood search. The algorithm also excels
in energy eﬃciency and tardiness reduction, lowering total energy consumption
by 7.1% in MK03 and cutting tardiness by 41.3% in MK04, attributed to its subpopulation collaboration and deadline-driven reordering strategies. While RVEA
and MOEA/D marginally outperform WDHS-NSGA-III in a few instances, the
proposed algorithm maintains superior Pareto front coverage and solution uniformity in high-dimensional scenarios through density-aware local search and
reference vector-guided global exploration. Overall, WDHS-NSGA-III outperforms baseline algorithms in 80% of cases, validating the robustness of its multistrategy optimization framework. By balancing machine selection and sequencing
adjustments, it eﬀectively resolves conﬂicting objectives, as seen in MK09, where
it signiﬁcantly improves energy consumption, machine load and tardiness despite
a slightly higher makespan compared to MOEA/D. Limitations arise in extreme
deadline-sensitive scenarios, these results underscore WDHS-NSGA-III’s potential for industrial scheduling applications, oﬀering a novel many-objective challenges in smart manufacturing systems.
The experimental results in Table 2 demonstrate that the proposed WDHSNSGA-III achieves better performance in terms of the HV across most test
instances. Compared to baseline algorithms such as NSGA-III, MOEA/D,
and RVEA, WDHS-NSGA-III consistently attains higher HV values, indicating improved diversity and convergence of the obtained Pareto fronts. Particularly in complex scheduling scenarios with high-dimensional objectives, the
algorithm exhibits enhanced robustness, maintaining balanced exploration of

Outlook

The proposed WDHS-NSGA-III framework presents a robust approach for
addressing many-objective ﬂexible job shop scheduling challenges, yet its evolution and application in real-world industrial settings warrant further exploration. Future research could focus on enhancing the algorithm’s adaptability to dynamic production environments, where real-time disruptions such as
machine failures, urgent order insertions, or ﬂuctuating energy costs necessitate online optimization capabilities. Integrating reinforcement learning or adaptive search mechanisms may enable the algorithm to dynamically recalibrate its
search strategies, balancing exploration-exploitation trade-oﬀs under evolving
constraints. Additionally, extending the algorithm’s scalability to handle largescale scheduling scenarios involving hundreds of jobs and machines remains critical for industrial adoption.

Abstract. This paper deals with the gantry cranes problem scheduling
at a container terminal. The scheduling of this equipment aims to minimize container assignment time for two diﬀerent operations. A mixed
integer linear programming model is developed to minimize execution
time. An optimized greedy heuristic algorithm (GHA) is also applied to
solve this problem, with a new best-scheduling update strategy included
to improve solution stability. Results show that the proposed GHA eﬃciently solves the RTG cranes scheduling problem.
Keywords: Container terminal · Yard crane scheduling problem ·
Optimization · Greedy heuristic algorithm

1

Introduction

Container transport services have grown exceptionally in recent years, and this
trend shows no signs of abating, as there is a growing demand for container
transport services. In a port container terminal, a large number of vessels are
attached to each other, and each vessel relies on a certain amount of equipment,
supported by a number of berths and mobile cranes unloading/loading. In a
second step, the unloaded containers are moved to the storage area by a conveyor
via internal trucks and placed in suitable positions by crane RTGs. Finally, the
containers are shipped from the storage area to their customers by external
trucks. For export operations, import containers are moved to the terminal’s
storage area by internal trucks and unloaded from trucks and several blocks
using RTG cranes in the terminal. The objective is to minimize the duration of
all these operations for a gantry crane waiting at a container block. Consequently,
the aim of this study is to determine the processing time for each container using

a gantry crane and a storage location, in order to complete all operations in the

while Fig. 2 is an example of the terminal operation of containers and gantry
crane, and, ﬁnally, Flowchart of the (GHAGGCSP) greedy heuristic algorithm
GCSP. are given in Fig. 3. In recent research, we have proposed exact methods
for solving the GCSP with non-interference constraints and container location
capacities at the container block in the port of Sfax-Tunisia.
However, building large instances takes a long time and is diﬃcult due to
memory saturation. The objective of this paper is to reduce execution time using
a (GHA). There are many researchers who have focused on (GCSP) in the literature. In Sect. 2, the authors review the literature on the (GCSP) in container
storage areas. Section 3 explains the deﬁnition of the principles of numerical
examples and the mathematical model of the container terminal gantry cranes
scheduling problem (GCSP). The structure of the approximate optimality solution, based on the greedy heuristic algorithm GHA, and Fig. 2 shows the process of assigning the gantry crane to the block container. is shown in Sect. 4, and
Sect. 5 contains illustrations and comparisons between CPLEX and the proposed
GHA. This section also deals with a large-scale problem. The last Sect. 6, then
contains the most essential conclusions of this document.

2

Related Works

Research into gantry crane scheduling problem (GCSP) has focused on improving
the performance of container terminals. In [4], the researchers analyzed the operational problem of loading and unloading trains at a container terminal. They
proposed a MIP approach for scheduling straddle carriers and gantry cranes to
minimize total train delay time, combining a branch-and-bound (B&B) method
with a simulated annealing heuristic. [10] proposed an integrated optimization
approach to determine crane yard schedules and truck placement positions, formulating a MILP model and a two-stage heuristic algorithm to solve the problem. For import and export containers, terminal managers usually know the
relevant data at the time of arrival. In [6], examines the problem of space allocation for export containers in automated container terminals, applying optimization integrated with simulation. Part of the objective function of an optimization problem corresponds to a simulation module for minimizing vehicle
waiting time. Then, to solve the target problem a (MILP) proposes with genetic
algorithm adopted from the simulation model that shows computational speed
performance resolution time. In [7] the authors compared automated QC and
gantry crane (GC) scheduling during ship loading and unloading. They had put
forward a (MILP) that included precedence rules and time windows, and aimed
to improve the eﬃciency of container port operations scheduling, as well as a
heuristic algorithm adapted to wait for the total objective of minimizing the
overall time of all seaside and landside resources. They propose a metaheuristic
genetic algorithm (GA) to address this problem. The genetic algorithm and MIP
to provide an optimal solution to the GCSP problem. On the other hand, in [2],

166

S. B. Zina et al.

have proposed a (MILP) to minimize the transport time of problematic automated guided vehicles (AGVs). With a strategy based on graph theory solved
by exact method and ﬁnally to optimize their result a heuristic algorithm adapts
and shows the eﬃciency of solution compared to other deﬁned approaches. Subsequently, numerous heuristic approaches have also been proposed to deal with
the various types of GCSP problems, with the aim of improving the quality of
gantry crane scheduling. In [5] develop a two-level stochastic model to optimize
storage space operations according to constraints linked to increasing container
traﬃc. By applying the model, they are able to determine how to minimize total
transport distances, although stochastic models provide a good measure of terminal performance. The ﬁrst model level is calculated to assign the ships in each
block, without considering the physical characteristics of the container block
location, and the second is a genetic algorithm-based solving system applied to
solve the ﬁrst-level model. A satisfactory solution result from the equilibrium
coeﬃcient share will be obtained based on the practices between the two levels
in a container terminal. Concerning GCSP this is a topical issue, [8] the authors
has developed a gantry crane (GC) displacement problem with the aim of ﬁnding
ways to improve the operational eﬃciency of container terminals and to consider
the integration of various automated container handling methods. This integration of resources minimizes container loading and unloading times, both in the
storage area and on the ship. To this end, two high-performance solution methods, inspired by the mathematical model proposed for the purpose of validating
a small-size instance and an illustrious large-size genetic algorithm (GA), are
developed to ﬁnd a solution approaching optimality. In all cases, both approaches
provide an eﬃcient result within an acceptable computing time. For container
import and export activists, data on the various destinations, boarding vessels
and collection times are normally known by the terminal manager at the time
of arrival. In another sense [1] develop a scheduling strategy for the yard crane
workload balance and resolution (MILP) proposed in the case study that shows
the operational optimization of terminals. In [9] have proposed a (MILP) to
minimize the transport time of problematic automated guided vehicles (AGVs).
With a strategy based on graph theory solved by exact method and ﬁnally to
optimize their result a heuristic algorithm adapts and shows the eﬃciency of
solution compared to other deﬁned approaches. Subsequently, numerous heuristic approaches have also been proposed to deal with the various types of (GCSP)
problems, with the aim of improving the quality of gantry crane scheduling.

This study focuses on the problem of scheduling site cranes to noninterference constraints and container location capacity for single container
blocks. Section 3 provides a mathematical model based on the model proposed
by [11] has a has a mixed integer programming (MILP) model solved using
CPLEX, and the exact method we propose for determining all possible choices
for assigning the storage area gantry cranes to the container blocks while considering the non-interference constraints between the RTG cranes and the container
location capacity. It is capable of calculating the optimum completion time for
a container block. We found that the exact (MILP) method outperformed the
port identiﬁcation method in terms of calculation time and completion time for
a small number of container locations. However, the diﬃculty with the (MILP)
method is that for large instances, such as 25 slots, it may take 2 h to complete
the operation. For these situations, we wish to propose in this study a (GHA)
to achieve results closer to the optimum, with fast execution for the yard gantry
cranes scheduling problem, as well as with capacity in each container location
following the dimension of the container block to storage area. In addition, in
[11] to examine the yard crane scheduling problem (YCSP), with the objective
of specifying the order in which containers are processed in the storage and
retrieval areas. To this end, they have developed a (MILP) formulation for handling gantry crane movements on the tank farm and a heuristic level that handles
the optimal sequence based on local search. The results show the performance of
the solution in large real instances obtained from a container port. The problem
of the yard gantry crane that we have studied meets the elements mentioned,
container scheduling is aﬀected on board a single container block, moving time
between slots for the gantry crane is negligible, with the length of the container
block location determined by the standard container size (20- 40 ft). The gantry
crane work is interrupted if a storage slot is not available for the slot it has
been allocated. Finally, the intermediate trucks arrive at the assigned location
once the gantry crane has ﬁnished loading or unloading, so there is always a
suﬃcient number of intermediate trucks available. This feature of the (GHA)
can considerably optimize the inconvenience of (MILP) in earlier studies by [3].

Mathematical Models for the GCSP

3.1

Mathematical Model

The mathematical model GCSP is represented as follows: Parameters:
Sets and indices:
– S: set of slots (storage locations per TEU), indexed by i = 1, 2, . . . , S.
– K: set of gantry cranes, indexed by k = 1, 2, . . . , K.
– Si : set of stacks in slot i, indexed by s = 1, 2, . . . , Si .
Parameters:
–
–
–
–
–
–

pi : processing time of slot i by a gantry crane.
ni : number of containers to be loaded or unloaded in slot i.
ti : number of stacks in slot i, indexed by s = 1, 2, . . . , ti .
h: time taken to assign containers in slot i.
fi : storage capacity of slot i per TEU.
M : large integer used to represent inﬁnity.

Decision variables:
– ci : integer variable representing the current completion time of stack s in slot
i.
– maxCi : makespan (maximum completion time).
– xik : 1 if and only if slot i is assigned to gantry crane k, 0 otherwise.
– yij : 1 if and only if slot i completes no later than slot j starts, 0 otherwise.
– zijkg : 1 if and only if slot i is assigned to gantry crane k and slot j is assigned
to gantry crane g, 0 otherwise.

The objective function (1) minimises the maximum of workload of container
operations in the storage area, which is the ﬁnal completion time from all slots.
Constraints (2) and (3) deﬁne the value properties the ﬁnal completion time
for variable decision and the completion time at the beginning of the handling
operation. Constraint (4) ensures that each slot must be assigned to at least one
gantry crane. Constraint (5) ensures that the processing time must be greater
than or equal to the workload (workload = number of containers time to assign a
container in the slot per gantry crane). Constraints (6) and (7) deﬁnition of the
linear of the decision variable yij : constraint (6) shows that yij = 1 if the (laste
completion time less than or equal to completion time at the latest - processing
time, if only if means that yij = 1 when slot i ends before slot j begins, constraint
(7) indicates the contrary. Constraint (8) ensures that each slot capacity i does
not exceed the stack tier (height), indicates that the two gantry cranes do not
process the same slot at the same time. And ﬁnally, constraint (9) and (10)
represent the interference constraint, that is, when slot i is assigned to crane k
and slot j is assigned to crane g at the same time and under the condition that
slot i that the processing time is lower than slot j than the processing time if
i < j and k < g. Constraint (11) ensure the nature of the used decision variables.

A Greedy Heuristic Algorithm

The greedy heuristic algorithm is specialized in assignment problems to solve
speciﬁc problems using a few simple steps. In this section, we develop a heuristic glutton algorithm for solving the problem of RTG crane scheduling and slot
assignment in block container to container terminal. The principle of this heuristic is to assign an RTG crane to the slot in the block container that can take
the least amount of processing time. This algorithm can compute the makespan
of S slot as a function of k RTG crane and respecting the following steps in S
iterations:

Fig. 3. Flowchart of the greedy heuristic algorithm for GHAGCSP.

Adaptive Greedy Heuristic Algorithm for RTG Crane Scheduling

171

Step 1. Start by initializing all parameters, such as the number of RTG
cranes, the assignment times in each slot, and the number of stacks (length and
height). Step 2. Sort the execution times and number of containers for each
slot in descending order. Step 3. Generate all possible combinations of RTG
crane slot assignments using a combinatorial approach. Create an assignment
function that calculates the total time needed to complete all assignments. Step
4. For each assignment generated, check that it respects the conditions of noninterference between RTG cranes. You can do this by checking that each slot
allocated to the same RTG crane does not overlap in time. Go to step 5 if the
container assignment is less than or equal to the slot capacity; otherwise, increase
the slot value and go on to step 2. Step 5. For the assignments that satisfy the
non-interference conditions, calculate the total time required to complete all
these assignments. Step 6. Choose the solution that oﬀers optimum handling
with minimum makespan.

Notation: RTGk : gantry crane, bay indexed k; sloti : bay indexed i; CCti : current completion time of the gantry crane; CATsloti : capacity of slot i; PTsloti :
processing time of slot i by a gantry crane; STsloti : number of stacks in slot i;

A ﬂowchart visualizes an algorithm, a process of tasks in the terminal port.
In this section, we propose a ﬂowchart for the greedy heuristic algorithm of the
Gantry Cranes Scheduling problem (GHAGCSP), which allows optimal solutions to be built from a single block container (Fig. 3). In this paper, we use a
greedy heuristic algorithm (GHA) to ﬁnd solutions close to reality, in order to
simplify the problem-solving process. The greedy heuristic algorithm for obtaining a nearoptimal solution is generally used for solving diﬃcult problems and
with easy sets of steps used. Initialization, at each stage, an initial local optimal
choice is made. This involves choosing the best existing option at that stage,
based on certain criteria speciﬁc to the problem, solution update, stop criterion,
execution.

5

Experimental Results

In this section, small-scale examples are given to compare the performance of
greedy heuristic algorithms (GHA) with the optimal solution obtained using
CPLEX. Due to the complexity of CPLEX in solving large-scale problems, evaluations for large-scale instances are only made using the optimal solution computed by CPLEX, comparisons for large-scale instances are only made between
them and the proposed heuristic algorithm. Several tested instances are solved
using CPLEX 12.4.1(a commercial software package for exact integer programming solving) and the greedy heuristic algorithm (GHA) which relates to the
reduction of a complex problem.

are given between [5, 60]. According to Table 1, since instance 21, the CPLEX has
been unable to produce a solution after more than 6 h of machine processing,
while the glutton algorithm provides a better solution in an extremely short time,
but instance 25 is the longest is no solution up to more than 5 h of executions.

solutions lies between 40.70 and 100 are more eﬃcient than the optimum solutions.

6

Conclusion and Future Work

It is generally accepted that container terminals play a central role in the world.
Play a central role in the world, indeed the performance of container terminals
will depend on their ability to meet the needs of their customers and to satisfy
them. The operation of container terminals depends on the allocation of resources
within the terminal. Storage RTGs are the most important equipment in the
storage area. This paper proposes a greedy heuristic algorithm for the RTG
crane storage yard scheduling problem is essential in container port terminal
operations. In this study, the problem of scheduling multiple storage yard crane
for container handling is treated as an important problem in port terminals.
Since it has been shown that the scheduling problem is an NP-hard problem, a
gluttonous heuristic algorithm based on real decisions has been proposed to ﬁnd
optimal schedules for this problem. To further develop the performance of the
solutions in terms of execution time and approximation to the optimal solution,
a metaheuristic approach was tested.

S. B. Zina et al.

Solving the Electricity Technician
Dispatch Problem
Haifa Zaidi and Malek Mouhoub(B)
Department of Computer Science, University of Regina, Regina, SK, Canada
{hzy487,mouhoubm}@uregina.ca

Abstract. Combinatorial optimization problems involve ﬁnding the
best solution from a wide set of possibilities, often arising in logistics,
scheduling, and resource allocation. These problems, such as the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and the Multi-Depot Vehicle Routing
Problem (MDVRP), are typically NP-hard, with solution spaces growing exponentially, making systematic search methods impractical. We
address a particular application of the MDVRP: the Electricity Technician Dispatch Problem (ETDP), which focuses on planning and optimizing routes for technicians providing maintenance services to customers
located at various geographical points. In order to ﬁnd the optimal travel
routes, we propose a variant of the Genetic Algorithm (GA). In the initial population, customers are assigned to the nearest depot and routes
are generated randomly for each technician. Then, during the improvement phase, our algorithm proceeds with its standard evolution process
to explore better solutions. To evaluate the performance of our proposed
method, we conducted experiments on several instances of the Cordeau
benchmarking dataset and compared the results with those obtained
from other methods, such as the Stochastic Local Search (SLS) and the
Nearest-Neighbor Algorithm (NNA). The results show the eﬀectiveness
of our method in terms of both solution quality and computational eﬃciency.
Keywords: Combinatorial Optimization · Genetic Algorithm ·
Nature-Inspired Techniques · Metaheuristics · Multi-Depot Vehicle
Routing Problem (MDVRP) · Electricity Technician Dispatch Problem
(ETDP)

1

Introduction

The Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) lies at the heart of transportation operations in industrial and commercial supply chains and is one of the most important
and intensively studied optimization problems in computational mathematics
and operations research [1,2]. The VRP involves a set of vehicles with a given
capacity that must deliver goods to a set of locations, starting and ending at
a given depot. The main objective is to minimize the cost of the tours while
meeting capacity constraints and ensuring that each location is visited exactly
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 177–188, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_13

178

H. Zaidi and M. Mouhoub

once [3]. The Multi-Depot Vehicle Routing Problem (MDVRP) is a variant of
VRP where there are multiple depots instead of a single one, from which vehicles
start and return after completing their services [4]. The Electricity Technician
Dispatch Problem (ETDP) is one of the main applications of the MDVRP. The
EDTP consists of optimizing the total cost related to the adopted tours taken by
technicians who are deployed to provide customer service in diﬀerent geographical locations, while meeting the availability and demand constraints of technicians and customers, respectively. Figure 1 shows an example of the EDTP with
3 depots, a maximum of 4 technicians per service center, and 19 customers who
need service.

Fig. 1. An instance of the ETDP problem

The ETDP requires an exponential running time (O(2E ) where E is the
number of edges in the related network) [4]. For that reason, new techniques
have been adopted to overcome this challenge in practice. These techniques
include metaheuristics and machine learning techniques, as an alternative to
exact methods [5–12]. Although these techniques do not ensure the optimality of the solution, they oﬀer a reasonable trade-oﬀ between the quality of the
returned result and the processing time. Solving the MDVRP (and consequently
the EDTP) comes with an additional challenge, as we are dealing with several
tours, each with its own starting depot. To address this diﬃculty (in addition to
the exponential time cost inherited by the underlying TSP), we propose a variant of the genetic algorithm (GA) that combines classical GAs and local search
operations. We ﬁrst generate the initial population by assigning each customer
to the nearest depot and randomly split each cluster to create the sub-tours
while guaranteeing the capacity constraint of technicians and the number of
technicians constraint per depot. We then apply the standard GA steps (selection, crossover, and mutation). To evaluate the performance of our method, we
conducted experiments on a set of problem instances from the Cordeau benchmark [13]. We compared our method with other techniques inspired by nature

Solving the Electricity Technician Dispatch Problem

179

and exact algorithms. Nature-inspired techniques include: the Stochastic Local
Search (SLS), Nearest-Neighbor Algorithm (NNA), a Hybrid Algorithm called
ILS-RVND-SP that combines an Iterated Local Search (ILS) based on a Variable Neighborhood Descent with Random Neighborhood Ordering (RVND) and
a Set Partitioning (SP) method [14], and an adaptive large neighborhood search
heuristic (ALNS) [15]. For exact techniques, we consider the Branch and Bound
(B&B) algorithm.

2

Related Work

Hwang [16] used an improved genetic algorithm to solve the VRP problem with
time constraints. To improve this algorithm, changes were made to the crossover
operator and a graphical interface was used to display the simulation results.
These results indicated that the proposed method is eﬀective in solving multidepot variants of VRP. Bell and McMullen [17] used Ant Colony Optimization
to solve VRP by making changes to the solution used to solve the TSP. The
results indicate acceptable performance for large instances. Szeto et al. [18] presented a population-based method called the Bee Colony algorithm to solve the
VRP problem. By improving the initial version of this algorithm, the authors
signiﬁcantly increased its performance, and their evaluation on two benchmark
samples showed the superiority of their proposed method. The proposed method
also obtained better results in terms of computational time compared to the
original Bee Colony algorithm. In [19], a clustering method based on GAs was
presented to solve MDVRP. The authors compared their proposed method with
the nearest neighbor algorithm and concluded that clustering provides better
solutions in terms of distance between each customer and each depot. The computational time was shorter compared to the nearest neighbour algorithm. Giosa
et al. [20] used a hybrid method to solve a variant of the MDVRP. They ﬁrst clustered the problem and then routed it. Their simulation results for the heuristic
method presented in large-scale problems indicate good performance in solution
quality and execution time. Yongle He et al. [21] used the Tabu search algorithm
in conjunction with a clustering technique to solve the MDVRP. In the proposed
clustering method, called variable cluster grouping, the multi-depot problem is
ﬁrst converted to multiple single-depots. The Tabu algorithm is then executed to
solve each of the clusters. The simulation results show the proper performance
of their method.
In the case of the EDTP the following were reported. Xu and Chiu [22]
proposed a method, based on GRASP, to solve the problem of dispatching technicians with time windows. In another study [23], constraint programming and
column generation methods were used to repair machines at Xerox in Chile to
dispatch technicians. In deﬁning this issue, each customer has diﬀerent priorities,
and their demand must be met within a period by the company’s technicians.
Therefore, reducing response time plays a vital role in service delivery. Kovacs
et al. [24] deﬁned the EDTP in which technicians are deployed to provide services with varying skill levels and time windows. The goal was to reduce the

180

H. Zaidi and M. Mouhoub

total cost of the route, and an adaptive large neighborhood search algorithm
was adopted for two samples of real and artiﬁcial world. Pillac et al. [25] took
advantage of a parallel approach to providing customer service by technicians
through EDTP. To achieve this purpose, the authors used VRP with Time Windows (VRPTW). The authors also used a method that combines a heuristic
method, a parallel adaptive Large Neighborhood Search, and mathematical programming to solve the problem. The authors tested their method on Solomon
VRPTW instances and list promising results. In another study, Cortés et al. [26]
deﬁned the EDTP with 20 technicians providing machine repair services to 70
customers. Using branch and price, the authors searched for optimal routes for
each technician with the shortest distance and travel time, and used constraint
programming methods to solve the problem. The simulation results showed that
the optimal answers were reached in an acceptable time. El-Yaakoubi et al.
[27] used mixed-integer linear modeling to model EDTP and used three methods that combined the Tabu search and memetic algorithm. The authors tested
their proposed method on random and real-world samples and compared the
results obtained with the CPLEX values in small and medium dimensions. For
large-scale instances, CPLEX could not ﬁnd a possible answer, while the proposed method could return the optimal solution in a reasonable time. In a new
study by Mathlouthi et al. [28] on the route of technicians for maintenance and
repair deployment, a Tabu search algorithm with adaptive memory was used.
Adding adaptive memory to the proposed method helped to make the generated
responses more diverse. The authors compared their approach with the branch
and price algorithm and achieved promising results. Guastaroba et al. [29] proposed a heuristic method based on adaptive large neighborhood search with a
three-phase decomposition to solve the routing of large-scale technicians for the
installation and repair services of electrical equipment. The simulation results
on large samples showed that optimal answers are obtained in a short time.
The authors also compared their proposed method with the results obtained by
CPLEX and came up with promising results. In a recent study, Sadeghilalimi et
al. [30] addressed the ETDP by implementing a two-phase hybrid approach that
combines clustering techniques with exact methods, heuristics and metaheuristics for routing. The results demonstrated promising performance compared to
other studies.

3

Problem Formulation

The EDTP can be represented as a graph G = (V, E) in which V = N ∪
D is a set of nodes that includes |N | = n customers who need service and
|D| = m technician depots. The set of edges E represents the distance between
customers/depots. The cost of traveling between the vertices i and j is deﬁned
as cij . Each depot has a maximum number of technicians K, each with capacity
Pk (1 ≤ k ≤ K). The following is the formulation of the EDTP with decision
variables, constraints, and objective.

Proposed Methodology

Initially, we assign each customer to its nearest depot and split each cluster to
random sub-tours with respect to the capacity constraint, which ensures that the
initial population consists of feasible individuals (possible solutions). The evolutionary process is then performed following the same standard steps as normal
GAs, including selection, crossover, and mutation operations. The subtours or
clusters obtained at the end of the GA process represent feasible solutions to the
EDTP. The following components of our method are described below.
Generate the Initial Population. Initially, each customer is assigned to the
nearest depot based on Euclidean distance and without imposing capacity limit.
We also introduce two categories of customers borderline and non-borderline. A
borderline customer is deﬁned as a customer for whom the relative diﬀerence
between the distance to a non-assigned cluster center and the distance to the
assigned cluster center is within a speciﬁed threshold. Mathematically, this can
be expressed as:
distance − shortestDistance
< borderlineThreshold
shortestDistance
where distance is the distance from the customer to the depot, the shortest
distance is the distance to the nearest (assigned) depot and limitThreshold is
the tolerance value that determines limit customers. These customers will be
selected later for inter-depot mutation.
Tournament Selection. At each generation, we must select parents for reproduction. This process uses a tournament selection method combined with an elite
retention strategy, as described in [22], to form a new population. Speciﬁcally,
a binary tournament selection approach is applied, which is based on ﬁtness. In
this approach, two individuals (called the tournament set) are chosen at random
from the population. In addition to the standard binary tournament method,
this work introduces a random factor, denoted as r, which is a value between 0
and 1. If r is less than a value empirically determined within the range (0.6 1.0),
the ﬁttest individual in the tournament set is selected for reproduction. Otherwise, any individual in the tournament set is randomly chosen for reproduction.

Solving the Electricity Technician Dispatch Problem

183

To ensure that the best individual is transferred to the next generation, an elite
model is used. The primary advantage of this elitist approach over traditional
probabilistic methods is that it guarantees that the best solution of the current
generation remains unchanged in the next generation. This prevents the best
chromosome from deteriorating as generations progress.
Best Cost Route Crossover. The crossover process involves several steps
to generate oﬀspring while maintaining feasibility in multi-depot vehicle routing
problems. First, two parent solutions (p1 and p2 ) are selected stochastically from
the population. Then a depot is chosen at random to undergo reproduction. From
each parent solution, a route (r1 from p1 and r2 from p2 ) is randomly selected.
Customers in r1 are removed from p2 , and customers in r2 are removed from
p1 to prevent duplication during the generation of oﬀspring. For each customer
in r1 , the algorithm calculates the cost of insertion to integrate that customer
into all feasible locations in p2 . The insertion costs are stored in an ordered list,
along with information on whether each insertion maintains feasibility. The costs
are then sorted in ascending order. A random number k ∈ (0, 1) is generated to
determine the insertion strategy. Based on the value of k, the algorithm either
selects the ﬁrst feasible insertion location or chooses a position from the list
regardless of feasibility. If no feasible location exists, a new route is created
containing only that customer. This process is repeated with the roles of r1 and r2
swapped, promoting diversity while balancing the exploration of unconventional
solutions and the exploitation of feasible ones.
Intra-depot Mutation: Intra-depot mutations increase diversity and help
genetic algorithms escape local minima by introducing new characteristics within
the routes of a single depot. Three types of mutation (reversal, single-customer
rerouting, and customer swapping) are applied with equal probability. In a reversal mutation, two cut points are selected in a chromosome, and the sequence of
customers between these points is reversed. Single Customer Re-Routing consists of randomly selecting a single customer, removing it from its current route,
and reinserting it at the best feasible insertion location within another route of
the same depot to minimize cost. In customer swapping mutation two routes are
randomly selected, and a customer is swapped between them. Mutations occur
with a low probability (0.2) for chromosomes within a randomly chosen depot
per generation. If selected, a mutation type is stochastically chosen to avoid
destructive changes while improving the quality of the solution.
Inter-depot Mutation: This method improves solution quality by swapping
customers between depots, optimizing costs or vehicle usage. It is applied every
10 generations, guided by the parameter Swapping Mutation Rate. Only customers whose reassignment reduces cost are considered, chosen from the list
of borderline customers identiﬁed during the initial clustering phase, as these
customers are assigned a candidate list of alternative depots for potential reas-

184

H. Zaidi and M. Mouhoub

signment. Customers close to their current depots or creating infeasible routes
are excluded.

5

Experimentation

To assess the performance of our proposed solution method, we conducted a set
of experiments on instances proposed by Cordeau et al. [13]. Python language
was used, and each algorithm was run 10 times for each instance. The best value
is then reported. The experiments were conducted on a computer with an Intel
Core i7-13650HX with 2.60 GHz and 24 GB of RAM running under Windows
11-64 bits. All the parameters guiding the algorithms we have used are tuned, to
their best, using the Chess Rating System (CRS-Tuning) [31]. In the case of GA,
and as explained earlier in the proposed methodology section, the best-cost route
crossover is chosen with probability 0.8, and for selection tournament selection
was adopted. For the mutation, we consider the inter-depot and intra-depot
mutation, respectively, with probability 0.05.

The results of the comparative experiments (the quality of the solutions
returned) are reported in Table 1. As stated earlier, the best results are reported
after 10 runs. Our solving techniques are compared with the adaptive large
neighborhood search heuristic (ALNS) [15], the Hybrid Genetic Algorithm with
Adaptive Diversity Control (HGSADC) [32], and the Hybrid algorithm combining Iterated Local Search (ILS) based on a Variable Neighborhood Descent
with Random neighborhood ordering (RVND) and the Set Partitioning method
(SP) [14]. In Table 1, n is the number of customers, k is the number of available
technicians (vehicles), and m is the number of service centers (depots). While
B&B returns the optimal solutions for most of the instances, it fails to solve
the other medium and large instances. This is justiﬁed by the exponential time
cost that B&B suﬀers from [33,34]. If we exclude instances successfully solved
by B&B, SLS, GA, and NNA demonstrated consistent performance by providing
promising results for all instances within an acceptable runtime.

6

Conclusion and Future Work

We propose a GA-based approach to solve the EDTP. The strategy begins with
assigning customers to their nearest depots and distinguishing between two types
of customer, borderline and nonborderline, based on a threshold value. More
speciﬁcally, the initial population is generated through a clustering and assignment process, resulting in a cluster per depot. Each cluster is then divided into
smaller groups, each to be served by a technician while respecting the capacity
constraints, to ensure feasible individuals in the initial population. The standard operations of genetic algorithms are subsequently applied to explore and
identify improved solutions. This method not only serves as solving technique
for the ETDP but also provides a near-optimal clustering for the other exact
and approximate techniques that are also used to ﬁnd the best tour for each
sub-cluster. To assess the performance of our GA-based method, we conducted
a set of experiments and reported promising results.
In the near future, further investigation of other metaheuristics [6,35–38]
will be conducted. Another research area that we will explore consists of solving
the ETDP in a dynamic environment. In this regard, the goal is to react in
real-time to unpredictable events that can occur due to car accidents, weather
conditions, or others. More speciﬁcally, after the optimal routes are provided
to technicians, we need to be able to review some of these routes in case they
are no longer feasible. The challenge here is to ﬁnd the next-best route(s) that
optimizes our objectives and satisﬁes the new constraints. In this context, we
will rely on dynamic combinatorial problem solving techniques [39–41], including
dynamic vehicle routing algorithms [42, 43].
