Exact Versus Bio-inspired Approximation
Methods for Solving Constrained
CP-Nets
Malek Mouhoub(B) , Sina Alizadeh, and Sifat E. Jahan
Department of Computer Science, University of Regina, Regina, SK, Canada
{mouhoubm,sau564,sjt588}@uregina.ca

Abstract. Eﬀective representation and understanding of user preferences play a vital role in various applications such as recommender
systems, product customization, resource allocation, conﬁguration, and
online auctions. Quantitative preferences are often represented using utility functions, while formalisms such as the semi-ring-based Constraint
Satisfaction Problem (CSP) and the Valued CSP extend the CSP framework to handle both quantitative preferences and constraints. However,
it might not always be convenient for users to quantify their preferences. A qualitative preference ordering can be more natural in these
circumstances. The Conditional Preference Network (CP-net) graphical model is designed to express users’ conditional preferences through
Conditional Preference Tables (CPTs). In many real-world combinatorial applications, conditional preferences coexist with hard constraints.
In this context, the CP-net has been extended to constraints through the
constrained CP-net (CCP-net) model. Solving the CCP-net consists in
ﬁnding a set of Pareto-optimal solutions that meet all constraints while
optimizing the qualitative preferences. Finding Pareto solutions in CCPnets using exact methods like branch and bound can be time-consuming.
Alternative methods, trading the quality of the returned solutions for the
execution time, can be found in metaheuristics, including nature-inspired
techniques. We introduce three bio-inspired methods to enhance the eﬃciency of ﬁnding Pareto solutions in CCP-nets. Additionally, we experimentally compare these techniques with those derived from an exact
method, focusing on the number of Pareto solutions identiﬁed and the
runtime performance. The results of the experiments, conducted on random CCP-net instances generated by the RB model, are reported and
discussed.
Keywords: Conditional Preference Networks · Constraint
Satisfaction · Metaheuristics · Nature-Inspired Techniques

1

Introduction

Preference and constraint often coexist in real-world applications, including
online auctions, recommender systems, resource allocation, and product conﬁguration. For example, a critical component of successfully deploying autonomous
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 189–200, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_14

190

M. Mouhoub et al.

agents is their ability to reason about user preferences. This includes representing and getting user preferences, as well as determining the best scenario based
on their preference statements. In addition, many agents operate in constrained
environments where the feasibility of a chosen scenario must also be considered.
Consider an online shopping assistant that helps the user choose a laptop.
The user may prefer a speciﬁc brand, lightweight design, with a speciﬁc CPU and
RAM capacity, screen size, and long battery life. However, constraints such as
limited budget, weight, and battery capacities that ensure compatibility between
components (e.g., a high-performance CPU requiring a larger power supply)
must also be considered. The agent must recommend a laptop conﬁguration
that meets all constraints while satisfying as many of the user’s preferences as
possible. As a second example, consider a restaurant menu assistant that helps a
user select a meal. The user may have preferences for appetizers, main courses,
and desserts but also face dietary restrictions such as avoiding allergens, a calorie
limit, or meal pairings. In addition, the user’s preferences may change depending
on the chosen appetizer or main course. The agent must recommend a menu that
adapts to these constraints and evolving preferences.
The above scenarios can be deﬁned as a preference-based constrained optimization problem, where the objective is to ﬁnd one or more solutions that are
feasible and not dominated (according to the preferences) by any other feasible
solution. Such solutions form the Pareto optimal set and identifying the latter is
generally an NP-hard problem [1]. Decision-makers typically aim to establish an
order of outcomes based on preferences. However, in multi-attribute contexts,
the number of potential outcomes forming the Pareto set grows exponentially
with the number of attributes, making it challenging to directly evaluate and
rank all possible preferences.
Addressing such problems for qualitative preferences can be achieved by
extending the CP-net framework, originally designed to manage qualitative preferences [2], to constraints between variables [3]. These additions enable the new
model (called the constrained CP-net or CCP-net) to represent both user preferences and the feasibility of outcomes, making it more eﬀective for solving constrained optimization problems. Backtracking search methods enhanced through
forward checking and full lookahead are commonly employed to determine the
feasible solutions of a CCP-net. These techniques systematically explore the
solution space while eﬃciently pruning infeasible regions based on constraint
propagation. Forward checking maintains domain consistency by pruning invalid
paths as variables are assigned values and backtracking when constraints are violated. The full look-ahead takes a broader approach, examining the impact of
assignments on all future variables to ensure constraint satisfaction. Although
computationally heavier, it oﬀers deeper pruning and greater eﬃciency in solving
constrained satisfaction problems, especially in preference-based reasoning and
optimization [4]. Two methods are used to preferentially compare two results
to determine the Pareto set in a given CCP-net: dominance testing through
ﬂipping sequence and ordering query. The ﬂipping sequence evaluates whether
one solution can be improved step-by-step to match another, while the ordering

Solving Constrained CP-Nets

191

query directly checks the preference order between the two solutions based on
the CP-net’s underlying structure and constraints.
Although backtracking techniques can guarantee the optimality of the
returned Pareto set, they often suﬀer from their exponential time cost. Alternately, metaheuristics [5] can be used as an alterative trading the quality of
the returned solutions for the running time. Metaheuristics eﬃciently search
for optimal or near-optimal solutions in complex optimization problems, balancing exploration and exploitation, and have broad applications across various
domains. Bioinspired algorithms such as Genetic Algorithms (GAs) are metaheuristics that use natural processes such as evolution, genetics, and swarming
to solve optimization problems. GAs are based on natural selection and genetics
and start with a population of chromosome-encoded solutions. A GA improves
solutions through iteration using selection, crossover, and mutation operators,
ensuring the survival of oﬀspring with the best ﬁtness.
We introduce three new GA-based models to ﬁnd Pareto solutions. Additionally, we experimentally compare these bio-inspired methods with those derived
from an exact method, focusing on the number of Pareto solutions identiﬁed and
the runtime performance. By evaluating these two metrics, we aim to assess the
trade-oﬀs between solution quality and computational performance, providing
insights into the applicability and scalability of both methods for solving CCPnets with varying levels of complexity. The results of experiments conducted
on random CCP-net instances generated by the RB model are reported and
discussed.

2

Background

2.1

Constrained Satisfaction Problems (CSPs)

A CSP is a computational problem in which the objective is to ﬁnd values for a
deﬁned set of variables subject to speciﬁed constraints such that all constraints
are satisﬁed. In a CSP, variables represent diﬀerent elements of a problem, and
their values need to be chosen from predeﬁned domains. Constraints deﬁne the
relationships or conditions that must prevail between the values of these variables. CSPs are widely applicable in various ﬁelds, including b scheduling, conﬁguration, and vehicle routing. Solving a CSP can be achieved through various
algorithms and techniques, such as backtracking, constraint propagation, and
local search methods. The complexity of solving CSPs depends on factors like
the number of variables, the structure of the constraints, and the domains of
possible values [4].
2.2

Conditional Preferences Networks (CP-Nets)

A CP-net is a graphical model to represent qualitative preference statements,
such as “I prefer A over B under the condition that X holds true” [3]. A CP-net
relies on the concept of preferential independency, which is based on the ceteris
paribus assumption–meaning “all other things being equal”. This assumption

192

M. Mouhoub et al.

provides a clear framework for interpreting user preferences [6]. A CP-net is
represented as a directed graph. In a CP-net, nodes represent attributes (or
variables) and their possible values (variable domains), whereas arcs reﬂect preference independence across features. A CP-net illustrates a partial ordering of
outcomes in a compact form. Upon eliciting preferences, the decision maker speciﬁes the variables inﬂuencing their preferences for each variable. This interdependency is captured using a directed graph, where the nodes represent variables
and the edges signify that the actual value of the parent node inﬂuences the
preferences of the child node. In a CP net, each variable X has a ceteris paribus
table (CP T (X)) that ranks diﬀerent X values based on the set of parents P a(X).
An outcome is deﬁned as a complete assignment of values to all variables within
their domains. Users typically seek to understand the preferences captured by
the CP-net, with a common query being to determine the most preferred outcome. An outcome oi dominates another outcome oj if oj can be obtained from
oi through a series of worsening ﬂips. A worsening ﬂip occurs when the value
of a variable is changed to a less preferred option, as speciﬁed in the CPT of
the variable [1]. Figure 1 shows a CP-net (graph on the left) with three variables (attributes) and its corresponding induced preference graph (on the right)
showing the ordering relations between outcomes, from the worst (x̄1 x̄2 x3 ) to
the best (x1 x2 x3 ). The CCP-net [3] is an extension of the CP-net by considering
constraints between some variables. Figure 1 illustrates a CCP-net corresponding
to the extension of the CP-net with the new constraint in red. Adding the new
constraints will make the optimal outcome of the undrelying CP-net (x1 x2 x3 )
unfeasible. As a result, there are now two Pareto optimal solutions as indicated
in the induced graph of Fig. 1.

Fig. 1. A CCP-net and its corresponding induced preference graph

2.3

Exact Methods

CSPs are well-known to be NP-hard. To address this complexity in practical
applications, various constraint propagation techniques based on local consistency algorithms have been developed [4, 7]. These techniques focus on enforcing

Solving Constrained CP-Nets

193

local consistency, typically Arc Consistency, within the constraint network both
before and during the search process. By eliminating values that are locally
inconsistent from their respective domains, these methods reduce the search
space and enable the detection of conﬂicts earlier in the search. In the context of backtracking search, two approaches are employed, distinguished by the
scope of their propagation. Forward checking (FC) [7] ensures the arc consistency incrementally during the search. Whenever a variable Xi is assigned a
value, arc consistency is enforced between Xi and any unassigned variable Xj
that is connected to Xi by a constraint c(Xi , Xj ). This process dynamically
removes inconsistent values from the domains of variables linked to Xi , thereby
pruning the search space as assignments are made. Maintaining Arc Consistency
(MAC) [7] expands FC by enforcing Arc Consistency across all unassigned variables, rather than limiting propagation to those directly connected to the most
recently assigned variable. While this broader propagation requires more computational eﬀort, it results in more comprehensive pruning, making it particularly
advantageous in highly constrained problems. Both FC and MAC play a crucial
role in improving the eﬃciency of backtracking search by reducing the search
space and identifying inconsistencies early, ultimately facilitating more eﬀective
solutions to CSPs.
2.4

Identifying Pareto Solutions in CCP-Nets

.
Solving a CCP-net is particularly challenging because the most preferred outcome of th eunderlying CP-net may not always satisfy the given constraints and
we have to look for the next best which cannot be unique (Parto set). A practical
approach to address this issue is to integrate constraint propagation techniques
into the backtracking search process. These techniques identify inconsistencies
earlier in the search, preventing the algorithm from exploring decisions that
would ultimately lead to infeasible outcomes.
The constraint propagation methods used include enforcing Arc Consistency
(AC) prior to search and using Forward Checking (FC) or Maintaining Arc
Consistency (MAC) during the search process [7–9]. AC reduces the search space
by pruning inconsistent values before the search begins, while FC and MAC
ensure dynamic consistency as the search progresses. Importantly, both FC and
MAC preserve all feasible solutions within the search space, ensuring that the
solving method remains complete and does not eliminate any valid outcomes.
A Pareto solution in a multi-objective optimization problem is a feasible outcome that cannot be improved in any objective without sacriﬁcing performance
in at least one other objective. In other words, it is a feasible solution where no
other feasible solution dominates it (N : N |= o1 > o2 , or N |= o2 > o1 ,), meaning no other solution is better in all objectives simultaneously. If there is no way
to prove that o1 is more preferred than o2 or vice versa (i.e., N = o1 > o2 and
N = o2 > o1 ), then both o1 and o2 can be considered Pareto solutions. This is
because neither outcome dominates the other, and both remain non-dominated
within the context of the given total CP-net N .

194

M. Mouhoub et al.

2.5

Flipping Sequence

The ﬂipping sequence is an approach to determining Pareto solutions in a CCPnet by iteratively improving a given outcome until no further improvement is
possible [10]. It starts by comparing two outcomes, o1 and o2 , where the goal
is to determine whether one outcome dominates the other or if both are nondominated. This is achieved by systematically ﬂipping variable values in o2 to
align with o1 , following the preference ordering deﬁned by the CCP-net. The
process begins with the rightmost variable in o2 and evaluates whether ﬂipping
its value, based on the conditional preference table (CPT), brings it closer to o1
without violating constraints. If such a ﬂip is possible, the variable is updated,
and the process proceeds to the next variable toward the left. The ﬂipping continues until either o2 matches o1 , proving that o2 is dominated by o1 , or no further
improvements can be made, indicating that o2 is a non-dominated outcome. If
o2 is non-dominated by o1 , the process is repeated in reverse, where o1 is compared to o2 using the same ﬂipping mechanism. If neither outcome dominates the
other after both comparisons, they are both considered Pareto solutions. This
method systematically explores dominance relationships and ensures that only
non-dominated solutions are included in the Pareto set, providing an eﬃcient
way to handle multi-objective optimization in CCP-nets.

3

Pareto Solutions Based on GAs

The proposed model can solve CCP-nets using GAs. To the best of our knowledge, although there is a study focusing on learning CP-nets using evolutionary
methods, no prior studies have reported on applying metaheuristics for CCPnets. To illustrate the proposed technique, we use the CCP-net depicted in Fig. 2.
The constraints are shown using the incompatible tuples (highlighted in red).
For example, if X0 has a value of X00 then X2 cannot be X21. The CCP-net
has four feasible solutions include two Pareto optimal outcomes.
The proposed score-based model is shown in Fig. 3, with the branch on the
left side illustrated in detail. The ﬁrst step is to create the dependency graph of
CP-net. The dependency relations are inferred from the dependency graph. The
dependency relations are then used to calculate the ﬁtness using the preference
table. The creation of constraint tables is the following stage, which is reliant
on the constraints speciﬁed with the provided CP-net. Chromosome encoding is
deﬁned using the standard GA methodology, applying the two-point crossover,
the random bit-ﬂip mutation, and rank selection. For the latter, chromosomes are
ranked based on their ﬁtness value and the highest-ﬁt chromosomes are chosen
for the next generation. The ﬁtness function, of a given outcome, corresponds to
the sum of both the violated constraints and the number of improving ﬂips to
the optimal outcome.

4

GA-Based Pareto Dominance

The Pareto dominance (population-based) technique consists of storing nondominated solutions so far from the population. The main idea is to save Pareto-

Solving Constrained CP-Nets

195

Fig. 2. Sample CCP-net

optimal or non-dominated solutions found in each iteration. Population-based
approaches are popular in Multi-Objective Optimization (MOO) problems and
have shown successful results. One of the most famous algorithms is the Strength
Pareto Evolutionary Algorithm (SPEA), which focuses on ﬁnding a diverse set
of solutions while maintaining a strong emphasis on Pareto optimality [11, 12].
SPEA provides an ideal balance between exploration and exploitation by including strength-based ﬁtness assignments and keeping an external library of nondominated solutions. Its goal is to identify a wide range of optimum solutions
in several competing objectives. The Population-based approach oﬀers several
notable advantages in MOO and preference-based networks. the approach promotes solution diversity by maintaining a varied set of candidates, which is
essential for eﬀectively addressing multi-objective challenges. Furthermore, GAs
exhibit a reduced tendency to converge prematurely on local optima, thereby
facilitating extensive exploration of the solution space. This approach is also
adept at identifying solutions that closely approximate the true Pareto front. In
addition, its population-based framework enables parallel exploration of multiple
solutions, enhancing its eﬃciency and eﬀectiveness in optimization tasks. For the
CCP-net evaluation, the proposed Pareto-dominance model can be visualized in
the middle of Fig. 3.
The GA-based Pareto dominance starts with a randomly generated population. The ﬂipping sequence technique is then applied to ﬁnd the non-dominated
solutions and remove the dominated ones from the population. The mutation
and crossover operators are the same as for the previous method. For selection,
for each iteration, random weights are applied to the individuals. Among them,
a random number of individuals are selected. The algorithm continues with the
new population that contains the newly generated solutions and the chosen parents.

196

M. Mouhoub et al.

Fig. 3. GA-based Models

5

Pareto Solutions Based on Non-dominated Sorting GA

Non-dominated Sorting Genetic Algorithm II (NSGA-II) [13] is a widely used
MOO algorithm that balances convergence and diversity by dividing solutions
into Pareto fronts and maintaining diversity within each front using a crowding
distance metric, preserving high-quality solutions between generations. The proposed NSGA-II model for CCP-nets is shown in the right branch of Fig. 3. The
method starts with a randomly generated population and has two objectives:
maximize the improvement ﬂip score, denoted as f1 (x) for a given outcome x,
and minimize the number of violated constraints, denoted by f2 (x). The population is sorted into Pareto fronts using non-dominated sorting after the ﬁtness
values are obtained, giving preference to solutions in lower-ranked fronts with
stronger dominance. Equation 1 shows the Pareto dominance notion that states
that outcome A dominates outcome B if it is superior in at least one objective.
A ≺ B ⇐⇒

fi (A) ≤ fi (B)
fi (A) < fi (B)

for all i ∈ {1, 2},
for at least one i ∈ {1, 2}.

(1)

Solving Constrained CP-Nets

197

Solutions within each front are further ranked using a crowding distance
metric for diversity and deﬁned as follows. The crowding distance di for a solution
i in a Pareto front is deﬁned in Eq. 2.
2

di =

fp (i + 1) − fp (i − 1)
fpmax − fpmin
p=1

(2)

where:
– fp (i + 1): Value of the p-th objective for the next solution in the sorted list
– fp (i − 1): Value of the p-th objective for the previous solution in the sorted
list
– fpmax : Maximum value of the p-th objective in the current Pareto front
– fpmin : Minimum value of the p-th objective in the current Pareto front
For boundary solutions: d1 = dN = ∞ where N is the number of solutions on
the Pareto front. To ensure that no good solutions are lost, NSGA-II selects the
best solutions for the next generation by combining the populations of parents
and oﬀspring (2 × population size). The GA operators are then used in the following ways. The crossover and mutation operators followed the same technique
as previous approaches, but the selection operator uses the binary tournament,
where it prefers solutions in lower-ranked fronts and breaks ties using the crowding distance (where solutions with larger distances are preferred). The ﬁnal set
of solutions is further evaluated using the ﬂipping sequence method to ensure
only Pareto solutions are retained.

6

Experimentation

To evaluate the performance of the three proposed GA-based methods (GA
score-based, Pareto-dominance based GA, and NSGA-II), we conducted a set
of experiments using CCP-net instances generated using a variant of the RB
model [9, 14]. We also use an exact method, based on branch-and-bound, for
comparative purposes. In the exact method, we apply constraint propagation
(Arc Consistency, or AC) at two stages: initially, to pre-process the CCP-net
instance by pruning inconsistent values from the domains, and dynamically during the search process through Forward Checking or Maintaining Arc Consistency (MAC) [4, 9]. This dual application of AC ensures that the search space
is continuously reﬁned, preventing the exploration of infeasible solutions and
improving the overall eﬃciency of the search process. Furthermore, to determine
the Pareto solutions, we applied the Flipping Sequence method, which compares
and improves the outcomes based on the conditional preference structure of the
CP-net. This approach ensures that only non-dominated solutions are retained,
accurately identifying the set of Pareto-optimal outcomes while respecting both
the preferences and hard constraints of the problem. The experimental results
present the number of solutions obtained, along with the execution time required

for each algorithm. The CCP-net instances are randomly generated based on the

Table 1 highlights the diﬀerences between the GA-based methods and the
exact method in terms of the number of identiﬁed Pareto solutions and their
respective running times. As the number of variables increases, both the number
of Pareto solutions and the related running time grow signiﬁcantly. For example,
with only two variables, the process quickly ﬁnds a Pareto solution in just 0.01 s.
When the problem size increases to 3 variables, the number of Pareto solutions
doubles to 2, and the running time only slightly increases to 0.01 s. However,
with 4 variables, the number of Pareto solutions jumps to 3, and the running
time increases more dramatically to 0.12 s. For 5 variables, the number of Pareto
solutions explodes to 27, and the running time sharply rises to 492.74 s. Finally,
for 6 variables, the number of Pareto solutions explodes to 41, and the running
time sharply rises to 626.55 s. This trend demonstrates how the combination of
forward-checking and ﬂipping sequence methods, while eﬀective in small problems, faces computational challenges as the number of variables increases. The
increase in both the number of solutions and the time required is due to the
growing complexity of the relationships between the variables, making it harder
to explore all possible solutions eﬃciently. As more variables are introduced,
the search space becomes larger, and the exact method, though accurate, takes
longer to compute the Pareto set. This suggests that while forward-checking and
ﬂipping sequence methods work well for small- to medium-sized problems, they
may need more time or alternative approaches for larger problems where time
eﬃciency becomes a concern.

Solving Constrained CP-Nets

199

GA-based methods ﬁnd more solutions as the population size increases. The
NSGA-II approach runs much faster, with execution times staying very low for
smaller CCP-nets and increasing optimally for larger ones. For example, the
execution time for six variables is 79.95 (the lowest among all) with a population
size of 50 and the quality is around 87.8%, which is acceptable compared to the
other two methods. The Pareto dominance-based method demonstrated superior
solution quality compared to the score-based GA and NSGA-II for 5 variables,
as indicated in Table 1. Although this approach required a longer execution time,
primarily due to the need to evaluate ﬂipping sequences in each iteration. On
the other hand, the execution time of the approximate methods is accelerated
by increasing the population size, however, it can achieve better solution quality.
Compared to the exact method, all three GA-based approaches showed strong
performance. The quality of the solution for smaller datasets (2, 3, and 4) reached
100%, while for larger datasets, it ranged between 85% and 92%. These ﬁndings
underscore the eﬃcacy of approximate methods in solving CCP-nets, demonstrating their potential to handle larger, more complex CCP-net instances. This
shows that while the GA method is much quicker and can ﬁnd many solutions,
the exact method takes more time because the approach checks any possible
solution (with respect to infeasible solutions) to ﬁnd the Pareto solutions. The
GA-based method is faster and works well for larger problems, ﬁnding more
solutions quickly. However, the exact method takes more time but is more precise and ensures that all possible solutions are thoroughly explored. If speed is
important, GA is better, but for accuracy, the exact method is more reliable.

7

Conclusion

We explore exact and GA-based approximation methods to ﬁnd Pareto solutions
of CCP-nets. The results of the experiments we conducted show that while the
proposed GA-based methods are signiﬁcantly faster and more scalable in ﬁnding
Pareto solutions, especially for larger CCP-nets, they may miss some Pareto
solutions due to their heuristic nature. On the other hand, exact methods provide
all the Pareto optimal solutions by thoroughly exploring all possible solutions,
but they suﬀer from their inherited exponential running time. This trade-oﬀ
highlights the strengths and weaknesses of each approach based on the quality
of the returned solutions and the corresponding running time.
In the near future, we will explore other nature-inspired techniques [5,15, 16].
We will also consider variants of the CCP-nets, such as constrained trade-oﬀ CPnets [17,18], Constrained LP-Trees [19], and Constrained Probabilistic CP-nets
[20].

Dependent Task Oﬄoading
in Cloud-Edge-End Environments
with Communication Heterogeneity
Guochen Zhang, Ya Zhao, and Chaoli Sun(B)
Taiyuan University of Science and Technology, Taiyuan 030024, China
s202220110923@stu.tyust.edu.cn, chaoli.sun@tyust.edu.cn

Abstract. With the increasing volume of data and diverse user requirements, the cloud-edge-end architecture must adapt ﬂexibly to address
these challenges. However, many existing studies overlook the complexities of heterogeneous networks and task dependencies, leading to a mismatch between computational models and real-world applications. This
paper addresses these issues by considering heterogeneity, diversity, and
dependencies. We ﬁrst establish a heterogeneous computing environment,
and model the complex task dependencies using a directed acyclic graph
(DAG). We then propose an improved evolutionary algorithm for task
oﬄoading and resource allocation. Experimental results show that our
method enhances resource utilization and overall system performance in
heterogeneous environments.
Keywords: Heterogeneous cloud-edge-end collaborative computing ·
Task oﬄoading · Genetic algorithm

1

Introduction

The rapid growth of the Internet of Things (IoT) has boosted the demand for
data processing, but IoT devices, with limited resources, often struggle to handle
compute-intensive tasks eﬃciently [1]. Cloud computing addresses data explosion
by oﬄoading tasks to powerful remote servers. However, it faces challenges like
high transmission latency, unsuitable for latency-sensitive tasks [2], and network
congestion due to large data transfers [3]. Edge computing has emerged as a
promising solution, bringing computation closer to data sources through edge
servers [4]. To address these limitations, cloud-edge-end collaborative computing
has gained attention, combining the strengths of cloud, edge, and end devices to
optimize performance, reduce latency, and enhance resource utilization.
Task oﬄoading is a key technology in cloud-edge-end computing. Diﬀerent
computing nodes have varying resource allocations, and users face diﬀering levels
of diﬃculty when uploading data to these nodes [5]. Thus, a key challenge in
task oﬄoading research is determining whether, how much, and where to oﬄoad
tasks.
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 201–212, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_15

202

G. Zhang et al.

The task oﬄoading problem has received signiﬁcant attention, but most studies, such as [6,7], focus on independent tasks, with few [8] addressing task dependencies, typically in single-device scenarios. As applications become more complex, task dependencies often span multiple devices. This paper addresses these
gaps by considering diverse user requests in a heterogeneous environment, focusing on oﬄoading dependent tasks and optimizing resource allocation to improve
scheduling eﬃciency.
In summary, We propose a three-tier heterogeneous network that includes
resource-constrained user devices, densely distributed edge servers, and a cloud
server, accounting for variations in computation and communication resources.
Task dependencies are modeled using a DAG, and novel models for tasks, communication, and computation are established to maximize scheduling eﬃciency.
We introduce a hierarchical scheduling strategy: local devices prioritize tasks that
enable rapid initiation of subsequent ones, while edge servers use CPU-demandbased priority scheduling, defaulting to ﬁrst-come-ﬁrst-served when demands
are equal, ensuring both fairness and eﬃciency. Additionally, we propose an
enhanced genetic algorithm to solve this problem. Experimental results show
that our approach outperforms existing methods in cloud-edge-end collaboration scenarios with task dependencies.

2

Related Work

In task oﬄoading research, tasks are typically classiﬁed as independent or dependent. Most studies focus on independent tasks. For example, [9] optimized content caching and task oﬄoading to minimize delay and energy consumption,
while [10] proposed an eﬃcient ﬁreﬂy algorithm for task scheduling in cloudedge computing. Other studies, such as [11–13], also explore independent task
oﬄoading.
Recently, there has been more focus on task dependencies. For instance,
[14] modeled dependent task oﬄoading as a Markov decision process and used
an actor-critic method, while [15] applied graph attention networks and deep
reinforcement learning to solve the problem. Other works [16] and [17] explored
dependent task oﬄoading in vehicle-edge-cloud and mobility contexts. However,
most studies still address dependencies within a single device, overlooking the
inter-dependencies that span across multiple devices in real-world scenarios.
Resource heterogeneity in task scheduling has been a growing focus of
recent research. For example, a group-based multi-task scheduling strategy was
proposed in [18] to optimize task allocation. Another study [19] developed a
heuristic algorithm to eﬃciently schedule tasks on servers with heterogeneous
resources. However, these studies often overlook the heterogeneity of communication resources and the complexity of communication processes in cloud-edgeend environments. To address these limitations, our work not only considers
bandwidth heterogeneity but also takes into account the selection of relay nodes
between end devices and cloud servers.

Dependent Multi-task Oﬄoading

3

System Model

3.1

Network Model

203

As shown in Fig. 1, we propose a cloud-edge-end collaborative computing oﬄoading framework, comprising the cloud server layer, the edge server layer, and
the device layer, with signiﬁcant heterogeneity in resource distribution. The
device set is deﬁned as .D = {1, 2, 3, ..., i..., N }, and the edge server set is
.E = {e1 , e2 , e3 , ..., em , ..., eM }. And there is only one cloud server, denoted as .C.
We assume that .pdi represents the processing power of device .i, while .pe denotes
the computing capacity of each edge server. In contrast, the cloud is assumed to
have nearly unlimited resources, represented by .pc .
3.2

Task Model

Let .Ai = {1, 2..., j, ...Qi } denote the set of tasks for device .i. The total system
task set is deﬁned as .A = {A1 , A2 , ..., AN }, where the number of tasks varies
across devices. Tasks within the same device exhibit dependencies. Additionally,
tasks across diﬀerent devices are also likely to be interdependent [20]. There are
two main types of dependencies: data dependencies, where a task requires the
output of a preceding task as part of its input data, and control dependencies,
where the completion of one task triggers the next. In the latter case, only
minimal task status information is transmitted, so we ignore it. We denote the
dependency type between two tasks as .δkz,ij . When .δkz,ij = 1, it indicates a data
dependency between task .k of device .z and task .j of device .i. When .δkz,ij = 0,
it signiﬁes a control dependency.

between edge servers and the cloud relies on a ﬁxed data transmission rate .R.

bandwidths for faster data transmission, while less powerful ones use lower bandwidths for stability. Ignoring channel interference, the data transmission rate .Ra,b

Computation Model

We deﬁne task .ai,j using the set .{Ii,j , Si,j , Oi,j }, where .Ii,j represents the input
data size for task .ai,j , .Si,j is its computational density (i.e., the number of CPU

variable for task .ai,j is .xi,j ∈ {0, 1, ...M, M + 1}. Speciﬁcally, .xi,j = 0 means the
task is executed locally, .xi,j ∈ {1, 2, ...M } means the task is oﬄoaded to the corresponding edge server, and .xi,j = M +1 means the task is oﬄoaded to the cloud.
The oﬄoading decision for device .i is represented by .Xi = {xi,1 , xi,2 , ..., xi,Qi }
and the system oﬄoading decision vector is .X = {X1 , X2 , ...XN }.

4

Algorithms

Given the complex interdependencies among tasks, ﬁnding an optimal oﬄoading solution is challenging. To address this, we propose an enhanced evolutionary algorithm. Genetic Algorithm is a global search and optimization method
inspired by the principles of biological evolution [22].
We use an integer encoding strategy to encode. The chromosome length is
N
determined by the total number of tasks across all devices, given by . i=1 Qi .
Each task can be executed locally, oﬄoaded to an edge server, or uploaded to
the cloud for execution. Thus, the value range for each gene is [0, M+1].
To generate a high-quality initial population, we propose a hybrid initialization strategy. First, we calculate the average computation time of all tasks.
Then, a portion of the population oﬄoads tasks based on heuristic rules: tasks
above the average cost are sent to the cloud, tasks below half the average remain
on the local device, and the rest are oﬄoaded to the edge server. The remaining
tasks are assigned randomly. Through multiple tests in a scenario with 100 tasks
and 500 dependencies, we found that a heuristic initialization ratio of 0.3, as
shown in Table 1, produces the best performance.
Table 1. Comparison of Diﬀerent Initialization Ratios

We combine (19) and (20) to give the ﬁtness function:
F (X) =

.

1
+ ERUX .
N ormSF TX

(21)

The selection operation selects some excellent individuals from the current
population and passes their genetic information to the next generation [23]. We
use binary tournament selection mechanism. Individuals are randomly paired
and compared based on their ﬁtness values. The individual with the higher ﬁtness
value is chosen as the winner. The crossover operation generates oﬀspring with
new characteristics by exchanging genetic information between individuals. To
ensure that the problem constraints are preserved in the oﬀspring, we use uniform
crossover. Each gene is to be inherited from either parent with equal probability,
thereby increasing population diversity.
We ﬁrst set some key parameters: population size .Np , crossover rate .pc ,
mutation rate .pm , and maximum iterations .GENmax , which guide the search
process. The algorithm begins with a hybrid population initialization. Based
on the constructed DAG, the ﬁtness of each strategy is calculated using the
concept of dynamic sorting. During each iteration, the algorithm selects the best
individuals through binary tournament selection, performs uniform crossover to
generate oﬀspring, and applies mutation to introduce genetic diversity. This
iterative process continues until the maximum number of generations is reached.

6

Conclusion

This paper investigates dependent task oﬄoading in a heterogeneous cloud-edgeend collaborative computing environment. We design a multi-level heterogeneous
network architecture and propose a task oﬄoading model that incorporates complex task dependencies. A hierarchical scheduling strategy is introduced, considering task order and resource requirements, along with an improved genetic
algorithm for optimal scheduling. Experimental results show that our method
outperforms existing approaches across various scenarios.

Foundation of China (No. 62372319) and the Key Research and Development Program
Project of Shanxi Province (No. 202102020101002).

Modelling, Simulation and Complex
Systems

Modeling Trust Dynamics
on a Multi-Agent System
Angelo Cangelosi1 , Carolina Crespi2 , Mario Pavone2(B) ,
and Marta Romeo3
1

University of Manchester, Manchester, UK
angelo.cangelosi@manchester.ac.uk
2
University of Catania, Catania, Italy
carolina.crespi@unict.it, mpavone@dmi.unict.it
3
Heriot-Watt University, Edinburgh, UK
M.Romeo@hw.ac.uk

Abstract. This paper introduces a preliminary agent-based model
designed to simulate trust dynamics within a multi-agent scenario where
virtual agents navigate unknown environments. The objective is to investigate how trust inﬂuences agent performance when they rely on an external source of information, referred to as a robot, versus relying on their
peers. In the model, the robot explores the environment and reports the
costs associated with diﬀerent paths. Agents are categorized as either
trustful or skeptical, deciding whether to trust the information provided
by the robot or rely on their fellow agents. We evaluate three distinct scenarios: No Robot (baseline exploration), Immediate Start (simultaneous
exploration by the robot and agents), and Early Start (robot-initiated
exploration preceding agents’ involvement). Evaluation metrics include
the number of agents successfully reaching the exit, exit times, and path
costs. Our ﬁndings demonstrate signiﬁcant advantages associated with
the presence of the robot, particularly when exploration begins early.
Trustful agents show improved performance by optimizing both the number of successful exits and the eﬃciency of path selection, resulting in
reduced exit times and path costs.
Keywords: Multi-agent Systems · Trust Dynamics · Agent-based
Models · Collective Behaviors · Intelligent Virtual Agents

1

Introduction

Trust and the management of trust are pivotal concepts in establishing and maintaining satisfying relationships. These encompass relationships between humans
and organizations, organizations and organizations, and even between humans
and robots [13]. In general, entities establish a certain degree of trust to collectively achieve better results than what might be attainable individually.
In recent years, the use of robots and automated systems has surged in
a variety of sectors, including the medical ﬁeld [22], mechanical engineering
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 215–226, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_16

216

A. Cangelosi et al.

[10, 16], and Human-Robot Interaction (HRI [1]). This is because robots have
been designed not only to replace humans in dangerous tasks but also to assist
them, make decisions in uncertain scenarios, or work as genuine teammates [13].
Deﬁning trust unequivocally, however, is a complex task due to its inherently
multidisciplinary nature. Psychologically, it is described as something an individual learns positively or negatively from experience, while sociologically, it
is deﬁned as the probability that a certain action corresponds to a beneﬁt [4].
Moreover, trust is dynamic, and identifying the factors inﬂuencing it depends
heavily on the context considered [4].
Despite the diﬃculty of deﬁning trust, in robotics, and in particular, in
Human-Robot Interaction (HRI), the most accepted deﬁnitions in the ﬁeld
include “the attitude that an agent will help achieve an individual’s goals in a situation characterized by uncertainty and vulnerability” [15] and “a belief, held by
the trustor, that the trustee will act in a manner that mitigates the trustor’s risk
in a situation in which the trustor has put its outcomes at risk” [23]. The latter
deﬁnition recognizes two subjects: trustor i, who decides to trust to maximize
their interest, and trustee j, who can impact the trustor’s interest [4].
Numerous studies have investigated the role of trust in HRI, leading to the
development of diverse trust models. In works such as [21], researchers have
aimed to identify the factors inﬂuencing trust in robots, while others, like [19,20],
delve into how trust ﬂuctuates in response to errors committed by robots. The
correlation between trust and performance in human-robot collaboration has
been a focal point, giving rise to performance-based trust models. An example of this is found in [25], where the authors reveal that team trust in robots
positively impacts performance, while trust in humans is more closely tied to
personal satisfaction. Additionally, [26] explores the impact of group identity
and social attraction toward robots on team performance and viability, establishing social attraction as a mediator for the inﬂuence of group identity on
these outcomes. Expanding this perspective, [2] establishes a profound connection between the concept of trust and multi-agent systems (MAS). MAS, computational systems with multiple decision-making agents, involve delegation, tasks,
cooperation, exploitation, exchange, and teamwork, all intricately tied to trust.
Trust emerges as a fundamental concept in MAS, shaping the dynamics of social
interactions in both human and artiﬁcial intelligence ﬁelds [2]. This is because,
in both cases, the dynamics involve autonomous components acting and interacting to achieve goals in uncertain and dynamic environments. Consequently,
trust and its ensuing systems are increasingly conceptualized, designed, and built
using agent-based techniques, advocated as the natural computation model for
such systems [18].
Building upon these foundational deﬁnitions and key concepts, this study
draws inspiration from related works to propose a preliminary agent-based model
designed to explore the dynamics of trust in multi-agent scenarios. This concept
model simulates interactions between agents and an external source of information, referred to as a robot, within an unknown environment. The model consists
of a group of agents navigating a virtual environment to ﬁnd an exit. Within

Modeling Trust Dynamics on a Multi-Agent System

217

this environment, the robot explores and provides information on the costs of
the paths it traverses. Agents aim to exit the environment as quickly and costeﬀectively as possible, using two strategies: trusting the robot’s information or
relying on the behavior of other agents. In this context, the agents act as trustors
i, and the robot serves as the trustee j, with the uncertainty arising from the
exploration of the unknown environment.
The goal is to investigate how trusting an external source of information
(the robot) impacts the agents’ performance in navigating and optimizing their
paths. Although the model is relatively simple, it is intended as a framework for
testing hypotheses about trust dynamics in exploratory settings.

2

The Model

The model is based on the Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) algorithm, a method
ﬁrst proposed by Marco Dorigo in the 1990s [9]. By emulating the foraging behavior of natural ant colonies, where chemical pheromones guide ants to discover
the most eﬃcient routes between their nest and food sources, this algorithm
has demonstrated its eﬃcacy in addressing various combinatorial optimization
challenges, such as scheduling [8], routing [11], coloring [14], robot path planning
[27], transportation [12], and feature selection [17]. Beyond classical applications,
ACO has also been used to study performance variations under structural and
behavioral changes, such as colony partitioning with memory-enhanced strategies [3], or heterogeneous ant roles with constructive or destructive eﬀects [6]. In
this context, we employed the algorithm’s mathematical framework to simulate
not only the environment but also the interactions between agents and between
agents and the environment. This approach has proven eﬀective in modeling
adaptive and dynamic behavior in agent-based simulations, including crowd scenarios characterized by cooperative and non-cooperative interactions [5,7]. The
model was implemented using NetLogo [24], a well-established programmable
modeling environment for multi-agent systems.
The environment is modeled as a weighted, undirected graph G = (V, E, w),
where V is the set of vertices, E ⊆ V × V the set of edges, and w : V × V → R+
assigns a positive cost to each edge, representing the traversal diﬃculty. In each
scenario, N agents start at the top-left node and aim to reach the exit at the
bottom-right.
At a speciﬁc time, t, an agent k on a vertex i selects one of its neighboring
vertices j as its destination. This choice is governed by a probability pkij (t) determined through the proportional transition rule of ACO, as deﬁned in Eq. 1. This
rule mirrors the decision-making process of ants as they navigate paths, considering the concentration of pheromones τij (t) along a route and the visibility
ηij (t) of a node. In our case, we apply the same rule to model how agents decide
on their paths, interpreting pheromones as traces inadvertently left behind by
agents during their movements and visibility as a measure of the desirability
associated with a path.

218

A. Cangelosi et al.

pkij (t) =

⎧
⎨
⎩0

τij (t)α ·ηij (t)β
τil (t)α ·ηil (t)β

l∈J k
i

if j ∈ Jik ,

(1)

otherwise,

k
} are all the possible displacements of the agent k
Here, Jik = Ai \ {πt−1
from vertex i, with Ai = j ∈ V : (i, j) ∈ E adjacent vertices to i, and π k (t) =
(π1 , π2 , . . . , πt ) the sequence of vertices visited by agent k at time-step t; τij (t)
is the trace intensity on edge (i, j), and ηij (t) is the desirability of the edge
(i, j) at a given time t; α and β, instead, are two parameters (user-deﬁned)
that shapes decision-making by weighing two types of information: the trace
τij unintentionally left by agents and the desirability ηij of a path intentionally
communicated by the robot.
The trace τij , weighted by the parameter α, reﬂects how often an edge has
been traversed. After each agent movement, the trace is updated according to
the ACO reinforcement rule: τij (t + 1) = τij (t) + K, where K is a user-deﬁned
constant. In this context, the rule models the interaction between agents and the
environment: agents leave traces as they move, indicating edge usage frequency.
Although these traces have no physical meaning, they provide useful information
about agent behavior and environmental dynamics (much like footprints revealing both the paths taken and how often they’ve been used). The desirability ηij ,
weighted by the parameter β, represents the information on path cost provided
by the robot.
The robot’s path exploration is guided by its eﬃciency re ∈ [0, 1], which
reﬂects its ability to identify and prefer lower-cost paths. A value of re = 1.0
leads the robot always to choose the least costly paths, while re = 0.0 results
in selection of the most costly ones. Intermediate values represent probabilities;
for example, with re = 0.8, there’s an 80% chance of choosing a cheaper path.
This parameter captures variability in the robot’s performance, reﬂecting realworld uncertainties. After exploring, the robot shares the desirability ηij of the
path based on perceived cost, helping agents reﬁne their navigation strategies.
Both agents and the robot expend time proportionate to the cost of traversing
an edge. Consequently, an optimal path not only minimizes expenses but also
necessitates less time for traversal.
Agents can adopt two distinct strategies to ﬁnd the exit. In the trustful (T)
strategy, agents tend to rely on the robot’s path cost information ηij over the
traces τT left by peers. This is modeled with αT = 0.5 and βT = 1.0, giving
priority to the robot’s guidance. Conversely, skeptical (S) agents disregard the
robot’s information, relying instead on peer traces τS , modeled with α = 0.5 and
β = 0.0, thus favoring socially acquired cues over external input.
The proposed model simulates trust dynamics in a multi-agent setting toward
a virtual robot and examines their impact on overall group performance. Agent
behavior is assessed using three key metrics: number of exited agents, exit times,
and path costs. These metrics help clarify how interactions between agents and
the robot inﬂuence collective outcomes. By exploring trust, skepticism, and

Modeling Trust Dynamics on a Multi-Agent System

219

decision-making, the model oﬀers a comprehensive framework for understanding
the role of robotics in multi-agent navigation through unfamiliar environments.

3

Experiments

The experiments used three distinct weighted graphs, with edge weights drawn
uniformly from the range ]0, 10]. Each graph had |V | = 400 nodes, and the
number of edges was determined by a user-deﬁned parameter called Probability
Connectivity, ranging from 0 (no links) to 1 (each node connected to its four
nearest neighbors). Intermediate values control the overall graph density and
topology. The three graphs, C1, C2, and C3, were generated with Probability
Connectivity values of 0.45, 0.50, and 0.55, resulting in |E| = 511, 561, and 599
edges, respectively. C1 represents the simplest scenario with sparser connections.
Despite its lower edge count, it still posed meaningful navigational challenges.
C2 increases edge density and introduces more variability, oﬀering a moderately
complex environment. C3, the most intricate scenario, features dense connections and greater variability in node links, requiring more adaptive navigation
strategies. The increased density and variability in connections in C3 presented a
greater challenge, necessitating adaptation to a more intricate network structure
and navigation through a wider array of possible interactions. In summary, the
deliberate construction of C1, C2, and C3 environments aimed to provide various
challenges, progressing from simpler to more intricate scenarios. This intentional
variation facilitated a comprehensive assessment of the agent’s adaptability and
performance across diﬀerent levels of environmental complexity.
The simulations involved NT = 50 trustful (T) and NS = 50 skeptical
(S) agents. Each agent released a trace amount speciﬁc to its kind, denoted
as KT,S = 0.1, with an initial value set at τT,S (t = 0) = 0.1. On the other hand,
the robot released information ηij = w1ij after crossing a path, where wij is the
cost of the crossed path.
For the experiments, diﬀerent kinds of situations were considered. In the No
Robot (NR) scenario, agents independently explore the environment, serving as
a baseline for comparison. In the Immediate Start (IS) scenario, the robot starts
the exploration simultaneously with the agents. Finally, in the Early Start (ES)
scenario, the robot begins the exploration before the agents.
Due to computational constraints, all agents have limited time to ﬁnd the
exit, with the duration calculated diﬀerently based on the investigated situation.
For the No Robot and Immediate Start scenarios, the time limit is set as Tmax =
|V |× N2 . Conversely, in the Early Start scenario, it is determined as Tmax = |V |×
N
N
2 + Rt , where Rt is deﬁned as |V | × 2 , representing the time that the robot has
to explore the environment before agents begin their exploration. This approach
ensures equal exploration time for agents across all three conﬁgurations.
To evaluate the inﬂuence of the robot’s presence, various experiments were
undertaken to investigate the correlation between agent performance and both
the type of analysis and the eﬃciency of the robot. In this study, we speciﬁcally
focused on two distinct values of robot eﬃciency: re = 0.2 and re = 0.8. It’s

220

A. Cangelosi et al.

important to note that the robot’s path selection is probabilistic, with the likelihood of choosing less costly paths increasing as its eﬃciency rises and vice versa.
The deliberate choice of these eﬃciency values, 0.2 representing low eﬃciency
and 0.8 representing high eﬃciency, was made to steer clear of extreme values,
preventing the depiction of overly speciﬁc or unrealistic scenarios. Instead, we
opted for values at the limits, striking a balance to ensure a thorough evaluation without overly complicating the experimental design, while maintaining the
representation of plausible real-world situations. Each of these eﬃciency levels
underwent 100 independent simulations to provide a comprehensive evaluation
of their impact.

4

Results and Discussion

This section presents the simulation results and their implications across diﬀerent
scenarios. The analysis focuses on three key metrics: the number of exited agents
(Fig. 1), exit times (Fig. 2), and path costs (Fig. 3).
These graphs are thoughtfully grouped to improve clarity and facilitate comparisons across diﬀerent scenarios. Each plot consists of ﬁve columns: No robot,
Immediate start with robot eﬃciency re = 0.2, Immediate start with robot eﬃciency re = 0.8, Early start with robot eﬃciency re = 0.2, and Early start with
robot eﬃciency re = 0.8.
At ﬁrst look, it appears that in all three scenarios, both trustful and skeptical
agents exit in nearly equal numbers, exhibiting comparable exit times and path
costs in the absence of a robot. However, a notable shift occurs when introducing
a robot, particularly with an early start and a robot eﬃciency value of 0.8,
especially evident in scenario C3. To validate the statistical signiﬁcance of these
observations and aﬃrm the impact of a robot’s presence, t-tests were conducted
on exit times and path costs. For clarity, graphs 1c, 2c, and 3c marked with (*)
indicate statistically signiﬁcant results as determined by the t-test.
Tables 1 and 2 represent the statistical analysis respectively for the path costs
and the exit times in scenarios C1, C2, and C3. The statistical measures include
t-values (t), degrees of freedom (df), p-values, and 95% conﬁdence intervals (CI).
Considering the path costs, results show a signiﬁcant impact from the presence of the robot, with very low p-values and wide conﬁdence intervals excluding
zero. In contrast, scenarios without the robot show minimal eﬀects, with higher
p-values and narrower conﬁdence intervals including zero. This indicates similar behavior between trustful and skeptical agents in the absence of the robot,
supporting the null hypothesis.
The analysis of exit times reveals scenario-dependent trends. In scenario C1,
the robot with an immediate start has a strong negative eﬀect, while an early
start at 0.8 reduces this impact. In C2, immediate start conditions also worsen
outcomes, while early starts have milder eﬀects. In C3, immediate starts significantly increase exit times, whereas both early start 0.2 and 0.8 improve them.
In summary, introducing a robot with diﬀerent starting modes leads to clear
improvements in exit times, especially in scenario C3, where timeframes are signiﬁcantly reduced. Diﬀerences between Skeptical and Trustful agents become

Figure 1 represents the number of exited agents across all scenarios. It can
be observed that the presence of a robot with an immediate start does not seem
to signiﬁcantly improve agent exit rates, especially in scenarios C1 and C2. This
suggests that in such scenarios, trusting the robot may not be advantageous. In
C3, this holds true only when the robot eﬃciency is 0.2. When the eﬃciency
rises to 0.8, there is a noticeable improvement in the performance of trustful
agents. This trend becomes more pronounced in the case of an early start with
robot eﬃciency re = 0.8. Trustful agents signiﬁcantly outperform skeptical ones
reaching the maximum number of exited agents.
When considering exit times (Fig. 2), an immediate start with robot eﬃciency
values of 0.2 and 0.8 does not lead to signiﬁcant improvements across the three
scenarios. This suggests that, under immediate start conditions, even higher
robot eﬃciency has little eﬀect on agent performance, especially for trustful
agents. In contrast, with an early start, trustful agents improve their exit times
for both eﬃciency values, while skeptical agents perform worse. Notably, in sce-

223

Fig. 3. Path cost for scenarios C1 (a), C2 (b), and C3 (c).

nario C3, trustful agents consistently exit faster than skeptical ones, regardless
of robot eﬃciency.
For the path costs (Fig. 3), the presence of a robot signiﬁcantly improves
trustful agents’ outcomes across all scenarios and conﬁgurations. Skeptical
agents, instead, show unchanged or even worsened performance with immediate starts, but improve under early starts, though still performing worse than
trustful ones.
Looking at all three evaluation metrics together, trustful and skeptical agents
perform similarly without the robot, with comparable exit times and path costs.
The model performs best in scenario C3 with an early start and 0.8 robot eﬃciency, where trustful agents achieve optimal outcomes.
The immediate start conﬁguration revealed disparities between exit times and
path costs, which can be attributed to the inherent eﬃciency-driven decisionmaking process of the robot and its initiation timing. The robot prioritizes path
cost optimization and navigation eﬃciency, indirectly inﬂuencing exit times. The
eﬃciency parameter, denoted by values of 0.2 and 0.8, guides the robot’s path
selection strategy. A higher eﬃciency setting consistently results in signiﬁcantly
lower path costs, especially for trustful agents, aligning with the expectation
of cost-eﬀective choices. However, the impact on exit times unfolds diﬀerently.
Immediate starts, despite enhancing path costs, adversely aﬀect exit times, particularly for trustful agents. This suggests that the immediate initiation of a more
eﬃcient robot may not necessarily expedite the exit process. On the contrary,

5

Conclusions

This study introduced a preliminary agent-based model to explore trust dynamics in multi-agent scenarios. Agents navigated an unknown environment to ﬁnd
an exit, and the model examined how the presence of an external source of information (named robot) inﬂuenced their performance in terms of exit times and
path costs.
We analyzed three main scenarios: No Robot, where agents acted independently; Immediate Start, where the robot and agents explored simultaneously;
and Early Start, where the robot began earlier. Without the robot, trustful and
skeptical agents performed similarly, though higher exit rates were observed in
more complex environments. When the robot was introduced, outcomes varied
with its eﬃciency. With low eﬃciency (0.2), trustful agents underperformed in
some cases, whereas high eﬃciency (0.8) notably improved their performance,
particularly when the robot started earlier. Overall, robot presence, especially
with high eﬃciency and early start mode, led to faster exits and better path
selection. Statistical analysis (t-tests) conﬁrmed that the robot had a signiﬁcant
eﬀect on outcomes, particularly on path costs.
These results suggest that trust in an external source of information can
be beneﬁcial in uncertain scenarios, provided that the source is reliable. Robot
eﬃciency proved to be a critical factor, inﬂuencing not only whether agents
exited successfully, but also how quickly and through which paths.
However, some limitations must be acknowledged. The model is intentionally simple, and the range of robot eﬃciency values tested was limited. Moreover,
agents’ trust levels were ﬁxed and did not adapt to the robot’s behavior. Future

Modeling Trust Dynamics on a Multi-Agent System

225

developments will aim to expand the model by including a wider range of eﬃciency levels and more dynamic, adaptive trust mechanisms.
