Knowledge Management Methodology
for Complex Context. The Defence Case
Ioana Filipas1(B) , François Marmier2 , Punita Raj2 , Bertrand Rose2 ,
Valentin Drouillard3 , and Déborah Lejuste3
1 CNRS BETA, Université de Strasbourg, 67000 Strasbourg, France

filipas@unistra.fr

2 CNRS ICUBE, Université de Strasbourg, 67000 Strasbourg, France
3 ARMTEK, 204 avenue Colmar, 67100 Strasbourg, France

Abstract. This paper identifies and presents accelerated and automated AI solutions for major knowledge management problems in complex systems, especially
in defense. From the interviews and surveys conducted on defense personnel and
using defending-against-knowledge-loss triangulation, the study points to the following enduring issues: paperwork, siloed knowledge, security measures, and
knowledge obsolescence due to employee turnover. The paper uses thematic analysis using the MAXQDA qualitative data analysis to elucidate the identified inefficiencies as barriers to decision-making and collaborative work that threaten organizational outcomes. In response to these issues, the study seeks to develop a
model offering new solutions for the mechanised adoption of knowledge management processes, including knowledge capture, distribution, and enhancement
of interoperability to support structured knowledge sharing. This model incorporates AI functionalities to automate processes and minimise paperwork to reduce
decision-making latency and advance cross-departmental knowledge flow with
the added advantage of accessing real-time information while conforming to the
security requirements through operational access privileges.
Keywords: Knowledge management · Complex systems · System engineering ·
Artificial intelligence · Defense

1 Introduction
1.1 A Subsection Sample
Knowledge Management (KM) is an important part of complex systems like defense.
Defense organizations are well equipped to optimize the handling of large volumes of
information for operational preparedness, decision support, and mission accomplishment
[1]. Nevertheless, there are limitations in how knowledge is managed across departments
and organizational units. These sectors involve rapidly proliferating technologies that
get intertwined with the ever-increasing need for coordinating and integrating diverse
activities and teams [1].
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 227–238, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_17

228

I. Filipas et al.

To overcome such knowledge management challenges, many defense sectors tried
to implement tools, methods, and even software applications to address and solve the
upcoming information flow and efficiency [2]. In the last ten years, different ERP systems, document management, and business process management software solutions
helped to integrate data and various processes within organizations [3]. However, commercial solutions have not always proved satisfactory in highly specialised and secure
domains of defense. These systems are generally ill-suited for addressing the requirements of the defense marketplace for secure and timely access to information [4]. To
address these challenges, there are various new-generation IT solutions like Artificial
Intelligence, Machine Learning, and Predictive Analytics. These technologies solve
the problem of fragmentation of knowledge, manual data entry data, and loss of tacit
knowledge.
However, to achieve optimum KM results in the defense sector, there is a lack of
research regarding the identification of KM systems that can meet defense needs along
with a satisfactory model that would incorporate AI automation capabilities with the
dynamic and security corresponding to defense work [5]. Hence, the paper develops an
artificial intelligence-based KM solution i-CDPA (Content Driven Process Automation).
The i-CDPA model provides possible solutions for storing and transferring the organizational knowledge converted from tacit knowledge to preserve efficient knowledge and
experience.
This paper begins with a detailed literature review to critically discuss different KM
models and technologies. Further, the paper outlines the methodological approach to
KM problems in organizations with a bias on interviews and surveys. The results section
of the paper provides an overview of the findings of this study and provides a correlation
between the main KM issues and the solution developed in this research through hypotheses. The discussion section gives a clear elaboration of the i-CDPA model to explain
how it fits the problem of the current KM practices. Finally, the conclusion describes the
findings and contributions of the study and outlines further research prospects in using
AI solutions for managing knowledge in the defense sector.

2 Literature Review
2.1 Knowledge Management in Defense
Knowledge Management (KM) is an important managerial initiative in organizations,
particularly in defense. It is an operational necessity, a strategic priority, and required
to maintain a comparative advantage over a potential foe [4]. The evolution of KM can
be dated back to the realisation of intellectual capital in the early 90s. New tendencies
in KM best practices emphasize the change of approach from tangible asset management to intangible ones, including intellectual property, expertise of employees, and
corporate culture [5]. The knowledge management practices are divided into two forms;
the explicit and the tacit. While explicit knowledge is codified and can be documented,
shared and stored, tacit knowledge is context-specific, personal, and can hardly be written down. In numerous defense organizations, tacit knowledge is lodged in personnel,
thereby, it dissipates when people retire from their positions or split from the military.
Although the application of KM is critically essential in defense, many organizations

Knowledge Management Methodology for Complex Context

229

face a challenge when applying it [6]. Moreover, an internal trend visible in KM is a
shift from traditional to digital KM tools and a growing ubiquity of artificial intelligence
[5]. Explicit management of knowledge as a strategic corporate resource is protected by
reliable security measures, numerical codes, encrypted information transfer, and storage
in databases and other IT systems.
2.2 Challenges in KM Implementation in Defense
The general KM environment in defense organizations faces many difficulties and challenges to the success of KM activities. Some of the greatest challenges that have contributed to the slow realization of KM practices include; cultural resistance, technology,
organizations, and security. The main cultural barrier encountered in the implementation of KM initiatives is the hierarchical organizational culture and the resulting folder
structure that fosters secrecy [5]. Organizational constraints include a lack of up-todate systems to support the dissemination of knowledge in real-time, or the inability of
the new systems to converge with the latest technologies in the market such as AI and
machine learning [6]. To overcome these challenges, defense organizations need to look
for a way to transfer knowledge within the organization as well as with external partners,
and to store it safely, using electronic databases and knowledge management systems
with the support of artificial intelligence.
2.3 KM Theories and Models with Their Strengths and Weaknesses
Knowledge management (KM) theories and models provide frameworks for organizations to conceptualise, implement, and optimise their knowledge-handling processes.
These models focus on creating, capturing, sharing, and applying knowledge, particularly
in complex organizational environments like defense [7]. Nonaka and Takeuchi’s SECI
Model is an influential model that emphasises the dynamic interaction between explicit
and tacit knowledge through four key processes: Socialization, Externalization, Combination, and Internalization (SECI) [8]. It is effective in illustrating how tacit knowledge
can be converted into explicit knowledge and vice versa, which is particularly relevant in
defense [9]. However, it lacks detailed practical application steps and underemphasizes
the importance of knowledge storage, protection, or retrieval. Karl Wiig’s KM Model
focuses on making knowledge useful in organizations by defining KM as a process of
building, holding, pooling, and using knowledge. It is practical and outcome-oriented
but does not provide specific steps for implementing KM practices. Boisot’s Knowledge
Asset Model focuses on managing knowledge as a strategic asset, presenting the I-Space
framework [6]. The Knowledge Spiral (Ba) Model is grounded in the concept of “Ba,” a
shared space for knowledge creation. Hedlund’s N-form Model encourages knowledge
sharing across silos and hierarchical barriers, but its implementation can be challenging
in large, rigid organizations like defense. Davenport and Prusak’s Knowledge-Based
Organization emphasises the importance of culture, human behaviour, and technology
in managing knowledge [10].

230

I. Filipas et al.

2.4 Research Gaps
AI has been used for KM in other industry sectors but in the defense sector a completely
automated model that can take care of the security aspect while making knowledge
available at the same time has not been developed fully [9]. The goal is to enhance KM
effectiveness and process in informational environments, to develop shared reference
ontology for strategic sectors. The situation will put unstructured data in a form that
will allow their usage by AI. Currently offered KM solutions are either overbearing or
only partially embedded or not equipped for dealing with the specific challenges that
inhibit defense operations [6]. Furthermore, there is a need to explore how AI-based
KM approaches offer interoperability among the different defense ecosystems along
with quantum computing in addition to the prescriptive security standards that need to
be followed.

3 Hypotheses Development
The hypotheses of this study are developed based on the main issues highlighted in the
literature on KM in complex contexts, especially in defense. All the hypotheses aim
at solving a different, although related issue highlighted in the research literature. H1:
Knowledge fragmentation and siloed systems hinder the successful flow of information,
leading to inadequacies. Studies in knowledge management continued to reveal the negative impact of fragmented systems, getting knowledge, and locked inside silos. Lack of
knowledge integration means the required knowledge is not available to the teams in time
causing delays in decision making and an increase in operation costs. In defense, these
silos can transform into critical challenges during dynamic and high-risk settings that
necessarily rely on information sharing for success. Drawing from this perspective, we
posit our hypothesis on the proposition that enhancing knowledge sharing means reducing such barriers within departments and between different organizational teams. H2:
Manual processes in knowledge dissemination and storage delay decision-making, and
inject mistakes in complex environments. Another commonly cited problem is the failure
of organizations to transform their KM into fully automated processes. The literature suggests that human-initiated systems of knowledge transfer including document reviews,
bulk emails, and particularly handovers can be fraught with errors and time-consuming.
This delay is very expensive, especially in such areas as defense where timely information defines or decides on the fate of sensitive operations. This hypothesis emerged from
research on the inevitability of the digitisation of KM practices. H3: The lack of structured knowledge transfer mechanisms leads to the erosion of critical tacit knowledge,
especially during personnel turnover. The literature on knowledge transfer—knowledge
that by its nature cannot be easily written down, but is important for organizational performance—highlights the need for formal systems of knowledge transfer. The problem
for defense organizations and similar complex organizations is that they often have the
same problem in retaining this form of knowledge when the employees who possess it
move on. This hypothesis is founded on the understanding that the implementation of
structured knowledge transfer mechanisms is essential for the retention of organizational
knowledge and for the continuity of knowledge particularly in this cognisance-reliant
industry of defense. H4: AI-driven KM solutions can automate knowledge processes,

Knowledge Management Methodology for Complex Context

231

improve real-time access, and enhance operational efficiency, but must address security
concerns. Artificial intelligence has been conceptualised and discussed in the present
literature as a revolutionary system for managing knowledge in complex industries. AI
technology can minimize many of the bottlenecks in the knowledge management chain,
making information available on-demand. Nevertheless, the inclusion of AI into KM
systems also brings along new security challenges. H5: Ontology-based systems will
improve interoperability among knowledge management platforms. As the challenges
in knowledge management challenges evolve to higher levels, the need for KM systems
that can interoperate effectively rises. The problem of structuring knowledge to increase
its breadth and depth of understanding and compatibility is noted in the literature as
addressed by ontology-based systems. This hypothesis is underpinned by the understanding that attaining better interoperability through the use of ontology solves a lot of
the current problem areas present in KM systems that are disparate and isolated from
one another.

4 Research Methodology
The research approach used in this study is aimed at identifying and responding to
knowledge management issues in multifaceted defence environments. This research is
thus structured into hypothesis formulation, exploration, and valorization which are
characterized by the following objectives and activities.
4.1 Process 1 - Hypothesis Formulation
The first step of the current research remains the development of the theoretical framework. First, an analysis of the literature was used to launch the research. This review
unveiled critical knowledge deficit related to the application of KM, especially in defence
environments with high degrees of system integration and data sensitivity. The purpose
was to define where ineffective knowledge communication and sharing processes, as well
as system integration take place. As a result of the knowledge gaps defined in the study,
it postulated the hypotheses relate to some of the problems that arise in organizations
(Fig. 1).
4.2 Process 2 - Exploration
The first phase is the exploration phase, which basically combines the process of data collection and data analysis in order to establish and prove some key challenges. To test the
theoretical ideas and research questions regarding real-life problems related to defense
knowledge management, a survey questionnaire was developed, specific to defense personnels. From the total of 100 participants, the respondents gave detailed description of
some key aspects of 312 km difficulties in the respondents’ organizations with reference
to sharing, security and integration. The survey and interview responses were coded,
categorized and analyzed systematically with the help of MAXQDA qualitative data
analysis. This process facilitated the categorization of repeat issues thereby arriving at

232

I. Filipas et al.

a list of 25 distinct challenges in defence KM. The combination of survey/interview
results enabled to indicate theoretical and practical issues.

Fig. 1. Conceptual Framework of Knowledge Management: from its design to the valorisation

4.3 Process 3 - Valorization
The last step of the research is related to ideas implementation for problems that have been
proposed and calculating the expected return. Regarding the categorized problems, it was
possible to indicate the short-term and long-term expectations. The short-term goals are
primarily centered on the achievement of short-term benefits in terms of knowledge
sharing and process efficiencies, the long-term goals, on the other hand, encompass
consistent knowledge retention and the ability of systems to integrate with those used in
other departments. Each solution is assessed on its abilities to be useful within defence
organizations.

5 Results
The results section of this paper synthesises the findings with hypothesis from the
interviews and surveys collected from the defense personnel.
5.1 Knowledge Fragmentation and Siloed Systems (H1)
Knowledge storage is one of the important components of knowledge management. It
takes far too long to secure the necessary approvals within the multiple different and discrete systems available in defense organizations to find out what knowledge is out there.
Respondents indicated that over 75% of knowledge is stored in disparate and disconnected data stores with very limited content sharing and data integration across systems

Knowledge Management Methodology for Complex Context

233

and only sometimes, the personnel have to enter queries into separate data sources to find
the information they need. Discrete general work activities are usually dispersed over
the organizational databases for maintenance, logistics, as well as operations planning
and execution – “We work with definitions that are not fixed, not definitive. And indeed,
there are cases where we are going to document a system that has been abandoned.” The
end-users feel that they constantly have to switch between multiple databases, request
data access to other departments, or work with separate spreadsheets containing data
acquired from multiple databases. This lack of compatibility is not only a problem with
operational tasks but also limits communication and cooperation between teams. The
fragmentation problem is made worse by the fact that there is no overarching knowledge management system through which all the available information sources can be
combined. The respondents demand efficient access to the data disseminated in different
sections of the company without the need to search for it manually. Due to fragmentation, system-of-record becomes the source of institutionalized detail where systems
hold older or different versions of the same information. This makes the workflow of the
systems complex and confusing and the possibility of making a mistake because some
of the information being used in the process may be outdated is very high.
5.2 Manual Processes and Duplication of Efforts (H2)
The data from the interviews showed that paper-based approaches constitute a major
problem for knowledge management in even the most diverse defense organizations
especially when dealing with the codification process, data input and distribution of
pertinent knowledge. Most of the respondents pointed out that a great deal of the intake
process is done through the entry of data by hand, a process that takes a lot of time and
also can produce a lot of errors – “The connection between the technicians and the head
of maintenance management is still done the old way, using papers and things like that.”.
They probably use manual systems and as is known with these systems, there is usually a
lot of duplication as this leads to inconsistencies and slows progress. The interviews also
revealed that the proliferation of the systems led to duplication of work in that information
had to be entered and re-entered on different systems. Such manual duplication also
takes significant time of staff and resources, while holding back critical processes and
lowering organizational efficiency. For instance, in environments where instant decisions
are needed, for example in defense operations, such rebounds result in operation logjams
and mission failures. Whistles occur in integrations also, which causes significant issues
and poor operational performance. This manual process of managing knowledge also
restricts the participation of others, because the time taken to exchange information in this
manner discourages the teams from being collaborative. The data also acknowledged
how the cultures of organizations create dilemmas in their efforts to shift from the
conventional use of manual systems. As shown above, the respondents acknowledged
the importance of developing an integrated knowledge capture and distribution system
though these cultural barriers need to be overcome.

234

I. Filipas et al.

5.3 Loss of Tacit Knowledge and Weak Knowledge Transfer Mechanisms (H3)
The data revealed that inexperienced employees are deprived of formal educational training to fill gaps in their knowledge and become familiar with important tacit knowledge
as key personnel turnover within an organization – “There is a double flow of information
for failure reports. The first is sent to SIMAT (EMAT code, plate, etc.) and the second is
sent by email to provide details (photo, video, etc.). This creates a waste of time and a
lack of traceability. We don’t even have a notification on the CRDs.”. Conclusions made
from the analysis of interviews include but are not limited to considerations regarding
inadequate or absent knowledge transfer practices as the cause of tacit knowledge leakage in defense organizations. It is vital to keep an organization running efficiently and
one of the most vital forms of knowledge is implicit, which means the knowledge of
the person with practical experience in a given field of work. However, several participants argued that such knowledge is not usually documented or passed to successors
when employees quit or retire thereby the organization incurs a massive loss of vital
Information in system knowledge. Of the respondents, 51% said that being devoid of
well-developed knowledge acquisition processes poses a critical threat to sustainability.
Current business archive systems are constructed to record only documented information in business organizations which include manuals, technical writing and procedural
writing opposed to implicit information, which is the expertise that personnel gain in
the organizations over the years. However, they frequently fail to use the information in
practical situations since they lack the context that comes along with such experience.
Many respondents acknowledged that there is a reluctance among some senior personnel
to share their knowledge, either because they do not see the value in formalizing their
experiences or because they feel that newer employees should “figure things out” on
their own. This reluctance contributes to the difficulty of transferring tacit knowledge
and perpetuates knowledge gaps.
5.4 AI-Driven KM Solutions and Operational Efficiency (H4)
Implementing material through AI-driven solutions can facilitate knowledge processes,
increase real-time access, and increase operational effectiveness while imposing security
risks. From the interviews carried out, there was a lot of support for AI KM solutions in
the defense organizations especially, in the areas of automation of manual procedures and
access to information in real-time. More than three-quarters of those surveyed said that
AI could help to decrease the proportion of tasks that remained in the realm of manual
document processing – which is generally time-consuming and prone to mistakes – “It
is frustrating because the person wants to save time by saying to themselves, instead of
reading 40 documents that will take me a month, you continue to amplify the problem.”
Other significant recognitions with AI systems comprise the improvement of real-time
access to knowledge, which is a significant requirement in a defense environment where
decisions are often required based on the most up-to-date obtainable knowledge. For
instance, by using AI it would be feasible to process Big Data on logistics, condition of
equipment, and operation reports and give the decision makers an insight which would
otherwise take considerable time to prepare by analysis of several statistics. However,

Knowledge Management Methodology for Complex Context

235

it was understood that there were authorities and risks related to the capabilities of AIdriven KM systems in defense organizations – “It is not necessarily difficult, but what is
especially good is to make the diagnosis. Having tools to diagnose or even having the
intellectual procedures (Often, it is like flowcharts.) to diagnose on different materials.”
In this case, about 50% of the responders noted that they are uncomfortable with AI
systems being able to access sensitive and classified information. Another emerging
issue was building and maintaining trust in AI decision-making. Many respondents
mentioned the need for a hybrid model where the machines and artificial intelligence
manage the bulk of data processing, storing, and retrieval.
5.5 Ontology-Based Systems and Interoperability (H5)
Knowledge management systems enhance compatibility when combined with ontologybased systems. Interview data analysis showed that the majority of respondents had
concerns about the need for enhancing the interfaces between KM systems used in
defense organizations. Once again, participants posted challenges when encountering
several separated systems that are incapable of effectively sharing their data, which is
stored in completely dissimilar forms and classifications, rendering it challenging for
departments to share and apply expertise. As one responded, “We have a database with
docs, but it’s really a mess. We often spend more time looking for the doc with the right
information.” This challenge is compounded by the fact that different teams are likely
to be using their systems or databases or even where there is one common database it is
likely to be fragmented and inconsistent. A large number of the respondents believed that
by defining how knowledge has to be categorized and managed at an organizational level,
ontology-based systems could hold the key to the problem. Ontology-based systems call
for common language and categorisations and can increase data readability and accuracy
across the departments to a great extent as they form a base understanding of essential
terms and their linkages that may differ from one department to the other. According to
the respondents, what they realised was that whenever they needed to obtain information
that would help in decision-making, they had to use a variety of systems, all of which
had different structures, and different categorisation schemes.

6 Discussion
The study highlights the need to have a central database with a KM system to integrate
information from different departments, to address operational dysfunction, complications, and successful knowledge management [11]. More than 70% of the respondents
said that security measures delay access to information. This means there is a need to
come up with a more flexible approach towards security to offer an enabling environment for the sharing of knowledge and information while at the same time avoiding
compromise of sensitive data. There is also another identified issue of tacit knowledge
degeneration because of the lack of transfer tools; more than 50% of employees interviewed claimed that as experienced staff members leave, the essential expertise and
working knowledge of their organization frequently disappears.

236

I. Filipas et al.

Fig. 2. AI-Automated Knowledge Management Model for Defense Ecosystem

As demonstrated in this study, there is a dire need to develop a new KM model that
would respond to the challenges outlined above. In light of the major difficulties that
defense organizations experience in the management of knowledge, the paper introduces
the Intelligent Content Driven Process Automation (i-CDPA) model as a new paradigm.
The i-CDPA model, based on the use of highly developed automation and knowledge
management, provides a conceptual solution to address these urgent challenges. The
CDPA is split into two main stages. In the first Content Driven Phase, learners focus more
on knowledge acquisition and knowledge construction. Engineers design, arrange, and
verify technical products, processes, schemes, substructures, documents, and handicrafts
in this phase (Fig. 2). The content is segmented, identified, and interconnected. In the
second Process Automation Phase, the real-time, and interactive exploitation of the
data is made possible automatically. The working data is gathered, assignments are
performed, and results are evaluated contemporarily. Thus, this integration of AI helps
in content quality to ensure the information being collected is well-refined and contextrelevant. It defines what kind of information will be provided to users depending on their
constraints of operation and capability. The i-CDPA model focuses on the objective of
integration with other defense systems to enable fast and effective information sharing.
This eliminates unnecessary intermediary steps which must otherwise be followed to
achieve the same goal and enhance effectiveness. The model also emphasizes detailed
responses, that is, member-level solutions for each user to meet specific contexts and
improve decision-making and problem-solving functions.
The proposed i-CDPA model makes the effective control and coordination of complicated defense systems possible and easy throughout the systems’ life cycles. The model’s
implementation involves several interrelated phases. These phases interactively respond
to the various KM issues that arise at the ecosystem level. The first hypothesis phase
identifies knowledge silos, knowledge fragmentation, and inadequacy in the defense
ecosystem. The second exploration phase explores knowledge gaps and bottlenecks in
the ecosystem that include severed modules, manual data entry, and low traceability.

Knowledge Management Methodology for Complex Context

237

In this phase, AI-driven tools are used to analyze hefty datasets and spot inadequacies in prevailing procedures. The next conception phase emphasises formulating the
AI-augmented Knowledge Management System to mechanise knowledge storing, capturing, processing, and retrieving through AI tools and technologies. This phase solves
the issues of low teamwork, resistance to change, and a growing multiplicity of tasks
without strong accountability. The prototype phase is developed to automate and include
various developments across the defense ecosystem through automated data entry, realtime knowledge accessibility, and enhanced traceability. The proficiency of this phase
is tested by the experimentation phase to report the identified barriers and gaps, such as
reducing errors, rationalizing catalogue updates, and improving the accuracy of documentation. It is rigorously observed and advanced through continuous feedback to ensure
its capacity to solve and manage the dynamic and intricate requirements of the defense
ecosystem. In the next Scaling phases, the model ensures it can handle the cumulative amount of knowledge while preserving real-time access and traceability across the
entire defense organization. In the Valorisation phase, the process is designed to ensure
the system remains flexible to future deviations in complication and brings cost-effective,
coarse, and justified solutions to KM challenges.

7 Conclusion
This study discusses critical challenges to KM within the defense sector including knowledge fragmentation, manual processes, security and knowledge retention/tacit knowledge. The i-CDPA model also mitigates data security breaches like unauthorized access,
data leaks, AI bias and manipulation, cyber-attacks through operational access privileges, encryption, and monitoring. The research underlined the need for the effective
implementation of the KM model and strategies based on AI, which integrate all tools
and back up various processes, providing facile decision-making based on up-to-date
information. Hence, the study formulates an operational knowledge management model
reinforced by AI to enable the delivery of granular responses capable of controlling the
cost of growing complexity within defense ecosystems. The proposed model fits the
desired environment and meets the requirements of defense organizations. Concerning
the objectives of the conceptual model, it is suggested that by integrating the knowledge
systems, automating the manual process, and formalizing the tacit knowledge transfer,
the overall effectiveness of the KM practices will be enhanced with the security requirements. Nevertheless, AI integration in KM can pose threats like AI bias problems, and
data security breaches; however, the model provides reliable, secure, and non-partisan
KM for defense work.

Exhaustive Search for Quantum Circuit
Optimization Using ZX Calculus
Tobias Fischbach(B) , Pierre Talbot , and Pascal Bouvry
Department of Computer Science, University of Luxembourg, Esch-sur-Alzette,
Luxembourg
{tobias.fischbach,pierre.talbot,pascal.bouvry}@uni.lu

Abstract. Quantum computers allow a near-exponential speed-up for
speciﬁc applications when compared to classical computers. Despite
recent advances in the hardware of quantum computers, their practical usage is still severely limited due to a restricted number of available
physical qubits and quantum gates, short coherence time, and high error
rates. This paper lays the foundation towards a metric independent approach to quantum circuit optimization based on exhaustive search algorithms. This work uses depth-ﬁrst search and iterative deepening depthﬁrst search. We rely on ZX calculus to represent and optimize quantum
circuits through the minimization of a given metric (e.g. the T-gate and
edge count). ZX calculus formally guarantees that the semantics of the
original circuit is preserved. As ZX calculus is a non-terminating rewriting system, we utilise a novel set of pruning rules to ensure termination
while still obtaining high-quality solutions. We provide the ﬁrst formalization of quantum circuit optimization using ZX calculus and exhaustive
search. We extensively benchmark our approach on 100 standard quantum circuits. Finally, our implementation is integrated in the well-known
libraries PyZX and Qiskit as a compiler pass to ensure applicability of
our results.
Keywords: Quantum Circuit Optimization · ZX Calculus ·
Exhaustive Search

1

Introduction

Quantum computers allow a near-exponential speed-up for speciﬁc applications
when compared to classical computers. Typical examples that are beneﬁtted by
quantum computing include the simulation of quantum systems, solving combinatorial problems, performing machine learning and breaking cryptography [23].
However, current quantum computers lack the resources to address complex
real-world problems. These restrictions concern the number of available physical
qubits and quantum gates, high error rates, and a short coherence time. Quantum error correction aims to mitigate these challenges at the cost of a higher
resource demand [9].
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 239–253, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_18

240

T. Fischbach et al.

Similarly to classical computing, quantum circuits describe quantum programs within the quantum gate model. These circuits are independent of an
underlying architecture and allow universal computation [10]. Users typically
use Cliﬀord gates with the T-gate as the chosen universal gate set because it
can be eﬃciently simulated on classical computers [1]. However, diﬀerent types
of gates require varying amounts of resources, with the T-gate requiring more
physical qubits and error correction code than Cliﬀord gates to be implemented
in a quantum device [19].
Inherent limitations in quantum architecture are the number of available physical qubits and quantum gates, as well as a short coherence time.
These limitations can be addressed by architecture-dependent optimization that
improves the mapping of a quantum circuit onto a speciﬁc quantum hardware.
Architecture-dependent quantum circuit optimization can be treated as a classical optimization problem that can be solved exactly [41]. Other methods include
heuristics and deep learning [20, 30].
In this paper, we target architecture-independent optimization which aims to
simplify a quantum circuit by reducing general and common limitation factors
across architectures such as the number of quantum gates, logical qubits and
the circuit depth. Despite the existence of inﬁnite universal gate sets, common
methods employ gate commutation rules [26] and circuit simpliﬁcation [35] for
frequently used universal gate sets. These heuristic approaches suﬀer from several
drawbacks. First, it is necessary to prove that each new simpliﬁcation rule is
correct, to make sure that the semantics of the original circuit is preserved.
Second, there is no guarantee of optimality, even for small circuits. And ﬁnally,
the heuristics are tailored to optimize one particular objective and must be
redesigned when the requirements change. To tackle these challenges, several
optimization methods based on ZX calculus (Sect. 2) recently emerged [15,38].
ZX calculus is a universal, compact and complete rewriting system [11,13].
An object in ZX calculus is depicted graphically and called a ZX diagram. Quantum circuits and ZX diagrams both represent a linear map between qubits. ZX
calculus is universal because every quantum circuit can be converted to a ZX
diagram. It is complete because applying any rule preserves semantics, which
means that the linear map of qubits remains unchanged [3,4,27]. Finally, ZX
calculus is compact because it consists only of two generators and eight rules. A
ZX diagram can be converted back to a quantum circuit, which is a non-trivial
process known as the circuit extraction problem [6].
However, some characteristics of ZX calculus make the design of an optimization algorithm challenging:
– High memory requirements: Real-world quantum circuits result in large
ZX diagrams with high memory requirements for every state.
– Non-terminating: Inﬁnite rewriting sequences exist.
– Failed states: The extraction of ZX diagrams to quantum circuit might fail
(or be prohibitively long) with current algorithms. Checking if a diagram is
extractable is time consuming and prevents us from exploring a large number
of nodes.

Exhaustive Search for Quantum Circuit Optimization Using ZX Calculus

241

In light of these challenges, we contribute a proof of concept to quantum circuit optimization by employing iterative deepening depth-first search (IDDFS)
to systematically explore the rewritten ZX diagrams. IDDFS is a well-known
state-space search strategy, which is simple, memory eﬃcient and provides a
good trade-oﬀ between exploration and exploitation [33]. Our approach is general in the sense that the same search strategy can be employed to optimize
diﬀerent metrics. A metric can be deﬁned on the basis of characteristics of the
ZX diagram or its corresponding quantum circuit. In particular, we aim to ﬁnd
a ZX diagram that minimizes the T-gate count due to its high impact on the
practicability of current quantum architectures. Furthermore, we demonstrate
the metric independence of our approach by optimizing the edge count of the
ZX diagram. In sum, the contributions of this paper are as follows:
1. A formal description of ZX diagram optimization and the ﬁrst state-space
search algorithm applied to ZX diagram optimization (Sect. 3).
2. Proof of concept implementation that is extensively benchmarked on 100
standard quantum circuits that is equating the state-of-the-art full reduce
algorithm on 89% of the circuits within 1.5 h. (Sect. 4.1).
3. A PyZX and Qiskit based transpiler pass to support the practical adoption
of our results.

2

Preliminaries

2.1

ZX Calculus

ZX calculus, introduced by Coecke and Duncan [12,13] in 2008, is a universal framework for diagrammatic reasoning between linear maps of qubits. It
provides a complete set of sound semantic-preserving rewriting rules, even for
arbitrary real phases [3,4, 27,28]. Completeness signiﬁes that the linear map of a
ZX diagram remains unchanged after modiﬁcations through a rewriting rule. ZX
calculus is universal and allows users to represent any quantum circuit as a ZX
diagram. The elementary building blocks of quantum circuits are quantum gates
and wires. Analogously, the elementary building blocks in ZX calculus—called
generators—are spiders, wires, swap and Bell states.
A spider is a tensor which operates on qubits in either the Z-basis {|0 , |1
(green) or X-basis
, |+ (red). Spiders possess n inputs, m outputs and
carry a phase α. The linear map of a spider for phase α = 0 results in the
identity matrix m1l n and therefore acts as a wire. Spiders with phases that are
2 ×2

multiples of π2 can implement all Cliﬀord gates. The T-gate corresponds to a
Z-spider with a phase of π4 . Cliﬀord gates and the T-gate form a universal gate
set together.

Wires connect the outputs of one spider with the inputs of other spiders. The
identity matrix 1l implements the linear map of wires.

242

T. Fischbach et al.

Fig. 1. The basic rewriting rules of ZX calculus.

A yellow box connected by wires indicates a Hadamard generator. Euler
decomposition, known as the Hadamard rule (hd), splits a Hadamard generator
into a sequence of Z and X spiders [16].
The swap generator swaps the spiders on a wire and implements the identical
linear map as the swap gate in the quantum circuit notation.
In ZX calculus, bent wires depict the Bell state and the Bell eﬀect and are
known as cup and cap.

A typical ZX diagram consists of many connected spiders and Hadamard generators. Matrix multiplication composes the linear map of sequentially connected
spiders. The tensor product composes the linear map between non-sequential
connected spiders and Hadamards, meaning that generators are parallel to each
other.
Only topology matters is an important concept in ZX calculus. It states that
the linear map between qubits of a ZX diagram remains unchanged as long as
its connectivity stays the same. As a consequence, bending wires (e.g. cups and
caps) and moving spiders do not change the ZX diagram [13].
2.2

Rewriting Rules

This section introduces the basic rewriting rules of ZX calculus that are outlined in Fig. 1 [13, 40]. All rules remain valid under colour inversion. We give an
example of the application of successive rewriting rules, explained below, on a
simple ZX diagram in Fig. 2.
Spider Fusion (f ). Connected spiders of the same colour fuse through modulo2π addition of their phases. The reverse unfusing operation is always possible,
because connecting additional spiders with a phase of α = 0 will not change the
modulo-2π addition. As a consequence, inﬁnite spiders can be unfused. Figure 2

Exhaustive Search for Quantum Circuit Optimization Using ZX Calculus

243

highlights the fusion of two green non-phase-carrying spiders with their neighbouring phase-carrying spiders.
Local Complementation (lc). The local complementation rule [34] originates
from graph theory. For all directly connected spiders of a target spider, local
complementation connects previously unconnected spiders and disconnects previously connected spiders. Local complementation of the highlighted red spider in
the bottom qubit row is illustrated in Fig. 2. The two green spiders connected to
the highlighted red spider connect via local complementation. Performing a second local complementation at the same red spider would disconnect the two
green phase-carrying spiders again. Pivoting describes a series of local complementations.
Colour Change (h). Adding Hadamard generators to each input and output
inverts the colour of a spider. In Fig. 2, all red spiders turn green with the
addition of Hadamard generators.
Identity Removal (i1, i2). Non-phase-carrying spiders that are directly connected to other spiders function as wires and leave the linear map of qubits
unchanged. The identity matrix m1l n represents the linear map of such spi2 ×2

ders. A single wire replaces a phaseless spider with n = 1 and m = 1. Similarly,
two directly connected Hadamard generators cancel each other out and act as a
wire. Applying the identity removal rule on the fused diagram in Fig. 2 removes
all non-phase-carrying spiders that possess one input and one output. Furthermore, identity rules are used to convert a ZX diagram to be graph-like (see
Deﬁnition 1) by ensuring that spiders always connect with each other through
Hadamard generators and by the addition of potentially missing spiders at the
input and output.
Bialgebra (b). The bialgebra rule originates from the algebraic commutation
relation between the copy and the or gate. It allows connected and oppositecoloured spiders to move through each other at the cost of potentially adding
spiders.
Copy ( π, c). π copying moves an input spider that carries the phase α = π
through an opposite coloured spider to all connected wires while multiplying the
phase by −1. If the input spider does not have any input wire (n = 0) and the
phase is a multiple of π, the opposite coloured spider vanishes. This second rule
is referred to as the state copying, because it copies the computational basis
through an opposite-coloured spider.
2.3

ZX-Based Circuit Optimization

Recent advances in quantum circuit optimization combine the ZX calculus with
algorithmic, heuristic or deep learning approaches [15,38,42]. PyZX is a popular
Python library to work with ZX calculus and supports state-of-the-art circuit
optimization with a focus on T-gate reduction [31,32]. To simplify ZX diagrams
using basic rewriting rules, PyZX assumes that the ZX diagrams are graph-like.

244

T. Fischbach et al.

Fig. 2. Successive applications of rewriting rules to a simple ZX diagram (to be read
from left to right and top to bottom).

Definition 1. Graph-like ZX diagrams are only composed of Z spiders (green)
that are connected by Hadamard wires. Input/Output possesses at most one wire
that can only connect to one spider.
The ﬁnal diagram in Fig. 2 is graph-like. Every ZX diagram can be converted
to be graph-like using the h-rule and the identity rules i1 and i2.
Reducing the number of T-gates in a given quantum circuit is crucial because
implementing the required quantum error correction on quantum hardware
demands signiﬁcantly more resources compared to Cliﬀord gates [9,14].
The full reduce algorithm is the main optimization algorithm of PyZX. It
aims to decrease the number of T-gates of a given graph-like ZX diagram by
targeting spiders that carry phase a multiple of π2 and π [15,32].
After optimizing a ZX diagram, the corresponding quantum circuit needs to
be extracted. While converting a quantum circuit into ZX diagram is a straightforward process, the opposite is #P-hard (at least as hard as NP-complete problems) and is known as the circuit extraction problem [6]. Polynomial time algorithms exist for ZX diagrams that are graph-like and preserve the generalized
ﬂow [5]. The disadvantage of current circuit extraction algorithms is that the connectivity of spiders in the ZX representation is replicated by two-qubit gates in
the resulting quantum circuit.
Recent advances optimize two-qubit gates by reducing the number of edges
in a given ZX diagram. Staudacher et al. [40] proposed a heuristic that calculates
the cost based on the number of edges after rule application and uses a greedy
or stochastic algorithm to select the next rule.
This paper focuses on ZX diagram optimization and not on the circuit extraction problem. Therefore, the standard PyZX extraction algorithm is used for all
experiments [15].

3

ZX Diagram Optimization

Let ZX be the inﬁnite set of all ﬁnite ZX diagrams, QC the set of quantum
circuits and LM the set of linear maps of qubits. We have two functions α :
QC → ZX and extract : ZX → QC ∪ {⊥} that convert a quantum circuit into a
ZX diagram and conversely. The function extract can map to a special element

Exhaustive Search for Quantum Circuit Optimization Using ZX Calculus

245

⊥ when it fails to extract a quantum circuit from a diagram. Additionally, we
have a function γ : QC → LM that maps a quantum circuit to its linear map
of qubits.
A quantum circuit optimization algorithm is a function f : QC → QC that
optimizes some properties of the quantum circuit. We say that f is semanticpreserving whenever, for all q ∈ QC, we have γ(q) = γ(f (q)).
Let R = {h, b, lc, f, i1, i2, π, c, hd} be the set of rewriting rules presented in
Sect. 2.2. A ZX rewriting rule r ∈ R is a function ZX → ZX such that the
function extract ◦ r ◦ α is semantic-preserving when the extraction succeeds.
A ZX-based quantum circuit optimization algorithm is searching for an
extractable ZX diagram that optimizes one or more properties of the quantum circuit. Let q ∈ QC be a quantum circuit and opt : ZX → Z ∪ {⊥} be the
optimization function mapping a ZX diagram to a metric (e.g. the number of
T-gates, edges or two-qubit gates). The function opt can map the special element
⊥ when it fails to compute a metric from a ZX diagram. Without loss of generality, we consider that we aim at minimizing opt. The ZX state-space of q is a
set W ⊆ ZX such that w ∈ W if there exists a ﬁnite sequence of rewriting rules
r1 , . . . , rn such that w = (rn ◦. . .◦r1 ◦α)(q). The set of solutions S ⊆ W are all the
extractable ZX diagrams in W , that is, ∀w ∈ W , extract(w) = ⊥ ⇔ w ∈ S. The
set of optimal solutions is the largest set O ⊆ S such that for all o ∈ O, s ∈ S,
we have opt(o) ≤ opt(s).
There are challenges pertaining to the exploration of the ZX state-space.
Firstly, real-world quantum circuits result in large ZX diagrams with a high
memory demand for every state. Secondly, the state-space to explore is inﬁnite
because the ZX rules rewriting system is non-terminating, e.g. unfusing phaseless
spiders and colour changing is always possible. Thirdly, to ﬁnd a solution we must
extract a circuit, which is a computationally expensive operation that may fail
if the general ﬂow is not preserved [5]. Although it is possible to optimize a
metric completely deﬁned on the ZX diagram (e.g. number of T-gates, vertices
and edges), other metrics of interest (e.g. number of two-qubits gates, circuit
depth and overall gate count) are deﬁned on the extracted circuit. Even though
we focus in our experiments on the T-gate and edge count, our approach is
general and can be reused for any of those metrics.
In this paper, we rely on iterative deepening depth-ﬁrst search (IDDFS),
which is a simple and eﬃcient optimization algorithm, to tackle these challenges [33]. More advanced optimization algorithms are diﬃcult to use in the
context of ZX optimization. For example, constraint-based combinatorial optimization such as linear programming and constraint programming require explicitly encoding the rewriting rules as constraints and do not usually support
unbounded rewriting sequences out of the box. Dynamic programming is an
interesting approach to avoid re-exploring the same state multiple times, but it
requires to store a prohibitively high number of states. We would need to ﬁnd a
more compact representation of states, which we leave for future work.
An interesting aspect of IDDFS is to strike the right balance between exploration and exploitation. It is based on depth-ﬁrst search (DFS) which is a back-

246

T. Fischbach et al.

tracking algorithm applying rules in sequence until it ﬁnds a leaf node, at which
point it backtracks to the previous decision made. The problem with DFS is that
wrong decisions taken early in the search tree condemn the search strategy to
explore large uninteresting subtrees. In the worst-case scenario, DFS reaches a
deep leaf node with a state that is unextractable. Instead, IDDFS applies DFS
with a depth bound, exploring successive search trees of increasing depth. It is
especially useful when we do not have a good heuristic to select the next node
to explore, which is the case here, since it is hard to predict which rules might
lead to a better solution. Another advantage of IDDFS is to use as much memory as DFS while exploring the search tree in breadth as well. Note that for
completeness, we experiment with DFS when evaluating our approach.
A leaf node is a ZX diagram d ∈ ZX such that one of these conditions is
true:
– For all rules r ∈ R \ {h} we have r(d) = d, that is, no rule besides colour
change is able to rewrite the diagram.
– One of the pruning conditions is true.
When we reach a leaf node, we test whether opt(d) is better than the previously
found solution, if any. In case of improvement, we check whether the ZX diagram
is extractable or not using extract(d) = ⊥. If it is extractable, we save the ZX
diagram as the current best solution.
Pruning conditions reduce the search tree eﬀectively and ensure that the
search terminates in ﬁnite time. The pruning conditions used throughout this
paper are the following:
– No spider unfusion. It is always possible to separate one spider into multiple
spiders as long as the mod π sum of all involved spiders remains unchanged.
– Rule bundling. If a rule can rewrite a given ZX diagram more than once, all
possible modiﬁcations are performed in a batch, hence generating only one
node in the search tree.
– No colour cycle. Disallowing consecutive colour changing rule application
avoids inﬁnite paths that consist only of recolouring spiders.
– Global time limit. After a set time limit is exceeded, the search is terminated
and the best solution found so far is returned.

4

Computational Experiments

The proposed DFS and IDDFS approaches are implemented in Python and evaluated against PyZX’s implementation of full reduce using a diverse set of quantum circuits [15, 37].
Although the search algorithms employed are fairly simple and well-known,
their combination with ZX-calculus and the pruning condition implemented in
a reproducible and integrated framework is not straightforward. A tight integration with PyZX and Qiskit, as well as the contribution of a Qiskit transpiler

Exhaustive Search for Quantum Circuit Optimization Using ZX Calculus

247

pass, ensures the reusability of our approach. The overall project is 7000 lines of
code.
Every search instance was executed on a Xeon Gold 6132 clocked at 2.6 GHz
and 64 GB of 2400 MHz DDR4 of available RAM running Rocky Linux 8.10 with
Python 3.12.
We evaluated the performance of the various algorithms on the complete set
of 100 standard quantum circuits using the pruning conditions introduced in
Sect. 3. A global timeout of 1.5 h is set for every instance. The rules are ordered
such that a change connectivity (e.g. pivoting and local complementation) takes
priority over spider count reduction (e.g. fusion and identity removal). Upon
completion of an instance, the optimized quantum circuit can be fed into a
transpilation pipeline for further processing. In a ﬁrst set of experiments, we
minimized the T-gate count of ZX diagrams. To demonstrate the generality of
our approach, a second set of experiments that minimizes the edge count of ZX
diagrams is executed. The full results for every instance, including a comparison
with full-reduce and the algorithm runtimes, are available in the supplementary
material.
4.1

T-Gate Reduction

Fig. 3. (a) Time evolution against the solution of full reduce. (b) Time evolution of
the best solution found. (Color ﬁgure online)

The DFS search exhibits poorer performance, only equating full reduce in
46% of the instances, compared to IDDFS search. IDDFS equates full reduce in
89% of the instances. Neither DFS nor IDDFS approaches are able to outperform
full reduce within the 1.5 h time limit. Nevertheless, it should be noted that
the DFS search is able to equate full reduce on three circuits on which IDDFS
leads to poorer results.

248

T. Fischbach et al.

Figure 3a shows the time evolution of the best solution of DFS (red) and
IDDFS (blue). As neither DFS nor IDDFS outperform full reduce, only solutions
that equate full reduce are shown. DFS almost immediately equals full reduce in
41% of the instances and only equating full reduce on additional 5% of the
instances for the remaining 1.5 h. In contrast, IDDFS requires 16 min to level
the performance of DFS and equals full reduce in 80% of the instances within the
ﬁrst 60 min. Overall, IDDFS equals full reduce on 89% of the instances within
the 1.5 h time limit.
4.2

Edge Reduction

Figure 3b visualizes the time evolution of the best solution of DFS (red) and
IDDFS (blue). Within the 1.5 h time limit, IDDFS is able to ﬁnd the best solution in 86% of the instances.
DFS results only in the best solution exclusively in 1% of the instances and
shares the best solution in 31% of the instances. IDDFS improves the performance of DFS and leads to an exclusively best solution for 54% of the instances
and shares its best solution in 32% of the instances.
It should be noted, that despite being designed to reduce the T-gate count,
full reduce achieves the exclusively best solution on 13% of the instances and
shares a best solution in an additional 2% of the instances.
Compared to the unoptimized quantum circuit, DFS improves the edge count
on average by 11%. IDDFS exhibits better performance and reduces the edge
count by 22% within the 1.5 h time limit. Full reduce improves the edge count
by 3%. We optimized the edge count to demonstrate the applicability of our
algorithm to other metrics. Furthermore, optimizing the edge count is interesting because current state-of-the-art circuit extraction algorithms replicate the
connectivity of spiders with two-qubit gates, therefore potentially increasing the
circuit depth and two-qubit gate count.
Our results are more contrasted as reducing the edge count did not necessarily
translate in a reduction of the of two-qubit gate count. On the contrary, more
two-qubit gates are added for the vast majority of instances. DFS results in a
higher two-qubit gate count in 41% of the instances and only reduces the twoqubit gate count in 1% of the instances. IDDFS yields to poorer performance
and adds two-qubit gates in 85% of the instances and reduces the two-qubit
gate count in 1% of the instances. translates in a reduction of the two-qubit gate
count. Staudacher et al. [40] showed that they were able to translate an average
reduction in the edge count by 23% in a reduction of the two-qubit gate count by
16%.

5

Related Work

Recent advances have been made to optimize the T-gate count in quantum circuits. Fault tolerant quantum computing introduces quantum error correction

Exhaustive Search for Quantum Circuit Optimization Using ZX Calculus

249

code that increases the resource demand, especially for the T-gate. Improvements in the T-gate count that leverages the quantum error code were achieved
with Matroid partitioning [2]. Template based techniques improve the quantum
circuit synthesis by reducing the T-gate count and circuit depth [8].
The ﬁrst proposed optimization strategy using ZX calculus is restricted to
Cliﬀord gates [18]. The state-of-the-art optimization algorithm full reduce targets Cliﬀord and T-gates [15, 32]. Other techniques optimize the T-gate count
through the treatment of Cliﬀord gates and Pauli operators as π4 rotations
around each other [43]. Additionally, new causal ﬂow preserving optimization
techniques ensure the extractability of a quantum circuit from a ZX diagram [24].
An improved T-gate count for arithmetic circuits, e.g. integer multiplication,
was found by applying the ZX rewriting rules [29]. Reinforcement learning strategies based on ZX calculus that target the T-gate and two-qubit gate count
emerged in recent years [38, 39]. Heuristics that target the two-qubit counts
ensures the usefulness of ZX calculus for photonic quantum computing and other
quantum hardware that does not perform error correction [40]. Other approaches
combine heuristics and ZX calculus for the architecture-aware optimization of
quantum circuits [21,42].
Heuristic approaches deal with the time complexity involved in quantum
circuit optimization. In principle, a heuristic pattern matching algorithm is
combined with gate commutation rules to minimize the total gate count [25].
Boolean satisﬁability is an exact approach for the optimization of classical circuits. Despite the challenging encoding of quantum gates, advances have been
made to bring this approach to quantum circuit optimization [7,36]. Recently,
reinforcement learning techniques emerged for quantum circuit optimization and
mapping of quantum circuits for speciﬁc quantum architectures [17,20]. Gate
commutation rules and templates proved also advantageous for the mapping of
quantum circuits [26].

6

Conclusion

This paper lays the foundation to apply exhaustive search to ZX diagrams for the
optimization of quantum circuits. The combination of the semantics-preserving
rewriting rule of ZX calculus with the exhaustive search algorithms depth-first
search (DFS) and iterative deepening depth-first search (IDDFS) enables to target metrics of the ZX diagram or its corresponding quantum circuit without
being designed for one speciﬁc metric.
Our results indicate that IDDFS is a more eﬀective approach for ZX diagram
optimization than DFS. Within the 1.5 h time limit IDDFS is able to equate
state-of-the-art algorithms that reduce the T-gate count in 89% of the instances
and competes with novel approaches that reduce the edge count, demonstrating
the applicability of our approach.
A Qiskit compiler pass that implements the DFS and IDDFS approach, with
conﬁgurable pruning conditions and integration with the PyZX library, is available on GitLab (https://gitlab.com/NetForceExplorer/zx_dfs/-/releases/v1.0OLA_2025).

250

T. Fischbach et al.

Our results demonstrate that not every reduction in the edge count translates into a reduction in the two-qubit gate count. Upcoming work could focus
on the enhancement of the edge count metric to better approximate the twoqubit gate count after circuit extraction. Future eﬀorts should address the
scalability issue for large circuits of the IDDFS and DFS based optimization.
The principal idea of ZX diagram optimization is to change the connectivity
and the fusion of spiders, hence a limited discrepancy search could improve
the performance [22]. The application of dynamic programming techniques
could trade computational performance for higher memory requirements. Finally,
bridging the gap from architecture-independent optimization towards quantum
architecture-aware optimization could address the execution of real-world quantum circuits on next-generation hardware.

Almost Linear Time Consistent Mode
Estimation and Quick Shift Clustering
Sajjad Hashemian(B)
University of Tehran, Tehran, Iran
sajjadhashemian@ut.ac.ir

Abstract. In this paper, we propose a method for density-based clustering in high-dimensional spaces that combines Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH) with the Quick Shift algorithm. The Quick Shift algorithm,
known for its hierarchical clustering capabilities, is extended by integrating approximate Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) using LSH to provide
eﬃcient density estimates. The proposed approach achieves almost linear time complexity while preserving the consistency of density-based
clustering.
Keywords: Density Based Clustering · Locality Sensitive Hashing ·
Quick Shift Clustering · Mode Estimation

1

Introduction

Density-based clustering algorithms are fundamental tools in data analysis due
to their ability to identify clusters of arbitrary shapes. The most popular densitybased clustering method is DBSCAN [8,18], which deﬁnes clusters based on the
concept of “density-reachability”. Mean Shift [3, 4,13] is another density-based
clustering algorithm that moves each point to the densest area in its vicinity,
based on kernel density estimation which is computationally challenging due to
its iterative nature and the need for density estimation, making it much less
scalable than DBSCAN.
To overcome this issue, Quick Shift [14, 19] generalizes Mean Shift by constructing a hierarchical clustering tree on density estimates. However, these
methods are computationally expensive, making them less scalable for large
datasets.
Jiang [14] established the consistency of the Quick Shift algorithm. This result
allows the consistency analysis for various density estimators, including k-nearest
neighbor [5]. Esfandiari et al. [7] demonstrated the use of certain types of LSH
for fast density estimation, enabling eﬃcient density-based clustering. Jang and
Jiang [12] introduced DBSCAN++, a modiﬁcation of DBSCAN that computes
densities for a subset of points, reducing computational cost while maintaining
performance. Xu and Pham [20] proposed sDBSCAN, a scalable density-based
clustering algorithm in high dimensions using random projections. These works
highlight the importance of eﬃcient density estimation for scalable clustering.
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 254–266, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_19

Fast Mode Estimation and Clustering

255

Building upon these, we utilize hashing-based kernel density estimators [2]
[1] to develop a fast and consistent mode estimator and extend the Quick Shift
algorithm as a result, achieving almost linear time complexity while preserving
consistency.

2

Preliminaries

Throughout this paper X (n) = {x1 , x2 , . . . , xn } ⊂ Rd denotes a dataset of n
points. We assume that the data points are drawn i.i.d. from a probability distribution F with density f supported on a compact set X ⊂ Rd .
2.1

Locality Sensitive Hashing

The Near Neighbor Search (NNS) problem is a fundamental problem in data
science and computational geometry. Given a dataset X, the goal is to preprocess
the data such that, given a query point q in the supported set, we can eﬃciently
return a point p ∈ X near to it, or report that no such point exists.
Classically known time-eﬃcient data structures for exact NNS require space
exponential in the dimension d, which is prohibitively expensive for highdimensional datasets. To address this, the (c, r)-Approximate Near Neighbor
Search problem ((c, r)-ANN) was introduced.
Deﬁnition 1 ((k, c, r)-ANNS). Given dataset X, distance threshold r > 0,
and approximation factor c > 1, the goal is to return k points p1 , p2 , . . . , pk ∈ X
such that dX (q, pi ) ≤ cr for each i, given a query point q with the promise that
there are at least k points in X within distance r of q.
Approximate Near Neighbor Search allows for eﬃcient data structures with
query time sublinear in n and polynomial dependence on d. A classic technique
for solving (c, r)-ANN is Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH), introduced by
Indyk and Motwani [11]. The main idea behind LSH is to use random space
partitions such that pairs of points within distance r are more likely to be hashed
to the same bucket than pairs of points at a distance greater than cr.
Proposition 1 (Optimal LSH for (c, r)-ANNS [17]). For the Euclidean
metric 2 and any ﬁxed r > 0, LSH yields data structures for solving (k, c, r)ANNS with space O(n1+ρ + dn) and query time O(dnρ ), where ρ = c12 − o(1).
2.2

Kernel Density Estimation

Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) is a widely used method in non-parametric
statistics for estimating the probability density function of a dataset. Given a
set of n points X ⊂ Rd sampled from an unknown distribution F , the goal is to
estimate the density at an arbitrary point x ∈ Rd .

a point x ∈ Rd is deﬁned as:

3

Algorithm

In this section, we present our proposed variant of QuickShift algorithm,
which achieves eﬃcient clustering with almost linear time complexity and space
complexity using approximate KDE, making it suitable for large-scale highdimensional datasets.
Theorem 1 (Computational Complexity). Providing a dataset X ⊂ Rd ,
there exist an algorithm (Algorithm 3) that performs density based clustering in
time and space O(dn1+o(1) ).
Proof. The time and space complexity of the LSH-QuickShift algorithm is determined by LSH Preprocessing, KDE, and graph construction steps.
Constructing the hash tables for Locality-Sensitive Hashing requires
O(n1+ρ + dn) time and space, where n is the number of data points, d is the
dimensionality, and ρ = c12 − o(1) for the given approximation factor using the
optimal LSH using Proposition 1.
For each point xi , the approximate kernel density estimate f˜(xi ) is computed
using the LSH-KDE oracle, with the total time of Õ(n) as Proposition 2.
Finally, the algorithm iterates over all xi ∈ X and add a directed edge to a
point with higher estimated density in G. We can do this eﬃciently by keeping
the maximum over all non-empty hash key, then we can answer this type of
query with the same complexity as the (c, h)—ANNS.
Combining these components and assuming that c ≤ τm is a constant
(Assumption 4), the overall time complexity is O(dn1+o(1) ). Also, the space
complexity is dominated by the LSH preprocessing step which provides the same
bound and completes the proof.

In this section we show that, despite the multiplicative error introduced by the
approximate KDE in proposition 2, Quick Shift’s assignment of points to moderooted trees remains consistent with the underlying density.
These properties form the foundation for consistent clustering guarantees,
analogous to those established in the exact KDE setting [14], but now achieved
with sub-quadratic computational complexity by virtue of using the approximate
KDE as an oracle.

Mode Estimation

We now describe the mode estimation problem under the Quick Shift clustering
procedure, where density evaluations are performed using the LSH-KDE oracles.
Let M ⊂ X denote the set of local modes of f . We assume that modes are
isolated and exhibit quadratic decay.
Assumption 4 (Modes). A point x0 ∈ X is said to be a mode of f if there
exists rM > 0 such that x0 is the unique maximizer of f in B(x0 , rM ) and there
exist constants Č, Ĉ > 0 for which
Č x − x0 2 ≤ f (x0 ) − f (x) ≤ Ĉ x − x0 2 ,

∀ x ∈ B(x0 , rM ).

Denote by M the (ﬁnite) set of all such modes.
Quick Shift (see, e.g., [19]) is an iterative procedure that assigns each sample
xi to a nearby point in its τ -ball with strictly higher density. In our algorithm,
each density evaluation is computed via f˜(x). We denote by M̂ the set of estimated modes returned by the algorithm.

Assignment of Points to Modes

In this section, we show how Quick Shift assigns each sample point to the basin of
attraction of a nearby mode under the approximate KDE oracle. In particular,
we establish that if two points are separated by a suﬃciently deep and wide
valley in the underlying density, they cannot lie in the same directed tree of the
Quick Shift graph.
Deﬁnition 3 ((r, δ)-separation, [5]). Let r > 0 and δ > 0. Two points
x1 , x2 ∈ X are said to be (r, δ)-separated if there exists a set S ⊂ X such
that:
1. Every path from x1 to x2 intersects S.
2. We have
sup
x∈S+B(0,r)

f (x) < ∞x∈B(x1 ,r) ∪ B(x2 ,r) f (x) − δ.

Equivalently, one may view S as the region forming a “deep valley” separating
x1 from x2 , whose density is at least δ below the density near x1 or x2 , plus a
margin of width r. The main technical claim is that if two points x1 and x2 are
(rs , δ)-separated, then with high probability there is no directed path in (G, f˜)
from x1 to x2 .
Theorem 3. Let (G, f˜) be the directed cluster tree constructed by Quick Shift
using the approximate densities f˜ from Assumption 3. Under the same conditions
as Theorem 2, there exists a constant C > 0 such that with probability at least
1 − 1/n, if x1 and x2 are (rs , δ)-separated, there is no directed path from x1 to
x2 in G, provided
supx∈X fˆ(x).
Proof. Suppose, for contradiction, that there is a directed path x1 = y0 → y1 →
· · · → yk = x2 in G. By deﬁnition of Quick Shift, each edge (yj , yj+1 ) implies
f˜(yj+1 ) > f˜(yj )

and

By Deﬁnition 3, every path from x1 to x2 intersects the seperator set S +
B(0, rs ). Hence, there exists at least one point yj∗ ∈ S + B(0, rs ) on the path.

So if n is suﬃciently large, provided that δ is chosen larger than a constant times

and for δ suﬃciently large relative to (x1 ), we deduce f˜(yj∗ ) < f˜(x1 )., Which
contradicts the requirement for a directed edge path that f˜(yj+1 ) > f˜(yj ) strictly
at each step. Hence, no such path can exist from x1 to x2 .
An immediate corollary is that points (rs , δ)-separated from a given mode x0
cannot join x0 ’s tree in G. Indeed, if x were assigned to x0 , there would exist a
directed path x → · · · → x0 in (G, f˜), violating Theorem 3. Hence, each point
is constrained to remain in the basin of attraction of exactly those modes not
separated from it by a deep valley.
Corollary 1 (Separation Implies Diﬀerent Trees). Under the same conditions as Theorem 3, if x and a mode x0 are (rs , δ)-separated, then x cannot lie
in the Quick Shift tree rooted at x0 .
Proof. If x were in the tree of x0 , then there would be a directed path x → · · · →
x0 . This contradicts Theorem 3 because x and x0 are (rs , δ)-separated, implying
no such path can exist.
In the absence of approximation error (i.e. = 0), these results coincide
exactly with the analysis in [14]. The only additional requirement here is that
δ exceed a constant times (and the usual KDE deviation term), ensuring that
approximate comparisons preserve the strict density gap. Thus, the same geometric intuition that “deep and wide valleys” prevent two points from being
assigned to the same root remains valid under approximate density evaluations.

S. Hashemian

5

Experiments

We evaluate the proposed algorithm on two tasks: clustering, and image segmentation on benchmark data as the most well-known tasks for Mean Shift
variants. All experiments were conducted on a standard workstation (16 GB
RAM, 2.4 GHz CPU).
5.1

Clustering

We compare the proposed algorithm against other popular density-based clustering algorithm and K-means as an scalable option on various clustering tasks in
Table 1. These comparisons are made using the Scikit-Learn1 and Scikit-Image2
implementation of Quick Shift and our own implementation of LSH-Quickshift in
C++ wrapped as a Python package using Pybind11, and used FAISS library [6]
for LSH implemention. To measure the quality of a clustering result, we use
the Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) [10] and the Adjusted Mutual Information
(AMI) [16] scores, comparing the clustering with the partitioning induced by the
labels of the data points. The benchmark datasets we use are labeled datasets
from UCI Repository3 , and we only cluster the features.
As expected by our theoretical analysis, the proposed method is well scalable
for large data sets in high dimensions. Also, this method outpreforms vector
quantization methods in complex regimes with high number of clusters.
5.2

Image Segmentation

We compare the proposed method to a number of baselines for unsupervised
image segmentation in Fig. 1. We include Felzenszwalb [9], Quick Shift [19], and
Mean Shift [3], three popular image segmentation procedures from the Python,
Scikit-Image library. For image segmentation, we run each algorithm on a preprocessed image with each pixel represented in a 5D (r, g, b, x, y) color channel and
spatial coordinates space and at maximum the size of our images was 46,500 pixels from the Berkeley Segmentation Dataset Benchmark (BSDS500)4 . For each
algorithm, the returned clusters are taken as the segments. Our image segmentation experiments show that LSH-Quick Shift is able to produce segmentations
that are nearly identical to that of Mean Shift.

6

Conclusion

In this work, we have introduced the LSH-QuickShift algorithm, which integrates Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH) with the Quick Shift clustering method
to achieve eﬃcient, provably consistent mode estimation in high-dimensional
spaces. By leveraging LSH for approximate kernel density estimation, the algorithm signiﬁcantly reduces computational complexity, making it suitable for
large-scale datasets. Theoretical analyses conﬁrm that the LSH-QuickShift algorithm maintains consistency with the underlying density, ensuring reliable clustering results.
However, several things remain for further consideration and applying the
LSH enhanced density based clustering algorithm. Investigating methods to
reconstruct the hierarchical structure of clusters post-clustering could provide
deeper insights into the data’s inherent organization and thus more eﬃcient
clustering algorithm. This extends the algorithm to perform regression tasks by
modeling the relationship between data points and their modes and thus could
broaden its applicability.
One may combine LSH-QuickShift with sub-sampling techniques and random projections and further enhance scalability and robustness, particularly in
extremely high-dimensional settings with large data sets, and exploring the algo-

Fast Mode Estimation and Clustering

265

rithm’s performance within massive parallel computing frameworks could lead
to signiﬁcant improvements in processing large-scale datasets, addressing both
time and space complexity challenges. Also, applying these methods to various
data modalities beyond numerical and image data, such as text or time-series
data, could demonstrate its versatility and eﬀectiveness across diﬀerent domains.

are relevant to the content of this article.
