Addressing Classic Constraint Satisfaction
Problems Through Optimisation Modulo
Theories
Ignacio Jorquera1(B) , Nicolás Gálvez Ramírez1 , Frederic Saubion2 ,
Eric Monfroy2 , and Carlos Castro1
1

Universidad Técnica Federico Santa María, Valparaíso, Chile
ignacio.jorquera@sansano.usm.cl, {nicolas.galvez,Carlos.Castro}@usm.cl
2
Université Angers, Angers, France
{frederic.saubion,Eric.Monfroy}@univ-angers.fr

Abstract. This paper explores the use of Optimisation Modulo Theories (OMT) to address classic Constraint Satisfaction (and Optimisation)
Problems. By leveraging the Z3 solver, we investigate the impact of different SMT logics—Linear Integer Arithmetic (LIA), Linear Real Arithmetic (LRA), Quantiﬁer-Free Bit-Vectors (QF_BV), and Quantiﬁer-Free
Arrays with Integer Arithmetic (QF_ALIA)—on problem-solving eﬃciency
and solution quality. A set of automatic encoding rules is proposed to
transform problems initially modelled in LIA into other logics, simplifying
the process for users without specialised expertise. We demonstrate how
logic selection can signiﬁcantly inﬂuence solver performance. For example, QF_BV excels in scheduling and sequential problems like the Nurse
Scheduling Problem and the Travelling Salesman Problem. In contrast,
LRA and QF_ALIA stand out in problems with broad numerical ranges,
such as the Unbounded Knapsack Problem. These ﬁndings underscore
the importance of an automatic logic encoder capable of dynamically
selecting and transforming models into the most appropriate SMT logic
for a given problem. This performance-driven logic selection approach
enhances the solving process. It extends the accessibility and practicality of OMT frameworks across diverse problem domains.
Keywords: Satisﬁability Modulo Theories · Optimisation Modulo
Theories · Constraint Satisfaction and Optimisation Problems · Linear
Programming · Z3

1

Introduction

A Constraint Satisfaction Problem (CSP) [13] can be formally deﬁned by a set
of variables .X = {x1 , · · · , xn }. Each variable .xi takes its values into a given
domain .Di . A solution is a tuple of values such that each variable is assigned to
a value from its corresponding domain. The tuple satisﬁes a set of constraints
.C, which are relations over the variables domains. The solutions can be evaluated according to an objective function, turning into a Constraint Optimisation
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 267–278, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_20

268

I. Jorquera et al.

Problem (COP). Constraint programming (CP) has been introduced to address
CSP and COP across various domains, allowing end-users to deﬁne variables
and express constraints independently from the underlying solving technologies.
It gave rise to the development of various modelling languages (e.g., Minizinc
[19] or Essence [15]) that may be connected to diﬀerent solvers. Turning to the
solving techniques, the main idea in CP is to provide various facilities related to
the domains of the variables and the type of constraint.
The choice of the model impacts solving eﬃciency and complexity. While
many CSPs can be modelled using Integer Linear Programming [12], they can
also commonly be mapped to Boolean Satisﬁability Problems (SAT) [4, 22]. Nevertheless, while SAT solvers exhibit eﬃciency on large problem instances, they
involve technical and complex encodings of some constraints, mainly when dealing with arithmetic constraints. Thus, Satisﬁability Modulo Theories (SMT) [20]
generalise SAT by considering several theories within a common propositional
logic framework, providing a comprehensive set of tools for mathematically modeling complex problems. SMT allows the user to combine variables with diﬀerent
domains: integer, real, pseudo-binary, arrays, bit-vector, and strings, as deﬁned
in the SMT-LIB [2]. Optimisation Modulo Theories (OMT) extends its capabilities to address both the satisﬁability and optimisation aspects of CSP and COP
[16]. OMT oﬀers SMT advanced modeling and solving techniques, making it
suited for problems that span or can be eﬀectively mapped to multiple domains.
The expertise required to encode or transform a problem across diﬀerent theories
often goes beyond the typical skill set of modellers. Since eﬀective modelling is
crucial for eﬃcient problem-solving, several eﬀorts have been made to enhance
SMT with advanced encoding capabilities [7, 9].
This work addresses the use of the Linear Integers Arithmetic (LIA) logic
to model and solve decision problems with Z3 [14] as an OMT solver and the
automatic encoding to transform these models into other SMT logic—Linear
Reals Arithmetic (LRA), Quantiﬁer-Free over the theory of ﬁxed-size BitVectors
(QF_BV) and Quantiﬁer-Free over the theory of Arrays extending Linear Integers
Arithmetic (QF_ALIA)—to ease the use of its solving procedure for a given problem while comparing the performance of these among diﬀerent selected decision
problems. The transformation is applied through a set of encodings, simplifying
the process of testing multiple logics, ultimately helping identify the one that
yields the best-performing model. The results show that the proposed set of
encoding rules is suﬃcient to transform a decision problem modelled in LIA into
any of the three other SMT logics. These rules allow the exploration of diﬀerent
SMT back-end logics to ﬁnd the best-performing model comparing the solving
of the same problem using diﬀerent logics. Some problems, like N-Queens or the
Travelling Salesman Problem (TSP), are easier to model in LIA due to their
similarity to natural language but may perform better with other logics, such
as BitVectors (QF_BV), where each bit in a bit-vector determines the position
of a queen or the order in which cities are visited. On the other hand, problems like the Unbounded Knapsack Problem (UKP) be solved more eﬃciently
when mapped to more specialised logics, such as Real numbers (LRA) or Arrays

Addressing Classic CSP Through OMT

269

of Integers (QF_ALIA), instead of using Integer numbers (LIA). These problems
beneﬁt from the encoding rules, enabling quick and eﬀortless transformations
from a natural logic to a more specialised one, optimising solver performance
with minimal manual adjustment.

2

General Problem Statement

Optimisation Modulo Theories (OMT) oﬀers powerful modelling capabilities but
poses a high entry barrier for new users. While many problems can be eﬀectively
modelled with Linear Programming tools, more complex problems requiring ﬁrstorder logic demand specialised knowledge in modelling techniques and problemspeciﬁc contexts. For example, using Floating Point or String theories requires
familiarity with binary representations and regular expressions. This challenge
grows when combining multiple theories within a single model, making OMT
accessible mainly to those with advanced expertise. Eﬀorts have been made to
improve the solution process in Satisﬁability Modulo Theories (SMT), particularly in enhancing solver strategies and performance. Research in this area has
focused on streamlining solver mechanisms to achieve faster results across various SMT applications [3,10,11,18]. However, the full use of OMT capabilities
still poses a complex barrier.
An ideal solution would be a system where users could deﬁne their problem in natural language, and the system would handle all modelling and solving
processes automatically. While advances in natural language processing bring us
closer to this vision, achieving a fully automated interface still demands rigorous
training and validation to ensure constraints are correctly translated and solutions are valid. A more feasible short-term approach could involve an automatic
encoding tool that translates models deﬁned in basic mathematical terms—such
as integer or real number domains—into complex SMT logic using predeﬁned
rules, enabling users with basic modelling skills to utilise SMT/OMT functionalities without deep technical expertise. Thus, the goal is to deﬁne a set of rules
that can automatically encode a CSP/COP modelled with Linear Integer Arithmetic into other SMT logics. This transformation would allow users to select the
best SMT logic for a given problem without manually re-encoding each model.

3

Related Work

The challenge of encoding constraints into SMT is known but scarce. In [6], a
CSP-to-SMT-LIB translation is introduced, showing the beneﬁts of automating the modelling process, but with its limitations on the restricted variety of
logics and solvers. Further research [7, 8] explores the translation from FlatZinc
to SMT-LIB format, oﬀering a versatile approach for using SMT/OMT solvers
in constraint programming. Regarding eﬃcient encoding, [9] provides comparative analyses of diﬀerent logic performances, including hand-crafted models
across multiple solvers and logics. It concludes that many problems can be solved
with Linear Integer Arithmetic (LIA), but speciﬁc problems perform better with

270

I. Jorquera et al.

pure SAT encodings or with Bit-Vectors (QF_BV), as demonstrated when solving
the Resource-Constrained Project Scheduling Problem [5]. Moreover, [9] highlights the impact of SMT encoding strategies by comparing the performance
of various logics, such as QF_LIA, QF_NIA, QF_IDL, and QF_BV, across diﬀerent
problem types. They demonstrate that QF_BV, which uses eﬃcient bit-blasting
to represent variables as ﬁxed-size bit-vectors, excels in hardware constraints
and binary decision-making problems due to reduced encoding complexity. Conversely, QF_LIA and QF_IDL perform better in formal veriﬁcation and scheduling
problems requiring linear arithmetic constraints. It also emphasises the comparative advantage of speciﬁc SMT encodings, noting that SAT or QF_BV encodings
can yield superior eﬃciency in scheduling problems where dependencies beneﬁt from the compactness and speed of bit-vector logic. These ﬁndings suggest
that selecting the right encoding approach, tailored to problem constraints and
logical requirements, is essential for optimising solver performance.

4

Towards Automatic Encoding

To address the challenge of choosing the appropriate SMT logic for a given
problem, we explore encoding strategies that transform problems modelled in
Linear Integer Arithmetic (LIA) into other SMT logics: Linear Real Arithmetic
(LRA), Quantiﬁer-Free Bit-Vectors (QF_BV), and Quantiﬁer-Free Arrays with Linear Integer Arithmetic (QF_ALIA). These transformations are performed by using
a structured set of rules. We also provide analysis and comparison of solver performance across diﬀerent problem domains.
4.1

Selected Problems and SMT Logics

Five classic CSP/COP, representative of diverse domains, are used to evaluate
the proposed encoding approach:
– N-Queens [1]: Placing .n queens on an .n×n chessboard so no queens threaten
others.
– Balanced Academic Curriculum Problem (BACP) [1]: Scheduling academic courses across periods while balancing credits and satisfying prerequisites.
– Nurse Scheduling Problem (NSP) [21]: Assigning nurses to daily shifts
while adhering to workplace constraints and maximising scheduling quality.
– Travelling Salesman Problem (TSP) [17]: Finding the shortest route
through a set of cities while minimising total distance.
– Unbounded Knapsack Problem (UKP) [1]: Filling a knapsack with items
to maximise utility while respecting capacity constraints.

Addressing Classic CSP Through OMT

Encoding Rules

1. LIA to LRA: Variables are relaxed from integers to reals. To preserve feasibility, new constraints are needed to enforce that the real variables take
integer-equivalent values within the feasible range.

2. LIA to QF_BV: Decision variables are encoded as ﬁxed-size bit-vectors, with
lengths determined by the maximum possible value in the domain. The encoding uses binary encoding when the domain is within the positive numbers or
two-complement if it also includes negative numbers because of the need for
an extra bit for the sign.
Binary Encoding: Xi ∈ N0 → Xi ∈ {0, 1} log2 (max_feasible_value) +1

.

3. LIA to QF_ALIA: Variables are represented as arrays. Single-dimensional
arrays store values directly, while multi-dimensional arrays encode variables
with multiple indices.

4.3

Applying Encoding Rules in the UKP

The Unbounded Knapsack Problem (UKP) shows how real-world problems can
be formulated mathematically and solved using optimisation algorithms. The
LIA model provides an intuitive way to represent the problem, ensuring that the
solution is computationally feasible while maintaining accuracy. By translating
this model into other SMT logics, the same problem can be explored in contexts
that beneﬁt from diﬀerent computational trade-oﬀs, leading to potentially faster
or more resource-eﬃcient solutions. In LIA, the problem is modelled with integer
decision variables, maximising utility subject to capacity constraints:
– Parameters (Fixed Inputs):
• .n: Number of unique items available, .n ∈ N0
• .C: Maximum weight capacity of the knapsack, .C ∈ N0
• .Wi : Weight of item .i, .Wi ∈ N0 , ∀i ∈ {1, ..., n}
• .Vi : Utility value of item .i, .Vi ∈ Z, ∀i ∈ {1, ..., n}
– Variables (Decision Inputs):
• .Xi : Number of times item .i is added to the knapsack, .Xi ∈ N0 , ∀i ∈
{1, ..., n}

knapsack:

Experiments and Results

We present the outcomes of using SMT logic to solve instances of CSP, focusing
on solution quality, execution time, and the practical impacts of each encoding
used from the proposed set of rules.
5.1

Problem Instances

Table 1 shows the list of problems and their instances. Each instance corresponds
to incremental levels of complexity, allowing for a broad comparison across different logics and problem features.
– N-Queens: Instances represent increasing board sizes and corresponding
numbers of queens.
– BACP: Instances diﬀer by the number of academic periods and prerequisite
constraints.
– NSP: Each instance speciﬁes the number of nurses, a seed value, and a
scheduling period, with gen 8 representing a week and gen 16 representing a
month.
– TSP: Instances vary depending on the number of cities in the travel route.
– UKP: Each instance has diﬀerent item weights and capacities, as indicated
by the unique identiﬁer in each instance name.

Experimental Setup

Each model was built with the Z3 SMT solver using the Python API, adhering to
SMT-LIB 2.6 standards. The tests were executed on a system with an Intel Xeon
E-2336 CPU and 16 GB RAM, running Rocky Linux OS. We set the following
constraints for each test:
– Execution Time Limit: Each instance had a maximum runtime of 3600 s
(1 h).
– Memory Limit: Tests were bound at 4048 MB of memory usage.

and QF_BV consistently completed instances within resource bounds. In NSP,
both achieved optimal results more often and faster than others. Encoding with
QF_BV allowed quick variable assignments in scheduling, facilitating eﬃciency.

6

Conclusions and Future Work

In this paper, we addressed several classic constraint satisfaction and optimisation problems (CSP and COP) through an OMT solver by using some SMT logic
models to analyse the impact of the logic selection on problem-solving eﬃciency
and solution quality. The choice of the Z3 solver allowed to test each problem
with four primary logics—LIA (Linear Integer Arithmetic), LRA (Linear Real
Arithmetic), QF_BV (Quantiﬁer-Free Bit-Vectors), and QF_ALIA (Quantiﬁer-Free
Arrays with Integer Arithmetic). By modelling each problem in the selected
SMT logics, our experiments show the inﬂuence of the logic selection procedure
on the solver performance, including solution accuracy and processing time. We

Addressing Classic CSP Through OMT

277

highlight LIA and QF_BV outperforming on scheduling and sequential problems,
such as the Nurse Scheduling Problem and N-Queens. At the same time, QF_BV
also ﬁnds good solutions for the Travelling Salesman Problem, using bitwise
operations to ﬁnd faster assignments. For problems with broad numerical ranges
like the Unbounded Knapsack Problem, LRA and QF_ALIA found better solutions and performed faster. These results suggest a high chance of substantially
improving OMT solver performance using a suitable selection and combination
of procedures from a set of SMT logics for each problem.
The rule encoding transformation set has the potential to be generalised into
a system that can automatically translate any mathematical model into multiple
SMT logics to optimise solver performance. Without such a system, users must
manually encode or transform problems into diﬀerent SMT logics to ﬁnd the
best-performing model for their speciﬁc problem. An automatic logic encoder
would streamline this process, saving time and eﬀort while enabling users to
achieve optimal performance by leveraging the unique strengths of each SMT
logic. Our ﬁndings suggest this approach would enhance model adaptability and
extend the practical applications of OMT solvers across various domains without
requiring any expert knowledge for each logic nuances.

following the submitted research.

Chance Constrained Optimization
with Complex Variables
Raneem Madani(B)

and Abdel Lisser

Université Paris-Saclay, CNRS, CentraleSupélec, Laboratoire des Signaux et
Systèmes (L2S), 91190 Gif-sur-Yvette, France
{raneem.madani,abdel.lisser}@centralesupelec.fr

Abstract. This paper introduces a novel formalism for a prominent
problem in stochastic optimization involving complex random variables,
termed the Complex Chance-Constrained Problem (CCCP). The study
speciﬁcally examines the linear CCCP under complex normal distributions for two scenarios: one with individual probabilistic constraints and
the other with joint probabilistic constraints. For the individual case, the
core methodology reformulates the CCCP into a deterministic SecondOrder Cone Programming (SOCP) problem, ensuring equivalence to the
original CCCP. For the joint case, an approximation is achieved by deriving suitable upper and lower bounds, which also leads to a SOCP formulation. Finally, numerical experiments on a signal processing application, speciﬁcally, the Minimum Variance Beamforming problem with
mismatch using MVDR, demonstrate that the proposed formalism outperforms existing approaches in the literature. A comparative analysis
between the joint and individual CCCP cases is also included.
Keywords: Stochastic Optimization · Chance-Constrained
Programming · Joint Constraint Problem · Second-Order Cone
Programming · Complex Normal Distribution · Adaptive Beamforming

1

Introduction

Mathematical optimization is the systematic process of maximizing or minimizing an objective while adhering to deﬁned constraints. However, many optimization problems are complicated by uncertainties in data or models. This leads to
a critical subﬁeld known as Optimization Under Uncertainty, or Stochastic Optimization, it was ﬁrst introduced by George Dantzig in the 1950s [7]. This ﬁeld is
widely studied because real-world problems often involve unpredictable factors
that cannot be fully anticipated. Addressing these uncertainties is essential to
develop solutions that are both reliable and eﬀective.
One prominent framework for handling uncertainty in optimization is chanceconstrained programming (CCP), introduced by Abraham Charnes and William
W. Cooper in 1959 [3]. CCP incorporates uncertainty directly into the constraints
by representing them as probabilistic conditions. In this approach, constraints
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 279–290, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_21

280

R. Madani and A. Lisser

must be satisﬁed with a predeﬁned conﬁdence level, ensuring a balance between
feasibility and risk. The importance of addressing CCP problems, particularly
when the decision variable is complex, is increasingly evident due to their applications in various real-world domains, such as signal processing [14] (refer to
Sect. 7). Notably, tackling these problems requires alternative approaches that
leverage the complex nature of the variables, the distribution of stochastic variables, and the relation matrix, which depends on both .z and its conjugate .z̄.
These approaches diﬀer signiﬁcantly from methods that convert complex variables into real ones using .CR-calculus (Wirtinger Calculus [17,20]). The latter
approach loses key exploitable properties [11,15].
Another critical reason for directly handling complex variables lies in the
computational implications of conversion. Transforming complex variables into
real ones doubles the problem’s dimensionality, potentially leading to increased
computational time as in Table 1, slower convergence rates, issues related to the
curse of dimensionality [2], and reduced algorithmic accuracy, as seen in [10],
the authors proved that solving optimization problems in the complex domain
yields better accuracy compared to converting the problem to the real domain
particularly when applied to quantum computing problems.
Table 1. Complex vs. real GD/SPSA performance for .min

Reformulation techniques are essential for managing chance constraints in
optimization by transforming them into more tractable forms for analysis and
solution. For individual linear chance constraints, an equivalent reformulation
as second-order cone programming (SOCP) is possible under certain conditions
[12,16]. For linear joint chance constraints with continuous random variables,
in [4], a full proof is provided using copula theory to convert the problem into
a deterministic one. Then, various convex approximations have been proposed,
such as [5], Cheng and Lisser derived lower and upper bounds using Taylor series
and piecewise methods.
This paper introduces a novel approach for addressing optimization problems
involving complex variables under uncertainty, referred to as Complex-ChanceConstraint Programming (CCCP). To the best of our knowledge, the general
form of CCP with complex random variables has not been previously formulated or tackled. Our work focuses on the linear case, where both the objective
and constraints are linear, and the random variables follow complex normal
distributions. We address two key problem classes: The ﬁrst class is the individual CCCP, where we transform stochastic problems into equivalent SOCPs

Chance Constrained Optimization with Complex Variables

281

by exploiting properties of the mean and variance. The second class is the joint
CCCP with dependent matrix rows. For this, we convert the joint CCCP into
a deterministic problem by employing copulas to handle dependencies among
random vectors. As the resulting problem is biconvex, we approximate the solution by deriving lower bounds using a Taylor series approximation and upper
bounds using the piecewise approach. The results have since been extended to
encompass nonlinear scenarios with respect to decision variables while remaining
linear in relation to random variables. To demonstrate their practical utility, we
apply these ﬁndings to a robust beamforming problem, speciﬁcally addressing
the MDVR (Minimum variance distortionless response) case with a mismatch.
The paper is organized as follows: Sect. 2 presents the preliminaries and problem formulation. Section 3 introduces the CCCP problem. Section 4 discusses the
normal distribution of complex aﬃne functions. Section 5 presents the IndividualCCCP. Section 6 delves into the Joint-CCCP, detailing the problem and providing upper and lower approximations. Section 7 presents applications from signal
processing (MVDR with mismatch), and simulation experiments comparing our
approach with approaches from the literature and also comparisons between
Individual and Joint CCCP. Finally, Sect. 8 concludes the paper.

Complex Chance Constraint Problem (CCCP)

The complex chance-constraint problem is given as follows:
(CCCP) min f (z, ξ)

.

s.t. g(z, ξ) = 0
P[h(z, ξ) ≤ 0] ≥ p,
R, .g : Cn
Ck , and .h : Cn
Rm . The deterministic deciwhere .f : Cn
sion variables are given by the complex vector .z ∈ Cn , while .ξ is the complex
random vector containing all uncertainties in the form of complex random variables. We have two classes, ﬁrst one is the individual CCCP which requires that
each constraint be satisﬁed independently with a certain probability, and then
.p = [p1 , · · · , pm ], pi ∈ [0, 1]; ∀i is the probability vector. The other class is the
joint CCCP which requires that all inequality constraints in the set be satisﬁed
simultaneously with a certain joint probability, and then .p ∈ [0, 1]. In this paper,
we assume that the feasible set is not empty and .p ≥ 0.5.

Simulation Experiments

The implementation was carried out in Python, with the optimization problem
solved using CVXPY. All experiments were run on a machine featuring an 11thgeneration Intel Core i7-1185G7 CPU (3.00 GHz) and 32 GB of RAM.
Adaptive Beamforming with Mismatch Problem. Our formalism has been
applied for adaptive Beamforming using MVDR [6]. Assume that we have several
sensors .N , the output signal of the narrowband beamformer is .y(t) = wH x(t),
where .t is the sample index, .w ∈ CM beamformer weight coeﬃcients, and .x(t) =
S(t) + v(t) is the snapshot vector of array observations. where .S(t) = s(t)as and
.v(t) are the desired signal and the interference-plus-noise components of .x(t),
respectively, .s(t) and .as are the desired signal waveform and its steering vector
(spatial signature). The optimal weight vector can be obtained by computing
the maximum of the SINR function:

steering vectors, respectively.

probability-constrained problem using our formalism to optimize .w. The CCCP

Chance Constrained Optimization with Complex Variables

8

289

Conclusion

This paper presents a novel approach to optimizing real-world applications
involving complex variables through complex chance-constrained optimization
programming (CCCP). Our work focuses on handling random experiments with
speciﬁc conﬁdence levels on constraints. We addressed a special case of complex
optimization, linear programming with complex random variables following a
complex normal distribution. We reformulated the problem to a deterministic
one, resulting in a deterministic SOCP for individual CCCP. For joint CCCP, we
formulated a deterministic problem involving the product of two inherently convex functions, using Taylor Series and Piecewise tangent approximations. The
optimal values obtained serve as a tight gap between the lower and upper bounds
for the original problem. Experimentation shows joint CCCP outperforms individual CCCP, providing enhanced accuracy for many applications.
Acknowledgements. This work was supported by the French government through
the France 2030 investment plan, under the QuanTEdu-France project (grant no. ANR22-CMAS-0001) and the France 2030 program (reference ANR-11-IDEX-0003) within
the OI H-Code.

Optimizing Retail Promotions
with Mixed-Integer Nonlinear
Programming and Cross-Elasticity Eﬀects
Govindaraju Uma Maheswari(B) , Srividhya Sethuraman ,
and Sharadha Ramanan
TCS Research, Chennai, India
{uma.mg,srividhya.sethuraman1,sharadha.ramanan}@tcs.com
Abstract. Promotion planning for a store group is a strategic approach
aimed at temporarily lowering prices to stimulate sales over a speciﬁc
period while meeting revenue and margin targets. The challenge lies in
selecting the optimal set of products and bundles for promotion and
determining their discounts, all within constraints like budget limits,
bundle conﬂicts, and business rules. Conventional methods using linearization or heuristics often lead to sub-optimal solutions, making them
unsuitable for real-world scenarios. This paper introduces a two-step
Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming (MINLP) solution to address the
non-linearities inherent in promotion planning. By integrating discrete
decisions, such as item selection, with continuous variables like discount
levels, MINLP solution oﬀers a comprehensive solution for balancing revenue and margin trade-oﬀs. It allows inclusion of important factors such
as hoarding, new bundles and cannibalization eﬀects. In practical settings, where promotions involve about 50–100 SKUs and bundles within
a category, the resulting MINLP problem remains computationally feasible and scalable. Compared to the historical discount from a large
retailer, promotion using MINLP gives 0.84% increase in revenue and
4.5% increase in margin with 89.9% total budget being used. Considering new bundles improved revenue by 21.32% and margin by 13.96%
using 81.22% of total budget. Additionally, detailed what-if analyses provide actionable insights, enabling retailers to evaluate varying strategies,
adapt to dynamic market conditions, and strengthen their negotiation
positions with vendors. This study highlights the potential of MINLP in
transforming promotion planning into a robust and eﬃcient process.
Keywords: Retail Promotion Planning · MINLP · Price
optimization · Bundle Optimization · New Bundles · Cross-Elasticity
Eﬀects · Heuristics · Scenario Analysis · Scalability

1

Introduction

Promotion planning for a store group is a critical component of retail strategy,
involving structured price adjustments over speciﬁc periods to boost sales and
achieve ﬁnancial goals. It includes regular promotions, which follow an annual
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 291–303, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_22

292

G. Uma Maheswari et al.

schedule, and event-based promotions, aligned with major occasions like Christmas or ‘Back to School’ campaigns. This paper focuses on optimizing regular
promotions, which require 4–6 months of advanced planning, based on category
managers’ forecasts. These forecasts shape the promotional calendar, determining the scope and frequency of planned activities.
A well-structured promotion plan integrates revenue and margin targets, vendor contributions, and a predeﬁned set of items and promotional strategies. Business rules deﬁne how promotions apply to speciﬁc products or bundles, ensuring
alignment with retailer objectives while meeting operational constraints. However, the complexity of promotion planning stems from cross-elasticity eﬀects,
where the pricing of one item inﬂuences demand for related products, and bundle
dynamics, where discounts on grouped items aﬀect overall category performance.
Managing these inter-dependencies is essential to optimize promotional discounts
while maintaining proﬁtability.
Retailers must also consider factors such as hoarding behavior, cannibalization eﬀects, and bundle conﬂicts when designing promotion strategies. Crosselasticity analysis provides insights into how discounts on one item aﬀect related
products, while bundle modeling captures the aggregated impact of multi-item
promotions. The introduction of new bundles further complicates optimization,
requiring sophisticated decision-making tools.
To address these challenges, retailers require advanced demand forecasting
tools that integrate historical sales data, price elasticity, budget constraints,
and operational considerations. These tools must support explainability, oﬀering clear insights into promotion drivers such as competitor actions, vendor
funding, and consumer behavior. Additionally, dynamic simulation capabilities
are essential for conducting what-if analyses, enabling retailers to reﬁne promotional strategies, assess ﬁnancial impact, and improve vendor negotiations.
Despite these complexities, existing solutions often rely on linearization or heuristic methods, which fail to capture the nonlinear interdependencies inherent in
promotion planning. This paper presents a Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming (MINLP) framework that models critical nonlinear factors, including crosselasticity eﬀects, bundle interactions, and business constraints. Leveraging historical data, price elasticity values, cross-elasticity insights, and scenario analysis,
the proposed framework optimizes category-level promotions for both individual
products and bundles.
Since planned promotions typically involve 50–100 SKUs and bundles per
category, the MINLP problem remains medium in scale, ensuring computational
feasibility and real-world applicability. This approach provides a comprehensive,
scalable, and eﬀective solution for optimizing retail promotions while achieving
ﬁnancial and operational objectives. In the next section we give a brief comparison of the state of the art methods and highlight their gaps and state how we
address them.

2

Related Work

Promotion pricing has been widely studied in marketing, operations management, and economics. Recent advancements focus on overcoming the limitations

Optimizing Retail Promotions with Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming

293

of traditional models by incorporating inter-item eﬀects and cross-elasticity.
Structural Equation Modeling (SEM) has been used to analyze interactions
between promotional oﬀers on multiple products [10]. Research employing crosssectional data explores how promotion pricing impacts consumer behavior and
ﬁrm performance, highlighting interdependencies across products and categories
[4]. Machine learning techniques have also been employed to identify patterns in
large datasets and develop predictive models for complex interactions between
prices, product characteristics, and consumer behavior. Our approach employs
a cross-price elasticity matrix to explicitly capture changes in product sales due
to price variations in related products and bundles (Refer Sect. 3.)
Studies have also investigated unintended eﬀects of promotion pricing, such
as hoarding and cannibalization [4,10]. Strategies like price anchoring, discount
timing, and promotional bundling have been proposed to address these issues
[10]. For example, anchoring prices helps mitigate hoarding by providing a reference point for evaluating other products. Additionally, research suggests optimal
timing of promotions can reduce cannibalization eﬀects by minimizing overlaps
between promoted and non-promoted products. We address these two challenges
by incorporating historical price data into our demand forecasting model and
using cross-price elasticity to account for the impact of bundles on individual
product sales (Refer Sect. 3.)
From an operational perspective, promotion pricing is often modeled as a
dynamic optimization problem with objectives such as maximizing sales, revenue, or proﬁt margins. Linear programming, integer programming, and stochastic programming are commonly employed to solve these problems. For instance,
linear programming optimizes pricing strategies by balancing immediate sales
with long-term proﬁtability. Integer programming considers binary decisions,
such as whether to oﬀer a discount. More recent studies incorporate stochastic
programming and machine learning to account for demand uncertainties and
market trends. Constraints such as budget limits, product availability, and supply chain factors are also widely considered.
Several studies have applied mathematical programming techniques to retail
promotion optimization. Despite these advancements, most studies rely on linearization or heuristic approaches, limiting their ability to address nonlinearities
in real-world scenarios [4, 5,7]. These methods often overlook critical aspects
such as bundles and what-if analyses, creating a gap in the literature [6,8].
The contributions of this work are as follows: 1) We present a novel twostep heuristics + MINLP framework for retail promotion planning, eﬀectively
addressing nonlinearities from cross-elasticity and bundle interactions. 2) Our
MINLP solution optimizes product selection, bundle conﬁgurations, and discounts while considering hoarding, cannibalization, business rules, and bundle
conﬂicts. 3) Application of our model for real-world data demonstrates a 27% revenue increase and a 16% margin improvement when incorporating new bundles.
4) Our framework provides actionable insights,ensuring alignment with ﬁnancial
and operational goals. 5) We include ‘what-if’ analyses that enhance decisionmaking, making this approach a scalable, data-driven solution for retailers. In
the next section, we discuss our mathematical formulation of the problem and
MINLP optimization framework in detail.

294

3

G. Uma Maheswari et al.

Mathematical Formulation of the MINLP Solution
for Nonlinear Planned Promotion Optimization

This section details the two-stage (heuristic and MINLP) approach for optimizing planned promotions at a store group level. The promotion optimization
system follows a structured workﬂow (refer Fig. 1): 1. Aggregation of transaction data, product attributes, and store-level information. 2. Clustering of stores
based on sales proﬁles. 3. Demand forecasting [3,11] at the product/bundle-store
group level. 4. Computation of price elasticity and cross-price elasticity coeﬃcients [2, 9] 5. MINLP-based optimization to determine the optimal selection of
promotional items, bundles, and their discounts.
The optimization problem aims to maximize category revenue while meeting
predeﬁned revenue and margin targets. The model enforces discount bounds,
budget constraints, bundle conﬂict resolution, and business rules. It integrates
price elasticity, cross-elasticity eﬀects, and demand forecasting to optimize
revenue-margin trade-oﬀs. Key inputs include promotional items, bundles, price
elasticity coeﬃcients, historical sales data, allocated budget, and business rules.
The outputs consist of optimal promotional selections, discount levels, and KPI
projections. The model assumes constant discounts during the promotion period,
stable inventory, and customer behavior aligned with historical trends. Our
two-step approach enhances eﬃciency. The ﬁrst step applies heuristics and preprocessing to ﬁlter items and bundles based on business rules and bundle conﬂicts, ensuring only viable candidates enter the optimization phase. The second
step employs a nonlinear MINLP framework to determine the optimal promotional items and discounts within deﬁned constraints. Refer to Table 1 for notation details and Algorithm 1 for the pseudocode of the proposed methodology.
3.1

Preprocessing

Before formulating the MINLP, we perform the following preprocessing steps:

Fig. 1. Architecture diagram.

Step 1: Scoring and Sorting: Items and bundles are scored based on the score
(Yi ) at optimal discount .(d∗i ). This score is calculated as:
where .Ri (d∗i ) and .Mi (d∗i ) are revenue and margin at optimal discount .d∗i . .λ is
weight that can be adjusted based on retailer preferences and historical data.
In our experiments, we used .λ = 0.6, reﬂecting a slightly greater emphasis on
revenue. .d∗i can be found by solving the below equation

.

.

d∗i = argmax(λ · Ri (di ) + (1 − λ) · Mi (di ))
di

either analytically or with the help of simulation. Items and bundles are then
sorted in descending order of the value of their score.
Step 2: Business Rule Filtering: Items and bundles are deselected based on
predeﬁned business rules. This creates a set of “mandatory de-list” items .(S),
which are removed from the sorted list. This is represented as: . i∈S δi = 0,
where .δi is a binary variable indicating whether item/bundle .i is selected for
promotion. These 2 steps give the ﬁltered list (F) of items and bundles as inputs
for the heuristics steps.
3.2

Complexity Analysis, Solver Eﬃciency, And Mo del Scalability

Solving Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming (MINLP) models is challenging
as these problems combine integer decision variables with nonlinear constraints,
making them NP-hard. To address this, we employ GEKKO with the APOPT
solver, which incorporates heuristic techniques to enhance computational eﬃciency and scalability. To enhance model scalability, we integrate heuristic techniques to provide a high-quality initial solution, reducing the computational
burden on the MINLP solver.
3.3

Heuristic Initialization

To provide a warm start to the MINLP solver, we use a two-step heuristic approach:
Greedy Selection: Items and bundles from the ﬁltered list (F, resulting
from preprocessing) are selected greedily. The algorithm iteratively selects
items/bundles and assigns a discount percentage while ensuring that the total
budget constraint is not exceeded.
The feasible discount is determined as the minimum of the optimal discount
and the closest discount to the optimal discount that ﬁts within the available
budget. The remaining budget is updated, and the process continues until the
budget is fully utilized. Refer Algorithm 1 in Fig. 2
Local Search: The greedy solution is iteratively improved using local search.
This involves adding or removing items or bundles and adjusting their discount
levels. The search continues until a maximum number of iterations is reached
or no further improvement in the objective function is observed. The best solution found during local search serves as the initial solution for reducing the

296

feasible search space, minimizing the number of branch-and-bound iterations
required. Refer Algorithm 2 in Fig. 2

of revenue and margin, while .w3 and .w4 are large values that ensure the targets
are met.

Algorithm 1: Pseudocode for Promo Optimization using MINLP
Framework
Input

: Targets, Total budget, Weights for objective, Penalty, bigM,
Business rules, Solver options
Variables: Optimal discount (d), Binary selection variable (δ)
Output : Total revenue r, total margin m, discount d, total budget used,
selected list of SKUs and bundles
Calculate sales, revenue, and margin for item and bundle level using
intermediate variables for optimal discount
Deﬁne objective (Refer Equation 1) with auxiliary variables (r and m) for total
revenue and total margin along with penalties for not reaching the targets
for SKU/Bundle i do
r[i] ≤ R[i] and r[i] ≤ bigM ·δ[i]
m[i] ≤ M [i] and m[i] ≤ bigM ·d[i]
Sum of margin loss due to promotion ≤ Total budget
Apply Business rules and resolve bundle conﬂicts
return Total revenue r, total margin m, discount d, total budget used, selected
list of SKUs and bundles

The ﬁnal output is the optimal set of promotional items, bundles and their
optimal discounts that maximizes the objective within the given constraints. In
the next section, we present our experiments and results on a real-world grocery
retailer data.

4

Experiments and Results

We developed a comprehensive Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming (MINLP)
model for promotion planning, integrating own-price and cross-price elasticity
using GEKKO’s APOPT solver [1]. This study utilizes two years of anonymized
grocery sales data, incorporating product attributes, pricing, and store performance. The analysis focuses on bundles and captures cross-product interactions
through a cross-price elasticity matrix. Historical sales data indicate that promotions typically span 5–6 days, with discounts ranging from 10% to 30%. Bundles,
consisting of complementary items (e.g., milk and bread) or multiple units of the
same SKU, are exclusively available during promotions. We give details of the

Optimizing Retail Promotions with Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming

299

model applied to a real-world grocery category, optimizing promotions for 10
product bundles and 5 individual products. Wall time for 10 SKUs was 484
ms, extrapolating to 716 ms for 50 SKUs, demonstrating sub-linear scalability,
making real-time deployment feasible.
4.1

to 35%, maximizing revenue and margin while adhering to constraints. Table
2 presents the selected items, optimal discount levels, and KPI values. P001,
P002, P003 are skus and the rest are bundles. Compared to historical discounting
strategies, our approach resulted in: 5.2% increase in revenue, 8.7% increase in
margin, 7% increase in sales units with 89.9% budget utilization.

Our framework allows retailers to prioritize revenue or margin by adjusting
objective function weights. Figure 3 shows the impact of weight variations:
Low weight on revenue (.w1) results in higher margin. High weight on revenue
increases revenue at the cost of margin. In Fig. 3, 3(a) corresponds to .w1 vs. .w2
vs. total revenue and 3(b) corresponds to .w1 vs. .w2 vs. total margin. In Fig. 3(a),
when .w1 is low and .w2 is high, total revenue is high. In Fig. 3(b), when .w1 is
high and .w2 is low, total margin is high.
4.3

Model Validation

Robustness and Sensitivity Analysis: The model was tested across multiple random starting points, with sensitivity analysis using discount perturbations
over 100 iterations. Results showed minimal deviation in KPIs, conﬁrming solution stability. While steeper discounts improved objective values, they violated
budget constraints, reinforcing the optimality of our two-step solution.

300

G. Uma Maheswari et al.

Fig. 3. Revenue and Margin frontiers with respect to weights.

Comparison with Existing Approaches: Comparison with Existing
Approaches: As shown in Table 3, our MINLP model outperformed greedy
heuristics and SLSQP-based nonlinear optimization, demonstrating superior performance: Greedy heuristic: 2.88% margin increase (Mgn Chd), SLSQP optimization: 3.71% margin increase and MINLP optimization: 4.5% margin increase.
While revenue was slightly lower, MINLP’s eﬃcient budget utilization makes it
a cost-eﬀective strategy.

Impact of Cross-Elasticity Eﬀects: Including cross-elasticity eﬀects led to:
7.6% increase in revenue (Rev Chd) and 10.05% increase in margin (Table 4).
This highlights the model’s ability to strategically adjust discounts to optimize
category performance.

.−1.85% and Margin decrease: .−1.83%. Increasing revenue and margin targets

uses more budget and gives discount to the de-listed item having less target
values and produces higher revenue and margin.

4.5

Forming New Bundles

Retailers often use product bundles to boost sales, targeting customer preferences
identiﬁed through historical sales data. We formed new bundles using Market
Basket Analysis on POS data to identify the top product combinations. One
example of a new bundle consisted of milk bundled in pairs with one or more units
of bread or eggs. We set the discounts as 2% higher than the sum of historical
discounts for the constituent products to make bundles more appealing. After the
inclusion of new bundles, one of the existing bundles got de-listed and four out of
ﬁve of the new bundles were listed. Considering new bundles improved revenue
by 21.32% and margin by 13.96% compared to without considering new bundles.
Higher revenue targets were achieved and low-performing bundles were excluded,
demonstrating the eﬃciency of this approach (see Table 5). We summarize the
key conclusions based on our work in the next section.

Conclusion

This study demonstrates the eﬀectiveness of Mixed-Integer Nonlinear Programming (MINLP) in optimizing retail promotion planning by addressing complex,
nonlinear factors such as cross-elasticity, bundle conﬂicts, hoarding behavior,
and budget constraints. Unlike conventional methods relying on linearization or
heuristics, MINLP directly models these nonlinear interactions, leading to superior performance in real-world retail scenarios. The framework outperformed
greedy heuristics and standard nonlinear solvers. It achieved a 4.5% margin
increase compared to historical discounts and with better budget utilization.
Additionally, incorporating new bundles led to a 21.32% revenue increase and
a 13.96% margin improvement, utilizing 81.22% of the allocated budget. The
MINLP approach is particularly suited for medium-scale retail settings involving
50–100 SKUs and bundles within a category, ensuring computational feasibility
while enabling optimal product selection, bundle conﬁgurations, and discount
strategies. The inclusion of what-if analyses enhances strategic decision-making,
allowing retailers to adapt to dynamic market conditions and improve negotiations with vendors. This research underscores the practical application of MINLP
in retail promotion planning and highlights its scalability. Future work could
extend this approach to larger-scale problems, real-time data integration, and
personalized promotions, as well as relaxing current assumptions to accommodate evolving retail strategies.

Research for their help in reviewing the paper. This work was done during their course
of their internship here.

Solving the Semivectorial Bilevel Hazmat
Network Design Problem
by a Deconstructive Heuristic Algorithm
José-Fernando Camacho-Vallejo(B) and José Emmanuel Gómez-Rocha
Tecnologico de Monterrey, Escuela de Ingeniería y Ciencias, Ave. Eugenio Garza Sada
2501 Sur, Monterrey, Nuevo León 64849, Mexico
fernando.camacho@tec.mx

Abstract. The transportation of hazardous materials (hazmat) poses
signiﬁcant risks to urban areas, necessitating careful planning to minimize potential exposure. This study addresses a variant of the Bilevel
Hazmat Network Design Problem, introducing an additional objective to
balance the usage of certain roads, thereby preventing the overexposure
of speciﬁc roads and zones within the city. The problem is mathematically formulated as a semivectorial bilevel optimization model, where the
leader’s objective seeks to minimize risk, and the follower optimizes two
objectives: minimizing transportation costs and balancing road usage. To
address the challenge of determining the follower’s optimal reaction to
the leader’s decisions, we obtain a Pareto front for the follower’s objectives. To simplify this process, we make a realistic assumption regarding
the follower’s response and propose a heuristic deconstructive algorithm
tailored to the problem’s complex structure. Computational experiments
are conducted on instances derived from real-life case study to evaluate
the performance of the proposed approach. The results demonstrate the
heuristic’s eﬀectiveness in designing low-risk networks based on the hazmat ﬂow determined by the follower. Additionally, managerial insights
are provided, emphasizing the approach’s practical applicability to urban
hazmat management.
Keywords: Hazmat Network Design · Bilevel Optimization ·
Semivectorial Optimization · Deconstructive Heuristic

1

Introduction

The transportation of hazardous materials (hazmat) in urban areas is essential
due to the regular generation and disposal of such substances by companies
across various industries. This topic has been clearly positionated in [10,11,
15, 20]. Most hazardous materials cannot be discharged into drainage systems
because of their detrimental impact on ecosystems. Simultaneously, companies
requiring hazmat for their operations must receive and transport these materials
via existing road networks, necessitating eﬃcient and safe logistics planning.
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 304–315, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_23

Semivectorial Bilevel Hazmat Network Design

305

While the transportation of hazmat is essential, it inherently carries the risk
of accidents that could adversely impact surrounding urban areas. To address
this concern, local authorities can implement policies aimed at mitigating these
risks, such as enforcing stricter safety regulations, optimizing the network design
of the transportation system, among others (see [2]).
In this paper, we focus on a government authority tasked with optimizing
the network design of available roads for transporting hazmat. The objective
is to reduce risk while accounting for carriers as independent decision-makers
within a lower-level hierarchy. Based on the network design determined by the
government authority, the carriers seek to minimize the costs of their routes
while also aiming to balance road usage. This second objective helps prevent
congestion and potential disruptions in hazmat transportation.
This secondary objective, considered by the carriers, is often neglected in
studies addressing the hazmat transportation problem. However, we argue that
overlooking it may result in the overexposure of speciﬁc zones, posing risks to
both drivers and local inhabitants. Hence, we promote equitable risk distribution
over the transportation network. Additionally, to the best of our knowledge, this
objective has not been previously addressed when studying this type of problems.
To address the problem under study, we propose a bilevel programming model
in which the higher-level hierarchy corresponds to the government authority
(hereafter referred to as the leader), while the lower-level hierarchy comprises the
carriers (hereafter referred to as the followers). The leader determines which road
segments to habilitate within the transportation network, whereas the followers
select the routes to transport their commodities. As outlined above, the leader’s
objective is to minimize risk, while each follower simultaneously aims to minimize
routing costs and balance the usage of the roads traversed.
It is noteworthy that the resulting bilevel problem falls into the category of
semivectorial bilevel problems, as the follower addresses a bi-objective problem
(see [5,8]). This introduces additional technical complexities, as the concept of
an “optimal solution” diﬀers from that of traditional single-ob jective problems.
Further details will be provided later.
To solve the problem under study, we propose a novel heuristic algorithm
based on a deconstructive framework combined with a nested approach. Given
the nature of the problem and the interaction between the leader and the follower, we adopt the pessimistic approach for bilevel problems (see [13,19]) aiming to ﬁnd the pessimistic solution as deﬁned in [1]. Computational experiments
validate our proposal, demonstrating that the algorithm is an eﬀective tool for
addressing this complex problem.
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 provides a brief
literature review of relevant bilevel optimization problems closely related to hazmat transportation. The semivectorial bilevel mathematical model is presented
in Sect. 3, followed by the development of the proposed algorithm in Sect. 4.
Section 5 discusses the computational experiments conducted and the results
obtained. Finally, Sect. 6 concludes the paper by highlighting key ﬁndings and
proposing directions for future research.

306

2

J.-F. Camacho-Vallejo and J. E. Gómez-Rocha

Literature Review

The hazmat transportation problem was ﬁrst modeled using a bilevel optimization approach in [12], where it was framed as a network design problem. Additionally, a path-based formulation was adopted for modeling the follower’s problem. By exploiting the structure of the resulting follower’s problem, a single-level
reformulation can be achieved using the KKT optimality conditions. Their approach was applied to a case study from Ontario, Canada, with 48 nodes, 57 arcs,
and 4 commodities. A similar network design approach was presented in [17],
where the same problem was studied, but alternative solutions that represent
a compromise between transportation risk and economic aspects for the leader
and the follower were obtained.
Another approach to studying this complex problem from a network design
perspective is proposed in [9]. The main diﬀerence lies in the fact that the followers do not adopt a path-based formulation but rather a ﬂow-based one. It is
important to emphasize that the model considered in our paper is based on the
bilevel formulation proposed in [9]. To solve the problem, they proposed a heuristic algorithm based on greedy solutions for each commodity and an overlapping
criterion to form the solutions. Numerical experimentation was conducted using
a case study from Ravenna, Italy, involving 105 nodes, 134 edges, and 5 commodities.
It is evident that a network design approach is suitable for modeling this
problem. Nevertheless, it can also be analyzed under a toll optimization approach. In this framework, the government authority imposes diﬀerent tolls on
certain arcs in the network, requiring carriers to decide whether to use the tolled
arcs by paying the toll. This scheme is considered in [7], where the follower’s
problem employs a path-based formulation, which is exploited in the constructive heuristic algorithm. Following the same approach, [21] uses tolls to minimize
risk; however, the followers are interdependent, and their user equilibrium must
be determined to ensure feasible bilevel solutions. A simulated annealing algorithm is developed for the leader’s solutions, while a diagonalization algorithm
is implemented to address the variational inequalities that arise in the followers’
problem. A case study from northern China is used, consisting of 18 arcs and a
single commodity.
Recently, another variant of the bilevel hazmat problem was studied in [18],
where a robust bilevel optimization model is proposed. The overall structure
remains the same: the leader addresses a network design problem and aims to
minimize risk, while the follower selects paths that minimize costs. However, the
novelty lies in considering the probability of accidents occurring on diﬀerent arcs.
The authors propose a single-level reformulation by leveraging the primal and
dual optimality conditions. Their approach is tested on a case study involving
Beijing, Tianjin, and Hebei, China, with 13 nodes, 42 arcs, and 2 commodities.
As evident from the previously cited references, in all cases, the leader aims
to minimize the risk associated with the transportation decisions made by the
followers. However, in [3], a biobjective version of the hazmat bilevel problem
is studied. In addition to considering risk, the leader also seeks to minimize

Semivectorial Bilevel Hazmat Network Design

307

the imposed tolls. Similar to previous studies, the followers aim to traverse the
cheapest routes. Due to the nature of this problem, a Pareto front was provided
as the leader’s solution, obtained using a particle swarm optimization algorithm.
A case study involving 19 nodes, 31 arcs, and 37 commodities, set in the southern
United States, is analyzed.
It is important to highlight that a biobjective bilevel problem is distinct
from a semivectorial bilevel problem. In the former, a Pareto front is obtained
as the solution to the problem, where the follower’s optimal reaction can also
be determined. In the latter, solving the problem involves obtaining a unique
leader’s solution, while a Pareto front represents the follower’s reaction. Certain
assumptions must then be made to select one solution from the follower’s Pareto
front. For more details, the reader is referred to [14]. To the best of the authors’
knowledge, this is the ﬁrst paper where a semivectorial bilevel approach is applied
to a variant of the hazmat problem.

3

Mathematical Formulation

The network design problem for the transportation of hazmat is deﬁned on a
directed graph .G = (N, A), where .N is the set of nodes and .A is the set of arcs in
the graph. In the context of our problem, a node corresponds to an intersection
of two or more roads, while an arc represents an enabled road segment of the
transportation network. We consider a multi-commodity problem, which requires
all commodities in .K to be transported from their respective origins to their
destinations. Speciﬁcally, each commodity .k ∈ K is associated with an origindestination pair .(o(k), d(k)). We assume that both origins and destinations are
intersections (nodes) of the network, meaning .o(k), d(k) ∈ N . The number of
shipments to be transported for each commodity .k ∈ K is denoted by .sk .
When a shipment of commodity .k ∈ K is transported through an arc .a =

aﬀected in the event of an accident. Similarly, there is a transportation cost .ckij
associated with each commodity .k ∈ K and each arc .(i, j) ∈ A.
Decision variables can be partitioned into the leader’s and follower’s variables.
The leader’s binary variable .yij denotes the habilitation or construction of arc
.(i, j) ∈ A and .(j, i) ∈ A in the network.
On the other hand, deﬁne .xkij as the follower’s binary decision variable, which
indicates whether commodity .k ∈ K is transported through the arc .(i, j) ∈ A
(.xkij = 1), or not (.xkij = 0).
To assist with the notation, for each commodity .k ∈ K, let us deﬁne the
auxiliary parameter .bk (i), which helps with the ﬂow balance at node .i ∈ N , as
follows:

Expression (2) reﬂects the leader’s objective to minimize the risk associated
with hazmat transportation. Constraints (3) indicate that if arc .(i, j) is habilitated, then arc .(j, i) will also be available. The binary nature of the leader’s
decision variables is denoted in (4). Expression (5) indicates that the follower’s
variables must be the optimal solution of another optimization problem. Speciﬁcally, in the problem under study, the follower considers a bi-objective problem.
The ﬁrst objective function aims to minimize the cost of transporting hazmat
from their origin to their destination, while the second aims to balance the
usage of the available arcs in the network. In other words, congestion or overuse
of some arcs is avoided. Equations (6) ensure ﬂow conservation in the network.
Constraints (7) guarantee that hazmat are transported only by available arcs.
Constraints (8) help count the maximum number of commodities using an arc,
while constraints (9) help identify the minimum number of commodities using
an arc in the network. Finally, (10) indicates the binary nature of the follower’s
variables.
3.1

Characteristics of the Semivectorial Bilevel Problem
and Assumptions

A key characteristic of the semivectorial bilevel programming model deﬁned by
equations (2)–(10) is that, although multiple followers are considered, each follower addresses their own independent routing problem. It is important to note

Semivectorial Bilevel Hazmat Network Design

309

that congestion is not taken into account; instead, the balance in the use of
enabled road segments is treated as a distinct concept. Consequently, the followers’ problems can be aggregated into a single equivalent problem encompassing
all followers, as demonstrated by [6]. Therefore, we will refer to a single follower
rather than the aggregated problem representing all followers.
Another important aspect worth highlighting is that a feasible solution to a
bilevel problem consists of a feasible solution for the leader and the corresponding
optimal response from the follower to that leader’s solution [16]. However, in
this speciﬁc problem, the follower’s problem is biobjective. This means that,
in general, the follower’s response consists of multiple non-dominated solutions
that form the Pareto front associated with the leader’s decision. While these
non-dominated solutions are mathematically equivalent in terms of optimality,
each may have a signiﬁcantly diﬀerent impact on the leader’s objective function.
Naturally, certain assumptions must be made before solving the problem. We
will focus on ﬁnding the pessimistic solution, which resembles a non-cooperative
scenario. Speciﬁcally, if the leader proposes a network design and the follower has
multiple non-dominated solutions in the corresponding Pareto front, the follower
is not obligated to select routes that minimize the risk (the leader’s objective).
Since cooperation is not deemed reasonable for this particular problem, the pessimistic solution is sought, which corresponds to the non-dominated solution for
the follower that leads to the highest risk.
Therefore, the pessimistic solution consists of the pair (.x, .y p ), where .x is a
feasible partial solution for the leader and .y p is the pessimistic follower’s response
from the associated Pareto front. The solution (.x, .y p ) that yields the minimum
risk is selected as the optimal solution for this problem under the pessimistic
approach. In other words, it is the best solution among the worst ones.
Finally, another important and reasonable assumption is that the leader’s
solution must ensure the creation of feasible routes for the follower. If it is not
possible to construct a route for any commodity, the network design proposed by
the leader is deemed infeasible and should not be considered. This assumption
aligns with the concepts outlined by [4], where it is emphasized that the follower
must have some capacity to respond to the leader’s decision.

4

A Nested Deconstructive Heuristic Algorithm

In this section, we detail all the phases involved in the proposed algorithm.
Recall that we are addressing a semivectorial bilevel problem. Consequently,
the algorithm proceeds as follows: ﬁrst, a feasible solution for the problem is
created. That is, the leader proposes a network design; and then, the follower
identiﬁes the corresponding Pareto front and selects the pessimistic solution;
now, the leader’s objective function value (risk) is computed; second, a greedy
deconstructive procedure is applied to reduce the size of the network; and third,
an intensiﬁcation phase is applied to improve the current solution. This threephase algorithmic scheme is repeated until a predeﬁned stopping criterion is
met.

310

J.-F. Camacho-Vallejo and J. E. Gómez-Rocha

4.1

Initializing a Feasible Bilevel Solution

In this section, we describe the manner in which feasible semivectorial bilevel
solutions are obtained. To achieve this, three steps are followed: creating the
leader’s solutions, obtaining the pessimistic follower’s reaction, and computing
the leader’s objective function value.
Constructing Leader’s Solutions. Since we are addressing a multicommodity problem, where each commodity may be associated with independent followers, a constructive procedure would provide limited insight into the
solution under construction until it is fully completed. Therefore, we opted to
start with the complete network, enabling all possible road segments. This initial
conﬁguration serves as the leader’s ﬁrst solution (.y C ).
Obtaining the Follower’s Pessimistic Reaction. Once the initial network
design is decided, the follower’s problem is solved. That means, the Pareto front
associated to .y C is obtained via the well-known .ε-constraint method.
Let us focus on the follower’s problem (5)–(10), parameterized by .y C . The
ﬁrst objective function in equation (5) is associated with the cost of transporting
hazmat, while the second objective aims to balance the usage of road segments to
prevent excessive exposure for inhabitants near those roads. This second objective function is deﬁned as the diﬀerence between the maximum and minimum
number of times all commodities use a speciﬁc arc. It is important to note that
this objective function ranges between 0 and .|K|, corresponding to a perfectly
balanced network and a scenario where all commodities are transported through
the same arc, respectively. Consequently, this second objective function is treated
as a constraint within the .ε-constraint method.
The single-objective problem iteratively solved for diﬀerent values of .ε is the
following:
.

this method.
By implementing this .ε-constraint method, the corresponding follower’s
Pareto front is obtained for that leader’s decision .y C , that is .ParetoFront(y C ).
The pessimistic solution .xp is selected as the follower’s response. Speciﬁcally:
xp ∈ arg max (2) : x ∈ ParetoFront(y C )

.

(18)

Evaluating the Leader’s Solution. Once a leader’s solution (.y C ) and the
corresponding follower’s response (.xp ) are known, the leader’s objective function
can be computed. Recall that the aim is to minimize the risk associated with
the designed network and its usage when transporting the commodities.
To simplify the notation, let us deﬁne the leader’s objective function
expressed in equation (2) as .L(y, x), and in this case, we are evaluating .L(y C , xp ).
4.2

A Greedy Deconstructive Procedure

First, we need to consider that we are dealing with a complete network, meaning
that all the arcs are habilitated. The greedy deconstructive procedure begins by
identifying the arc with the highest risk for each commodity, which is then eliminated from the network only if there exists an alternative route for transporting
that speciﬁc commodity. This procedure is carried out for each commodity.
Once the minimal network is deconstructed in a greedy fashion (.y G ), the
follower’s pessimistic response (.xp ) must be obtained by following the procedure
described above. Finally, the leader’s objective function is evaluated, that is,
G
p
.L(y , x ) is computed.
The aim of this deconstructive procedure is to reduce the size of the network
while minimizing the risk associated with hazmat transportation.
4.3

Improvement of the Feasible Semivectorial Bilevel Solution

This phase aims to enhance the currently created solution (.y G , xp ). The proposed procedure consists of selecting a predeﬁned number of habilitated arcs (.γ)
and replacing them with .γ non-habilitated arcs. By doing this exploration, we
maintain a minimal network.
As expected, the assumption that at least one route must exist for each
commodity may be violated under this scheme. In such cases, the neighboring
solution is discarded. Otherwise, the pessimistic follower’s response is obtained
by implementing the .ε-constraint method explained above. This particular characteristic adds the “nested” term to the algorithm’s description.
Certainly, diﬀerent neighborhoods may be explored to reduce infeasibility or
to repair these infeasible network designs. However, this is beyond the scope of
this study. Our goal is to ﬁnd a new network design (.y N ) that yields a lower
risk, that is, .L(y N , xp ) < L(y G , xp ).

312

J.-F. Camacho-Vallejo and J. E. Gómez-Rocha

To add diversity, the greedy deconstructive procedure is repeated, but with
the order in which the commodities are considered for reducing the size of the
network varied. Then, the improvement phase is also repeated. The complete
cycles of the proposed heuristic algorithm are repeated until a maximum number
of iterations without improvement is reached.

5

Computational Experimentation

To validate the proposed heuristic algorithm, we consider the most recent case
study addressing the hazmat transportation problem, presented in [18]. The case
study involves 13 nodes, 42 arcs, and 2 commodities. To further demonstrate the
robustness of our proposed algorithm, we extended the number of commodities
to 5, 10, 15, and 20.
The computational environment consists of a personal computer equipped
with an Intel R CoreTM i7-11370H @ 3.30 GHz processor and 32 GB of RAM
memory. The algorithm was implemented in Python 3.10 and the .ε-constraint
method used Gurobi 12.0.
After preliminary experimentation, the parameters of the heuristic algorithm are set to .γ = 2, meaning that two habilitated arcs are replaced by two
non-habilitated arcs. Additionally, the maximum number of iterations without
improvement is set to .M axIter = 10.
The obtained results are shown in Table 1. The ﬁrst column indicates the label
of the instance (derived from the case study), the next three columns deﬁne the
size of the instance. The ﬁfth column reports the minimum leader’s risk obtained
by the pessimistic solution. The last column indicates the computational time
required to solve the problem in seconds. It is important to highlight that the risk
values reported in Table 1 correspond to the pessimistic solution in the context
of semivectorial bilevel problems. While other solutions may yield diﬀerent risk
values, we consider the pessimistic approach to be more appropriate for this
problem. To illustrate the evolution of risk within the algorithm, Fig. 1 presents
the leader’s risk obtained at each iteration when solving instance case-study5.

the arcs connecting them, which consequently increases the risk. Moreover, the

6

Conclusions and Further Research Directions

In this paper, we study a variant of the well-known bilevel hazmat transportation
problem. As is typical, the leader determines the network design with the objective of minimizing the risk associated with the routes chosen by the follower.
The follower, in turn, is responsible for shipping commodities and deciding on
the routes to optimize both costs and the network’s usage balance. This additional feature transforms the problem into a semivectorial bilevel one, further
increasing its complexity, as the follower’s optimal response involves selecting a
solution from the corresponding Pareto front for a given leader’s decision.
Based on the relationship between the decision-makers in this problem, we
adopt the optimistic approach and develop a nested deconstructive heuristic
algorithm to obtain extreme pessimistic solutions. We apply our proposed algorithm to a real case study and test it on various instances. The results demonstrate that our algorithm eﬃciently solves the problem.
Future research directions include exploring alternative solutions such as optimistic, rewarding, deceiving, best compromise, or neutral, which are based on
diﬀerent assumptions and aim to capture varying follower behaviors. Additionally, the computational experimentation could be improved by incorporating a
wider range of synthetic and real-world instances to further validate the applicability of the proposed algorithm.
Disclosure of Interests. The authors have no competing interests to declare that
are relevant to the content of this article.

Applications in Industry, Energy
and Supply Chain

Optimizing and Reducing Changeover Time
to Enhance Line Balancing in Industrial
Injection Processes
Yasmine El Belghiti1,2(B)

, Abdelfattah Mouloud1,2
and Aziz Soulhi2

, Mehdi El Bouchti1 ,

1 ESITH Higher School of Textile and Clothing Industries, Casablanca, Morocco

Yasmine.elbelghiti@esith.net
2 ENSMR National Higher School of Mines of Rabat, Rabat, Morocco

Abstract. The purpose of this paper is to propose a comprehensive methodology
for reducing mold changeover time in the plastic injection molding industry by
using Lean Manufacturing tools, the approach, and SMED techniques enhanced
with artificial intelligence (AI) for decision- making and optimization.
First, we will identify the problem using the 5-Why tool. The measurement
phase involves the collection of data using time studies and computer-aided design
(CAD) to identify failures. In the analysis phase, we will try to address critical
tasks and support our analysis with Failure Mode and Effect Analysis (FMEA).
The innovate phase enables us to improve the sequence of external and internal
tasks, using real-time data to adjust the process. In addition, the methodology
helped to balance the production line by redistributing tasks and reducing bottlenecks, hence improving flow and reducing downtime.
Application of this approach not only reduced mold changeover time by 65%,
but also improved the overall balance of the production line, therefore boosting
productivity and operational efficiency. The control phase led to standardize these
improvements, re-establish a continuous process improvement cycle, and establish
a sustainable line balance.
Furthermore, continuous improvement is ensured through standardized control mechanisms powered by fuzzy decision systems, thus establishing sustainable
and optimized line balancing.
Keywords: Artificial intelligence · Line balancing · Injection Processes ·
DMAIC · SMED

1 Introduction
In the highly competitive landscape of modern manufacturing, optimizing operational
efficiency and productivity is essential for sustaining profitability and securing a competitive advantage [1]. The plastic injection molding technology is essential in the automotive sector but is under constant pressure regarding the prolonged mold changeover
times (MCT). An extended MCT is bound to a great deal of hours of machine downtime,
production being interrupted, and a corresponding reduction in overall efficiency, thus
necessitating some innovative strategies towards reducing the MCT [2].
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 319–328, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_24

320

Y. E. Belghiti et al.

This study will address the issue of reducing MCT in plastic injection molding, integrating the methodology of SMED into the Lean Manufacturing framework. The research
adopts the DMAIC (Define, Measure, Analyze, Improve, Control)-driven approach to
systematically identify inefficiencies, eliminate non-value-adding activities, and implement targeted improvements [3, 4]. The fuzzy logic augments Hot Request to improve
flexibility and nearby decision-making via a predictive layer that models the uncertainty
of situations that may arise in a factory setting. Using real-time data, fuzzy logic allows
for dynamic process optimization and improves general responsiveness across systems
[5].
The methodology combines Value Stream Mapping (VSM), time-motion study, and
statistical tools in recognizing the bottlenecks and building the structured intervention
on these issues [6]. By transferring this time-consuming task from internal to external
scope, for instance, through the standardization of processes, this method will lead to a
significant reduction in changeover time and improvement of Process Cycle Efficiency
(PCE). The research develops a fuzzy controller for scheduling and equipment utilization
for improved flexibility of a more adaptive and resilient production system [7].
The results of this study indicate successful integration of Lean Manufacturing and
intelligent decision-support systems, resulting in significant reduction of MCT, enhanced
production line balance, and long-term operational benefits. The amalgamation of lean
tools with fuzzy logic offers great potential of data-driven approaches towards modern
manufacturing-a route towards flexible and intelligent production [8].
Fuzzy logic is proving to be a useful tool when dealing with several complex problems
that point conventional decision-making systems to their limit. It was applicable to six
sigma as fuzzy logic characterizes the uncertainty and imprecision reflecting operating
data. In a predictive situation, fuzzy logic uniquely allows performance and change
requirements to be predicted from linguistic variables and nonlinear interactions that
are difficult to express through standard mathematical methods. This opens doors to
practical solutions that can withstand uncertainty and versatility in an industrial context.

2 Experimental Design and Approach
In this research project, a Lean Six Sigma framework that utilizes the DMAIC methodology is used to reduce molded changeover times from plastic injection molding. DMAIC
enables a structured approach for operational improvement that incorporates identifying
bottlenecks and non-value-adding activities [9].
The Define stage engaged setting key objectives related to the reducing mold-change
time for productivity and flexibility. Measure involves collecting some baseline data
of mold-change times and machine downtime with techniques such as time-motion
studies. The Analyze phase uses some tools, such as the Fishbone and Value Stream
Mapping, to determine the root cause of inefficiencies, especially in tooling preparation
and scheduling [10, 11].
The Improve phase delivers a solution that is targeted toward root causes that will
allow a smoother flow, better line balance, and decrease in variations and interruptions.
Control calls for generalization of these improvements, leveraging artificial intelligence
and fuzzy logic to monitor and continually optimize these processes [12, 13].
