Fig. 1. Fuzzy Inference Process

3 Results and Interpretations
3.1 Implementation of Lean Tools in the Mold Change Process
Post this implementation, the improvement phase will focus on reducing the time taken
for mold changeover and to fix process inefficiencies thereafter. Some of the key methods
employed to achieve drastic reductions in changeover times while enhancing overall
productivity are SDMED and the 5S technique [3, 14].
Implementation of SMED
A detailed analysis of the mold changeover process was conducted through time measurement and process flow evaluations. The SMED (Single-Minute Exchange of Die)
methodology was implemented to categorize tasks into value-added and non-valueadded activities. Steps that contributed to delays or resource waste were identified for
removal. Meanwhile, value-added tasks were further classified into internal (performed
while the machine is idle) and external (carried out while the machine is operational).
The objective was to reduce internal activities by converting as many as possible into
external ones, thereby minimizing downtime and eliminating inefficiencies [15].
The equipment operators went through special training programs to equip them with
satisfactory mold change-over procedures and minimize production stoppages. This
step-by-step changeover process was put in writing in a standard operating procedure so
that it is guaranteed uniform, and less variation of performance occurred. Also, workforce development could be based on Total Productive Maintenance, training operators
in relevant skills for preventive and corrective maintenance. Such an approach reduced
downtimes for equipment and stabilized production flow.
Implementation of the 5S Methodology
The 5S methodology was applied in this study to address recurring machine stoppages
and disorganized work areas that contributed to operational inefficiencies [16].
This approach follows five structured steps:
• Sort: Discernment among necessary and unnecessary tools, materials, and instructions has allies in an organized and efficient disposition of the workspace.

322

Y. E. Belghiti et al.

• Set in Order: Systematically arrange essential tools and equipment to ensure quick
access and minimize search time.
• Shine: Maintain a clean and inspected work environment to prevent hazards and
ensure seamless production.
• Standardize: Determine a uniform procedure for the changeover of molds to assure
uniformity in task execution.
• Sustain: Routinely audit and heighten the standards of compliance to maintain a neat
and orderly workspace.
Improved VSM for Mold Change Operations
The implementation of Lean tools did, however, show many concrete results concerning
the VSM of the mold change process: an increase in value-added time and an 86.72%
decrease in non-value-added time. The new VSM is shown in Fig. 1.

Fig. 2. Improved value stream of the assembly operation.

With the considerable improvement in the non-value-added time, it is now possible
to carry out a mold change procedure with more efficient and productive speeds being
adopted. Besides, robots were introduced into the molding processes, thus reducing
human operations’ necessity by 40%, improved speed, precision, and consistency. This
further led to shorter cycle times and reduced defects.
3.2 Future PCE of the Mold Change Process
The benchmarks of minimum 25% for Process Cycle Efficiency have been exceeded,
thanks to the adoption of Lean principles. Initially it stood at 27.50%, and later it was
recorded at overwhelming rate of 72.50%, an overall improvement of 45.5% [17]. It
is significant in this regard to consider that a combination of optimal use of resources
and elimination of waste has brought forth a 46.8% improvement in PCE. Also, there
is a 27.9% cut-off lead time as compared to the first baseline. An evident proof of an
effective application of Lean tools, the reduction of process time with the enhancement
of operational efficiency [18].
The control phase focused on maintaining performance improvements by continuously monitoring quality and measuring system output against predefined bench- marks.

Optimizing Mold Changeover Times in the Automotive Injection Industry

323

Effective tracking mechanisms were implemented to detect deviations from set standards promptly. To safeguard these improvements, a Standardized Work Plan (SWP)
was developed to formalize procedures, particularly for mold assembly and component
integration.
Additionally, a structured document review process was established under the compliance team’s supervision to ensure traceability and support the ongoing enhancement
of operational practices. Regular training and refresher sessions were also conducted for
operators, reinforcing continuous improvement methodologies such as 5S and SMED,
integrating these principles into daily operations.
Control charts were used to ensure process stability by analyzing variations in the
duration of mold changeovers to other desired limits. All of these together enhance the
integration of Lean Six Sigma tools leading to a subsequent reduction of changeover
times and improved reliability of the processes involved [9, 10, 19].
3.3 Standardization and Continuous Optimization with Fuzzy Logic
In the Improve phase, any improvements implemented should be standardized and monitored to ensure stability throughout the years. Traditional monitoring systems deploy
fixed thresholds, which may not efficiently handle variations that arise when mold
changeovers occur. As a solution to this challenge, a fuzzy logic SMED model coupled with an adaptive and intelligent decision-making process was developed to tackle
the evolving dynamics of the environment of production [20].
• Implementation of the Fuzzy SMED Model
The fuzzy logic system aids decision-making through selected factors influencing
mold changeover durations. These factors include the mold size small, medium, or
large; how well the robots adapt fully or partially; and complexity of the mold simple or
complex.
At the starting point, all input variables are continuous membership functions, providing for a smoother shift between categories instead of being expressed as firm classifications. Based on the input applied, fuzzy rules would then be assessed by the machine,
providing the expected changeover time as short, medium, or long. This is intended to
be an operational decision support, with a view to optimizing the mold changeover time
through lowering the effort required for adaptation.
• Fuzzy-Based Decision Support for SMED
The fuzzy inference rule base is constructed based on both expert knowledge and
historical production data.
When small, the robotic arm is optimized fully, and the design has a low level of
complexity associated with it allowing short changeover time and basically all other
features related to faster molds.
But as the mold grows large, the robot hand will be faced with a lot of challenges to
adapt to the changes its design has to undergo, causing it to take quite a long changeover
time.
Conditions on the mid-range, however, are evaluated using flexible methods instead
of rigid, fixed classifications.

324

Y. E. Belghiti et al.

The adaptive aspect of this system ensures that all the components contributing to
error in actual production environments will be considered in the standardization phase.
As a result, it will be possible to obtain more realistic predictions of changeover time,
which greatly enhances the system’s responsiveness to change in process.
• Control System Integration
The prominent operations done during the fuzzy control system’s implementation
are:
– Mold changeover times are monitored with fuzzy logic to detect variations early, and
this information can be used to mitigate potential production bottlenecks.
– There is live visual monitoring of mold changeover performance through integration
with monitoring systems such as Qi Macros in Excel.
– The system assures operator feedback, assisting decision-making adjustments in relevant variables and thereby preventing problems from escalating or rising, avoiding
delays.
– Benefits of the Fuzzy SMED Model
Some advantages of fuzzy logic in the Control phase are:
– Adaptive Decisions: In real time, performance is used to make the decision as to
whether a mold changeover was acceptable based on ongoing changes in the process
and not on set time for targets.
– Real-Time Monitoring: The extremely fast identification of inefficient activities,
which enables the organization to address issues before they disrupt the operations.
– Sustainable Changes: The continuous analysis of changeover activities allows the
system to quickly highlight inefficiencies and address the root causes.
Use your confidence, fuzzy logic combines with SMED in the Control phase to
solidify improvements while continuously adjusting to real-time production floor data,
this gives way for enhancement in decision-making, reducing variability in operations,
and ensuring the mold changeover process continually runs efficiently.

Fig. 3. Output variable “time change”.

3.4 Interpretation of Results
The membership curves of fuzzy logic presented in Fig. 2 give a very clear idea of how
this system acts upon the input and output variables to support decision-making. The
input variables are size classification, with the choice of small, medium, or large; robot
hand adaptation, defined as adapted or unsuitable; and mold complexity is classified
as simple or not. These membership functions allow smooth transitions between categories, taking into consideration the uncertainties and variations typical to manufacturing
environments.
Figure 2 gives representation to the output variable that pertains to changeover time
which is divided into short, medium, and long durations. By suitably combining these
input variables with fuzzy inference rules, the framework is able to provide an adaptive
and flexible prediction for changeover time. This in turn permits better estimations of
setup time and contributes to improvements in the efficiencies of process flow. Figures 3
and 4 demonstrate how some of the key factors affect mold changeover time.

Fig. 4. Surface Visualization of Mold Size, Adaptation, and Changeover Time.

The analysis reveals a strong correlation between mold size and the adaptability
of robotic handling systems. Specifically, larger molds combined with insufficiently
adapted robotic arms tend to result in significantly longer changeover durations. This is
further compounded by mold complexity, where intricate geometries and configurations
necessitate additional adjustments, thereby extending the setup time. The cumulative
effect of these variables underscores the importance of considering both physical and
operational constraints in changeover optimization.
Moreover, the study highlights the critical role of robotic adaptability in mitigating
these delays. Enhanced robotic flexibility—particularly in systems capable of adjusting to varying mold sizes and complexities—demonstrates a measurable reduction in
changeover time, even under challenging conditions. The 3D surface visualizations provide a comprehensive view of these interactions, illustrating how fuzzy logic effectively
models the nonlinear relationships between key parameters.
This hybrid approach, combining SMED with fuzzy inference systems, proves especially valuable for production line balancing. By dynamically adjusting to real-time
input variables and uncertainties, the system ensures a more even distribution of tasks
and resources across the line. This leads to reduced idle time, improved synchronization between operations, and a more resilient production flow. Ultimately, the integration of intelligent decision-making into traditional Lean tools offers a scalable and
adaptive solution for achieving sustainable line balancing in complex manufacturing
environments.

326

Y. E. Belghiti et al.

4 Value of Line Balancing Through the Proposed Methodology
This methodology demonstrates a strong capacity to improve the balance of production lines significantly. In this study, tasks were carefully examined and restructured
using Lean Six Sigma tools in combination with fuzzy logic. This strategic approach
effectively reduces bottlenecks and promotes a more balanced and efficient allocation
of workloads. As a result, idle times and resource inefficiencies are minimized, while
operational coordination is enhanced, leading to smoother process flows and increased
productivity. The integration of real-time data and adaptive decision-making mechanisms
further strengthens the system’s ability to respond dynamically to changing production
conditions. Consequently, the production line becomes more agile, robust, and capable
of sustaining high performance over time.

5 Study Limitations
The results of this study reveal a significant interdependence between mold size, robot
adaptability, and mold complexity in determining changeover durations. Larger molds,
when paired with robotic systems that lack sufficient adaptability, tend to generate
extended setup times. This effect is further amplified when the mold design is complex, requiring additional calibration and handling efforts. These findings underscore
the necessity of integrating intelligent systems capable of managing such variability.
The use of fuzzy logic in this context proves particularly effective, as it allows
for the modeling of nonlinear relationships and uncertainties inherent in real-world
manufacturing environments. Through adaptive inference mechanisms, the system can
predict and adjust changeover strategies in real time, based on multiple input variables.
This not only enhances the precision of operational decisions but also contributes directly
to the dynamic balancing of the production line.
Line balancing, a critical factor in manufacturing efficiency, benefits greatly from
this hybrid approach. By redistributing tasks based on real-time data and system feedback, the methodology ensures a more uniform workload across stations. This reduces
idle time, minimizes bottlenecks, and improves the overall flow of operations. Furthermore, the ability to continuously adapt to changes in mold characteristics and equipment
performance makes the production system more resilient and scalable. In essence, the
integration of SMED with fuzzy logic not only optimizes changeover times but also
establishes a robust foundation for sustainable and intelligent line balancing in complex
industrial settings.

6 Conclusion
This study confirms that the integration of Lean Six Sigma (LSS), Single-Minute
Exchange of Dies (SMED), and fuzzy logic constitutes a powerful methodology for
reducing mold changeover times in plastic injection molding environments. The implementation of this hybrid approach led to a remarkable 65% reduction in changeover duration and a substantial increase in Process Cycle Efficiency (PCE), rising from 27.5% to
72.5%. Additionally, value-added activities increased by 59.3%, while non-value-added

Optimizing Mold Changeover Times in the Automotive Injection Industry

327

operations were reduced by 71.9%, reflecting a significant enhancement in operational
efficiency and resource utilization.
Beyond these quantitative improvements, the methodology also contributes meaningfully to production line balancing. By leveraging real-time data and adaptive decisionmaking through fuzzy logic, the system dynamically redistributes tasks and adjusts
workflows to match operational demands. This ensures a more even distribution of
workload across stations, reduces process variability, and mitigates bottlenecks—key
factors in achieving a stable and efficient production line. The ability to maintain this
balance under varying production conditions highlights the robustness and scalability of
the approach.
These findings not only validate the effectiveness of combining Lean tools with
intelligent systems but also position this methodology as a strategic enabler for smart
manufacturing. Future research could explore its application across different production
stages or integrate simulation-based validation to further generalize its benefits [3].

A Hybrid Approach for Pallet Loading
in Ceramic Tile Industry
Marco Taccini1(B) , Matheus Aguilar de Oliveira2 ,
André Gustavo dos Santos2 , Thiago Alves de Queiroz3 ,
and Manuel Iori1
1

Department of Sciences and Methods for Engineering, University of Modena and
Reggio Emilia, Reggio Emilia, Italy
{marco.taccini,manuel.iori}@unimore.it
2
Department of Informatics, Federal University of Viçosa, Viçosa, MG, Brazil
{matheus.a.aguilar,andresantos}@ufv.br
3
Institute of Mathematics and Technology, Federal University of Catalão,
Catalão, GO, Brazil
taq@ufcat.edu.br

Abstract. Motivated by a real-world case study in ceramic tile production, this paper addresses the problem of determining the minimum
number of pallets required to load a given set of boxes. The problem must
be solved quickly to give customers an expectation of the transportation
cost of their orders. In addition, not all constraints and instance data can
be easily determined in advance, and the items are loaded onto the pallets by operators who mostly rely on their personal experience. Therefore,
traditional model-based solution methods do not apply well, and datadriven approaches are preferable. To solve the problem, we propose a
hybrid algorithm in which a machine learning technique is trained over a
company dataset comprising two years of customer orders, with the aim
of predicting the number of pallets required by an order. The accuracy
of the machine learning technique is largely improved by including additional features, such as lower and upper bounds, in the dataset, obtained
using quick optimization algorithms. The resulting hybrid algorithm has
been compared with the model-based software currently used at the company, consistently providing better-quality results in shorter computing
times.
Keywords: Cutting and packing problems · Distributor’s pallet
loading · Ceramic tile · Machine learning

1

Introduction

The global ceramic tile market has grown signiﬁcantly over the past decade, with
worldwide production reaching 16.8 billion square meters [1]. Italy is a leading
exporter, generating €7.2 billion in revenue, highlighting the sector’s importance to the national economy. Eﬃcient logistics optimization in the ceramic
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 329–340, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_25

330

M. Taccini et al.

tile industry, including loading of tiles on pallets and transportation of pallets,
is crucial for cost reduction and company competitiveness. Loading operations
involve transferring speciﬁed quantities of ceramic tile boxes, either of the same
size or diﬀerent sizes, from production pallets to shipping pallets to fulﬁll customers’ orders. When loading pallets, the company needs to carefully consider
weight and volume constraints and keep pallet stability and balance to ensure
the safe transport of tiles while using the minimum number of shipping pallets.
In this paper, we handle a variant of the distributor’s pallet loading problem
(DPLP) [12] that emerges from an international ceramic tile company headquartered in Italy. The company has an e-commerce platform to receive customers’
orders. As the company does not provide the transportation of the shipping pallets, customers need to collect their pallets by using their vehicles or third-party
carriers. Consequently, the company should provide the number of shipping pallets associated with each order or at least predict this number accurately, especially for customers who need to contract a third-party carrier. This prediction
is complex, even if using specialized software to address pallet loading problems [19], because the company faces speciﬁc constraints in its operational daily
routine, necessitating extensive customization of such software.
In this paper, we propose a hybrid approach to predict the number of shipping pallets required for a given customer order. The approach combines machine
learning (ML) techniques to gain information from a large dataset of previously
shipped pallets with techniques from the optimization ﬁeld to enrich the dataset
by quickly providing additional features such as lower and upper bounds on the
number of pallets. The hybrid approach is then compared with solutions currently in use at the company, including manual operators’ decisions and outputs
from a given specialized software [14]. The computational experiments show that
the proposed approach is a valuable tool for the company.
The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 contains a brief
review of the literature related to the DPLP. Section 3 describes the company
problem with its constraints and requirements. Section 4 presents our proposed
hybrid approach. Section 5 contains the numerical experiments on real-world
instances. Finally, Sect. 6 concludes the paper and provides several directions for
future research.

2

Literature Review

The literature on cutting and packing problems is extensive. According to [20],
the ﬁrst scientiﬁc papers on these problems date back to the 1940s. The most
recent surveys concern problems in one-, two-, and three-dimensions and contain
detailed discussions on solution methods based on exact and heuristic approaches
(see, e.g., [10] and [22]). An essential characteristic of these problems is given by
the number of practical constraints they may include, such as complete shipment,
conﬂicting items, cargo stability, load-bearing, multi-drop, and load-balancing
[13]. Concerning pallet loading problems, [19] surveyed the manufacturer’s variant, in which all items are identical, and the objective is to pack the maximum

A Hybrid Approach for Pallet Loading in Ceramic Tile Industry

331

number of items onto a single pallet. Besides commenting on the diﬀerent data
sets and solution methods, the authors emphasized that the computational complexity of this problem is still an open question. The DPLP, on the other hand,
assumes that items are not necessarily identical and is an NP-hard problem. Its
objective is to ﬁnd the minimum number of pallets necessary to load all items.
The DPLP is also referenced as the multi-pallet loading problem [21] and the
pallet building problem [6].
One of the ﬁrst authors to handle the DPLP was [9], considering the transport of palletized cargo by the U.S. Air Force. The author proposed a dynamic
programming-based algorithm and tested it on instances having 30 boxes. In
[21], the DPLP was solved using an algorithm based on branch-and-bound. The
authors considered practical constraints such as weight limits, load-bearing, stability, and grouping of items. In [4], the problem had no limit on the number of
boxes to load, and boxes could have multiple orientations. The authors developed a recursive partitioning algorithm, which integrated a recursive ﬁve-block
heuristic and an L-approach. The authors could not ﬁnd an instance for which
this algorithm failed in ﬁnding an optimal solution (such an instance was later
found in [17]). In [6], the DPLP emerged from a robotized task of loading boxes
into layers, which were then stacked while meeting load-bearing requirements.
Constraints like contiguity, visibility, and multiple orientations were considered.
The authors proposed a mathematical model and a reactive greedy randomized adaptive search procedure for solving realistic, large-sized instances. A new
DPLP variant in which pallets may have diﬀerent sizes was handled in [12]. The
problem emerged from a lamp and lighting manufacturing company that needed
to load many carton boxes onto pallets of multiple sizes. Constraints such as
load-bearing and multiple orientations were taken into consideration.
Using artiﬁcial intelligence techniques to handle combinatorial optimization
problems is a promising area of research. The survey in [3] discussed recent
advances in combining ML techniques with optimization methods to eﬃciently
handle hard combinatorial optimization problems. The authors also commented
on the beneﬁts (e.g., speed and generalization) and challenges (e.g., training
and accuracy) of using ML purely. Concerning cutting and packing problems,
an increasing number of publications combine ML or artiﬁcial intelligence techniques with exact or heuristic algorithms to handle, e.g., bin packing [8] and
container loading problems [16].

3

Problem Description

The problem under investigation is a variant of the DPLP with speciﬁc operational and product-related constraints. After being produced, the ceramic tiles
are ﬁrst loaded on production pallets, which are only used for inbound logistics.
An automated system then delivers the production pallets to the loading area.
The company operators load the products onto shipping pallets using hydraulic
machines. The automated system is also responsible for other logistics activities
inside the warehouse. As a result, products are not available simultaneously, as

332

M. Taccini et al.

their arrival sequence depends on the system’s decisions and the warehouse’s
current state. For instance, machinery downtime may alter the order in which
production pallets are sent to the loading areas. Moreover, the loading area
cannot have more than four production pallets at a time. Due to this space limitation, operators need to load the products onto the pallets as soon as possible.
As other products arrive, operators continue loading until the shipping pallets
of that order are completed before moving to the next order. When loading a
shipping pallet, operators must also satisfy load requirements such as vertical
stability, load-bearing, and restricted box orientations.
Our DPLP can be formally described as follows. A customer order consists of
a set B of three-dimensional boxes, each containing a given number of tiles. Each
box belongs to a type t ∈ T = {1, 2, ..., m}, corresponding to a speciﬁc tile size.
A box of type t has width wt ∈ Z∗+ , depth lt ∈ Z∗+ , height ht ∈ Z∗+ , and weight
pt ∈ Z∗+ . Let dt ∈ N denote the number of boxes of type t required by the order.
Let P represent the set of identical shipping pallets, each with a two-dimensional
loading surface of width W ∈ Z∗+ and depth L ∈ Z∗+ . The DPLP’s objective is to
load all boxes requested in the order by using the minimum number of pallets.
Constraints on the maximum pallet height H ∈ Z∗+ and weight Wmax ∈ Z∗+ need
to be satisﬁed.
Due to these complex company constraints, obtaining a solution for this
DPLP variant is not easy, especially for large-sized and heterogeneous orders
(i.e., composed of boxes of diﬀerent sizes). Even if a DPLP solution satisfying
all constraints is available, it could suﬀer from warehouse uncertainties, possibly
making it impractical for the company operators. Besides that, the company
should give the number of pallets associated with each customer’s order at the
moment of purchase on its e-commerce platform. Therefore, the company is currently interested in a fast and accurate tool capable of predicting the number of
shipping pallets, rather than solving a dedicated but time-consuming optimization problem.

4

Hybrid Model

We propose a hybrid approach to the DPLP under investigation that considers
optimization algorithms to enhance the predictive performance of an ML model.
The company provided data on two years of sales and the corresponding number
of pallets generated for each order. This data includes the maximum number of
boxes per shipping pallet for each box size t, denoted by Ftmax , and the maximum
weight (Wmax ) and volume (W LH) a shipping pallet can support.
An initial training dataset is created from the company-provided data. We
start calculating the frequency Ft = dt (i.e., the number of boxes of type t),
the weight Wt = dt pt , and the volume Vt = wt lt ht dt for each type t ∈ T .
The dataset is labeled with the number of shipping pallets Zj generated by
each order j ∈ {1, ..., n}. Since training on the initial dataset can take a long
time, all intermediate training steps (Sections 4.1–4.3) are conducted using a
reduced dataset. The complete dataset is only used on the ﬁnal computational

A Hybrid Approach for Pallet Loading in Ceramic Tile Industry

333

experiments (Sect. 5). The reduced dataset is created by randomly selecting a
subset of orders from the initial dataset, resulting in a training set with about
1
8 of the total number of orders.
4.1

Machine Learning Model Selection

We developed a pipeline to train and test four diﬀerent machine learning models. These models, which were chosen based on the literature review and our
experience, consist of: XGBoost [7], LightGBM (LGBM) [11], Random Forest,
and Support Vector Machine (SVM) from scikit-learn [15]. Each model passes
through a hyperparameter tuning phase on the Mean Squared Error (MSE) and
Mean Absolute Error (MAE) metrics, obtained by performing a standard grid
search. Table 1 reports the hyperparameters and their possible values, besides
the best values found along with MSE and MAE metrics. The possible values
were deﬁned using a trial-and-error approach and following the indications in
each model documentation and the literature (see, e.g., [2]).

The results in Table 1 show that XGBoost outperforms the other models
regarding the MSE and MAE values. Consequently, we decided to use only

of this model’s hyperparameters. Table 2 contains the outcome of this additional
tuning test, which leads to better MSE and MAE values for the XGBoost model.

shown in Fig. 1. Furthermore, tile sizes that appear less frequently in the dataset
exhibit higher error rates, as shown in Fig. 2.

Incorporation of Optimization-Based Features

Aiming to reduce the errors further, three additional features (i.e., HS1 , HS2 ,
and HS3 ) computed with quick algorithms are introduced into the training
dataset. To ensure computational eﬃciency, the proposed algorithms have the
worst-case time complexity of at most O(Fo2 ), where Fo is the total number of
boxes in the customer’s order. The proposed algorithms are as follows:
m

– HS2 : calculated as HS2 = t=1 FFmax

t
of boxes of type t that can be loaded onto a pallet. This is, instead, an
upper bound (UB) for the DPLP instance, computed by considering that all
pallets are built with only homogeneous tiles (and this can easily be done by
satisfying vertical stability and load bearing constraints);
– HS3 : described in Algorithms 1 and 2. This algorithm loads pallets by stacking layers of same-sized boxes, starting with those having the largest area until
the pallet limits are reached. Once full, it begins a new pallet and continues
until all boxes are packed. This may be seen as a quick heuristic solution

336

M. Taccini et al.

approach, but, in fact, it does not consider vertical stability and load bearing constraints, and hence it only provides an approximation function of the
minimum number of pallets.

Computational Experiments

All the codes were implemented in Python 3.10 and executed in a computer with
an Intel Core i5-1135G7 2.40 GHz processor, 8 GB of RAM, and Linux Mint 21
Cinnamon as the operating system. We evaluate the XGBoost model considering
the structure S3 (i.e., the one enriched with the additional optimization-based
features) and the complete two-year dataset with all orders. This corresponds
to our hybrid approach. In Table 5, we observe the impact of the two groups
Oh and Of ull on the training phase. Consequently, we train the model using the
structure S3 , ﬁrst including all orders O and then keeping only mixed orders
Omix (which we recall is equal to O − (Oh ∪ Of ull )).
Table 5. MSE and MAE values for each group of orders and the dataset structure S3 .

As reported in Table 5, applying the model to all orders O yields slightly
better results compared to using Omix . However, using only Omix can reduce
the training time by around 51% while keeping satisfactory predictive results.
We next compare in Table 6 the proposed hybrid approach with PackVol [14],
a specialized software the company uses to solve the DPLP heuristically. PackVol was conﬁgured by the company employees to satisfy the speciﬁc operational
constraints of the DPLP as closely as possible. This means that PackVol is not
able to capture some constraints. For example, as mentioned in Sect. 3, ceramic
tiles of a given order are not all available simultaneously due to the warehouse’s
current state. As a result, PackVol could underestimate the number of pallets.
We also present the number of pallets obtained from the company operators
for comparison purposes. This experiment considers 30 real instances, consisting
of 30 orders randomly selected among the heterogeneous orders made in 2024
(which are not included in the training set). The real instances I are all heterogeneous, and are sorted by increasing number of box types m. Its maximum
value is 6 since 99.32% of the orders in the training dataset contain six or fewer
types. Table 6 contains the instance number and the solution value (i.e., number of shipping pallets) obtained with PackVol, the proposed hybrid approach,

338

M. Taccini et al.

and the company operators. We assume the operators’ solution is the basis for
comparison, so the rows “Diﬀ.” contain the diﬀerence with such a solution for
each instance. Positive values indicate the usage of more pallets, which could
be acceptable. Negative values indicate fewer pallets in the solution and could
represent additional logistic issues for customers (e.g., the need for contracting
“on the ﬂight” an extra vehicle).

Observing the results in Table 6, PackVol achieves an MSE of 2.13 and an
MAE of 0.93, whereas our hybrid approach achieves signiﬁcantly better results,
with 0.50 and 0.43 values of MSE and MAE, respectively. These outcomes highlight that our approach outperforms PackVol in both metrics, demonstrating its
capability to predict the number of pallets following the real context of the
company with its operational requirements and workﬂows. Another important
aspect is the computation time required by the proposed approach. Although
the training phase may be time-consuming, after training, it solved all instances
in just 0.016 s, while PackVol required around one second per instance. From
Table 6, we can also observe that PackVol returns solutions diﬀerent from those
by the company in 16 cases, returning fewer pallets for 15 cases and achieving
a largest deviation of -4 pallets. This may bring issues for customers. On the
other hand, the hybrid approach returns solutions that diﬀer from the company
ones for just 12 instances, returning fewer pallets for only 4 of them and having
the largest deviation of just 2 pallets. These values conﬁrm that our approach,
trained on real-world data, eﬀectively models the problem constraints and results
in accurate predictions.

6

Concluding Remarks

We proposed a hybrid approach composed of machine learning and optimization components to predict the number of pallets required to ship an order of
ceramic tiles. We evaluated four machine learning models, and after ﬁne-tuning,
we selected XGBoost. Its performance was improved by adding new features
that capture general information about the company’s operational constraints.

A Hybrid Approach for Pallet Loading in Ceramic Tile Industry

339

We also incorporated three measures obtained by quick optimization algorithms,
leading to signiﬁcant reductions in both MSE and MAE values. The resulting
hybrid approach obtained better results than PackVol, the specialized modelbased software currently used at the company.
The computational experiments highlight that our hybrid approach, once it
is trained on real-world data, is fast and well-suited for the company-speciﬁc
operational constraints. It allows more accurate and practical predictions than
PackVol, resulting in more solutions equal to the ones obtained by the company
operators and a reduced number of solutions requiring fewer pallets. This is
remarkably achieved without feeding the approach with any direct information
about the pallet loading constraints (i.e., stability, load-bearing requirements,
and orientation), making the approach a very ﬂexible tool.
Future research could propose new features to the hybrid approach (e.g.,
solutions obtained with heuristics based on local search). Another interesting
research avenue is to work on a more general problem, considering pallets of
diﬀerent sizes, which will lead to a more challenging objective function. We
ﬁnally plan to test the approach to other related pallet loading, cutting, and
packing problems, as well as scheduling problems [5,18].

A Comparative Analysis of the Algorithm
Results for Designing Two-Stage Supply
Chains with Fixed Costs
Corina Pop Sitar , Petrică C. Pop(B) , and Adrian Petrovan
Technical University of Cluj-Napoca, North University Center of Baia Mare, Dr. V.
Babes 62A, 430083 Baia Mare, Romania
corina.pop@econ.utcluj.ro, petrica.pop@mi.utcluj.ro,
adrian.petrovan@ieec.utcluj.ro

Abstract. This paper addresses the two-stage supply chain network
design (TS-SCND) problem with ﬁxed costs, that models a distribution network in a supply chain that contains manufacturers, distribution
centers (DCs) and retailers. We formulate the investigated problem as
a mixed linear integer programming model that contains two kinds of
ﬁxed costs: ones corresponding to the distribution routes and the others
corresponding to opening the DCs. In this study, our aim is to examine
the characteristics of the existing metaheuristic algorithms for solving
the investigated problem: four ant colony optimization algorithms and a
hybrid genetic algorithm. Based on the performance comparison results,
we conduct a systematic analysis of the considered solution approaches.
The performed analysis points out that the hybrid genetic algorithm has
the best performance regarding the count of obtained optimal solutions
and the relative percentage deviations. These ﬁndings were validated
through statistical analysis.
Keywords: Two-Stage Supply Chain Network Design problem ·
genetic algorithms · ant colony optimization algorithms · statistical
evaluation

1

Introduction

Transportation problem (TP) has a long-standing history, being deﬁned for the
ﬁrst time by the French mathematician G. Monge in 1781. In this problem,
goods are transported from a collection of sources to a collection of destinations,
each with speciﬁc supply and demand constraints, respectively, while the goal is
minimizing the total transportation cost, fulﬁlling all the mentioned supply and
demand requirements.
Motivated by the real-world applications, TP was extended by considering
ﬁxed costs related to the arcs linking sources to destinations, alongside transportation costs that depend on the quantity of goods transported along these
arcs. This setup leads to what is commonly referred to in the literature as the
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 341–352, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_26

342

C. P. Sitar et al.

ﬁxed cost transportation problem (FCTP), mentioned for the ﬁrst time by Balinski [2]. For further details on the TPs and FCTPs, including a review of solution
approaches developed to solve them, we refer to Kacher and Singh [13].
Due to the unpredictability of the demand requirements in real-world applications, the classical single-stage TPs have been extended to two-stage version
[10], where in addition to the source and destination nodes, we have nodes associated to the distribution centers (DCs).
In the literature, there have been investigated three variants of two-stage
ﬁxed cost transportation problems in supply chain networks:
1. The two-stage ﬁxed cost transportation problems in supply chain networks
with ﬁxed costs related to the routes connecting manufacturers to DCs and
DCs to retailers. Further information regarding this variant can be found in
[12, 17, 18].
2. The two-stage ﬁxed cost transportation problems in supply chain networks
with ﬁxed costs related to opening the DCs. Further information regarding
this variant can be found in [4–7].
3. The two-stage ﬁxed cost transportation problems in supply chain networks
two categories of ﬁxed costs: those for opening DCs and those associated with
the routes connecting manufacturers to DCs and DCs to retailers.
We conﬁne in our paper with the last variant of the two-stage ﬁxed cost transportation problems in supply chain networks. The speciﬁc form of the problem
investigated in our paper, was originally proposed by Hong et al. [11]. They
provided a mixed integer linear programming (MILP) model for the problem
and developed an ant colony optimization-based solution approach, which they
tested on 150 instances partitioned into three types: small, medium, and large.
Sabo et al. [19] presented a valid MILP formulation of the problem and highlighted certain discrepancies in Hong et al. [11] paper. Cosma et al. [8] developed
a hybrid genetic algorithm (HGA) achieved by embedding a linear programming
(LP) optimization problem into the structure of a genetic algorithm (GA). We
tested our developed hybrid algorithm on a collection of 150 instances having
the same characteristics as those generated by Hong et al. [11], and an additional type of instances containing 50 larger instances. Mostafa and Elshaer [14]
described three ACO algorithms using diﬀerent pheromone trails. They tested
their solution approaches on a set 120 instances divided into four types: small,
medium, large and larger. Mostafa et al. [15] investigated the impact of the ﬁxed
costs related to the routes and opening the DCs on the ﬁxed costs on the optimal
supply chain design.
As it can be observed, the proposed metaheuristic algorithms for solving the
investigated problem have been tested on diﬀerent data sets, and the only one
available was provided by Cosma et al. [20].
The goal of our paper is to present a comparative analysis of the existing
algorithm results for designing two-stage supply chains with ﬁxed costs, taking as
a benchmark the solutions provided by the solver LINGO, respectively CPLEX
for solving the MILP model of the problem.

A Comparative Analysis of the Algorithm Results

343

The remainder of the paper is structured as follows: the deﬁnition of the
TS-SCND problem is provided in Sect. 2, while in Sect. 3, we provide a valid formulation of the investigated transportation problem with ﬁxed costs. Section 4 is
dedicated to comparative analysis of the existing algorithm results for designing
two-stage supply chains with ﬁxed costs regarding the count of optimal solutions
achieved and the relative percentage deviation, followed by a statistical analysis.
Finally, the last section draws some conclusions.

2

Definition of the TS-SCND Problem with Fixed Costs

The TS-SCND that we investigate in this paper comprises manufacturers, characterized by a production capacity, distribution centers characterized by a storage capacity, and retailers that have a known demand that has to be fulﬁlled,
as illustrated in Fig. 1.

Fig. 1. Representation of the considered TS-SCND problem with ﬁxed costs

The ﬁnal products produced by the manufacturers are supplied to the retailers via distribution centers. The potential locations for DCs are predeﬁned in
the problem, each incurring a ﬁxed cost to establish. The distribution process
has two stages: among manufacturers and DCs, respectively among DCs and
retailers, incurring unit transportation costs and ﬁxed costs for using a certain
route.
The objective of the TS-SCND problem with ﬁxed costs corresponding to
the transportation routes and for opening the DCs, is to determine the DCs to

344

C. P. Sitar et al.

be opened and the routes used for the distribution of the products, as well as
the quantities to be transported along these routes, satisfying the demands of
the retailers, the capacity constraints of the manufacturers and the constraints
related to storing capacities of the DCs, while minimizing the total costs.

3

The objective of our investigated problem is to minimize the total cost, which
consists of unit transportation costs, ﬁxed costs associated with the routes, and
the ﬁxed costs for opening the DCs. The set of restrictions (1) ensures that
the capacity of each manufacturer is not exceeded. The set of restrictions (2)
guarantees that the demand of each retailer is satisﬁed. The set of restrictions (3)
represents the ﬂow conservation conditions, ensuring that the units collected by a
given DC from manufacturers are equal to the units distributed from that DC to
the retailers. The set of constraints (4) guarantees that the storage capacities of
the DCs are not exceeded. Finally, restrictions (5–7) deﬁne the decision variables.

4

Comparative Computational Analysis

Our comparative computational analysis of the existing metaheuristic algorithms
for solving the TS-SCND problem was guided by the following research question:

346

C. P. Sitar et al.

RQ: Which is best performing algorithm for solving the TS-SCND problem with
ﬁxed costs?
4.1

Data Sets

To check their proposed mathematical formulation and to assess the performance of their proposed ACO algorithm, Hong et al. [11] generated randomly
150 instances divided into three types based on their dimension: small instances
containing 2 manufacturers, 5 DCs and 10 retailers, medium instances containing 4 manufacturers, 8 DCs and 15 retailers, and large instances containing 6
manufacturers, 10 DCs and 20 retailers.
As the test instances used by Hong et al. [11] were not available in the
literature, Cosma et al. [8] generated a new set of 150 instances in the same
way as those in Hong et al. [11]. In addition, they generated 50 larger instances
containing 10 manufacturers, 15 DCs and 30 retailers. All their instances are
available in [20].
Mostafa and Elshaer [14] asses the performance of their proposed ACO
approaches on 90 instances divided in small, medium and large as considered
by Huang et al. [11] and Cosma et al. [8]. In addition, they generated 30 larger
instances containing 10 manufacturers, 20 DCs and 30 retailers.
The existing data sets from the literature are synthesized in Table 1. The
table is organized as follows: the ﬁrst column presents the data set type, the
second one the size of the instances, the next three columns display the number
of manufacturers, DCs and retailers, while the last column presents the authors
that used the instances in order to evaluate the eﬃciency of their developed
solution approaches for solving the TS-SCND problem with ﬁxed costs.

4.2

Comparison Methods

The comparative computational analysis was made on the existing methods for
solving the TS-SCND problem with ﬁxed costs, which are going to be described
brieﬂy next:

A Comparative Analysis of the Algorithm Results

347

1. Hong et al. [11] proposed an ACO algorithm for solving the problem under
investigation. In addition, they solved their proposed mathematical model
using the solver LINGO.
2. Cosma et al. [8] developed a HGA obtained by embedding a LP optimization
problem into the structure of a GA. Furthermore, they solved their mixed
linear integer programming formulation using the solver CPLEX 12.7.0.
3. Mostafa and Elshaer [14] described three ACO algorithms using diﬀerent
pheromone trails. Moreover, they solved their proposed mixed linear integer
programming problem model using the solver LINGO.

4.3

Implementation Aspects

Hong et al. [11] did not provide any information regarding their implementations
and the computer used to carry out their computational experiments.
Cosma et al. [8] implemented their proposed mathematical MILP formulation
using CPLEX solver version 12.7.0 and their hybrid genetic algorithm in Java
8, and all the experiments have been evaluated on a machine equipped with
Intel Core i5-4590 3.3GHz, 4GB RAM, and the Windows 10 Education 64 bit
operating system.
Mostafa and Elshaer [14] implemented their three variants of the ACO
algorithm using Microsoft C#. All their experiments for solving the problem
instances with ACO algorithms and LINGO have been evaluated on a laptop
with the following characteristics: Intel(R) Core(TM) i5-6300U CPU @ 2.40 GHz
2.50 GHz, RAM 8.00 GB.
4.4

Comparative Computational Analysis

Synthesizing the computational results displayed by Hong et al. [11], Cosma et
al. [8] and Mostafa and Elshaer [14], in the next two tables, we present a comparative analysis between the existing metaheuristic algorithms and the solutions
achieved by solving the MILP formulation of the TS-SCND problem with ﬁxed
costs by LINGO, respectively CPLEX, regarding the count of obtained optimal
solutions and the relative percentage deviations.
The Relative Percentage Diﬀerence (RPD) is used to measure the quality of
solutions produced by two distinct solution approaches [16]. It is calculated using
the following formula: .RP D(A1 , A2 ) = (ZA1 − ZA2 )/ZA2 , where .A1 , .A2 are two
distinct solution approaches, .ZA1 represents the solution cost delivered by the
proposed solution approach .A1 , and .ZA2 represents the solution cost delivered
by the existing solution approach .A2 . In our situation, .ZA1 is the cost obtained
by one of the developed metaheuristics by Hong et al. [11], Cosma et al. [8] and
Mostafa and Elshaer [14], while .ZA2 is the cost obtained by solving the MILP
model by LINGO, respectively CPLEX.
In Table 2, we displayed the number of obtained optimal solutions by the
existing metaheuristic algorithms for each type of instances. The highest number
of obtained optimal solutions is emphasized in bold, and the symbol ”-” indicates

348

C. P. Sitar et al.

that for that type of instances Hong et al. [11] did not provide solutions. The
last line contains the total number of obtained optimal solutions.

From the analysis of the results displayed in Table 3, we notice that the best

– for all the small and medium instances the average RPD is .0 because our
HGA delivered the optimal solutions achieved as well by CPLEX;
– for the set of large instances, the average RPD is negative because in the case

– and for the set of larger instances again the average RPD is negative because

HGA is followed by the ACO3, ACO2 and ACO1 all proposed by Mostafa
and Elshaer [14], while the worst performance in terms of RPD is achieved by
the ACO algorithm proposed by Hong et al. [11].
4.5

Statistical Analysis

To assess the statistical signiﬁcance of the diﬀerences observed among the investigated solution approaches over the considered data sets, taking as a benchmark
the solutions provided by the solver LINGO, respectively CPLEX for solving the
MILP model, we used the principles proposed by [9] and the Friedman statistical
test to reject the null hypothesis.
Our tests were performed on results divided by the diﬀerent types of
instances: small, medium and large, revealing that the null hypothesis could
be rejected. This indicates a statistically signiﬁcant diﬀerence among the classiﬁers. Following these remarks, we performed a Wilcoxon-Holm post-hoc test as
recommended in [3]. The outcomes of these comparisons were displayed in the
form of critical diﬀerence (CD) plots for a signiﬁcance level of 0.05, see Fig. 2. To
analyze the plots, every calculated result was placed horizontally according to its
average ranking across the collection of the evaluated instances. If the diﬀerence
between two values was smaller than the corresponding critical diﬀerence, than
the eﬃciency of those methods was evaluated equal, with no signiﬁcantly diﬀerences in terms of performance. This was illustrated in our graphics by horizontal
bars linking the respective metaheuristic algorithms.

Fig. 2. Critical diﬀerence plots of statistical diﬀerences of the achieved results for small,
medium and large instances

350

C. P. Sitar et al.

Based on the three CD diagrams for small, medium, and large instances
presented in Fig. 2, we draw the following conclusions:
– HGA consistently ranks as the best-performing algorithm across all instance
sizes (small, medium, and large). Its performance is robust and reliable.
– ACO2 and ACO3 perform well, being the second-best group in medium and
large instances. However, they lag slightly for small instances compared to
ACO1.
– ACO1 performs relatively well for small instances but falls behind ACO2 and
ACO3 for medium and large instances.
– The basic ACO algorithm consistently underperforms across all instance sizes,
suggesting it may not be well-suited for the problem or requires further optimization.

Fig. 3. Mean plot and LSD intervals of the achieved results for small, medium and
large instances.

A Comparative Analysis of the Algorithm Results

351

To evaluate the performance of the tested algorithms (ACO1, ACO2, ACO3,
ACO, and HGA) across diﬀerent problem instance sizes, we performed a statistical analysis using Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) followed by a Least Signiﬁcant
Diﬀerence (LSD) post-hoc test. These methods enable us to determine whether
the diﬀerences in algorithm performance are statistically signiﬁcant and to identify which speciﬁc algorithms outperform others. The results of the ANOVA and
LSD analyses are visualized in Fig. 3. This ﬁgure illustrates the distribution of
RPD values for each algorithm along with the 95% conﬁdence intervals for the
means, highlighted by the LSD bounds.
The ANOVA results, supported by the LSD post-hoc analysis, clearly demonstrate that HGA consistently outperforms the other algorithms across all problem sizes, showing both lower RPD values and less variability. Among the antcolony-based algorithms, ACO2 and ACO3 perform better than ACO1 and ACO,
with performance gaps becoming more pronounced in larger problem instances.
These ﬁndings emphasize the eﬀectiveness of the HGA for tackling the problem
under investigation.

5

Conclusions

The aim of this paper was to examine the characteristics of the existing metaheuristic algorithms for solving the two-stage supply chain network design (TSSCND) problem with ﬁxed costs: four ant colony optimization algorithms and a
hybrid genetic algorithm. Based on the performance comparison results, we conduct a systematic analysis of the considered solution approaches. The performed
analysis points out that the hybrid genetic algorithm has the best performance
regarding the count of obtained optimal solutions and the relative percentage
deviations. These ﬁndings were validated through statistical analysis.
Acknowledgments. This work was partially supported by GNaC 2023 ARUT grant
“Study of diploid memetic algorithms in solving the routing problems of electric and
heterogeneous vehicles”, Research Contract no. 29/01.07.2024, with the ﬁnancial support of the Technical University of Cluj-Napoca.

Optimizing Supplier Performance: Advanced
Analytics and Predictive Modeling Approaches
Sarah Al Zaied, Faisal Issa, Tala Hassouna, and Dua Weraikat(B)
Rochester Institute of Technology-Dubai, Dubai, UAE
{sha3229,fmi8750}@g.rit.edu, {trh7022,dxwcad}@rit.edu

Abstract. Supplier selection is a fundamental aspect of supply chain management (SCM), playing a crucial role in enhancing operational efficiency, cost management, and overall performance. This study evaluates supplier performance
using advanced data analytics and predictive modeling techniques. Leveraging
a comprehensive dataset sourced from Microsoft Ignite, key performance metrics such as defect quantity, downtime, and categorical identifiers were analyzed
to uncover trends and actionable insights. Visualizations created using Power
BI dashboards highlighted critical performance trends, guiding targeted interventions for operational improvement. Various methodologies, including logistic regression, multinomial and ordinal regression, random forest models, and
Gaussian Naive Bayes, were applied using Minitab, Power BI, and Python to
explore the relationships between predictor variables and performance outcomes.
The initial analysis revealed limitations in linear models due to weak correlations
among categorical variables, prompting the adoption of multinomial and ordinal logistic approaches for improved classification accuracy. This study demonstrates how robust modeling and visualization frameworks can inform strategic
decision-making in supply chain management, fostering sustainable and adaptive supplier relationships. Despite these advancements, the findings emphasize
the necessity for further refinement to address complex interdependencies and
enhance predictive accuracy.
Keywords: Supplier Performance · Data Analytics · Predictive Modeling ·
Supply Chain Efficiency · Operational Productivity

1 Introduction
Supplier performance evaluation is crucial for enhancing supply chain efficiency, product quality, and overall organizational success. In high-tech industries, where supplier
decisions can account for up to 80% of operating costs, evaluating supplier performance impacts cost structure, profitability, and competitiveness [4]. Effective evaluation
reduces risks, optimizes operations, and fosters long-term strategic partnerships, helping
companies improve quality, meet customer demands, and stay competitive in customerdriven markets [4]. Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) are essential tools in this process, offering insights into areas like quality, delivery, cost, innovation, and flexibility.
They enable continuous supplier monitoring and improvement, driving data-informed
decision-making and collaboration [1].
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 353–369, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_27

354

S. Al Zaied et al.

With the increasing availability of data and advanced analytical tools, companies
can adopt data-driven methods to streamline supplier evaluation and maximize supply
chain value. This case study uses a dataset from Microsoft Ignite [17], containing 6,145
entries per column, with performance indicators such as defect quantities, sub-category
ID, plant ID, vendor ID, material ID, defect type ID, material type ID, and downtime
in minutes. The study focuses on critical issues like defect rates and downtime, which
can disrupt manufacturing and inflate costs. Through data analysis, the study aims to
uncover patterns and correlations between these indicators and the performance metric,
providing valuable insights into supplier performance.
Beyond evaluation, this study highlights the broader impact of supplier performance
on cost optimization, downtime reduction, and supply chain resilience. By developing a
predictive evaluation model, it underscores the importance of strong performance frameworks in fostering collaboration and building a competitive, adaptive supply chain. The
findings will contribute to improving supplier performance and supporting sustainable
success across the supply chain.

2 Literature Review
2.1 Importance of Supplier Evaluation in Supply Chain Management
Supplier evaluation is key to supply chain management, aiming to improve efficiency,
align supplier practices with organizational goals, and address sustainability. Despite its
importance, several challenges persist, such as performance measurement gaps, environmental compliance, and strategic integration. Routroy and Pradhan (2020) emphasized
the necessity for tools to track performance and prioritize suppliers for better delivery reliability and efficiency. They employed Analytical Hierarchy Process (AHP) and
Performance Value Analysis (PVA) to rank suppliers and identify gaps [1].
Zeydan et al. (2019) and Mahmoudi et al. (2021) highlighted the complexities of
incorporating sustainability into supplier evaluations. They introduced hybrid frameworks combining Fuzzy AHP and Data Envelopment Analysis (DEA) [2, 7]. Cheng
and Carrillo (2018) underscored the role of partnerships in enhancing cost efficiency
and delivery using t-tests and ratio analysis [3]. Xia et al. (2020) focused on aligning
supplier metrics with strategic goals and reducing biases in evaluations by combining
AHP with fuzzy logic for consistent benchmarking [4].
Maestrini et al. (2017) stressed the importance of diagnostic tools for improved
collaboration and sustainability, utilizing Partial Least Squares (PLS) regression and
a dyadic survey [5]. Shen et al. (2016) and Chiarini (2015) addressed environmental
sustainability, including pollution control and resource efficiency, through frameworks
like the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) and fuzzy Technique for Order of Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution (TOPSIS) with sensitivity analysis [6, 8]. Chatterjee et al. (2021) examined the role of interdependencies and environmental metrics
in green supply chains using Rough Decision-Making Trial and Evaluation Laboratory (DEMATEL) and Multi-Attribute Ideal-Real Comparative Analysis (MAIRCA)
[9]. Aksoy and Öztürk (2019) highlighted supplier classification challenges in Just-inTime (JIT) systems, focusing on reliability using Neural Networks [10]. Kang (2020)
discussed sustainability and operational issues in the semiconductor industry, applying

Optimizing Supplier Performance

355

a hybrid AHP-DEA framework [11]. Dey et al. (2018) and Ho et al. (2019) explored
operational inefficiencies such as poor delivery and inventory mismanagement, combining Quality Function Deployment (QFD) and AHP, and using regression analysis and
DEMATE [12, 15]. Finally, Sluis and De Giovanni (2019) examined the impact of trust,
integration, and flexibility on supplier contracts and performance using multinomial
logistic regression [16].
2.2 Addressing Supplier Evaluation Complexities
This research adopts a combination of advanced methodologies to address gaps in supplier evaluation. Inspired by Chiarini’s “Environmental Policies for Evaluating Suppliers’ Performance Based on GRI Indicators” [8], data preprocessing techniques, including
linearity tests, scatter plots, and correlation analysis, were applied to assess relationships
and trends among supplier attributes. Logistic regression, as employed by Ho et al. in
“Using Modified IPA to Evaluate Supplier Performance” [15], was used to model the
relationship between key supplier attributes and performance outcomes, identifying the
most impactful factors.
Multinomial regression, following the approach of Sluis and De Giovanni in “The
Selection of Contracts in Supply Chains: An Empirical Analysis” [16], was applied to
evaluate and rank suppliers based on multiple categorical criteria, such as sustainability and reliability. Additional techniques, such as Naive Bayes and ordinal regression,
enhanced predictive accuracy and provided insights into ordinal data. Finally, Random
Forest models, inspired by Aksoy and Öztürk’s “Supplier Selection and Performance
Evaluation in Just-in-Time Production Environments” [10], were employed for classification tasks to group suppliers into performance tiers. These methodologies offer a
comprehensive framework for evaluating supplier performance, addressing operational,
strategic, and sustainability dimensions.
The reviewed literature highlights the persistent challenges and complexities in aligning supplier performance with operational, environmental, and strategic objectives. The
integration of advanced tools and methodologies in supplier evaluation is crucial for
informed decision-making and fostering sustainable, competitive supply chains. The
findings are summarized in Table 1.

3 Methodology
This analysis evaluates supplier performance using the Microsoft Power BI Sample
- Supplier Quality Analysis dataset [17], which includes 6,145 records per column.
It offers a comprehensive foundation for assessing key performance indicators (KPIs)
such as efficiency, defect rates, downtime, and overall quality management. The dataset
contains categorical variables like Sub-category ID, Vendor ID, Plant ID, Material ID,
Material Type ID, Defect ID, and Defect Type ID, enabling supplier classification and
segmentation. Additionally, it includes quantitative variables such as defect quantity and
downtime in minutes. The primary target variable, Defect Quantity, is used to predict
performance trends. This analysis uncovers patterns and provides actionable insights to
improve supplier management.
Before analysing the data, Power BI was used to create an interactive data model
for visualizing trends. Four dashboards were developed to assess performance metrics:
vendor and plant performance, subcategory-specific trends, and comparisons. The Vendor Dashboard analysed defect quantities and downtime, the Plant Dashboard identified
operational improvements, the Subcategory Dashboard examined logistics, and the Comparative Dashboard highlighted patterns across vendors and plants, providing real-time
insights for data-driven decisions.
Data pre-processing involved cleaning the dataset for consistency. The data did not
contain any missing values or duplications and it was completely clean. Most variables
were categorical (e.g., subcategory ID, plant ID, vendor ID), while downtime in minutes
and defect quantity were discrete. A correlation matrix (Fig. 1) was also used to identify
multicollinearity and irrelevant features. Scatterplots generated in Minitab (Fig. 2, 3, 4,
5, 6, 7, 8 and 9) examined relationships between these variables and defect quantity,
the initial target variable. The plots revealed no linear relationships, ruling out linear
regression as a predictive model.

358

S. Al Zaied et al.

ID

Fig. 3. Scatterplot Defect Qty vs Plant ID

Fig. 4. Scatterplot Defect Qty vs Vendor ID

A performance metric was introduced to simplify supplier evaluation using thresholds for defect quantity and downtime. Suppliers were classified as low-performing if
defect quantity exceeded 5127 or downtime surpassed 20 min, and high-performing if
both thresholds were met. Logistic regression was performed in Python using defect

quantity, downtime, subcategory ID, and material ID, and others as predictors. While
the model converged efficiently and highlighted key indicators, the binary metric and
logistics regression lacked depth, limiting analysis.
To address this, a refined metric categorized suppliers into three levels: high, medium,
and low performance and used to perform multinomial regression. High performers had
minimal downtime and defects, medium performers met acceptable thresholds, and low
performers exceeded limits. Then, ordinal regression was applied to capture the ordered
nature of performance levels, offering a more granular analysis.
Random Forest analysis was then conducted to uncover complex relationships in
the dataset. This method effectively identified hidden patterns, including a previously
unnoticed link between material type and supplier performance, providing new insights
into efficiency factors. Lastly, Gaussian Naive Bayes was applied as a predictive tool.
Assuming defect quantity and downtime followed a Gaussian distribution, it efficiently
classified supplier performance into specific categories. The model offered probabilistic,
interpretable results, complementing logistic regression by capturing trends in the data.
By integrating logistic regression, ordinal regression, Random Forest, and Gaussian
Naive Bayes, the analysis provided a comprehensive evaluation of supplier performance.
Key factors like defect quantity, downtime, and material type were identified, delivering
actionable insights to improve efficiency and supply chain resilience.
