S. Al Zaied et al.

4 Results, Analysis and Discussion
Before proceeding with the dataset analysis, using PowerBI, dashboards were created
to help visualize key data metrics and identify initial trends (Fig. 10).

Fig. 10. Data Model in PowerBI

The “Vendor Analysis” dashboard offers a clear view of supplier performance by
analysing total defect quantity and downtime minutes across categories like Electrical,
Logistics, and Mechanical. Bar charts display defect quantities and downtime for each
vendor, complemented by a detailed table for precise metrics. This tool helps identify
disruptions, supporting informed supplier management. The analysis reveals Quotelane
as the top contributor to defects, exceeding 2.1 million, primarily from Electrical and
Materials & Components. However, Quotelane has moderate downtime compared to
Plustax and Reddit, which show fewer defects but higher downtime. Saazoomtex and
Recode have zero downtime despite notable defects, reflecting efficient recovery processes. These insights enable targeted improvements in supplier performance and supply
chain efficiency (Fig. 11).

Fig. 11. Vendor Analysis

Optimizing Supplier Performance

361

The “Plant Analysis” dashboard evaluates plant performance by analysing total
defect quantities and downtime minutes across plant IDs and categories. Bar charts
offer a high-level overview, while scatter plots reveal maximum downtime and defect
contributions by material type. A detailed table breaks down these metrics to identify
inefficiencies.
Plant 7 leads in defects, exceeding 6.6 million, driven by Electrical and Materials &
Components, but maintains relatively low downtime (11,488 min), indicating effective
recovery processes. Plant 3 follows with 3.8 million defects, primarily in Logistics and
Electrical, and the highest downtime at over 17,000 min. Scatter plots highlight Electrical
and Mechanical as key contributors to peaks in defects and downtime. These insights
guided further analysis by prioritizing audits and interventions to enhance supply chain
stability and operational efficiency (Fig. 12).

Fig. 12. Plant Analysis

In the data pre-processing phase, relationships between categorical variables were
examined using scatterplots and a correlation matrix. Since all variables are categorical,
scatterplots do not exhibit a linear relationship, as noted in the methodology section.
The absence of linear trends suggests that linear regression is unsuitable for predicting
supplier performance.
The correlation matrix shows generally weak correlations, with the highest being
between Vendor ID and Material ID (0.329), indicating a small to moderate positive
relationship. Most correlations are close to zero, reflecting minimal linear dependence
among variables. Specifically, Downtime and Defect Quantity, two key performance
metrics, show very weak or no correlation with other variables.
Given the lack of significant correlations and linearity, logistic regression was deemed
more suitable for predicting performance outcomes using a binary performance metric,
as detailed in the methodology. Python Colab was used for all analyses, with the logistic
regression model predicting the likelihood of an acceptable supplier performance rank
based on key predictors shown in Fig. 13.
The analysis identified several significant predictors of supplier performance. Subcategory_ID had a positive, highly significant coefficient (p < 0.001), indicating that

362

S. Al Zaied et al.

Fig. 14. Odds Ratio

Fig. 13. Logistics Regression Iteration 1

suppliers in certain subcategories are more likely to achieve acceptable rankings. This
suggests that subcategory-specific factors, such as product complexity or specialization, play a key role. Similarly, Plant_ID contributed positively to rankings (p < 0.05),
highlighting the impact of plant-level factors like operational efficiency or geographic
advantages.
In contrast, Material_Type_ID showed a negative and significant coefficient for some
material types, indicating that material selection can adversely affect performance by
increasing defect rates and downtime. Downtime itself was a significant negative predictor, aligning with expectations that increased downtime disrupts production timelines
and quality standards.
The model’s pseudo R-squared value of 0.9062 suggests it explains 90.62% of the
variation, but this likely indicates overfitting, as reflected in the low test accuracy of
37.1%. This underscores the need for model refinement or incorporating nonlinear effects
to improve predictions. The odds ratios from the logistic regression, detailed in Fig. 14,
provide further insights into each predictor’s influence on achieving an acceptable rank.
The odds ratio analysis highlights key factors influencing supplier performance.
Sub_Category_ID and Plant_ID, with odds ratios above 1, indicate that specialized subcategories and plants with better process control are more likely to achieve acceptable
performance. Conversely, Material_Type_ID, with odds ratios below 1, reflects a negative impact due to higher defect rates or inefficiencies. Downtime, with a significantly low
odds ratio, emerges as a critical factor, where increases drastically reduce performance
likelihood, emphasizing the need to minimize downtime and align material selection
with processes.
While the analysis identifies key improvement areas, logistic regression shows limitations. Including defect quantity and downtime inflated the Pseudo R-squared (0.9062)
since these variables directly define the performance metric. Excluding them reduced it to
0.02735, revealing the model’s inability to predict performance using Sub_Category_ID,
Material_ID, Plant_ID, and Material_Type_ID alone. This highlights the need for alternative models, like multinomial, ordinal regression or random forest, to better capture
nonlinear relationships and provide reliable insights into supplier performance (Fig. 15).

Optimizing Supplier Performance

363

Fig. 15. Logistics Regression Iteration 2

To address the limitations of the binary logistic regression model, a multinomial
regression was conducted using the new target variable, New_Metric, which classified
performance into three levels: high-performing (rank 1), medium-performing (rank 2),
and low-performing (rank 3). This approach provided a more detailed analysis of the
relationship between predictors and performance outcomes, uncovering patterns missed
by the binary model.
The multinomial logistic regression results (Fig. 16) revealed a statistically significant model (LLR p-value = 2.693e-113), confirming the collective influence of predictors like Sub Category ID, Plant ID, Material ID, and Material Type ID on performance.
However, the low pseudo R-squared value (0.04215) indicates the model explains only
4.2% of performance variability, suggesting unaccounted factors.
Plant ID showed a significant negative relationship with performance levels 2 and 3,
underscoring the impact of plant-specific characteristics on outcomes. Material ID had
statistically significant but small coefficients, indicating a minor influence, while Sub
Category ID was not significant, playing a limited role in rankings. Material Type ID
was significant for both performance levels, with negative coefficients highlighting its
role in performance differences.
While the multinomial model offered insights into performance variability, its low
explanatory power and inability to account for ranking order highlight the need for additional predictors or advanced techniques. Nonetheless, it provides a deeper understanding of supplier performance and identifies key areas for further analysis and operational
improvements.

364

S. Al Zaied et al.

Fig. 16. Multinomial Regression

To better capture the ordinal nature of the supplier performance metric, ordinal
logistic & probit regression models were employed, as shown in Fig. 18. These models are
designed to analyze ranked outcomes, such as New_Metric, while respecting the ordered
structure of the response variable. This approach provided a nuanced understanding of
the predictors’ influence.
The evaluation revealed critical insights into the relationship between categorical
predictors and the ordinal response variable. Variance Inflation Factor (VIF) values were
all below 5, indicating minimal multicollinearity. In the logistic model, Sub Category
ID and Plant ID showed significant positive coefficients (p < 0.01), demonstrating a
strong association with higher supplier performance rankings. Material Type ID, also
significant (p < 0.01), had a negative coefficient, highlighting its adverse impact on
performance. The thresholds for rank boundaries were highly significant, confirming
the robustness of the model’s segmentation.
The probit regression yielded similar findings but with slight differences in coefficient
magnitudes and standard errors. It showed a marginally better fit (AIC = 8750, BIC
= 8788) compared to the logistic model (AIC = 8773, BIC = 8811), suggesting a
slight preference for modeling the latent variable underlying supplier performance. Both
models demonstrated moderate precision, recall, and F1 scores across ranks, with overall
accuracy at approximately 42%, pointing to opportunities for improvement through
feature engineering or adding explanatory variables.
These results emphasize the critical roles of supplier-specific categories and plant
operations in shaping performance outcomes, providing actionable insights for strategic
supply chain optimization (Figs. 17 and 18).
Since the data provided is complex in its nature, it is highly possible that there are
some relationships which are not being captured in the regression models. Hence, a
Random Forest model was utilized to capture these complex relationships. The random forest model was utilized to evaluate the importance of four key predictors—Sub
Category ID, Plant ID, Material ID, and Material Type ID—in determining supplier
performance. To refine the model and identify the most impactful set of variables, two
additional configurations were tested: one excluding Material ID and another excluding

Optimizing Supplier Performance

365

Fig. 18. Ordinal Regression (2)

Fig. 17. Ordinal Regression (1)

Material Type ID. This approach aimed to determine which combination of predictors
achieved the highest accuracy and offered the most reliable insights for performance
evaluation.
The original model, incorporating all four predictors, demonstrated balanced feature
importance, with Material ID emerging as the most influential variable, followed by
Material Type ID, Plant ID, and Sub Category ID. This configuration achieved an overall accuracy of 69.4%, highlighting its robust predictive capability. The classification
metrics also indicated strong precision, recall, and F1-scores, confirming the value of
incorporating all predictors in the model (Figs. 19 and 20).

Fig. 19. Classification Report; Four
Variables

Fig. 20. Feature Importance

When Material ID was excluded, the model’s accuracy dropped slightly to 58%.
While Material Type ID gained prominence in feature importance, the absence of Material ID reduced the model’s ability to capture critical patterns in the dataset. Similarly,
excluding Material Type ID in Fig. 23 & Fig. 24 resulted in an accuracy of 68%, though
the model’s predictive performance across specific categories varied slightly. Both modifications highlighted the essential roles that Material ID and Material Type ID play in
accurately predicting supplier performance (Figs. 21 and 22).
Based on this analysis, the original model with all four predictors proved to be
the most effective for evaluating supplier performance. Its higher accuracy and balanced

366

S. Al Zaied et al.

Fig. 22. Feature Importance
Fig. 21. Classification Report; Material
ID Excluded

Fig. 24. Feature Importance
Fig. 23. Classification Report; Material
Type ID Excluded

feature importance provided a comprehensive understanding of the variables influencing
performance. The results highlight that both Material ID and Material Type ID are
essential for capturing the complexities of supplier metrics, and excluding either weakens
the model’s predictive power and interpretability.
Another methodology employed, as outlined in the methodology section, was the
Naïve Bayes classifier. This probabilistic model assumes conditional independence
among predictors. Despite this simplifying assumption, it performs well in many cases
by leveraging probabilities for classification. In the context of supplier performance, the
Naïve Bayes model used the predictors shown below to compute posterior probabilities
for acceptable or unacceptable rankings. It calculated these probabilities by combining
the likelihood of each predictor, as demonstrated in the Python output provided.
The Naive Bayes model provides probabilistic predictions, such as a near-certain
likelihood (e.g., 99.9%) of acceptable performance under specific conditions like downtime = 3 and material type = 2. These probabilities enable scenario analysis and targeted
decision-making by highlighting the conditional effects of predictors like downtime and

Optimizing Supplier Performance

367

material selection. Small changes in these factors can significantly shift performance
likelihoods, offering actionable insights for operational strategies.
The Gaussian Naive Bayes classifier achieved 99.4% accuracy on the test dataset,
effectively distinguishing between acceptable and unacceptable outcomes. Precision,
recall, and F1-scores were 0.99 for both “No” and “Yes” classes, demonstrating the
model’s reliability in minimizing false positives and negatives. The dataset’s balance contributed to this performance, and the algorithm’s simplicity allowed rapid computations
without sacrificing accuracy.
However, the model’s assumption of predictor independence may oversimplify realworld complexities, as correlations between predictors like plant location and material type might influence results. Future iterations could explore feature interactions to
address this limitation. Despite this, the model is an efficient and interpretable tool
for supplier performance classification, with exceptional accuracy and potential for
refinement (Figs. 25 and 26).

Fig. 26. Yes, No predictions graph

5 Conclusion and Recommendations
This project explored various data analysis methods to understand supplier performance
and identify key factors in supply chain efficiency. Logistic regression provided insights
into how Plant ID and Material Type ID influence performance, but its binary framework highlighted the need for more nuanced approaches. Multinomial logistic regression
added three performance levels, revealing variability across suppliers, but its low pseudo
R-squared suggested the need for more data.
Ordinal logistic and probit regression models respected performance rankings and
showed that Plant ID and Sub Category ID positively contribute to performance, while
Material Type ID has a negative effect. Probit regression offered a slightly better fit,
but both models had limitations. Random Forest, with 69.4% accuracy, captured nonlinear relationships and identified Material ID and Plant ID as key factors. Gaussian
Naive Bayes excelled in binary classification with 99.4% accuracy but assumed predictor
independence, limiting its real-world application.

368

S. Al Zaied et al.

To improve evaluation, expanding the dataset with additional metrics and using
advanced models like Gradient Boosting or Neural Networks will better capture complex relationships. Focusing on high-impact materials and plants can yield immediate
benefits, while an integrated framework combining predictive analytics and actionable
metrics will drive long-term supplier development. Implementing these recommendations will refine evaluation processes, offer deeper insights, and strengthen supply chain
strategies for greater efficiency and adaptability in a changing market.

Systematic Review of Machine Learning (ML)
and Artificial Intelligence (AI)
in Pharmaceutical Supply Chain (PSC)
Resilience: Identifying Gaps and Future
Research Directions
Dua Weraikat1(B)

and Shireen Al-Hourani2

1 Rochester Institute of Technology, Dubai, Dubai, UAE

dxwcad@rit.edu

2 University Canada West, Vancouver, Canada

shireen.al-hourani@ucanwest.ca

Abstract. The resilience of the pharmaceutical supply chain (PSC) is crucial to
ensure the continuous availability of essential medicines and other products, particularly during logistics disruptions. This article presents a systematic review
of the literature on machine learning (ML) and artificial intelligence (AI) techniques and their importance to PSC resilience. By analyzing existing literature
from multiple databases, including Web of Science and IEEE Xplore, over the
past five years, we identified key areas where ML and AI have been effectively
utilized. These areas include demand forecasting, risk management, and inventory
optimization. This review also highlights significant research gaps and proposes
future directions for investigation. Our findings suggest that while ML and AI
offer promising solutions for improving supply chain resilience, there is a need
for more studies that integrate various ML and AI approaches into PSC. Our analysis reveals that there are no clear government regulations related to the usage of
ML or AI in PSC, no robust real-world applications addressing the challenges of
adopting these technologies, and no clear predictive models to assess their impact
on PSC resilience. This ongoing work aims to provide a foundation for future
research, ultimately fostering more adaptive and resilient PSCs.
Keywords: Pharmaceutical Supply Chain · Resilience · Artificial Intelligence
(AI) · Machine Learning (ML)

1 Introduction
The Pharmaceutical Supply Chain (PSC) is critical to ensuring the continuous availability of medications and health products to populations around the globe. As a complex
network involving manufacturers, suppliers, distributors, and healthcare providers, the
PSC must balance the demand and supply of pharmaceutical products, manage inventories, and maintain regulatory compliance. The COVID-19 pandemic exposed significant
© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 370–382, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_28

Systematic Review of Machine Learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI)

371

vulnerabilities and complexities inherent within the PSC and revealed significant challenges such as supply disruptions, logistical inefficiencies, and demand surges. These
issues highlight the urgent need for innovative solutions to enhance the PSC’s robustness
and reliability [1].
Machine Learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) offer promising solutions
to the above-mentioned challenges. Integrating ML and AI into the PSC can transform
data analysis and decision-making processes, providing predictive insights and automation that significantly enhance efficiency and resilience [2]. For instance, ML algorithms
can be employed to forecast demand, optimize inventory levels, and streamline logistics, reducing the risk of stockouts and overstock situations [3]. AI-driven analytics can
improve supply chain visibility, enabling better risk management and response to disruptions. Moreover, AI technologies such as natural language processing and computer
vision can enhance regulatory compliance and quality control processes or government
regulations, ensuring pharmaceutical products’ safe and timely delivery [4].
Integrating ML and AI into PSC comes however with its set of challenges. These
include concerns over data privacy, the substantial investment required for technology
and infrastructure, and the need to seamlessly adopt AI systems with existing supply
chain processes. Despite these obstacles, the potential benefits, such as improved operational efficiency, cost savings, and better patient outcomes, highlight the crucial role of
ML and AI in building a resilient PSC [5].
This review focuses on reviewing the available literature to identify the gaps in
understanding related to the application of ML and AI in PSC resilience. In addition to
that, this work aims to provide future research directions that can guide researchers in
addressing the critical challenges and opportunities in this field.

2 Literature Review
The pharmaceutical supply chain (PSC) is a complex network that requires robust strategies to ensure resilience and sustainability, especially during disruptions such as disease
outbreaks or global crises. ML and AI have emerged as a powerful tool to optimize various aspects of the supply chain, from supplier selection to inventory management and
risk mitigation. By leveraging ML algorithms, pharmaceutical companies can improve
demand forecasting, optimize inventory management, and streamline logistics, thereby
reducing the risk of stockouts and overstock situations [1, 2]. While there is almost
nothing in the literature related to using machine learning to improve resilience in PSC,
its potential can be inferred from its application in other supply chains.
2.1 Demand Forecasting and Inventory Management
Accurate demand forecasting is crucial for maintaining high performance in PSCs. LuhPutu et al. [6] demonstrated that ML algorithms, such as random forest and simple
trees, significantly enhance demand forecasting accuracy, with improvements ranging
from 10% to 41%. Similarly, Fourkiotis and Tsadiras [7] compared traditional statistical methods with advanced ML techniques like LSTM neural networks and XGBoost,

372

D. Weraikat and S. Al-Hourani

finding that ML models performed better than traditional methods in predicting pharmaceutical sales. These studies emphasize the potential of ML to optimize production,
storage, and distribution strategies in the pharmaceutical industry that can untimely
improve PSC resilience.
Integrating ML with frameworks like the Supply Chain Operations Reference
(SCOR) model can significantly enhance inventory management through supplier selection and order allocation. The gradient boosting algorithm has been shown to effectively
classify and rank suppliers based on acceptability scores, aiding in the selection of the
most resilient and sustainable suppliers [8, 9]. Recently, Angula and Dongo [10] proposed a decision support framework combining fuzzy multi-criteria decision-making
techniques with multi-objective programming to optimize supplier selection and order
allocation, considering both sustainability and resilience. The model focused on forecasting medication demand and supply in public healthcare systems. Their findings
indicate that linear regression and random forest models are predominant in predicting
medication quantities, despite challenges such as data entry inaccuracies and organizational barriers. The study highlighted the need for further research to improve forecasting
algorithms and incorporate additional patient-associated factors.
2.2 Risk Management and Resilience Analytics
ML and AI can play a crucial role in enhancing supply chain resilience by developing capabilities such as transparency, last-mile delivery, and agile procurement strategies. These technologies help minimize the impact of disruptions and offer personalized
solutions to stakeholders which can enhance supply chain risk management through
quicker responses to disruptions [4]. Furthermore, simulation-based approaches can
model complex supply chain environments, allowing for better decision-making and
trade-offs between robustness, operational efficiency, and environmental concerns [11].
For example, Golan et al. [12] advocated using resilience analytics to model and enhance
the vaccine supply chain’s ability to predict, absorb, recover, and adapt to disruptions.
Their research highlights the importance of AI-driven analytics in improving supply
chain transparency and agility. Predictive analytics using ML algorithms can also forecast disease outbreaks, enabling adjustments in supply chain operations to manage risks
effectively.
Lately, Kamran et al. [13] developed a stochastic multi-objective, multi-period, and
multi-commodity simulation-optimization model to address the complexities of vaccine
production and distribution, incorporating system dynamics to estimate vaccine demand.
Likewise, Kochakkashani et al. [14] developed a robust supply chain model to manage
uncertainties in vaccine and pharmaceutical distribution during the COVID-19 crisis,
emphasizing the importance of flexibility and adaptability in supply chain planning.
Their model integrates ML techniques to optimize inventory levels and ensure a resilient
supply chain.
2.3 Digitalization and Advanced Technologies
Digitalizing supply chain systems using machine learning and other digital technologies
can lead to more sustainable and resilient operations. Strategies such as identifying

Systematic Review of Machine Learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI)

373

constraints, following a digital roadmap, and maintaining agile systems are essential for
improving the delivery and quality of pharmaceutical products without compromising
sustainability [9, 10]. The integration of blockchain and AI as analyzed by Charles
et al. [15], highlighted their potential to enhance supply chain security, efficiency, and
productivity. They found that blockchain could provide a secure, transparent ledger for
tracking pharmaceutical products, while AI algorithms optimize various supply chain
processes.
Marmolejo-Saucedo [16] explored the integration of digital twins in supply chains,
which can improve inventory management by providing real-time data and predictive
analytics. This approach helps in dynamically adjusting replenishment policies, thereby
reducing operating costs and enhancing responsiveness to environmental changes. The
adoption of Industry 4.0 technologies, such as IoT and automation, further enhances
supply chain visibility and flexibility. Qader et al. [17] used structural equation modelling to analyze the relationships between Industry 4.0 adoption, supply chain resilience
(SCRes), and supply chain performance (SCP), finding that these technologies significantly enhance both resilience and performance. Recent studies have highlighted the
effectiveness of these approaches.
2.4 Future Directions and Challenges
The literature indicates a growing interest in applying ML and AI to enhance PSC
resilience. Future research should focus on developing comprehensive resilience frameworks, integrating advanced technologies, and addressing data security challenges.
Empirical studies are needed to validate the conceptual benefits of these technologies
and explore their practical implications in diverse healthcare settings. For example,
Jauhar et al. [18] proposed a no-code AI approach to predict and minimize inventory
distortions, making ML accessible to non-technical users and enhancing supply chain
resilience. Rehman and Ali [19] used Multi-Criteria Decision-Making (MCDM) techniques in a fuzzy environment to prioritize resilience strategies for healthcare supply
chains, emphasizing the importance of advanced decision-making techniques.

3 Methodology
The Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses PRISMA was
adopted to ensure a structured and transparent review process [20]. The methodology
encompassed four key phases: identification, screening, eligibility, and data extraction.
These phases were meticulously executed to identify relevant articles, evaluate their
quality, and extract valuable insights for analysis. The primary aim of this review is to
investigate the impact of ML and AI techniques in enhancing PSC resilience. Specifically,
the focus was on identifying their applications, assessing their impact on various PSC
resilience dimensions, and highlighting significant research gaps.
3.1 Search Strategy
A comprehensive search was conducted across two major databases, Web of Science and
IEEE Xplore Digital Library, to retrieve relevant peer-reviewed articles and conference

proceedings from the past five years. The search strategy employed combinations of
Boolean operators and keywords. The detailed search protocol is summarized in Table 1.

3.2 Screening and Eligibility Process
The initial search results for each database and keyword combination are detailed in
Table 3. The search initially yielded 270 articles. After removing duplicates, 223 articles
remained and were screened based on their titles and abstracts for relevance. Following this, full-text eligibility assessments were conducted for 76 studies, with 69 being
excluded due to misalignment with the inclusion criteria (e.g., irrelevant focus, insufficient methodological detail). Ultimately, 7 studies met the criteria and were included
for detailed analysis. This limited number of relevant articles supports the necessity and
rationale for this research.

3.3 Data Extraction and Classification
From the identified literature, the classification process was conducted as follows. Key
information, including study objectives, ML and AI applications, resilience dimensions,
functional areas, methodologies, and performance outcomes, were extracted and organized into a predefined classification framework. This framework categorized studies
and articles based on the following parameters:
– Resilience Dimension: Agility, flexibility, visibility, redundancy, collaboration, risk
management.
– ML/AI Application: Demand forecasting, inventory optimization, risk mitigation,
disruption recovery.
– Functional Area: Planning, procurement, logistics, inventory management, customer
service.
– Study Type: Empirical, theoretical, simulation, case study.
– Performance Metrics: Accuracy, efficiency, scalability, real-world application.
– Regulatory Challenges: Mention of compliance, privacy, and ethical concerns.

4 Results and Discussions
This section presents the findings of the systematic review, organized into key themes,
including ML and AI applications, resilience dimensions, functional areas, and performance outcomes. The discussion integrates these findings to interpret their implications,
identify challenges, and highlight research gaps.
4.1 General Overview of the Found Articles
From the 223 reviewed articles (after removing the duplicated articles), Demand Forecasting emerged as the most frequently studied application, reflecting its critical role in
ensuring medicine availability. Supervised learning techniques such as time series analysis and regression models were most commonly applied. While Inventory Optimization
demonstrated measurable improvements in cost and efficiency, few studies addressed the
scalability of ML models across global supply chains. Disruption Recovery and Data
Sharing still need to be explored, with limited evidence of practical implementation
despite their importance for resilience.
Most studies focused on agility, visibility, and risk management, aligning with immediate operational needs. Redundancy and collaboration were underexplored dimensions,
highlighting a gap in long-term PSC resilience strategies.
Supervised Learning was the predominant technique for demand forecasting and
risk mitigation. Techniques like neural networks and decision trees improved prediction accuracy. Optimization Algorithms demonstrated strong potential for improving
logistics and inventory efficiency. Reinforcement Learning was rarely applied, despite
its suitability for dynamic decision-making under uncertainty. Blockchain Integration
showed promise for data sharing and tracking, though its adoption remains limited due
to regulatory and scalability challenges.

Systematic Review of Machine Learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI)

Improved accuracy and cost reduction were consistent across studies, demonstrating tangible benefits of ML in PSC. However, evidence of real-world scalability and
implementation still needs to be improved, particularly in low-resource settings.

as shown in Table 4.

Systematic Review of Machine Learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI)

379

Table 7 above highlights the pivotal role of ML and AI in enhancing PSC resilience.
Key resilience dimensions addressed include agility, flexibility, and collaboration, with
applications ranging from demand forecasting and inventory optimization to supplier
selection and risk management. The reviewed studies demonstrate significant advancements in functional areas such as logistics, inventory management, and supplier operations. Prominent ML techniques, including gradient boosting, random forests, and
multi-objective optimization, were employed to improve decision-making accuracy and
operational efficiency. However, the research reveals substantial gaps, including limited
scalability of proposed frameworks, inadequate real-world implementations, and underexplored dimensions such as redundancy in building buffers and safety stocks or backup
suppliers and collaboration among PSC entities. These findings underscore the need for
broader adoption of ML and AI techniques in PSCs and the development of scalable,
adaptive frameworks to address global supply chain challenges effectively.
4.3 Discussion and Research Gaps
From the results discussed in Sects. 4.1 and 4.2, the following gaps were identified.
1. Integration of Advanced Technologies in Real-World Settings
Despite the theoretical promise of ML and AI in strengthening supply chain
resilience, the current literature is predominantly limited to simulations and conceptual frameworks. Empirical studies validating the effectiveness of these technologies in real-world PSC are limited. Future research should focus on large-scale
pilot projects and cross-sector implementations to provide concrete evidence of their
impact on PSCs.
2. Development of Comprehensive Resilience Frameworks
Existing studies often adopt a fragmented approach by focusing on specific aspects
of resilience, such as demand forecasting or supplier selection. However, there is a
need for comprehensive frameworks that integrate technological, and process-based
strategies across multiple dimensions of supply chain management. Such frameworks
could combine elements like risk management, collaboration, and redundancy with
advanced analytics to provide a comprehensive blueprint for supply chain resilience.
3. Scalability of ML and AI Models
ML and AI models have yet to be tested at scale in global supply chains. Most
studies remain confined to localized or hypothetical scenarios, raising questions about
the adaptability of these models to the complexities of multinational supply networks.
Future research should emphasize developing scalable and adaptive models capable
of addressing global supply chain challenges.
4. Addressing Data Security, Privacy, and Regulatory Barriers
The adoption of digital technologies in supply chains raises critical concerns about
data security, privacy, and compliance. Current research often overlooks these challenges, focusing primarily on technical capabilities. To enable broader adoption of
AI and ML, future work must prioritize robust data governance frameworks and standardized regulatory guidelines that safeguard sensitive information while fostering
ethical and scalable innovation.

380

D. Weraikat and S. Al-Hourani

5. Underexplored Resilience Dimensions
Key resilience dimensions such as collaboration, redundancy, and disruption
recovery are underrepresented in the current literature. These dimensions are critical for long-term supply chain adaptability and require focused research to uncover
effective strategies and technological interventions. By addressing these challenges,
ML and AI can serve as vital tools in creating robust, adaptive, and resilient PSCs
that are prepared to withstand future disruptions.

5 Conclusion
This systematic review has explored the integration of Machine Learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) within the Pharmaceutical Supply Chain (PSC), emphasizing their
potential to enhance resilience. The PSC faces significant challenges, including supply
disruptions, logistical inefficiencies, and fluctuating demand, all of which were exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic. These challenges necessitate innovative solutions,
and ML/AI techniques present promising avenues for improvement.
By applying the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and MetaAnalyses (PRISMA) framework, this study meticulously identified, screened and analyzed relevant articles from leading databases. The findings indicate that ML/AI applications, such as demand forecasting, inventory optimization, risk mitigation, and disruption
recovery, have shown substantial potential in enhancing various resilience dimensions
of the PSC. For instance, ML models have improved demand forecasting accuracy by
up to 30% and reduced inventory holding costs by 20–25%.
Despite these advancements, significant gaps remain. The majority of studies are still
in the theoretical or simulation stage, with limited real-world implementation. Issues of
scalability and regulatory barriers persist, and several resilience dimensions, such as
collaboration and redundancy, are underexplored. Addressing these gaps requires realworld pilot projects, the development of comprehensive regulatory frameworks, and
focused research on underrepresented areas.
The practical implications of adopting ML and AI in PSCs are comprehensive, and
promising to transform operations for manufacturers, distributors, healthcare providers,
and patients. Therefore, industry stakeholders, policymakers, and researchers must
collaborate on initiatives that leverage ML and AI for PSC resilience.
Future research should aim to bridge these gaps, enabling the practical and scalable
adoption of ML and AI technologies in the PSC, and ultimately ensuring a more robust
and reliable supply chain for pharmaceutical products. Additionally, developing userfriendly ML tools for non-technical users and creating standardized protocols for data
governance will be crucial.
In conclusion, while ML and AI offer substantial benefits for enhancing the resilience
of the PSC, their full potential can only be realized through concerted efforts to overcome
existing challenges. This work is ongoing, and additional databases such as Scopus are
currently being reviewed to provide more comprehensive coverage of the topic. Enhancing PSC resilience will not only improve supply chain efficiency but also contribute to
better public health outcomes and greater preparedness for future pandemics.

Evolutionary Techniques
for Combinatorial Reverse Auctions
in Electricity Consumption
Sifat E. Jahan and Malek Mouhoub(B)
Department of Computer Science, University of Regina, Regina, SK, Canada
{sjt588,mouhoubm}@uregina.ca

Abstract. The probability of power outages increases as electricity
demand increases. To solve this problem, utility companies have started
purchasing electricity through e-auctions. We investigate a method that
involves the acquisition of energy from several suppliers, balancing several goals, and solving a challenging winner-determination problem to
maximize resource acquisition to meet increasing power demand. Using
exact methods is impractical to solve these NP-hard problems. As an
alternative, nature-inspired methods are more eﬀective, as they can trade
in the quality of the solution with the required computational time.
Previous studies focused on the application of diﬀerent nature-inspired
techniques (Genetic Algorithms (GAs), Whale Optimization Algorithm
(WOA), Ant Colony Optimization (ACO), Particle Swarm Optimization
(PSO) and Fireﬂy Algorithm (FA)) in terms of eﬀectiveness in producing
high-quality solutions for various instances of the Combinatorial Reverse
Auction (CRA) problem. This study investigates the eﬀectiveness, in
solution quality and time performance, of variants of Multi-Objective
Optimization (MOO) methods in tackling the CRA problem. These variants include the Strength Pareto Evolutionary Algorithm (SPEA) with
diﬀerent clustering techniques, the Non-dominated Sorting Genetic Algorithm II (NSGA-II) and the Pareto-Dominance Evolutionary Algorithm
(EA). Moreover, a comparison has been conducted between evolutionarybased single-objective and multiobjective approaches to solve instances
of CRA problems.
Keywords: Combinatorial reverse auctions · Electricity auctions ·
Retail markets · Winner determination · Evolutionary techniques

1

Introduction

The energy demand has grown dramatically as a result of rapid economic development, infrastructural expansion, and the growth of the population. Consequently, many nations are exploring strategies to upgrade their electrical infrastructure to improve supply and meet increasing demand. Due to its eﬀectiveness
and increasing competitiveness in the market, online e-auctions are gaining popularity, and utility companies have started purchasing electricity to resolve the
c The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026
B. Dorronsoro et al. (Eds.): OLA 2025, CCIS 2808, pp. 383–395, 2026.
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-13589-6_29

384

S. E. Jahan and M. Mouhoub

energy demand. Traditional approaches have often relied on recommendation
systems to manage consumer usage rates, raising privacy concerns, since these
recommendations are based on personal lifestyle data. On-line electricity auctions, on the other hand, can integrate a diverse range of energy sources. Despite
their potential, research on online electricity auctions, particularly those using
CRAs, remains limited. Most studies emphasize the advantages of CRAs while
overlooking the technical challenges. Only a handful of researchers have investigated solutions to the electricity demand problem using both exact and approximate methods. In addition, managing online electricity trading to balance the
needs of buyers and suppliers presents a highly complex task. The constraints
involved in this process signiﬁcantly complicate the winner-determination problem, making it diﬃcult to achieve an optimal solution. In this work, we present a
study that investigates practical approaches to eﬀectively resolving the problem
of determining the winner of electricity consumption, following the description of
the problem and the suggested GA-based solution in [1]. In our previous study,
we analyzed various trade-oﬀs of GAs alongside other meta-heuristic techniques
[2]. In this study, we are going to address new strategies to eﬃciently solve the
winner determination problem for electricity consumption that produce feasible
solutions that can be optimal or at least nearly optimal solutions.
This study plans to explore larger-scale electricity markets using multiobjective evolutionary algorithms to determine the best trading-oﬀ mechanism.
It will focus on the diﬀerent multi-objective optimization techniques: SPEA,
NSGA-II and Pareto-Dominance EA [3]. In the case of SPEA, several clustering
techniques will be considered. The ultimate objective is to determine the eﬃcacy,
in solution quality and time performance, of these methods for the online electrical auction market. It should be mentioned that, to the best of our knowledge,
no prior research has been done on using multi-objective evolutionary algorithms
for the CRA problem in the electricity market.

2

Background

2.1

Auctions, Electricity Auction, and Winner Determination

CRAs are complex problems that involve identifying auction winners using exact
or approximate methods [1, 2,4–9]. However, there is a lack of research on CRAs
for electricity markets [1], and designing auctions with bidding features can be
challenging. Multi-attribute auctions are time-consuming and complicated, with
features such as duration and service quality that go beyond price [10]. Online
auctions, whether sealed or open, have unique characteristics and connections
with buyers and sellers [11,12]. Most research on electricity auctions emphasizes
their advantages but lacks a speciﬁc mechanism for design and experimental
analysis.
Combinatorial electricity auctions are recognized for their eﬃciency in
resource allocation and proﬁt maximization, but their inherent complexity has
limited thorough research. The development of CRAs in the electricity sector is
particularly challenging [1,5], and only a few studies have demonstrated their

Evolutionary Techniques for CRAs

385

signiﬁcant market inﬂuence [1, 13]. An approach proposed is the Probability Bidding Mechanism (PBM), a double combinatorial auction protocol aimed at minimizing prices and maximizing quantities [14], although it remains theoretical
with no practical implementation. In [13], a recent study presents a method for
reducing buyer costs by using single units and attributes, supported by a userfriendly interface and the IBM CPLEX optimizer to determine auction winners.
Electricity auctions have a substantial economic impact, particularly in Brazil, a
leading country in the generation of renewable energy [6]. Implementing appropriate strategies in electricity market auctions can lead to notable reductions in
annual costs [15]. On the other hand, online auctions are prone to fraud, necessitating eﬀective fraud prevention mechanisms. One study introduces a novel
in-action fraud (IAF) monitoring system, providing a comprehensive analysis of
fraud types and detection methods [7]. The CRA model in [1] aims to facilitate
the purchase of electricity from various energy sources in anticipation of peak
load periods. Energy suppliers, including homeowners, participate in the bidding
of items with conﬂicting objectives of quantity and price. The model has two
rounds, one restricted to highly weather-dependent and environmentally friendly
energy such as wind and solar energy, and the second considering other sources
such as controllable load and renewable energy. The CRA problem is addressed
using a GA-based approach, which serves as the foundation for our work. We
are further exploring alternative methods to eﬃciently solve the CRA problem
by incorporating nature-inspired techniques and MOO.
In the winner determination problem, metaheuristics are eﬀective techniques
that compromise the quality of the returned solutions for running time [16].
They include methods inspired by nature, such as the multi-attribute winner
determination problem [8]. In [17] various multi-attribute auction situations are
discussed. As mentioned in [18], the stability of CRAs in large-market auctions
can be guaranteed by applying the Nash equilibrium concept and presuming
that all bidders and sellers adhere to the same standard.
The study in [19] introduces a GA-based technique to optimize resource allocation in combinatorial reverse auctions, to maximize the quantities sold by sellers, and to solve the problem of determining the winner. In the energy sector,
GAs have been used to solve multi-attribute combinatorial dispatching problems in multiprocessor environments, as well as to optimize warehouse service
time and storage policies [20,21]. The GAMICRA technique, based on GAs, is
proposed as a feasible solution to determine the winner in auctions of the electricity market, while another study presents an innovative GA-based solution
considering characteristics, limitations and limitations [1,8,9].
2.2

Multi-Objective Optimization (MOO)

MOO involves optimizing two or more conﬂicting objectives simultaneously while
adhering to a set of constraints. It provides a set of trade-oﬀ solutions called the
Pareto front, where the objective is the function to be optimized. Several natureinspired algorithms are used for MOO, including GAs, PSO, and ACO. Also,
NSGA-II is a popular MOEA for ﬁnding a well-distributed Pareto front, while

386

S. E. Jahan and M. Mouhoub

Decomposition-Based MOEA (MOEA/D) decomposes the problem into subproblems and solves them simultaneously. MOO has applications in real-world
scheduling including university timetabling, healthcare scheduling, manufacturing, and cloud computing.
The Pareto-dominance technique consists of storing non-dominated solutions
so far from the population. The main idea is to save Pareto-optimal or nondominated solutions found in each iteration. Pareto-dominance approaches are
popular in MOO problems and have shown successful results. One of the most
famous algorithms is SPEA, which focuses on ﬁnding a diverse set of solutions
while maintaining a strong emphasis on Pareto optimality [3].
The Pareto-dominance approach oﬀers several notable advantages in MOO
and preference-based networks. It promotes solution diversity by maintaining
a varied set of candidates, which is essential for eﬀectively addressing multiobjective challenges. Furthermore, GAs exhibit a reduced tendency to converge
prematurely on local optima, thereby facilitating a wide exploration of the solution space. The Pareto-dominance approach is also adept at identifying solutions
that closely approximate the true Pareto front. Additionally, its Pareto dominance framework enables parallel exploration of multiple solutions, enhancing
its eﬃciency and eﬀectiveness in optimization tasks.
SPEA is an EA designed to solve MOO problems by ﬁnding Pareto-optimal
solutions [3]. It was ﬁrst proposed by Eckart Zitzler and Lothar Thiele in 1999
and is known for its eﬀectiveness in maintaining a diverse set of solutions and
converging towards the Pareto front. SPEA’s key components include an external archive, ﬁtness assignment based on Pareto dominance, a selection process
for solutions to undergo crossover and mutation, environmental selection using
clustering techniques to prevent overcrowding, and recombination and mutation using standard evolutionary operators to create new solutions for the next
generation. The algorithm’s focus on improving the quality and diversity of solutions in MOO problems is evident in its eﬀectiveness. SPEA provides an ideal
balance between exploration and exploitation by including strength-based ﬁtness assignments and keeping an external library of non-dominated solutions.
Its goal is to identify a wide range of optimum solutions in several competing
objectives. It involves generating an initial population and an empty archive,
calculating ﬁtness based on dominance relationships, updating the archive by
adding non-dominated individuals, selecting individuals for crossover and mutation, and replacing the current population with oﬀspring. The advantages of
SPEA include the maintenance of diversity, convergence, and the retention of the
best solutions found so far. Its clustering mechanism ensures a well-distributed
set of solutions along the Pareto front, and its archive management allows for the
retention of the best solutions. It is used in constraint scheduling problems, engineering design, and ﬁnancial optimization to ﬁnd non-conﬂict, optimal solutions
using multiple criteria.
NSGA-II is another widely used MOO algorithm to solve problems with
conﬂicting objectives [22,23]. NSGA-II classiﬁes solutions into Pareto fronts
based on dominance. The ﬁrst front contains non-dominated solutions, while

Evolutionary Techniques for CRAs

387

subsequent fronts are identiﬁed by iteratively removing solutions of earlier
fronts. Within each front, the crowding distance is calculated to maintain diversity, preferring solutions in less crowded regions of the objective space. This
ensures a well-distributed and diverse set of high-quality solutions. NSGA-II
integrates non-dominated sorting and crowding distance with genetic operators
like crossover and mutation to solve multi-objective problems eﬃciently. Initialize a random population, evaluate objectives, and iteratively reﬁne solutions
by selecting and generating oﬀspring. The elitist strategy and preservation of
diversity enable NSGA-II to converge to the Pareto-optimal front, making it a
popular choice in optimization research and applications. It is a key component
of MOO, which balances exploration and exploitation in various ﬁelds, such as
engineering design or resource allocation, setting a standard in academic and
industrial research.

3

Proposed CRA Solving Techniques

The multi-attribute reverse auction is a winner-determination problem that can
be impractical when using exact methods due to their inherited exponential
time cost. Approximate methods are preferred due to their practicality, eﬃciency, and potential for optimal or nearly optimal solutions [24]. In this section,
we will present several evolutionary methods to solve the problem of determining the winner of the electricity auction, considering constraints and objectives
as deﬁned in [1]. In the CRA we are considering, buyers can buy electricity
from various suppliers, while suppliers can produce it from various sources. The
winners may be multiple suppliers, but only one for each item (time slot) of
electricity demand is considered.
3.1

Problem Description and Representation

Following the deﬁnition of the CRA problem in [1, 2], we consider the scenario
in which we have one buyer providing the minimum and maximum electricity
demand for a series of time slots. The buyer also provides the maximum price
for each time slot. On the other hand, we have a set of sellers who provide the
minimum price for each time slot. The auction will then start where each buyer
bids for the chosen time slots. The winner will then correspond to the optimal
assignment of buyers to time slots such that both the total price is minimized
and the quantity is maximized. These two objectives are, respectively, denoted
in Eqs. 1 and 2 [2].

bidP ricei is the bid price for the item (time slot) i(1 ≤ i ≤ n), minP ricei and
maxP ricei are the minimum and maximum prices, respectively, set by the seller
and the buyer (minP ricei ≤ bidP ricei ≤ maxP ricei ), bidQuantityi is the bid
quantity for the item i, and minQuantityi and maxQuantityi are the minimum
and maximum quantity set by the buyer (minQuantityi ≤ bidQuantityi ≤
maxQuantityi ). Figure 1 shows a candidate solution (chromosome) where each
entry contains the seller ID, the bid quantity, and the bid price. For all of the
techniques used, we apply the uniform crossover and the swap mutation.

3.2

Pareto-Dominance Evolutionary Algorithm

The ﬂowchart of the proposed Pareto dominance solving technique is shown in
the left branch of Fig. 2. The algorithm starts with a randomly generated population. Then, Pareto dominance is used by considering the two objectives (price
and quantity, as deﬁned in Eqs. 1 and 2) to ﬁnd the non-dominated solutions and
remove the dominant ones from the population. The Pareto dominance relation
(≺) is deﬁned in Eq. 3 between two candidate solutions A and B.
A ≺ B ⇐⇒

fi (A) ≤ fj (B) for all i, j ∈ {1, 2},
fi (A) < fj (B) for at least one i, j ∈ {1, 2}.

(3)

The algorithm continues with the new population that contains the newly
generated solutions and the chosen parents. In each iteration, a random number
of individuals (non-dominated) are selected.
3.3

SPEA

SPEA maintains two populations: a randomly generated population and an
archive (external storage) of a ﬁxed size. We used half of the population size
as the archive size. SPEA stores the Pareto optimal solutions found externally.
It also performs clustering to reduce the number of non-dominated solutions
stored without destroying the characteristics of the Pareto optimal front. In
each iteration, a binary tournament is applied between the current population
and the archive, and the best-ﬁtted ones are selected. The clustering techniques
used for the implementation of SPEA are listed in Table 1. The ﬁtness function
of outcome A is deﬁned as the number of solutions in the archive that dominate
A. The SPEA ﬂow diagram is shown in the middle branch of Fig. 2.

K-means clustering

Partition-based method that divides data into K clusters by minimizing intra-cluster
variance. It is simple and computationally eﬃcient and is the best for spherical clusters
with equal variance

Density-Based Spatial Density-based method forms clusters that group data points based on density. It
Clustering of Noise
identiﬁes clusters as dense regions separated by low-density areas, making it eﬀective for
Applications (DBSCAN) detecting arbitrarily shaped clusters and noise (outliers)
Agglomerative
Clustering

Hierarchical method that recursively merges pairs of clusters until all data points belong
to one cluster or a speciﬁed number of clusters is reached

Aﬃnity Propagation

Message-passing method that automatically determines clusters by passing “messages”
between data points based on similarity

Gaussian Mixture
Models (GMM)

Model-based methods that assume data is generated from a mixture of Gaussian
distributions and estimate parameters using Expectation-Maximization (EM)

Fuzzy C-Means (FCM) A soft clustering method that assigns membership probabilities to each cluster instead of
hard assignments, suitable for overlapping clusters and partial membership in multiple

NSGA-II

The ﬂow diagram of NSGA-II is described in the right branch of Fig. 2. NSGAII starts with a randomly generated population and evaluates individuals based
on two objectives: price (to minimize) and quantity (to maximize). NSGA-II
employs non-dominated sorting, which organizes the population into Pareto
fronts, prioritizing solutions in lower-ranked fronts that exhibit better dominance. The solutions within each front are further ranked based on a crowding
distance metric, which ensures diversity by selecting less crowded solutions in
the objective space as given below. The crowding distance di for a solution i in
a Pareto front is deﬁned as:
M

di =

fm (i + 1) − fm (i − 1)
max − f min

m=1

where: M is the number of objectives, fm (i+1) is the value of the m-th objective
for the next solution in the sorted list, fm (i−1) is the value of the m-th objective
max
is the maximum value of the m-th
for the previous solution in the sorted list, fm
min
is the minimum value of the m-th
objective in the current Pareto front, and fm
objective in the current Pareto front. For boundary solutions: d1 = dN = ∞,
where N is the number of solutions on the Pareto front. To create the next
generation, NSGA-II combines parent and oﬀspring populations (2 × population
size) to select the best solutions for the next generation, ensuring that no good
solutions are lost. In each iteration of NSGA-II , a binary tournament is applied;
it prefers solutions in lower-ranked fronts and breaks ties using the crowding
distance (where solutions with larger distances are preferred).

4

Experimentation

The purpose of the experiments reported in this section is to evaluate the
performance, in practice, of the evolutionary techniques adopted to solve the
CRA problem, namely Pareto dominance, SPEA, and NSGA-II. Moreover,
SPEA has been evaluated using various clustering techniques including K-means,
DBSCAN, Agglomerative, Aﬃnity Propagation, GMM, and Fuzzy C-means. We
have also considered the following three variants of the GA-based technique to
solve the CRA as a single-objective optimization problem.
1. Single-Price: GA-based technique focusing on price (to minimize, as per Eq. 1)
2. Single-Qty: GA-based technique focusing on quantity (to maximize, as per
Eq. 2)
3. Single-PriceQty: GA-based technique that focuses on optimizing a combination of both objectives according to Eq. 4 below.

The experiments are implemented in Python and executed on an Intel(R)
Xeon(R) CPU with 2.20GHz processor speed and RAM of 12.7 GB. Each data
set is run 20 times as part of the implementation of the CRA system to determine the average, best, and standard deviation of both the computation time
and the quality of the solution. We use ﬁve datasets where the number of items
and sellers are, respectively: 8 and 5 for Dataset 1, 15 and 20 for Dataset 2, 75
and 50 for Dataset 3, 100 and 200 for Dataset 4, and 250 and 500 for Dataset
5. For Dataset 2 the diﬀerent clustering techniques are visualized in Fig. 3 using
cluster size 4. Figure 4 shows the diﬀerent clustering techniques with population
variation in dataset 2 using diﬀerent cluster sizes varying from 2 to 8 for various
population sizes (25, 50, 75, 100, 200, 300). To evaluate the diﬀerent clustering
techniques used in SPEA, we have used the Silhouette Score metric. This metric
measures the similarity of each point to its own cluster, considering cohesion
and separation. The score ranges from –1 to +1, with a higher score indicating
better clustering with distinct, well-deﬁned clusters. Points on the border of the
two clusters have a lower score. For smaller populations, Algomerative, GMM,
and Fuzzy were superior to others on average. However, the best result has been
achieved by Aﬃnity Propagation without high deviation. In addition, kMeans
performs well; however, it has a larger deviation. DBSCAN remains stable for
all sizes of populations. For larger population sizes, the clustering results are
quite similar; however, GMM has a high deviation and a lower silhouette score
that indicate bad performance compared to the rest of the clustering techniques.
Table 2 shows the execution time (in milliseconds) of GA with a single objective,
Pareto dominance, SPEA, and NSGA-II in the diﬀerent data sets. All techniques use GA as the base algorithm with population size 50 and 100 iterations.
The execution times for Single-Price and Single-Qty are better than those for
Single-PriceQty, and the performance of the price objective is slightly better than
that of the quantity objective. Moreover, Single-PriceQty objective has higher
variation than Single-Price and Single-Qty. Single-PriceQty execution times are
much better than the EA approaches (Pareto-dominance, SPEA, and NSGAII). Among the EA approaches, the NSGA-II approach is the best performer.
Table 2 shows the ﬁtness function calling rate, where all the techniques perform
well for larger datasets. However, for small datasets, there are some discrepancies in which the performance of the EA techniques was not up to the mark,
although the Pareto dominance was the best performer. Table 3 shows the quality of the solution obtained from the diﬀerent techniques. For smaller datasets,
single objectives give better results than Single-PriceQty but by increasing the
dataset complexity, Single-PriceQty performed better even though the quality
is almost similar. Among the EA techniques, SPEA and NSGA-II have better
quality for larger datasets than Pareto Dominance. However, for the ﬁrst round
of bidding, the Pareto dominance technique performed better than the other EA
techniques with less variation, and the highest variation is given by the SPEA
technique.

5

Conclusion

We investigate the eﬀectiveness, in solution quality and time performance, of various Multi-Objective Optimization (MOO) methods in tackling the CRA problem in the particular case of electricity consumption. In this context, we conducted a set of experiments comparing GA with multi-objective, single-objective,
and MOO techniques (Pareto-dominance, SPEA, and NSGA-II) to evaluate the
eﬀectiveness of solutions in terms of time and solution quality. In the near future,
we plan to explore other nature-inspired and evolutionary techniques [25–27]
with multi-objective optimization to determine the best trade-oﬀ.
